
THE POLITICAL ECONOMY OF 
THE CREATIVE AND CULTURAL 
INDUSTRIES 
The creative and cultural industries (CCIs) are often seen by markets as criti‑
cal drivers of economic growth and by governments as feasible ways to foster 
development. The link between these two perspectives lies in the CCIs’ capac‑
ity to generate both economic value and social innovation simultaneously. 
While markets value their direct contribution to GDP and employment, gov‑
ernments recognise their potential to catalyse broader societal development 
through cultural expression, innovation and community engagement. 
Divided into key sections covering theoretical foundations, economic 
growth and development, innovation, policy and data analysis, this book pre‑
sents a multifaceted view of CCIs’ role in modern economies and societies. It 
challenges traditional economic models, advocates for a holistic approach to 
socio‑economic development and examines the transformative impact of dig‑
ital technologies on creative work. This work makes signifcant contributions 
to the feld by advancing the political economy of CCIs, synthesising current 
debates and clarifying critical concepts. It explores innovative approaches to 
measuring the economic impact of CCIs and proposes a roadmap for har ‑
nessing their full potential in driving sustainable and inclusive growth. With 
its fexible structure allowing for customised learning experiences, this book 
is an essential resource for understanding the complexities of creative indus‑
tries in our rapidly changing world. 
This interdisciplinary volume bridges theory and practice, providing in‑
valuable insights relevant to students of CCIs, economics, arts and cultural 
management, as well as for academics, policymakers and practitioners. 
Leandro Valiati Senior Lecturer in Creative and Cultural Industries at the 
University of Manchester, UK.

University of Valencia taught me the importance of timing; Sorbonne (Paris

INTRODUCTION 
1.1 An Infnitely Personal, Atomic‑Scale Preface: Serendipity’s 
Role in My Creative and Cultural Industries Journey 
My journey into the creative and cultural industries (CCIs) began with a 
chance encounter, like the start of an unexpected song. Imagine a young eco‑
nomics student wandering through the campus of the Federal University of 
Rio Grande do Sul in Porto Alegre, Brazil. This southern city, known as the 
“Brazilian Manchester” for its lively rock scene, art, cold and erratic weather 
and progressive spirit, was about to shape my career. 
That day, the unpredictable parallel 30 weather unleashed a heavy down‑
pour. Looking for shelter, I ducked into a university café, its aroma of me‑
diocre espresso starkly contrasted with the noise of typical university people. 
As I shook of the droplets, my eyes wandered across the bulletin board – a 
kaleidoscope of campus life. Amidst concert posters (The Strokes were com‑
ing to town!) and thesis defence announcements, a particular fyer caught 
my attention: a call for papers on Culture and Economics sponsored by the 
Brazilian Development Bank. 
At that moment – bang! – two worlds collided. As an economics student 
whose heartbeat is more in time with Beatles chord progressions than with 
the steady rhythm of equilibrium microeconomics equations, this seemed 
like a harmonious blend of my passions. Music and economics tell stories 
anyway – one beautiful and soul‑stirring, the other often cynical yet crucial 
to understanding our world. I have always believed that economics ultimately 
shapes lenses to understand well‑being, happiness, human behaviour and the 
positive and negative impacts of our choices as a society. Arts, among several 
other things, are pretty much the same. Why not bridge these narratives? 
 
  
2 The Political Economy of the Creative and Cultural Industries 
Inspired, I wrote a paper on artistic value and public arts funding. To my 
absolute surprise, my work impressed the judges (or perhaps the Strokes fy‑
ers got all the attention from the prize call), winning me a prize and a chance 
to present it at a meeting of lusophone economists in Portugal and Angola. 
That was the scariest (nothing to do with the old and good A340) and one of 
the most beautiful fights I ever made. Was it just luck? Maybe. But it wasn’t 
random. It felt like the start of something bigger. 
More lines were added to this song sheet: a master’s degree, then a PhD, 
each step building on the last. I found myself teaching and researching the 
Economics of Arts and Culture, a nice mixture appealing to diferent aca‑
demic groups. My work took me to teach and research in universities world‑
wide – France, Spain, Italy and the United Kingdom – each country adding to 
my understanding of creative industries. I received awards, including one for 
outstanding academic work in Creative Industries (hey, British Academy). 
I’ve written books and papers – often juggling with deadlines after long peri‑
ods of “cracking the code.” I’ve worked with UNESCO, OEI, UNCTAD and 
the UN, adding more global perspectives to my research. I’ve spoken to vari‑
ous audiences in over 20 countries about creativity, culture and economics. 
Now I’m based in Manchester  –  maybe the British version of Porto 
Alegre? – where new ideas fow as steadily as the rain in this revolutionary 
and fabulous city. From here, I invite students, artists and curious minds: I 
hope you fnd your own moment of inspiration in this book. Whether seeking 
shelter from a metaphorical storm or just passing the time, I hope these pages 
ofer ideas that might help any element in your path through CCIs. 
After all, isn’t that what art and economics try to do? To change views, 
question assumptions and create new harmonies from the mix of human ex‑
periences? So, as you start this journey through the CCIs, remember that the 
most beautiful song sometimes begins with a single, unexpected note. 
Looking back, I realise this book contains everything I wish I’d known 
when I frst saw that fyer in Porto Alegre. It’s a collection of knowledge, 
experiences and insights I’ve gathered from many sources throughout my 
career – from academic theories to real‑world applications, from policy dis‑
cussions to industry innovations. This book represents the connections I’ve 
made and the references I’ve collected, now combined into a comprehensive 
resource. I hope it serves as both a guide and motivation for those starting 
their adventures in the CCIs, ofering a foundation and perspective for new‑
comers and stablished creatives in this academic, policy and practical feld. 
1.2 The Creative Industries Atelier: A Sewing Kit  
for the 21st Century 
Welcome, dear reader, to a unique journey through the landscape of CCIs. 
This book is not just a collection of pages bound together; it’s a carefully cu‑
rated sewing kit flled with threads of various colours, textures and strengths. 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
Introduction 3 
Each chapter is a scissor, each concept a needle, and each theory a stitch tech‑
nique. With these tools, you’re invited to craft your creation of understand‑
ing, policy and innovation in CCIs. 
1.2.1 A Patchwork of Ideas 
In the ground‑breaking novel Hopscotch, Julio Cortazar challenged the lin‑
ear nature of storytelling; I invite you to approach this book in a similar 
non‑linear fashion. Start wherever your curiosity leads you. Jump from chap‑
ter to chapter. Create your pattern through the array of ideas presented here. 
There’s no wrong way to read this book – only diferent designs to discover 
and insights to uncover. Play and have a fun journey. 
1.2.2 The Threads We Ofer 
In this sewing kit, you’ll fnd threads of various hues and textures. Each rep‑
resents a unique contribution to the feld of CCI studies: 
1 The Golden Thread of Bridge‑building: the book combines theoretical 
backgrounds rarely combined in CCI studies, creating fresh insights. By 
intertwining economic theory with cultural studies, sociology and policy, 
you will fnd a multifaceted perspective on the value and impact of creative 
industries. This thread allows you to stitch together concepts from diverse 
felds, revealing patterns and connections previously hidden from view. 
Too often, these felds exist in separate silos, but the reality of creative 
industries demands a more integrated approach. Use this thread to weave 
together insights from multiple disciplines, creating a more robust, more 
resilient fabric of understanding. 
2 The Political Economy Silk: A delicate yet strong fbre that contributes to 
establishing the political economy of CCIs as a vital feld of study. This 
thread illuminates the complex interplay between creative production, 
market forces and policy decisions. By incorporating it into your work, 
you’ll gain a nuanced understanding of how power structures and eco‑
nomic systems shape the creative landscape. 
3 The National Models Cotton: A versatile thread that captures ongoing 
debates in academia, policy and markets regarding Creative Industries 
Models at the national level. From the UK’s DCMS model to Brazil’s Plan 
of Creative Economy, this thread allows you to compare diferent ap‑
proaches, adapting and combining elements to suit your context. 
4 The Conceptual Wool: A warm and fuzzy strand that helps defne and 
diferentiate creative industries, creative economy and cultural industries 
for academic and policy purposes. This thread ofers clarity and precision 
in a feld often plagued by terminological ambiguity. Use it to craft clear 
arguments and develop well‑defned policy proposals. 
 
 
 
 
 
4 The Political Economy of the Creative and Cultural Industries 
5 The Data and Policy Polyester: A durable, easy‑care thread that discusses 
data production context for CCIs and sources and summarises histori‑
cal and contemporary policy debates. This practical thread grounds your 
work in empirical evidence and real‑world policy contexts, ensuring that 
your creative piece is beautiful but also functional and relevant. 
1.2.3 Crafting Your Understanding 
As you fip through these pages, think of yourself as an artisan. Pick up the 
threads that intrigue you. Combine them in ways that spark your creativity. 
Use the needles of critical thinking to stitch together concepts. Apply the 
stitches of practical experience to protect yourself from oversimplifcation. 
You might start by threading the golden needle of new knowledge, creat‑
ing a piece that spans academic disciplines. For instance, you could explore 
how economic innovation theories intersect with sociological concepts of cul‑
tural capital, revealing new insights into the dynamics of creative clusters. Or 
you’ll prefer to embroider with the political economy silk and the national 
models’ cotton, crafting a policy proposal that’s both theoretically sound and 
practically applicable. This could lead to a comparative analysis of how dif‑
ferent national strategies for promoting CCIs interact with global economic 
forces, potentially revealing best practices for nurturing local creative econo‑
mies in a globalised world. For those more inclined towards data‑driven ap‑
proaches, try combining the data and policy polyester with the conceptual 
wool. This could result in a rigorous empirical study that not only measures 
the economic impact of CCIs but also grapples with the thorny question of 
how to quantify cultural value. 
1.2.4 A Living Creation 
Remember, the piece you create with this book is not meant to be static. It 
should be dynamic, adaptable and open to constant reimagination like the 
CCIs. As new threads emerge in technological advancements, policy shifts or 
cultural trends, don’t hesitate to incorporate them into your work. Consider, 
for example, how the rise of artifcial intelligence is transforming creative 
processes across industries. Or refect on how the global pandemic has ac‑
celerated digital transformation in the cultural sector. Your creation should 
evolve to refect these changes, incorporating new patterns and textures as 
the landscape of CCIs continues to shift. 
1.2.5 An Invitation to Co‑Create 
This book is not just a monologue but an invitation to dialogue. I encourage 
you to share your creations as you work with the threads and tools provided 
here. How have you combined diferent concepts? What new patterns have 
you discovered? What policy proposals have you crafted? 
 
  
Introduction 5 
By sharing creations, we develop a rich, diverse and ever‑expanding under‑
standing of CCIs. We become co‑creators of knowledge, collectively pushing 
the boundaries of what’s possible in this dynamic feld. 
So, dear reader, are you ready to pick up your needle? The world of CCIs 
awaits your unique perspective. Let’s start sewing, and together, let’s craft a 
future where creativity, culture and commerce intertwine in beautiful and 
sustainable ways. 
Welcome to your Creative Industries sewing kit. The frst stitch awaits 
your inspiration! 
1.3 Introduction: The Rise of CCIs: From the Dawn of 
Globalisation in 2000 to the Fractured World of 2024 
As Big Ben chimed in with the arrival of the year 2000, most of the large 
crowd hadn’t anticipated the massive changes that would sweep through the 
global creative, cultural and economic landscape in the next decade. The new 
millennium launched an era when creativity, technology and content would 
become the lifeblood of a new economy, challenging and invigorating our 
understanding of work, culture and innovation. 
The dawn of the 21st century was a transformative era for digital commu‑
nication and social media, with the years 2000–2010 witnessing the birth and 
explosive growth of platforms that would fundamentally alter global connec‑
tivity and information sharing. This decade saw a cascade of innovations that 
reshaped the digital landscape: Wikipedia emerged in 2001, revolutionising 
collaborative knowledge sharing; LinkedIn launched in 2003, professional‑
ising online networking; Facebook debuted in 2004, swiftly becoming the 
dominant social networking site; YouTube arrived in 2005, democratising 
video sharing; Twitter introduced microblogging and real‑time updates in 
2006; and the iPhone’s 2007 release heralded the smartphone era, paving 
the way for mobile‑frst services and media usage. These developments col‑
lectively laid the foundation for a hyperconnected and digital‑centric world, 
forever changing how people interact, consume information and express 
themselves online. 
In the same context, the world saw the aftermath of Cool Britannia, and 
the United Kingdom found itself at the forefront of a global movement to rec‑
ognise and nurture the economic potential of creativity. The Department for 
Culture, Media and Sport boldly declared creative industries rooted in “indi‑
vidual creativity, skill and talent” were the new drivers for economic growth. 
This proclamation resonated far beyond our shores, inspiring policymakers 
worldwide to reconsider the value of sectors once dismissed as marginal. 
The world was shrinking, or so it seemed. Globalisation, a force gathering 
momentum since the fall of the Berlin Wall, suddenly accelerated. Borders 
became more porous, not just to goods and capital but also to ideas and 
cultural expressions. In this fertile soil, the seeds of the creative industries 
began to sprout and fourish. Picture a fashion designer in Manchester, her 
 
 
 
 
 
6 The Political Economy of the Creative and Cultural Industries 
creations no longer confned to local boutiques in the Northern Quarter but 
fnding their way into New York and Tokyo wardrobes. Imagine a musician 
in Bristol, his melodies reaching ears in Sydney and São Paulo, all at the click 
of a button. The world had become a vast, interconnected stage, and crea‑
tives were eager to take their bow. 
This shift wasn’t limited to any one country or industry. From Silicon 
Valley startups to European design houses, from Asian tech hubs to African 
mobile payment systems, a wave of creative disruption began to reshape so‑
cial and economic exchanges worldwide. Traditional business models were 
revolutionised, while new forms of artistic expression fourished in the digital 
realm. The lines between disciplines blurred as technology and art, science, 
and design began to interconnect in unprecedented ways. 
Yet, this global stage was not a level playing feld. The transformation also 
brought challenges, such as the concentration of power and a new, somewhat 
global division of labour. The international division of labour in the crea‑
tive economy often mirrored existing global inequalities. Following histori‑
cal paradigms in past industrial revolutions, the 21st‑century revolution also 
depended on placing countries (and people) at diferent stages to generate 
unbalanced results and maximise profts. While some sectors thrived in this 
new creative economy, others needed help to adapt. The rapid pace of change 
left many wondering about the future of work and the skills required to suc‑
ceed in this evolving landscape. 
While developed nations like the United Kingdom, the United States of 
America and parts of Europe positioned themselves as hubs of innovation 
and high‑value creative work, developing countries often found themselves 
relegated to roles of content production or digital labour. The promise of a 
democratised creative economy was tempered by unequal access to technol‑
ogy, education and global markets. This disparity led to a new form of crea‑
tive colonialism, where ideas and cultural products from the Global North 
dominated. In contrast, creative voices from the Global South struggled for 
recognition and fair compensation in the international arena. 
1.3.1 The Shifting Landscape: 2000 vs 2020 
The world underwent a profound transformation between 2000 and 2020, 
marked by signifcant shifts in globalisation, political climate, confict, and eco‑
nomic policies. The dawn of the new millennium was characterised by unbri‑
dled optimism towards globalisation, fuelled by the fall of the Berlin Wall and 
the end of the Cold War, which seemed to herald an era of increased interna‑
tional cooperation and economic integration. However, by 2020, this optimism 
had given way to a more nuanced and often sceptical view, as the 2008 fnan‑
cial crisis exposed the vulnerabilities of an interconnected global economy. 
 
 
 
Introduction 7 
The political landscape also experienced a dramatic shift during this 
period. The year 2000 saw many Western democracies embracing centrist 
“Third Way” politics, exemplifed by fgures like Tony Blair in the United 
Kingdom and Bill Clinton in the US, which sought to blend market econom‑
ics with social welfare policies. In stark contrast, by 2020, political centrism 
had largely given way to increased polarisation, with the rise of populist 
movements on both the right and left challenging established political norms. 
Events such as Brexit in the United Kingdom, the election of Donald Trump 
in the US, and the growth of far‑right parties in Europe signalled a shift away 
from globalisation and towards nationalism. 
The nature of war and confict evolved signifcantly during these two dec‑
ades. While regional conficts persisted in 2000, including the ongoing ten‑
sions between Israel and Palestine, there was hope for a more peaceful world 
order following the end of the Cold War. By 2020, however, the landscape 
of confict had dramatically shifted. The 9/11 attacks and subsequent “War 
on Terror” ushered in an era of asymmetric warfare and non‑state actors, 
while the rise of cyber warfare and information warfare blurred the lines be‑
tween war and peace. The Israel‑Palestine confict remained unresolved, and 
Russia’s annexation of Crimea in 2014 and the subsequent confict in eastern 
Ukraine marked a return to more traditional geopolitical tensions. 
The economic policy landscape witnessed a notable transformation. The 
early 2000s saw a continued push towards free trade and fnancial liber ‑
alisation. However, by 2020, protectionist sentiments had surged in many 
countries. The US‑China trade war, initiated under the Trump administra‑
tion’s “America First” policy, marked a signifcant shift away from free trade 
orthodoxy, while even traditionally free‑trade advocates like the United 
Kingdom grappled with new trade realities post‑Brexit. 
The COVID‑19 pandemic, which emerged in late 2019 and spread glob‑
ally in 2020, was a stark punctuation mark to these two decades of change. 
It further exposed the vulnerabilities of global supply chains and interdepend‑
ence, accelerated the adoption of digital technologies and amplifed existing 
political and economic tensions. The pandemic challenged the foundations 
of globalisation, forcing countries to close borders, restrict travel and priori‑
tise national interests in ways unseen since World War II. It also highlighted 
global inequalities, particularly in healthcare access and economic resilience, 
and intensifed the debate about balancing international cooperation and na‑
tional self‑reliance. 
This period thus encapsulates a dramatic shift from a world of optimism, 
globalism and economic openness to one characterised by scepticism, po‑
larisation and protectionist tendencies, further complicated by persistent re‑
gional conficts, emerging geopolitical challenges and a global health crisis 
that reshaped societies worldwide. 
 
 
8 The Political Economy of the Creative and Cultural Industries 
1.3.2 Impact on Creative Industries 
These shifts have profoundly impacted the creative industries, reshaping the 
landscape in which artists, content creators and cultural institutions oper ‑
ate. The digital transformation, while not a new phenomenon, has acceler ‑
ated dramatically, creating a paradoxical environment where globalisation 
in the traditional sense may have slowed, but digital globalisation has surged 
forward. This dual trend has opened unprecedented opportunities for crea‑
tive industries to reach global audiences, while simultaneously intensifying 
competition on an international scale. Content creators compete for atten‑
tion in a vast digital marketplace where geographic boundaries have become 
increasingly irrelevant. 
In response to these changes, a trend towards cultural protectionism has 
emerged in various countries. Governments, recognising their creative sectors’ 
economic and cultural signifcance, have implemented measures to safeguard 
local industries. These range from content quotas for streaming services, en‑
suring a certain percentage of locally produced material, to subsidies for do‑
mestic flm production. Such policies aim to preserve cultural diversity and 
support local creative economies in the face of global competition. 
Geopolitical tensions have cast a long shadow over the creative industries, 
afecting cultural exchanges and collaborations in ways that were less pro‑
nounced at the turn of the millennium. The complex relationship between 
major powers, particularly the United States and China, has led to restric‑
tions on technology companies that have become integral to content dis‑
tribution. The challenges faced by platforms like TikTok in some Western 
countries exemplify how geopolitical considerations can directly impact crea‑
tive platforms’ global reach and operations. 
The COVID‑19 pandemic served as a catalyst for rapid and widespread 
change within the creative industries. Faced with lockdowns and social dis‑
tancing measures, these sectors swiftly adapted to a world of limited physical 
interaction. This unprecedented situation accelerated existing trends towards 
digital consumption and virtual experiences. Museums launched virtual 
tours, musicians streamed concerts from their homes, and flm festivals went 
online. These adaptations helped the creative industries survive a challeng‑
ing period and opened new avenues for audience engagement and content 
distribution that are likely to persist and evolve in the post‑pandemic world. 
These developments collectively paint a picture of creative industries in‑
fux, navigating a complex landscape shaped by technological innovation, 
geopolitical realities and global crises. As they move forward, these industries 
must continue to adapt, fnding ways to harness the opportunities presented 
by digital globalisation while addressing the challenges of increased competi‑
tion, cultural preservation and rapidly changing consumer behaviours. 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
Introduction 9 
1.3.3 The Path Forward for Creative Industries 
As we move further into the 2020s, the creative industries face a landscape of 
both unprecedented challenges and exciting opportunities. The sector must 
navigate this complex terrain, balancing the opportunities of digital globali‑
sation with the challenges of an increasingly fragmented and uncertain world 
order. Some crucial elements to be considered are: 
1 Embracing Digital Transformation: The acceleration of digital trends 
presents a double‑edged sword. On one hand, it ofers creatives unprec‑
edented access to global audiences and new forms of expression. For in‑
stance, virtual reality (VR) and augmented reality (AR) technologies are 
opening up new frontiers in immersive storytelling and experiential art. 
On the other hand, the digital realm brings challenges of oversaturation, 
shorter attention spans, and the need for constant innovation to stand out 
in a crowded digital marketplace. 
2 Navigating Cultural Protectionism: As nations become more protective of 
their cultural heritage and domestic industries, creative professionals must 
respect local sensibilities while pushing for global cultural exchange. This 
might involve more co‑productions, cultural fusion projects or leveraging 
digital platforms to bypass traditional gatekeepers. The challenge will be 
maintaining artistic integrity and freedom of expression in the face of po‑
tential censorship or market access restrictions. 
3 Adapting to Geopolitical Realities: The creative industries will need to 
become more adept at navigating complex geopolitical landscapes. This 
might involve diversifying supply chains, building more resilient distribu‑
tion networks or fnding innovative ways to collaborate across borders 
despite political tensions. The creative sector can also play a role in foster‑
ing international understanding and dialogue through cultural diplomacy. 
4 Post‑Pandemic Resilience: The lessons learned during the COVID‑19 pan‑
demic will continue to shape the sector. This includes the acceleration of 
digital consumption and a renewed appreciation for live, in‑person experi‑
ences. Creative industries must develop hybrid models that can seamlessly 
blend digital and physical elements, ensuring resilience against future dis‑
ruptions while meeting evolving consumer expectations. 
5 Addressing Inequality: As the world grapples with growing inequality, 
creative industries have a role in refecting these challenges and proposing 
solutions. This might involve more diverse storytelling, inclusive hiring 
practices or using art and media to raise awareness about social issues. 
There’s also a need to address inequality within the creative sector it‑
self, ensuring fair compensation and opportunities for creators from all 
backgrounds. 
 
 
 
 
  
10 The Political Economy of the Creative and Cultural Industries 
6 Sustainability and Ethics: With increasing awareness of climate change 
and ethical consumption, creative industries must prioritise sustainability 
in their practices. This could range from eco‑friendly flm productions to 
sustainable fashion design. Creatives can also lead the way in imagining 
and promoting more sustainable futures through their work. 
7 Balancing AI and Human Creativity: As artifcial intelligence becomes 
more sophisticated, creative industries must fnd the right balance between 
leveraging AI tools and preserving the unique value of human creativity. 
This might involve using AI for tasks like data analysis or essential content 
generation while focusing human eforts on higher‑level creative direction 
and emotional storytelling. 
8 New Business Models: The changing landscape will necessitate innovative 
business models. This could include more direct‑to‑consumer approaches, 
subscription‑based services or new collective ownership and proft‑sharing 
forms in creative projects. Blockchain technology, for instance, might rev‑
olutionise how intellectual property is managed and monetised. 
In navigating these challenges and opportunities, the creative industries have 
a chance to adapt to the new global realities and play a leading role in shap‑
ing them. By fostering cross‑cultural understanding, imagining new possibili‑
ties and giving voice to diverse perspectives, the creative sector can help build 
bridges in an increasingly fragmented world. 
As we look to the future, the resilience, adaptability and innovative spirit 
that have always characterised the creative industries will be more crucial 
than ever. By embracing change while staying true to the core values of crea‑
tivity and cultural expression, these industries can continue to thrive, inspire 
and drive positive change in the years to come. 
1.4 About This Book: Navigating the Complex Landscape  
of CCIs 
This book ofers a comprehensive and multifaceted exploration of CCIs. 
These dynamic and rapidly evolving industry sectors have gained signifcant 
attention from academics, policymakers and practitioners in recent years. 
Our approach to this complex subject is deliberately interdisciplinary and 
fexible, designed to accommodate our readers’ diverse interests and needs. 
The Political Economy of Cultural and Creative Industries represents an in‑
terdisciplinary framework that examines the complex relationships amongst 
creative production, market forces, and policy decisions in the cultural and 
creative sectors. What distinguishes PECCIs is its normative approach, which 
goes beyond merely analysing how things are (positivist) to questioning and 
proposing how they should be. 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
Introduction 11 
1.4.1 Structure and Approach 
This book begins by unpacking the fundamental concepts of cultural indus‑
tries, creative industries, and the creative economy (Chapter 1). It explores how 
these interrelated ideas have evolved, shaping our understanding of creativity’s 
role in economic and social development. The text then shifts gears to examine 
how CCIs ft into economic growth theory (Chapter 2), challenging traditional 
models and showcasing how these industries, with their unique characteristics, 
push the boundaries of how we think about economic growth. 
The book broadens its scope beyond conventional economic metrics to 
examine CCIs’ impact on socio‑economic development (Chapter 3). It ad‑
vocates for a more holistic view of progress, exploring how creative indus‑
tries contribute to social inclusion, well‑being and cultural preservation. The 
focus then narrows to the workforce (Chapter 4), examining how digital 
transformation and the gig economy reshape creative occupations (structure) 
and how the workers respond to that using tactics tackling the challenges and 
opportunities facing creative professionals in this rapidly evolving landscape. 
The narrative explores the symbiotic relationship between CCIs and innova‑
tion (Chapter 5), illustrating how CCIs are at the forefront of “creative de‑
struction,” constantly reshaping cultural production and consumption. The 
book then takes a global perspective, presenting a comparative analysis of 
public policies for CCIs across various countries (Chapter 6). It highlights the 
diverse ways governments support and shape their creative sectors and the 
challenges they face in doing so. Grappling with the complex task of measur‑
ing CCIs’ economic impact, the text explores innovative approaches to cap‑
ture the full value of creative activities. It moves beyond traditional economic 
indicators to more nuanced, context‑sensitive frameworks (Chapter 7). The 
book concludes by looking ahead and exploring the future role of CCIs in 
economic development (Chapter 8). It outlines key areas for future research 
and policy focus, proposing a roadmap for harnessing the full potential of 
creative industries in driving sustainable and inclusive growth. 
Within these sections, readers will fnd chapters that can be read inde‑
pendently or in conjunction with others, allowing for a customised learning 
experience. 
1.4.2 Key Contributions 
This volume aims to make several signifcant contributions to the feld of CCI 
studies: 
1 Interdisciplinary Knowledge Creation: ofering fresh insights and perspec‑
tives by connecting theoretical backgrounds from diverse disciplines in di‑
alogue with CCIs. This multidisciplinary approach combines mainstream 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
12 The Political Economy of the Creative and Cultural Industries 
and heterodox economic theory, cultural studies, sociology and other rel‑
evant felds to understand CCIs comprehensively. 
2 Advancing the Political Economy of CCIs: contributing to establishing the 
political economy of CCIs as a distinct and signifcant feld of study within 
creative and cultural studies, both in the United Kingdom and globally, 
by exploring the complex interplay between creative production, market 
forces and policy decisions. 
3 Synthesising Current Debates: This synthesis brings together various ongo‑
ing debates in academia, policy and markets regarding Creative Industries 
Models at the national level. It provides readers with a comprehensive 
overview of current thinking and practice in the feld. 
4 Conceptual Clarifcation: By exercising a theoretical analysis, we aim to 
clarify and diferentiate critical concepts such as creative, creative econ‑
omy and cultural industries. This conceptual work is crucial for both aca‑
demic studies and policy formulation. 
5 Data Sources and Policy Analysis: serving as a handbook, compiling rel‑
evant data sources for further research and summarising historical and 
contemporary debates in CCIs and social‑economic development. 
1.4.3 Intended Audience 
This book is designed to serve a broad audience, including: 
• Academics and researchers in the felds of CCIs, arts management, eco‑
nomics, cultural studies and related disciplines 
• Policymakers and government ofcials involved in CCI policy 
• Practitioners and professionals working in various sectors of the CCIs 
• Students at undergraduate and postgraduate levels studying CCIs, eco‑
nomics, arts management, cultural policy or related subjects 
• Any person interested in recent transformations in the world 
In presenting this comprehensive exploration of CCIs, the readers are in‑
vited to engage critically with the ideas, debates and evidence presented. The 
non‑linear structure of the book encourages active engagement. It allows 
readers to create their pathways through the material, refecting the dynamic 
and interconnected nature of the creative industries. This volume will con‑
tribute to the academic discourse on CCIs and inform policy decisions and 
industry practices. In an era where creativity and culture play an increasingly 
central role in our economies and societies, understanding the complexities 
of these industries is more crucial than ever.

CREATIVE AND CULTURAL INDUSTRIES 
An Overview 
The intersection of creativity, culture and economics has given rise to a 
complex and evolving conceptual landscape. This framework explores three 
interconnected concepts: economy of culture, creative industries, cultural in‑
dustries and the creative economy. Each concept ofers a nuanced perspective 
on the role of creativity and culture in economic and social development, 
refecting shifts in policy approaches and academic discourse over the past 
few decades. While there is considerable overlap between these concepts, 
they each emphasise diferent aspects of the relationship between creativity, 
culture and economic activity. Understanding these nuances is crucial for 
policymakers, researchers and practitioners. Below, you can fnd a theoreti‑
cal exercise that will serve as a reference for this book each time these terms 
are used: 
1.1 Economy of Creative and Cultural Industries 
The economy of creative and cultural industries (CCIs) is a broad concept 
encompassing the application of economic science theoretical tools in cul‑
tural production, distribution and consumption. It recognises culture as a 
source of intrinsic value and a driver of economic activity. This concept has 
evolved and is approached from various perspectives, emphasising diferent 
aspects of the relationship between culture and economics. The mainstream 
approach to the economy of culture, often aligned with neoclassical eco‑
nomic thinking, focuses primarily on the measurable economic impacts of 
cultural activities. It emphasises culture’s potential as a resource for economic 
growth, promoting cultural entrepreneurship and market‑oriented strategies. 
This perspective has been infuential in shaping policies that aim to maximise 
 
 
 
 
14 The Political Economy of the Creative and Cultural Industries 
the economic potential of cultural assets, often through commercialisation 
and investment in cultural infrastructure. 
In contrast, the political economy of CCIs ofers a critical framework that 
examines how economic structures, political institutions and power dynam‑
ics infuence cultural production and consumption. This approach challenges 
the commodifcation of culture and the application of market logic to all 
cultural spheres. It highlights issues such as labour conditions in creative 
industries, the role of large corporations in shaping cultural content and the 
global fows of cultural goods and services. The political economy perspective 
argues that cultural production and consumption are not neutral processes 
but are deeply embedded in broader socioeconomic and political contexts. 
It calls attention to inequality, cultural diversity and the sustainability of 
growth‑oriented cultural economy strategies. This approach emphasises the 
need to consider cultural policies’ broader social and political implications 
beyond their economic impacts. 
Both perspectives contribute to our understanding of the economy of 
CCIs, albeit with diferent emphases. The mainstream approach has been 
instrumental in elevating the status of culture in economic policy discussions. 
At the same time, the political economy of CCIs perspective provides a cru‑
cial counterpoint, highlighting the complexities and potential pitfalls of view‑
ing culture primarily through a fnancial lens. 
1.2 Creative Economy 
The creative economy represents the most comprehensive and expansive of 
these concepts. As defned by the United Nations Conference on Trade and 
Development (UNCTAD), it’s “an evolving concept based on creative as‑
sets potentially generating economic growth and development” (UNCTAD, 
2008). This approach extends beyond specifc industries, considering how 
creativity, knowledge and information catalyse economic growth and devel‑
opment in an increasingly globalised world. 
The creative economy paradigm represents a fundamental shift in under ‑
standing the role of creativity and culture in economic and social develop‑
ment. It suggests that in the 21st century, fnancial success will increasingly 
depend on the ability to generate ideas, innovate, and leverage intellectual 
capital rather than relying solely on traditional factors of production. 
Key features of the creative economy include: 
1 Integration of economic, cultural, technological and social aspects of 
development 
2 Emphasis on innovation across all economic sectors, not just traditionally 
“creative” or “cultural” ones 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
Creative and Cultural Industries: An Overview 15 
3 Recognition of the growing importance of intellectual capital and intangi‑
ble assets in economic growth 
4 Promotion of social inclusion, cultural diversity and human development 
5 Incorporation of both formal and informal creative activities 
6 Focus on the role of digital technologies and the internet in facilitating 
creative production and distribution 
7 Recognition of the importance of creative clusters and ecosystems in fos‑
tering innovation 
The creative economy approach has gained signifcant traction in policy cir ‑
cles, with many countries and cities developing strategies to foster their crea‑
tive economies. For instance, the European Union’s New European Agenda 
for Culture emphasises the role of culture and creativity in social cohesion 
and economic growth (European Commission, 2018). 
Moreover, the concept of the creative economy has expanded to include 
ideas such as the “Orange Economy” in Latin America, which emphasises 
the transformative potential of creativity and culture in developing econo‑
mies (Buitrago & Duque, 2013). It also aligns with broader economic trends 
such as the experience and knowledge economies. 
Some critics argue that this concept can sometimes oversimplify complex 
cultural and social processes, potentially leading to policies prioritising 
economic outcomes over cultural and social values. Given the intangible 
nature of many of its outputs, there’s also an ongoing debate about how 
to measure the creative economy efectively. Despite these challenges, the 
creative economy remains a robust framework for understanding the evolv‑
ing role of creativity and culture in contemporary economic and social 
development. 
1.3 Creative Industries 
The concept of creative industries, frst popularised by the UK’s Department 
for Culture, Media and Sport (DCMS) in 1998, marked a signifcant shift 
in understanding the economic potential of creativity (DCMS, 1998). Ac‑
cording to the DCMS, creative industries are “those originating in individual 
creativity, skill and talent and which have a potential for wealth and job 
creation through the generation and exploitation of intellectual property” 
(DCMS, 2001). 
This defnition encompasses various sectors, including advertising, archi‑
tecture, art, crafts, design, fashion, flm, music, performing arts, publishing, 
software, television and radio, and video games (UNESCO, 2013). The crea‑
tive industries approach emphasises the economic value of creativity, viewing 
it as a resource that can drive innovation, economic growth and job creation. 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
16 The Political Economy of the Creative and Cultural Industries 
Key characteristics of creative industries include: 
1 Reliance on individual creativity and talent 
2 Generation and exploitation of intellectual property 
3 Utilisation of new technologies, particularly ICT 
4 Creation of both economic and cultural value 
5 Potential for innovation and cross‑sector spillovers 
6 A high degree of risk and uncertainty in product development 
7 Short product lifecycles and rapid obsolescence 
The creative industries concept has gained widespread adoption in policy 
circles and academia. It has infuenced urban development strategies, with 
many cities worldwide seeking to foster creative clusters or districts (Landry, 
2000). The approach has also been linked to ideas of the ‘creative class’ and 
its role in economic development (Florida, 2002). 
However, the creative industries concept has faced criticism. Some schol‑
ars argue that it overly commodifes culture and creativity, potentially ne‑
glecting forms of creative expression that don’t have immediate commercial 
value (Galloway & Dunlop, 2007). Others point out that the focus on in‑
dividual creativity may overlook the collaborative nature of much creative 
work (Pratt, 2008). Besides the criticisms, this concept remains infuential, 
evolving in response to technological changes and shifting economic para‑
digms. Recent developments include increased attention to the role of digital 
platforms in creative production and distribution and growing recognition of 
the importance of creative skills across the broader economy. 
1.4 Cultural Industries 
The concept of cultural industries, as defned by UNESCO, focuses on sectors 
that combine the creation, production and commercialisation of intangible 
and cultural content (UNESCO, 2009). This approach emphasises the dual 
nature of cultural goods and services, recognising their economic and cul‑
tural value. 
The concept of cultural industries has its roots in critical theory, par ‑
ticularly the work of Adorno and Horkheimer (2002), who used the term 
‘culture industry’ to critique the commodifcation of culture. Over time, the 
concept has evolved to encompass a more neutral, descriptive approach to 
understanding how cultural production intersects with economic systems. 
Key aspects of cultural industries include: 
1 Focus on intangible, cultural content 
2 Emphasis on cultural diversity and heritage preservation 
3 Recognition of both economic and cultural value 
 
 
 
 
 
 
Creative and Cultural Industries: An Overview 17 
4 Promotion of democratic access to culture 
5 Acknowledgement of the role of public institutions and policy in support‑
ing cultural production 
6 Consideration of non‑proft and informal cultural activities 
7 Attention to issues of cultural rights and identity 
The cultural industries approach is closely tied to cultural diversity and her ‑
itage preservation ideas. It acknowledges these industries’ crucial role in 
shaping and refecting cultural identities, promoting cultural diversity and 
ensuring democratic access to culture (Hesmondhalgh & Pratt, 2005). UNE‑
SCO’s framework for cultural statistics (2009) identifes several core cultural 
domains within the cultural industries, including cultural and natural herit‑
age, performance and celebration, visual arts and crafts, books and press, 
audio‑visual and interactive media, and design and creative services. This 
broad scope refects the diverse ways in which culture is produced and con‑
sumed in diferent societies. 
The cultural industries concept has been particularly infuential in inter ‑
national development, promoting sustainable development while preserving 
cultural diversity. It has informed UNESCO’s Convention on the Protection 
and Promotion of the Diversity of Cultural Expressions (2005), recognising 
the distinctive nature of cultural goods and services as vehicles of identity, 
values and meaning. 
Critics argue that it can sometimes lead to an overly broad defnition of 
culture, making it difcult to develop targeted policies. There’s also an on‑
going debate about balancing economic and cultural objectives within this 
framework (Throsby, 2008). Despite these challenges, cultural industries re‑
main a crucial tool for understanding the complex interplay between culture, 
economics and society in the contemporary world. 
1.5 Models and Classifcations 
Various organisations have developed diferent models and classifcations for 
CCIs. These approaches refect varying emphases and priorities, often tai‑
lored to specifc contexts or mandates. Understanding these other models is 
crucial, as the choice of defnition and classifcation can signifcantly impact 
how the sector is measured, supported and developed. 
Table 1.1 gives a comprehensive overview of the main approaches. 
Several models have been developed to categorise and understand the 
scope of creative industries, each emphasising diferent aspects of creative 
production and its economic impact. The Symbolic Texts model, primarily 
used in Europe, focuses on popular culture and how culture is produced, 
distributed and consumed. This model places core creative arts at the cen‑
tre, surrounded by core cultural industries (such as flm, broadcasting and

policymaking, particularly in Europe and East Asia, due to its emphasis on 
the economic potential of creative activities. 
 
  
 
 
 
 
Creative and Cultural Industries: An Overview 19 
The WIPO (World Intellectual Property Organization) Copyright model 
focuses on industries involved in creating, manufacturing, producing, broad‑
casting and distributing copyrighted works. This model distinguishes be‑
tween core copyright industries, interdependent copyright industries and 
partial copyright industries, refecting the varying degrees to which difer ‑
ent sectors rely on copyright protection. Other signifcant models include 
the Concentric Circles model proposed by David Throsby, which arranges 
creative industries in concentric circles based on the cultural content of their 
output, and the UNCTAD model, which divides creative industries into four 
broad groups: heritage, arts, media and functional creations. Each model 
ofers a unique perspective on the creative industries, refecting diferent pri‑
orities and understandings of the relationship between culture, creativity and 
economic activity. The choice of model can signifcantly infuence policy de‑
cisions and strategies for supporting and developing creative sectors. 
1.6 Critical Distinctions between Creative Economy, Cultural 
Industries and Creative Industries 
The creative economy concept represents a broader contemporary economic 
movement involving diverse processes and larger sectors. Analysed as inte‑
gral components of the creative economy, CCIs represent a paradigm shift 
in our understanding of creativity and culture’s role in economic and social 
development. This new model suggests that future fnancial success will in‑
creasingly depend on the ability to generate ideas and innovate rather than 
relying solely on traditional factors of production. This framework is built 
on systems that value imaginative qualities over conventional resources like 
land, labour and capital. It encompasses the production and distribution of 
innovative goods and services, considering the entire value chain – from the 
initial spark of an idea to its transformation into products and ultimate dis‑
tribution to consumers. 
An essential feature of this approach is its recognition of the complex 
interplay between economic, cultural, technological and social aspects of de‑
velopment. It acknowledges that innovation doesn’t exist in isolation but is 
part of a broader ecosystem spanning all economic sectors. This intersectoral 
perspective highlights how creative thinking can drive progress in tradition‑
ally ‘artistic’ felds and areas as diverse as healthcare, education and manu‑
facturing. This model also emphasises intellectual property rights as crucial 
tools for fostering and protecting creative endeavours, refecting the increas‑
ing importance of intangible assets in our knowledge‑based economy. 
Importantly, this concept looks beyond purely economic measures to 
consider the broader social impacts of creativity and culture. It recognises 
the non‑monetary value that innovative activities can contribute to social 
inclusion, cultural diversity and human development. This holistic view 
20 The Political Economy of the Creative and Cultural Industries 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
acknowledges that the true worth of creativity extends far beyond fnancial 
metrics. The “creative class” concept is central to this idea – knowledge 
workers, artists and other innovative professionals who are increasingly 
seen as critical economic and cultural development drivers. This under ‑
scores the importance of human capital and talent in driving growth and 
innovation. 
This paradigm shift has signifcant implications for policymaking. Govern‑
ments should focus on supporting specifc industries and fostering a broader 
ecosystem that encourages creativity across all sectors. This might include ed‑
ucational policies, urban planning, technological infrastructure and intellec‑
tual property rights, all aimed at creating an environment where innovation 
can fourish. The creative economy represents a new way of thinking about 
development that places creativity, knowledge and innovation at the heart of 
progress. As we move into the 21st century, understanding and harnessing 
this approach will be crucial for nations, cities and organisations looking to 
thrive in an increasingly idea‑driven world. 
The concepts of creative industries, cultural industries and the creative 
economy ofer diferent perspectives on the role of creativity and culture in 
economic and social development. While closely related, they each emphasise 
diferent aspects and priorities. Understanding these nuances is crucial for 
developing efective policies, measuring impact and fostering growth in these 
vital areas of the modern economy. 
As the global economy evolves, these concepts will likely continue to 
be debated, refned and reimagined. Policymakers, researchers and indus‑
try stakeholders must stay informed about these developments to efectively 
navigate the challenges and opportunities presented by the increasing impor‑
tance of creativity and culture in economic and social spheres. 
1 Scope: creative industries have the broadest scope, including any indus‑
try that relies on creativity for economic value. Cultural industries focus 
specifcally on cultural expressions and heritage. The creative economy is 
the most comprehensive, considering creativity’s broader economic, social 
and cultural impacts on the whole economy. 
2 Focus: creative industries emphasise the commercial exploitation of crea‑
tivity. Cultural industries prioritise cultural expression and preservation. 
The creative economy focuses on how creativity drives innovation and 
development across all sectors. 
3 Economic Approach: creative industries are often viewed through a more 
traditional economic lens of industry sectors. Cultural industries are seen 
as vehicles for cultural expression with fnancial implications. The crea‑
tive economy represents a paradigm shift in how creativity and culture 
contribute to economic and social development. 
Creative and Cultural Industries: An Overview 21 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
Understanding these distinctions is crucial for several reasons: 
1 Policy Development: Diferent approaches are needed for supporting crea‑
tive industries versus preserving and promoting cultural industries. The 
creative economy concept informs broader economic and social policies. 
2 Measurement and Analysis: Each concept requires diferent metrics and 
methodologies for assessing economic impact and social value. 
3 Investment and Support: The rationale for investing in or supporting these 
areas difers based on whether the focus is on commercial potential, cul‑
tural preservation or broader economic transformation. 
4 International Cooperation: A clear understanding of these concepts fa‑
cilitates more efective dialogue and collaboration in global creative and 
cultural exchanges. 
By recognising these nuances, policymakers can craft more targeted and 
practical strategies, researchers can conduct more precise comparative analy‑
ses, and industry stakeholders can better articulate their unique contributions 
and needs. This shared understanding is essential for fostering the growth of 
creative and cultural sectors while leveraging their potential to drive broader 
economic and social development in the 21st century. 
BOX 1.1 WHY A CROSS‑SECTOR GOVERNMENTAL BODY 
SHOULD CONDUCT CREATIVE ECONOMY POLICIES 
1 Economic Signifcance: The creative economy contributes signifcantly to 
GDP, job creation and innovation, extending beyond traditional cultural 
sectors. 
2 Cross‑Sectoral Nature: Intersects with technology, education, urban plan‑
ning and international trade, requiring diverse expertise. 
3 Innovation and Technology: Often at the forefront of technological inno‑
vation, needing support from tech‑oriented sectors. 
4 Business and Entrepreneurship: Requires business development, fnance 
and entrepreneurship support expertise. 
5 International Trade and Competitiveness: Signifcant in exports, requiring 
the involvement of trade and foreign afairs ministries. 
6 Education and Skills Development: Needs coordination between educa‑
tion, higher education and vocational training sectors. 
7 Urban and Regional Development: Crucial in urban regeneration and re‑
gional development strategies. 
8 Digital Infrastructure: Relies heavily on digital infrastructure, managed by 
communications or digital economy ministries.

9 Intellectual Property Rights: Extends beyond copyright to patents and 
trademarks, often managed by justice or commerce ministries. 
10 Risk of Cultural Bias: Placing it solely under a Ministry of Culture risks pri‑
oritising traditional sectors over newer, commercial creative industries. 
Recommended Approach: 
• A cross‑departmental task force or dedicated unit within economic afairs or 
innovation ministry. 
• Include strong representation from the Ministry of Culture. 
• Implement a ‘Cultural Diversity Impact Assessment’ framework. 
• Appoint a ‘Cultural Diversity and Inclusion Ofcer.’ 
• Balance economic objectives with cultural diversity promotion. 
This approach aims to harness the full potential of the creative economy while 
safeguarding cultural diversity. 
The concept of creative industries has gained signifcant traction among poli‑
cymakers and urban planners in recent decades, often presented as a panacea 
for economic growth and urban regeneration. However, this “enchantment” 
with creative industries branding poses substantial challenges for cultural 
policymakers and can lead to approaches that inadvertently harm the very 
cultural sectors they aim to support. 
The allure of creative industries often stems from their association with 
innovation, economic dynamism and urban revitalisation. However, this en‑
chantment can lead to several problematic outcomes: 
1 Overgeneralist policies driven by appealing discourse: Policymakers may 
create broad, sweeping policies based on the alluring narrative of creative 
industries. While sounding promising, these policies often need to ade‑
quately address the needs of cultural sectors with less market power, in‑
stead primarily benefting more commercially oriented creative industries. 
The result can be a widening gap between well‑funded, market‑driven cre‑
ative sectors and underfunded, less commercially viable cultural activities. 
2 One‑size‑fts‑all approaches: Blanket creative industries policies may fail 
to account for the diverse needs of diferent cultural sectors, leading to in‑
efective or even harmful interventions. This approach often overlooks the 
unique challenges various cultural practitioners and organisations face, 
particularly those operating outside mainstream commercial structures. 
3 Neglect of traditional cultural institutions: An excessive focus on “crea‑
tive” sectors might reduce support for traditional cultural institutions like 
 
  
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
Creative and Cultural Industries: An Overview 23 
museums, libraries and heritage sites, which play vital roles in preserving 
and disseminating culture. This shift can undermine long‑standing cultural 
infrastructure and practices essential for maintaining cultural diversity 
and social cohesion. 
4 Unrealistic policy scope and implementation: Policies branded under 
creative economy or creative industries should be broader and more 
ambitious to efectively implement by departments with limited politi‑
cal and budgetary power, such as Ministries of Culture acting alone. 
This can lead to well‑intentioned but ultimately unfeasible initiatives 
that fail to deliver meaningful support to the cultural sector. The mis‑
match between policy ambitions and institutional capacity can result in 
frustration and disillusionment among cultural practitioners and poli‑
cymakers alike. 
These issues are exacerbated by the signifcant heterogeneity within the 
creative industries and the power imbalances between diferent sectors. The 
creative industries encompass a wide range of activities, from multinational 
media corporations to individual artisans, each with vastly diferent needs, 
resources and market positions. This heterogeneity is characterised by: 
1 Varying degrees of commercialisation: Some sectors, like advertising or 
software development, are highly commercialised, while others, such as 
fne arts or experimental theatre, often operate on diferent economic 
models. 
2 Disparate access to resources: Larger, more commercially oriented sectors 
often have greater access to capital, technology and political infuence, po‑
tentially overshadowing minor, less commercially viable cultural activities. 
3 Diferent labour conditions vary dramatically across sectors, from stable, 
well‑paid jobs in some industries to precarious, project‑based work in 
others. 
4 Uneven policy infuence: More powerful sectors within the creative indus‑
tries often have a louder voice in policy discussions, potentially leading to 
policies favouring their interests over those of smaller or less commercially 
oriented cultural activities. 
5 Varying dependence on public support: While some creative sectors are 
primarily self‑sufcient, others rely heavily on public funding or subsidies, 
making them more vulnerable to shifts in policy priorities. 
To address these challenges, cultural policymakers need to: 
1 Develop a more nuanced understanding of the creative industries, ac‑
knowledging their diversity and the diferent roles they play in cultural, 
social and economic spheres. 
24 The Political Economy of the Creative and Cultural Industries 
 
 
 
 
 
2 Resist the temptation to view creative industries as a cure‑all for eco‑
nomic development and instead consider their broader cultural and social 
impacts. 
3 Engage in extensive consultation with various stakeholders, ensuring that 
voices from all parts of the cultural sector are heard in policy discussions. 
4 Recognise the limitations of broad creative economy or creative industries 
policies when led solely by departments lacking signifcant political and 
budgetary power. Efective implementation often requires cross‑depart‑
mental cooperation and substantial resources. 
5 Maintain a balance between supporting commercially viable creative ac‑
tivities and preserving and nurturing cultural expressions that may not 
have immediate economic value but are crucial for cultural diversity and 
social wellbeing. 
A signifcant risk lies in the tendency to overemphasise the economic aspects 
of CCIs at the expense of their cultural and social value. Policies focusing 
solely on traditional economic metrics may inadvertently prioritise commer‑
cially viable creative outputs, potentially marginalising important cultural 
expressions that don’t generate immediate profts or large audiences. More‑
over, the diverse and often informal nature of creative work is frequently 
overlooked in traditional economic models, leading to policies that may 
not adequately support freelancers, small enterprises or non‑proft cultural 
organisations. There’s also a danger of gentrifcation in urban areas where 
creative districts are developed without consideration for existing communi‑
ties. As we progress, policymakers must develop a nuanced understanding of 
CCIs, recognising their multifaceted nature and the intricate balance between 
economic, cultural and social outcomes. This requires interdisciplinary ap‑
proaches, extensive stakeholder engagement and fexible policy frameworks 
that can adapt to the rapidly changing landscape of creative production and 
consumption. 
By adopting a more critical and nuanced approach to creative industries 
policies, cultural policymakers can better support the rich tapestry of crea‑
tive and cultural activities that contribute to the vibrancy and diversity of 
our societies. This approach requires moving beyond the enchantment with 
creative industries branding to develop targeted, realistic and inclusive poli‑
cies that address the needs of the entire cultural ecosystem, not just its most 
commercially successful components. 
As the 21st century progresses, there’s a growing emphasis on harness‑
ing creative potential as a driver of economic success and social progress 
for nations, cities and organisations. However, this shift towards a “creative 
economy” paradigm carries signifcant risks if not properly understood and 
implemented. The complex nature of CCIs often leads to oversimplifcation 
Creative and Cultural Industries: An Overview 25 
 
in policy approaches, potentially resulting in strategies that fail to address the 
sector’s unique challenges and may even harm the very creative ecosystems 
they aim to support.
