CREATIVE AND CULTURAL INDUSTRIES 
AND ECONOMIC GROWTH 
2.1 The Growth in Classics: An Overview of Smith, Malthus 
and Ricard’s Perceptions 
2.1.1 Adam Smith’s and the Invisible Hand 
Adam Smith, born in 1723 in Kirkcaldy, Scotland, was a philosopher and 
economist whose seminal work “An Inquiry into the Nature and Causes 
of the Wealth of Nations” (1776) laid the foundation for classical growth 
theory. Smith’s optimistic view of economic progress centred on several key 
concepts that continue to infuence economic thought to this day and can be 
summarised in fve axioms: 
First, Smith championed the Division of Labor as a primary driver of pro‑
ductivity growth. His famous example of a pin factory demonstrated how 
breaking down production into specialised tasks could dramatically increase 
output. Smith wrote eloquently, “The greatest improvement in the produc‑
tive powers of labour, and the greater part of the skill, dexterity, and judg‑
ment with which it is anywhere directed, or applied, seem to have been the 
efects of the division of labour” (Smith, 1776, Book I, Chapter I). 
Second, Smith emphasised the role of capital accumulation in driving eco‑
nomic growth. He posited that profts from current production could be re‑
invested in capital goods, further increasing future production capacity. This 
process of capital accumulation, as later elaborated by Kurz and Salvadori 
(2003), was seen as a key engine of growth. 
Third, Smith believed in the self‑reinforcing nature of economic growth 
through market expansion. As markets expanded, they allowed for 
greater specialisation, leading to higher productivity and further growth. 
Creative and Cultural Industries and Economic Growth 27 
 
 
This concept of increasing returns to scale was ahead of its time and would 
be revisited by later economists, as noted by Lavezzi (2003). 
Fourth, Smith recognised the importance of a stable institutional frame‑
work in facilitating economic growth. He emphasised the role of property 
rights, contract enforcement, and the rule of law in creating an environment 
conducive to economic activity, ideas later explored in depth by North and 
Thomas (1973). 
Fifth, Smith introduced the concept of the “Invisible Hand,” suggesting 
that individuals pursuing their self‑interest in a market economy would un‑
intentionally promote the general welfare of society. As Rothschild (1994) 
points out, this idea has infuenced economic thought about the efciency of 
market economies. 
Adam Smith’s economic theories, primarily outlined in The Wealth of Na‑
tions, explained how economies could grow through specialisation, capital 
accumulation and market expansion. However, his earlier work, The Theory 
of Moral Sentiments, is a crucial yet often overlooked part of Smith’s intel‑
lectual legacy. This book explores human morality and behaviour, providing 
a foundation for understanding the ethical considerations that underpin eco‑
nomic interactions. These works ofer a complete picture of Smith’s vision, 
showing how moral philosophy and economics intertwine. While his work 
predated the marginal revolution in economics, many of Smith’s insights 
from both books continue to infuence modern economic and philosophical 
thought, demonstrating the enduring relevance of his ideas. 
To know more, you could explore the following references (see section): 
Kurz, H. D., & Salvadori, N. (2003). 
Lavezzi, A. (2003). 
North, D. C., & Thomas, R. P. (1973). 
Rothschild, E. (1994). 
Smith, A. (1776). 
Smith, A. (1759/2002) 
2.1.2 Malthusian and the Population Theory 
Thomas Robert Malthus (1766–1834) was an English cleric and scholar who 
became infuential in political economy and demography. Born into a pros‑
perous family, Malthus was educated at Jesus College, Cambridge, where he 
later became a fellow. 
Malthus’s seminal work “An Essay on the Principle of Population” (1798) 
presented a sobering perspective on long‑term economic growth, fundamen‑
tally reshaping economic thought in the early 19th century. Malthusian the‑
ory’s core lies in the dynamic relationship between population growth and 
28 The Political Economy of the Creative and Cultural Industries 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
fnancial resources, particularly food production. Malthus posited that popu‑
lation, when unchecked, increases at a geometric rate, while food produc‑
tion can only grow arithmetically. He famously wrote, “Population, when 
unchecked, increases in a geometrical ratio. Subsistence increases only in an 
arithmetical ratio” (Malthus, 1798, Chapter I). 
This inherent mismatch between population growth and resource ex‑
pansion led to what economists later termed the “Malthusian trap.” In 
this scenario, any temporary increase in per capita income inevitably trig‑
gers population growth, pushing living standards back to subsistence lev‑
els (Clark, 2007). This cyclical pattern, Malthus argued, was the primary 
reason for persistent poverty throughout human history. To explain how 
populations historically remained in check, Malthus identifed two types 
of population controls: preventive and positive checks. Preventive checks 
involve voluntary limitation of population growth, such as delayed marriage 
or abstinence. Positive checks, more grimly, include factors that increase 
mortality rates, such as famine, disease and war. These checks, Malthus 
contended, were nature’s way of maintaining a balance between population 
and resources. 
Malthus’s theory had profound implications for how poverty was under ‑
stood in his time and beyond. Unlike many of his contemporaries, Malthus 
argued that poverty was not primarily a result of social institutions or gov‑
ernment policies but rather an inevitable consequence of population pressure 
on limited resources (Winch, 2013). This perspective signifcantly infuenced 
social and economic policies in 19th‑century Britain and beyond. 
While Malthus’s dire predictions did not materialise in developed econ‑
omies due to unprecedented technological progress and the demographic 
transition, his ideas resonate in contemporary discussions on sustainable de‑
velopment, resource scarcity and population policy. The concept of “Malthu‑
sian dynamics” remains relevant in understanding pre‑industrial economies 
and some developing countries (Galor & Weil, 2000). 
To know more, you could explore the following references: 
Clark, G. (2007). 
Galor, O., & Weil, D. N. (2000). 
Malthus, T. R. (1798). 
Winch, D. (2013). 
2.1.3 Ricardo’s Theory of Diminishing Returns 
David Ricardo (1772–1823) was a British political economist, often credited 
as one of the most infuential classical economists alongside Adam Smith and 
Thomas Malthus. Born in London, Ricardo began his career as a successful 
Creative and Cultural Industries and Economic Growth 29 
 
stockbroker before focusing on economics, inspired by Adam Smith’s The 
Wealth of Nations. Despite his lack of formal education, Ricardo’s keen intel‑
lect and analytical skills led him to make ground‑breaking contributions to 
economic theory. 
Ricardo’s seminal work, “On the Principles of Political Economy and 
Taxation,” published in 1817, signifcantly advanced economic theory and 
cemented his place as a pivotal fgure in classical economics. At the heart of 
his economic philosophy was the principle of diminishing returns, which pos‑
ited that as more of a variable factor of production (such as labour) is applied 
to a fxed factor (for instance, land), the marginal product of the variable 
factor would eventually decline. This insight, used initially in agriculture, 
would have broad applications across various economic sectors and remains 
a fundamental principle in modern economics. 
One of Ricardo’s most enduring contributions was his theory of difer ‑
ential rent. This theory explained how land of varying qualities would be 
brought into production as demand for agricultural products increased. Ac‑
cording to Ricardo, as population growth led to increased demand for food, 
less fertile land would be cultivated, resulting in higher rents for more pro‑
ductive land. This model provided insights into agricultural economics and 
laid the groundwork for understanding income distribution between difer ‑
ent factors of production. 
Building on Adam Smith’s work, Ricardo refned the labour theory of 
value, arguing that the relative prices of goods were primarily determined by 
the relative amounts of labour required for their production. While the mar‑
ginal revolution in economics would later challenge this theory, it remained 
infuential in classical economics and played a crucial role in the development 
of economic thought. 
Ricardo’s most widely recognised contribution is his theory of compara‑
tive advantage in international trade. This revolutionary idea suggested that 
countries could beneft from trade even when one country had an absolute 
advantage in producing all goods. Countries could increase their overall eco‑
nomic welfare by specialising in producing goods for which they have a com‑
parative advantage and trading for others. This concept continues to be a 
cornerstone of international trade theory and policy. 
Ricardo’s economic outlook could have been more uniformly optimistic, 
however. He predicted that diminishing returns in agriculture would eventu‑
ally lead to what he defned as a “stationary state,” where economic growth 
would cease. In this state, all profts would be absorbed by rents, leaving no 
incentive for further capital accumulation. While Ricardo acknowledged the 
potential for technological progress to ofset diminishing returns, he believed 
such progress would be temporary and that the economy would eventually 
return to a stationary state. 
30 The Political Economy of the Creative and Cultural Industries 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
  
 
Despite this pessimistic prediction, Ricardo’s work provided a more rigor‑
ous analytical framework for understanding economic growth and distribu‑
tion. His ideas on diminishing returns and resource constraints continue to 
infuence modern economic thought, particularly in discussions of sustaina‑
ble development and resource management. Ricardo’s contributions bridged 
the gap between Smith’s qualitative insights and the mathematical models of 
later neoclassical economists (see next session), cementing his place as one of 
the most infuential fgures in the history of economic thought. 
Ricardo’s legacy extends far beyond his own time. His analytical approach 
to economics, focusing on abstract models and logical deduction, set a new 
standard for economic reasoning. Subsequent generations of economists 
adopted and further developed this methodology, shaping how economic 
analysis is conducted nowadays. 
To know more, you could explore the following references: 
Blaug, M. (1997). 
Kurz, H. D., & Salvadori, N. (2003). 
Maneschi, A. (1998). 
Mokyr, J. (1990). 
Ricardo, D. (1817). 
Stigler, G. J. (1958). 
2.2 Neoclassical Growth Theory: The Solow‑Swan Model 
Neoclassical economics has produced several infuential models of eco‑
nomic growth, each ofering unique insights into the dynamics of long‑term 
economic development. These include the Ramsey‑Cass‑Koopmans model 
(Cass, 1965; Koopmans, 1965; Ramsey, 1928), which introduces endog‑
enous saving rates; the Overlapping Generations model (Diamond, 1965), 
which incorporates demographic factors; and the Uzawa‑Lucas model 
(Lucas, 1988), which emphasises human capital accumulation. However, 
this book will primarily focus on the Solow‑Swan model to analyse creative 
industries. 
The Solow‑Swan model, independently developed by Robert Solow (1956) 
and Trevor Swan (1956), marked a signifcant advancement in growth the‑
ory. It provided a coherent mathematical framework for analysing how 
capital accumulation, labour force growth and technological progress con‑
tribute to economic growth. Our choice to emphasise this model is deliberate 
and strategic, based on several key considerations. Firstly, the Solow‑Swan 
model’s relative simplicity makes it an ideal starting point for examining the 
unique characteristics of creative industries. The model’s core equation, Y = 
F (K, L, A), where Y is output, K is capital, L is labour, and A is technology, 
Creative and Cultural Industries and Economic Growth 31 
 
provides a clear structure for analysing how these factors interact in creative 
sectors. For instance, in flm production, K might represent physical capital 
like cameras and studios, L the creative workforce, and A the technological 
know‑how and innovative techniques. 
Secondly, the model’s focus on capital accumulation and technological 
change aligns well with the innovation‑driven nature of creative sectors. Cre‑
ative industries are often at the forefront of adopting new technologies and 
developing novel production methods. The Solow‑Swan model’s emphasis on 
technological progress as a critical driver of long‑term growth is particularly 
relevant here, as it can help explain the rapid evolution and economic impact 
of sectors like digital media, video games and interactive entertainment. 
Thirdly, the Solow‑Swan model’s concept of total factor productiv‑
ity (TFP) ofers a valuable lens through which to view the intangible assets 
and knowledge externalities 1 crucial in creative industries. In these sectors, 
growth often stems from factors not easily captured by traditional measures 
of capital and labour – factors like creativity, artistic innovation and cultural 
value. The model’s residual, often interpreted as technological progress, can 
be expanded in creative industries to encompass these intangible but vital 
contributors to growth. 
Moreover, the Solow‑Swan model’s predictions about convergence and 
diminishing returns to capital have exciting implications for creative indus‑
tries. In traditional sectors, these principles might suggest that less developed 
economies will grow faster and eventually catch up with more advanced 
ones. In creative industries, however, we might observe diferent patterns. 
The unique nature of creative capital – which often appreciates rather than 
depreciates with use – and the winner‑take‑all dynamics usually seen in these 
industries could lead to divergent growth patterns that challenge the stand‑
ard Solow‑Swan predictions. Another advantage of using the Solow‑Swan 
model is its fexibility and potential for extension. While the basic model as‑
sumes exogenous technological progress, later extensions have incorporated 
endogenous technological change. This is particularly relevant for creative 
industries, where innovation is often driven by internal industry dynamics 
rather than external factors. By adapting these extended versions of the So‑
low‑Swan model, we can better understand how creative industries generate 
and sustain growth. 
The model also provides a framework for understanding the role of hu‑
man capital in creative industries. While the original Solow‑Swan model 
treated labour as a homogeneous factor, later developments (such as the 
Mankiw‑Romer‑Weil model – see Box 2.1) incorporated human capital as 
a distinct input. This extension is crucial for analysing creative industries, 
where workforce skills, talents and knowledge often drive value creation and 
growth. 
32 The Political Economy of the Creative and Cultural Industries 
 
 
 
 
BOX 2.1 SUMMARY OF THE MANKIW‑ROMER‑WEIL MODEL 
The Mankiw‑Romer‑Weil Model (1992) represents a signifcant extension of 
the Solow‑Swan model by incorporating human capital as a distinct factor of 
production. It introduces an augmented production function, Y = K^α × H^β × 
(AL)^(1–α–β), where H represents human capital and includes separate ac‑
cumulation equations for both physical and human capital. This modifcation 
substantially improves the model’s empirical ft, enhancing its ability to explain 
cross‑country income diferences. The model predicts a slower convergence 
rate than the original Solow‑Swan model, aligning more closely with empirical 
observations. Moreover, it provides a valuable framework for analysing the im‑
pact of education and training policies on economic growth, emphasising the 
crucial role of human capital in driving economic development. By explicitly 
accounting for human capital, the Mankiw‑Romer‑Weil Model ofers a more 
nuanced understanding of growth dynamics and has  become an important 
tool in theoretical and applied economic research. 
Furthermore, the Solow‑Swan model’s long‑run implications can shed light 
on the sustainability of growth in creative industries. The model’s concept 
of a steady state, where per‑capita output growth is driven solely by techno‑
logical progress, raises important questions about the long‑term trajectory 
of creative sectors. How do these industries continue to innovate and grow 
despite potentially diminishing returns? The model provides a framework for 
exploring these questions. 
Lastly, the Solow‑Swan model’s simplicity makes it an excellent pedagogi‑
cal tool. It allows us to introduce fundamental concepts in growth theory 
and then gradually build upon them to address the specifc complexities of 
creative industries. This approach enables readers to develop a solid founda‑
tional understanding before grappling with more intricate industry‑specifc 
phenomena. 
While more complex models might ofer additional nuances, the So‑
low‑Swan model provides a robust foundation for understanding the funda‑
mental growth dynamics in creative industries. Its focus on capital, labour 
and technology as key growth drivers, combined with its fexibility and po‑
tential for extension, makes it an excellent choice for our analysis in this 
book. By applying this model to creative industries, we can gain valuable 
insights into the sources of growth, the role of innovation and the long‑term 
prospects of these vital sectors of the modern economy. 
Creative and Cultural Industries and Economic Growth 33 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
2.2.1 Assumptions and Key Components 
The Solow‑Swan model, a cornerstone of neoclassical growth theory, rests 
on a foundation of carefully constructed assumptions that simplify complex 
economic realities. These fundamental assumptions include: 
1 Constant Returns to Scale: The production function exhibits constant re‑
turns to scale, meaning doubling all inputs will double output. 
2 Diminishing Marginal Returns: Each additional unit of capital or labour 
yields a more minor increase in output than the previous unit. 
3 Constant Savings Rate: A fxed proportion of output is saved and invested. 
4 Closed Economy: There is no international trade or capital fows. 
5 Total Employment: All available labour and capital are fully utilised. 
6 Perfect Competition: All markets are perfectly competitive. 
The model’s key components interact within this framework to drive eco‑
nomic growth under a specifc production function, often expressed in the 
Cobb‑Douglas form Y = A × K^α × L^(1–α), encapsulating the relation‑
ship between output (Y), capital (K), labour (L) and technology (A). This 
Cobb‑Douglas function captures several critical economic concepts. The ex‑
ponents α and (1–α) represent the share of output that goes to capital and 
labour, respectively, aligning with the empirical observation that these shares 
tend to be relatively stable over time in many economies. The function ex‑
hibits constant returns to scale: Y also doubles if both K and L are doubled. 
Moreover, it incorporates diminishing marginal returns to each factor: as 
more of one input is added while holding the other constant, the incremental 
increase in output diminishes. 
The parameter A in the Cobb‑Douglas function represents technological 
progress in the Solow‑Swan model. Increases in A allow more output to be 
produced with the same levels of capital and labour, representing productiv‑
ity improvements that are not attributable to increases in observable inputs. 
In the basic Solow‑Swan model, growth in A is treated as exogenous, occur‑
ring at a constant rate of g. 
Complementing the production function, the capital accumulation equa‑
tion, ΔK = sY –  δK, describes the evolution of the capital stock over time. 
Here, s represents the savings rate, determining the fraction of output in‑
vested, while δ is the depreciation rate of capital. This equation forms the 
core dynamic of the Solow‑Swan model, determining how the economy 
evolves. Population growth is assumed to occur at an exogenous rate, n, 
while technological progress grows at a g rate. These factors and the savings 
and depreciation rates are critical determinants of the economy’s long‑run 
growth path and steady‑state equilibrium. 
34 The Political Economy of the Creative and Cultural Industries 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
A central concept in the Solow‑Swan model is the steady‑state equilib‑
rium, where key ratios (such as capital per worker) remain constant over 
time. In the steady state: 
• Capital per efective worker (k = K/(AL)) is constant. 
• Output per efective worker (y = Y/(AL)) is constant. 
• The growth rate of total output equals the sum of the growth rates of 
technology (g) and the labour force (n). 
The model predicts convergence to this steady state, with countries having 
lower initial capital per worker growing faster than those with higher initial 
capital per worker. This concept of conditional convergence has been infu‑
ential in empirical growth research. 
In the basic Solow‑Swan model, technological progress is treated as an 
exogenous factor, like a “manna from heaven.” This assumption has several 
important implications: 
1 Long‑run per capita growth is driven solely by technological progress. In 
the steady state, the growth rate of output per worker equals the rate of 
technological progress. 
2 The model needs to explain the source of technological progress. It treats 
it as an unexplained external force that impacts the economy. 
3 Policy interventions can’t afect the long‑run growth rate. Since techno‑
logical progress is exogenous, policies can only afect the level of output, 
not its long‑run growth rate. 
This limitation of the model led to endogenous growth theories, which at‑
tempt to explain technological progress within the model itself (Aghion & 
Howitt, 1998). These newer theories explore how factors such as research 
and development, human capital accumulation, and knowledge externalities 
can drive technological progress and long‑run growth. 
The Solow‑Swan model’s elegance lies in its ability to capture fundamen‑
tal growth dynamics with relative simplicity. It provides a framework for 
understanding how capital accumulation, population growth and technolog‑
ical progress interact to determine long‑run economic growth. While its as‑
sumptions may seem restrictive, they allow for clear insights into the drivers 
of economic growth and the factors that infuence an economy’s long‑term 
trajectory. 
Despite its simplifcations, the model has proven remarkably useful in 
explaining cross‑country economic performance diferences and predicting 
long‑run growth patterns. It has served as a springboard for more complex 
growth theories and is a valuable tool. However, while the Solow‑Swan 
model provides useful insights into the mechanics of economic growth, 
Creative and Cultural Industries and Economic Growth 35 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
  
 
its treatment of technological progress as an exogenous factor represents 
a signifcant limitation. By assuming that technological advancement oc‑
curs independently of economic forces, the model must be more robust 
in capturing the complex innovation and knowledge‑creation dynamics 
that drive modern economies. This simplifcation overlooks the crucial 
role of human capital, research and development investments and institu‑
tional factors in fostering technological progress. Consequently, the mod‑
el’s policy implications could be more extensive, suggesting that long‑run 
growth rates are impervious to policy interventions. This perspective is at 
odds with empirical evidence suggesting that policies promoting educa‑
tion, research and innovation can infuence long‑term economic growth 
trajectories. 
However, dismissing the Solow‑Swan model entirely based on this critique 
would be remiss. The model’s simplicity, both its weakness and strength, 
allows for clear analytical insights into the role of capital accumulation 
and diminishing returns in the growth process. Its prediction of conditional 
convergence has found support in empirical studies and has infuenced de‑
velopment economics. Moreover, the model’s limitations have spurred the 
development of more sophisticated theories, such as endogenous growth 
models, which attempt to explain technological progress within the economic 
system. In this light, the Solow‑Swan model can be seen as a crucial stepping‑
stone in the evolution of growth theory, providing a foundation upon which 
more complex and realistic models have been built. 
2.3 Integrating Creative and Cultural Industries 
into Growth Theory 
Integrating CCIs into economic growth theory signifcantly evolves our un‑
derstanding of modern economies. These industries have become increasingly 
recognised as essential contributors to economic growth and development. 
This section explores how these industries ft into and extend traditional 
growth theories, ofering new perspectives on the drivers of economic pro‑
gress in the 21st century. 
2.3.1 Knowledge Economy and Human Capital 
The rise of the knowledge economy has highlighted the crucial role of hu‑
man capital in driving economic growth. CCIs are at the forefront of this 
shift, serving as producers and incubators of human capital, occupying a dis‑
tinctive niche in the modern economy. They serve not merely as production 
sectors but as dynamic incubators of human capital and wellsprings of tacit 
knowledge. This dual role positions them at the forefront of the knowledge 
economy, driving innovation and economic growth in ways that transcend 
traditional industrial paradigms. 
36 The Political Economy of the Creative and Cultural Industries 
 
 
 
   
  
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
2.3.1.1 CCIs Diverse and Adaptive Human Capital 
Unlike conventional sectors characterised by standardised skill sets, creative 
industries foster the development of a multifaceted, adaptable and innovative 
workforce encompassing scientists, engineers, artists, and cultural creatives, 
underscoring these industries’ centrality to the knowledge economy. The cre‑
ative sectors employ highly skilled individuals and catalyse the enhancement 
of the broader workforce’s capabilities through their outputs and pervasive 
spillover efects. Consider the flm industry: beyond creating employment for 
actors, directors and technicians, it cultivates many transferable skills. From 
project management and digital literacy to collaborative problem‑solving 
and cross‑cultural communication, the competencies lying in flm production 
fnd applicability across diverse economic sectors. This phenomenon aligns 
with Potts and Cunningham’s (2008) characterisation of creative industries 
as “nurseries” for novel ideas and skills, functioning as crucibles of innova‑
tion with economy‑wide implications. 
2.3.1.2 The Pivotal Role of Tacit Knowledge 
Integral to the creative industries’ capacity for human capital generation is 
their reliance on and propagation of tacit knowledge. This form of knowl‑
edge, encapsulated by Michael Polanyi’s (1966) assertion that “we can know 
more than we can tell,” resists codifcation and straightforward transfer. In 
creative and cultural sectors, tacit knowledge is embodied in individual ex‑
pertise, organisational routines and cultural contexts, forming the bedrock of 
innovative processes and outputs. 
Asheim and Hansen’s (2009) typology of knowledge bases  –  analytical 
(science‑based), synthetic (engineering‑based) and symbolic (arts‑based) – provides
 a valuable framework for understanding the unique knowledge dynamics of 
creative industries. These sectors predominantly operate within the symbolic 
knowledge base, characterised by its highly tacit and context‑specifc nature. 
Crucially, the accumulation of tacit knowledge in creative industries is often 
achieved through learning‑by‑doing. Arrow (1962) introduced this concept, 
particularly relevant to creative and cultural sectors. Skills in these industries 
are frequently acquired and refned through practice and experience rather than 
formal education alone. This continuous learning and improvement process can 
lead to increasing returns to scale, a key factor in endogenous growth models. 
Throsby (2001) extends this idea to cultural capital, which encompasses 
both tangible and intangible cultural assets. He notes that cultural capital ac‑
cumulates through a process akin to learning by doing. Artists and cultural 
producers develop their skills and reputations over time, potentially increas‑
ing productivity and innovation. This phenomenon challenges traditional as‑
sumptions about diminishing returns and suggests that creative industries 
might exhibit diferent growth dynamics than other sectors. 
Creative and Cultural Industries and Economic Growth 37 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
The reliance on tacit, symbolic knowledge engenders several signifcant 
economic implications: 
1 Barrier to Entry: The difculty in replicating or transferring tacit knowl‑
edge creates natural barriers to entry, potentially generating innovation 
rents and sustaining competitive advantages. 
2 Clustering Imperative: The necessity for face‑to‑face interactions in shar ‑
ing tacit knowledge promotes geographical clustering and agglomeration 
efects, fostering innovation ecosystems. 
3 Value Enhancement: The uniqueness embedded in tacit knowledge en‑
hances the value proposition of creative outputs, contributing to product 
diferentiation and market competitiveness. 
4 Adaptive Capacity: The fuidity of tacit knowledge equips creative in‑
dustries with superior adaptive capabilities, enabling rapid responses to 
changing market conditions and consumer preferences. 
The interplay between human capital development and tacit knowledge ac‑
cumulation in creative industries creates a self‑reinforcing cycle of innovation 
and growth. As these sectors cultivate diverse skill sets and nurture tacit ex‑
pertise, they simultaneously enhance their capacity for future innovation and 
strengthen their economic resilience. This symbiosis between skill develop‑
ment and knowledge‑creation positions creative industries as crucial drivers 
of economic dynamism in the 21st century. By fostering adaptable human 
capital and leveraging the power of tacit knowledge, these sectors contribute 
to their growth and serve as catalysts for innovation and development across 
the broader economic landscape. 
2.3.2 Innovation and Technological Progress in Creative Industries 
Creative industries stand at the forefront of innovation and technological 
progress, ofering unique perspectives that challenge and extend traditional 
growth theories. These sectors serve as repositories of change, embodying the 
dynamic processes that drive economic evolution in the contemporary era. 
2.3.2.1 Creative Destruction 2 
Joseph Alois Schumpeter (1883–1950) was an Austrian‑born economist 
and political scientist who signifcantly contributed to economic theory. He 
served as Austria’s Finance Minister in 1919 and later held professorships 
at the University of Bonn and Harvard. Schumpeter is best known for his 
entrepreneurship, innovation and creative destruction theories. Schumpet‑
er’s (1942) concept of creative destruction fnds its essential expression in 
cultural industries. These sectors are characterised by constant innovation, 
38 The Political Economy of the Creative and Cultural Industries 
 
 
 
where new styles, formats and technologies regularly displace existing ones. 
The music industry provides a vivid illustration of this process, having un‑
dergone multiple waves of creative destruction – from vinyl to cassettes, CDs 
and digital streaming platforms. Each transition transformed consumption 
patterns and reshaped the industry’s entire value chain. 
Potts and Cunningham (2008) further this idea, positioning creative in‑
dustries as integral components of the economy’s innovation system. They 
argue that these sectors specialise in the origination and adoption of new 
ideas, continuously refreshing the economy’s knowledge base. This perspec‑
tive elevates creative industries from mere producers of cultural goods to 
critical drivers of the “creative destruction” that Schumpeter saw as essential 
to economic progress. By constantly challenging the status quo and introduc‑
ing novel concepts, creative industries catalyse innovation across the broader 
fnancial landscape. 
2.3.2.2 Open Innovation: Collaborative Creativity 
The concept of open innovation, popularised by Chesbrough (2003), has 
found particularly fertile ground in creative industries. These sectors often 
rely on collaborative and networked models of innovation, where ideas fow 
freely between organisations and individuals, blurring traditional boundaries. 
The software industry, for instance, especially in open‑source develop‑
ment, exemplifes this approach. Projects like Linux demonstrate how open 
collaboration can lead to robust, innovative products that often outperform 
their closed‑source counterparts. Similarly, the fashion industry thrives on 
the rapid dissemination and reinterpretation of ideas, driving a continuous 
cycle of innovation that spans global networks of designers, manufacturers 
and consumers. 
Potts and Cunningham (2008) extend this concept with their “social net‑
work market” model for creative industries. This model states that the value 
of cultural and creative goods is determined through complex social interac‑
tions, suggesting that innovation in creative sectors is inherently open and 
socially embedded. This perspective challenges traditional closed innovation 
paradigms and highlights the unique dynamics of value creation in creative 
economies. 
2.3.2.3 Digital Revolution: Transforming Creative Landscapes 
Digital technologies have precipitated a profound transformation in creative 
industries, revolutionising cultural goods’ production, distribution and con‑
sumption. Nicholas Negroponte’s (1995) prescient predictions about the dig‑
ital revolution have been fully realised in the creative sector, with far‑reaching 
consequences. 
Creative and Cultural Industries and Economic Growth 39 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
Key aspects of this digital transformation include: 
1 The Democratisation of Production Tools: Digital technologies have sig‑
nifcantly lowered barriers to entry, enabling a new generation of creators 
to produce professional‑quality content with minimal resources. This de‑
mocratisation has led to creativity and diversity in cultural production. 
2 Global Digital Distribution Platforms: The emergence of digital platforms 
has exponentially expanded the market reach of creative content. Stream‑
ing services, online marketplaces and social media platforms have created 
global audiences for even niche cultural products, fundamentally altering 
the economics of creative industries. 
3 New Formats and Mediums: The digital revolution has spawned entirely 
new forms of creative expression. Virtual and augmented reality technolo‑
gies, for instance, are opening unprecedented possibilities for immersive 
storytelling and interactive experiences, pushing the boundaries of what’s 
possible in creative content. 
4 Data‑Driven Decision‑Making: The digital age has ushered in new content 
creation and marketing approaches. Big data analytics now inform every‑
thing from script development in the flm industry to personalised music 
recommendations on streaming platforms, leading to more targeted and 
efcient creative processes.

new technologies. Innovations pioneered in these sectors frequently difuse 
to other industries, positioning creative sectors as technological trailblazers 
in the broader economic ecosystem. The intersection of creative destruction, 
open innovation and digital transformation in creative industries presents 
a compelling case for their central role in driving economic growth and in‑
novation. These sectors generate cultural value and serve as dynamic engines 
of technological progress and economic renewal. As economies increasingly 
rely on knowledge, creativity and innovation for growth, the models and 
practices emerging from creative industries ofer valuable insights for policy‑
makers and business leaders alike. 
By constantly pushing the boundaries of technology and collaborative in‑
novation, creative industries are shaping the future of work, consumption 
and economic value creation. Their ability to adapt to and harness techno‑
logical change positions them as key players in the knowledge economy, driv‑
ing progress within their domains and across the entire economic landscape.

2.3.2.3.1 Challenges and Ethical Considerations 
While the digital revolution has undoubtedly propelled creative industries 
forward, it has also given rise to signifcant challenges and ethical considera‑
tions. The digital divide remains a pressing issue, potentially exacerbating 
inequalities in access to both the consumption and production of creative 
content. This divide exists between nations and within societies, risking the 
marginalisation of certain groups from the benefts of the creative economy. 
Data privacy and the breach of personal information safety have become crit‑
ical concerns as creative industries increasingly rely on data‑driven strategies. As 
Cadwalladr and Graham‑Harrison (2018) analysed, the Cambridge Analytica 
scandal3 underscored the potential to misuse personal data in digital creative 
spaces. This incident raised important questions about the ethical implications 
of data collection and usage in targeted content creation and distribution. 
Moreover, the rise of artifcial intelligence and machine learning in creative 
processes poses new challenges. While these technologies ofer unprecedented 
opportunities for personalisation and efciency, they also raise concerns 
about the potential displacement of human creativity and the authenticity of 
AI‑generated content. Using AI in art creation blurs the lines between human 
and machine creativity, challenging traditional notions of authorship and 
artistic value. 
The gig economy, which has become prevalent in many creative sectors, 
presents another double‑edged sword. While it ofers fexibility and opportu‑
nities for freelancers, it also often needs more stability and benefts associated 
with traditional employment, as Graham et al. (2017) pointed out. This shift 
in employment patterns could have long‑term implications for the sustain‑
ability of careers in creative industries. 
Lastly, the dominance of a few large tech platforms in distributing creative 
content raises concerns about market concentration and the diversity of cul‑
tural expression. As Hesmondhalgh and Meier (2018) discussed, the increas‑
ing power of streaming platforms and social media giants in shaping cultural 
consumption could potentially homogenise content and limit the visibility of 
niche or alternative creative voices. 
Addressing these challenges will ensure that creative industries’ innovative 
potential is realised equitably, ethically and sustainably. As these industries 
evolve, policymakers, industry leaders and creators must grapple with these 
complex issues to shape a future where technological progress and creative 
expression coexist harmoniously with societal values and individual rights. 
2.3.2.4 Spillover Efect, Externalities and Network Efects 
CCIs generate signifcant positive externalities, contributing to economic 
growth beyond their direct output. These industries, encompassing felds 
such as art, music, flm, literature and digital media, serve as catalysts for 
innovation and development across the broader economy. 
42 The Political Economy of the Creative and Cultural Industries 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
2.3.2.4.1 Knowledge Spillovers from Creative to Other Sectors 
Knowledge spillovers occur when innovations or practices from one sector 
beneft other sectors without total compensation. Creative industries are par‑
ticularly prone to generating such spillovers due to the general applicability 
of many creative skills and innovations. This cross‑pollination of ideas often 
leads to unexpected breakthroughs and efciency gains in seemingly unre‑
lated felds. 
For example: 
1 Design thinking, originating from creative disciplines, has been widely 
adopted in business and technology sectors. Companies like Apple and 
Google have embraced design thinking to create user‑centric products and 
services, revolutionising their respective industries. 
2 Storytelling techniques from the entertainment industry are increasingly 
used in marketing and corporate communications. Brands now craft com‑
pelling narratives to engage consumers, moving beyond traditional adver‑
tising to create emotional connections and brand loyalty. 
3 Visualisation tools developed for creative purposes fnd applications in 
scientifc research and data analysis. Advanced graphics software, initially 
designed for flm CGI, now aids in complex scientifc simulations and big 
data visualisation. 
Bakhshi et al. (2008) provide evidence of these spillovers, demonstrating how 
creative industries contribute to innovation in the broader economy. Their 
research highlights the disproportionate impact of creative sector innova‑
tions on productivity and growth across various industries. 
2.3.2.4.2 Externalities 
Cultural externalities refer to the broader social and economic benefts of 
cultural activities. These frequently intangible benefts can have profound ef‑
fects on societal well‑being and economic development. They extend beyond 
the immediate economic transactions associated with cultural products or 
events. 
These can include: 
1 Enhanced social cohesion and community identity. Cultural activities of‑
ten bring diverse groups together, fostering understanding and shared ex‑
periences. This can lead to more robust, more resilient communities that 
are better equipped to face economic challenges. 
2 Improved quality of life and well‑being. Access to cultural amenities and 
activities has been linked to higher life satisfaction and mental health. This 
can indirectly boost productivity and reduce healthcare costs. 
Creative and Cultural Industries and Economic Growth 43 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
3 Increased attractiveness of locations for residents and businesses. Cities 
with vibrant cultural scenes often attract skilled workers and innovative 
companies, creating clusters of economic activity. This “cultural capital” 
can signifcantly infuence urban regeneration and economic development 
strategies. 
Throsby (2001) argues that these cultural externalities contribute to sustain‑
able economic development by enhancing social and cultural capital. Whilst 
challenging to quantify, these externalities are crucial in creating environ‑
ments conducive to economic growth. They form the “soft infrastructure” 
that underpins long‑term fnancial success and resilience. 
BOX 2.3 EXTERNALITIES IN ECONOMICS 
Defnition 
In economics, an externality refers to a cost or beneft that afects a third party 
who did not choose to incur that cost or beneft. Externalities can be either 
positive or negative and can stem from either the production or consumption 
of a good or service. The concept of externalities is crucial in understanding 
market failures, which often necessitate government intervention to correct the 
imbalance in certain economic activities. 
Critical Characteristics of Externalities: 
1 Impact on Third Parties: The efect is on those not directly involved in pro‑
ducing or consuming the good or service. 
2 Not Refected in Market Price: The cost or beneft needs to be incorporated 
into the market price of the good or service. 
3 Can Be Positive or Negative: Externalities can create either benefts or costs 
for society. 
4 Occur in Both Production and Consumption: They can arise from either the 
production process or the consumption of goods and services. 
BOX 2.4 EXTERNALITIES: TYPES AND ECONOMIC 
IMPLICATIONS 
Types of Externalities 
1 Negative Externalities 
Occur when production or consumption imposes costs on third parties. 
44 The Political Economy of the Creative and Cultural Industries 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
  
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
Examples: 
• Environmental Pollution: Factory waste, vehicle emissions 
• Noise Pollution: Airport operations, construction work 
• Overuse of Common Resources: Overfshing, excessive groundwater 
extraction 
• Health‑related: Secondhand smoke, antibiotic resistance 
2 Positive Externalities 
Occur when production or consumption benefts third parties. 
Examples: 
• Education: Improved workforce productivity, reduced crime rates 
• Research and Development: Technological spillovers, medical advan‑
cements 
• Vaccination: Herd immunity 
• Green Spaces: Increased property values, improved air quality 
• Network Efects: Increased value of communication networks 
Economic Implications 
1 Market Failure: 
• Prices don’t refect true social costs/benefts 
• Results in over/under‑production of goods with externalities 
2 Efciency Loss: 
• Market equilibrium doesn’t maximise social welfare 
• Causes misallocation of resources 
3 Intervention Needs: 
• Government: Regulations, taxes, subsidies 
• Private: Coase Theorem, corporate social responsibility 
4 Price Distortion: 
• Inaccurate market prices lead to suboptimal decisions 
5 Equity Issues: 
• Externalities often afect groups disproportionately 
6 Long‑term Impact: 
• Can infuence economic growth and development 
7 Policy Challenges: 
• Difculty in measuring and addressing externalities efectively

Additional Considerations

2.3.2.4.3 Network Efects in Creative Industries 
Many creative industries exhibit strong network efects, where the value of 
a product or service increases with the number of users. Katz and Shapiro 
(1985) described this phenomenon in the context of technology adoption, 
and it is particularly relevant to digital creative industries. In the creative 
sector, these network efects can lead to rapid scaling and market dominance. 
For instance: 
1 Social media platforms become more valuable as their user base grows. 
Each new user adds content and connections, making the platform more 
attractive to others. This self‑reinforcing cycle has led to the global domi‑
nance of platforms like Facebook and Twitter. 
2 Online marketplaces for creative goods beneft from having more buyers 
and sellers. Platforms like Etsy or Redbubble become more valuable to 
artists as they attract more customers, and more appealing to customers 
as they ofer a more comprehensive range of products. 
3 Collaborative creative tools improve with increased user contributions. 
Software like GitHub for coding or Figma for design becomes more valu‑
able as more people contribute templates, plugins or code snippets. 
These network efects can increase returns to scale, a key feature of en‑
dogenous growth models. In creative industries, this can manifest as rapid 
growth once a critical mass of users is achieved. They also contribute to 
forming “winner‑take‑most” markets, where successful creative products 
or platforms can achieve dominant market positions. This concentration 
can drive innovation but also raises concerns about market power and the 
need for appropriate regulation. Combining knowledge spillovers, cultural 
externalities and network efects positions creative industries as robust eco‑
nomic growth and innovation drivers with impacts far beyond their direct 
economic output. 
2.3.3 Urban Development and Creative Industries 
2.3.3.1 Creative Clusters and Economic Growth 
Creative clusters, geographical concentrations of interconnected creative 
businesses and institutions, have become a focus of academic research and 
46 The Political Economy of the Creative and Cultural Industries 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
policy initiatives. Porter’s (1998) work on industry clusters provides a foun‑
dation for understanding these creative agglomerations. 
Creative clusters can drive economic growth through: 
1 Knowledge spillovers and informal learning networks. 
2 Specialised labour markets that facilitate skill matching. 
3 Development of specialised suppliers and services. 
4 Increased opportunities for collaboration and innovation. 
Empirical studies, such as those by Scott (2000) and Pratt (2004), have dem‑
onstrated the economic impact of creative clusters in cities like Los Angeles, 
London and Paris. 
2.3.3.2 Cultural Amenities and Local Development 
Cultural amenities, such as museums, theatres and music venues, play a cru‑
cial role in urban development and economic growth. These amenities con‑
tribute to a city’s quality of life, making it more attractive to highly skilled 
workers and innovative businesses. Glaeser et al. (2001) argue that provid‑
ing urban amenities is crucial to city growth, particularly in attracting and 
retaining human capital. Cultural amenities can also drive tourism, a signif‑
cant economic sector in many cities. 
However, the relationship between cultural amenities and economic 
growth is complex. While they can contribute to urban revitalisation, there 
are concerns about displacement and uneven development. Zukin’s (1995) 
work on the “symbolic economy” of cities highlights these tensions between 
culture, authenticity and economic growth. 
2.3.4 Globalisation, International Trade and Creative Industries 
In the last decades, globalisation has emerged as a powerful force, weav‑
ing intricate patterns across CCIs, both commercial and cultural exchanges. 
This phenomenon has not merely touched these sectors; it has fundamentally 
transformed them, altering how cultural goods and services are created, dis‑
seminated and consumed globally. 
2.3.4.1 Cultural Goods in Global Trade Theory: A Paradigm Shift 
International trade has long been governed by established theories and mod‑
els, with concepts like comparative advantage as cornerstones of economic 
thought. However, the unique nature of cultural goods has challenged these 
traditional paradigms, forcing economists and policymakers to reconsider 
long‑held assumptions. 
At the heart of this challenge lies the dual identity of cultural goods – they 
are not merely economic commodities but also vessels of cultural signifcance 
Creative and Cultural Industries and Economic Growth 47 
 
  
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
and symbolic value. This duality introduces a layer of complexity that tra‑
ditional trade theories struggle to encompass fully. David Throsby, a pio‑
neering voice in cultural economics, advocates for incorporating “cultural 
exception” in trade policy, acknowledging that cultural goods often defy 
conventional economic logic. 
Consider, for instance, a centuries‑old painting. While potentially high, 
its market price fails to capture its value as a cultural artefact, a piece of his‑
tory and potential future valorisation. This intrinsic value, intangible yet pro‑
found, necessitates a diferent approach in trade discussions and economic 
modelling. The recognition of cultural goods’ unique nature found formal 
expression in UNESCO’s Convention on the Protection and Promotion of 
the Diversity of Cultural Expressions, adopted in 2005. This landmark docu‑
ment explicitly acknowledges these goods and services’ dual economic and 
cultural nature. It serves as a testament to the global community’s under ‑
standing that cultural products are not just commodities to be traded but also 
essential elements of human expression and diversity. 
This dual nature introduces a myriad of complexities in international trade 
agreements. How does one quantify cultural value in economic terms? How 
can trade policies protect cultural diversity while also promoting economic 
growth? These questions challenge policymakers and economists, sparking 
debates and driving trade theory and practice innovation. 
2.3.4.2 Comparative Advantage in Creative Sectors:  
A Global Mosaic of Specialisation 
While the unique characteristics of cultural goods complicate their treat‑
ment in traditional trade models, the concept of comparative advantage 
still fnds resonance in the creative industries. Rather than being rendered 
obsolete, this economic principle has adapted, taking on new dimensions 
in the context of cultural production and dissemination. Countries and 
regions worldwide have carved out niches in particular creative sectors, 
leveraging their unique cultural heritage, institutional frameworks and hu‑
man capital to establish dominance in specifc areas. This specialisation 
has given rise to a rich, diverse global landscape of artistic production, 
where diferent regions contribute their unique favours to the world’s cul‑
tural ecosystem. 
Let’s embark on a journey around the world to explore some examples of 
these specialised hubs: 
1 Hollywood: the hills of Southern California have long been synonymous 
with cinematic magic. Hollywood’s dominance in the global flm indus‑
try exemplifes the power of clustering creative talent, robust institutional 
support and a well‑developed ecosystem of auxiliary services. From cut‑
ting‑edge special efects to masterful storytelling, Hollywood’s comparative 
48 The Political Economy of the Creative and Cultural Industries 
 
 
 
 
advantage lies in its ability to consistently produce high‑budget, techni‑
cally sophisticated flms that reach high audiences. 
2 Bollywood: In the metropolis of Mumbai, India’s flm industry – known as 
Bollywood – has carved out its own niche. Specialising in vibrant musicals 
and epic dramas, Bollywood’s infuence extends far beyond India’s bor ‑
ders, captivating audiences across South Asia and increasingly worldwide. 
Its comparative advantage lies in its unique blend of music, dance, drama 
and storytelling that resonates deeply with South Asian cultural values. 
3 The British music: the United Kingdom’s rock music scene stands as a bea‑
con of musical innovation, with Liverpool and Manchester playing pivotal 
roles. With a rich musical heritage from the 1960s, the United Kingdom 
has cultivated a robust ecosystem of musicians, producers and venues. Its 
comparative advantage in rock music stems from working‑class creativity, 
a thriving pub and club culture and a tradition of musical rebellion. The 
Beatles from Liverpool revolutionised popular music globally, setting the 
stage for decades of British musical dominance. Manchester has been at 
the forefront of subsequent movements, giving birth to infuential bands 
and subgenres. Manchester’s distinctive sound – exemplifed by bands like 
The Smiths, Joy Division and The Stone Roses – leaves an indelible mark 
on the industry. The city’s musical legacy, often called “Madchester,” con‑
tinues to thrive, with contemporary acts such as Aitch, Blossoms and El‑
bow carrying the torch. This ongoing musical evolution produces iconic 
artists and fosters a unique cultural identity that inspires new generations 
of musicians worldwide, cementing Manchester’s status as a critical player 
in the global rock music scene. 
4 K‑pop in South Korea, particularly Seoul: Turning our attention to East 
Asia, South Korea’s K‑pop industry stands as a beacon of modern pop 
culture innovation, with Seoul at its epicentre. With a musical heritage 
that has rapidly evolved since the 1990s, South Korea has cultivated a 
sophisticated ecosystem of entertainment companies, training academies 
and multi‑talented performers. Its comparative advantage in pop music 
stems from rigorous talent development, cutting‑edge production values 
and a strategically cultivated global appeal. Seoul, in particular, has been 
the heart of this movement, giving birth to globally recognised acts and 
innovative music styles. From PSY’s viral hit “Gangnam Style” to BTS’s 
Billboard chart‑toppers, K‑pop continues to reshape global music tastes, 
with Seoul’s distinctive blend of catchy melodies, synchronised choreogra‑
phy and visual aesthetics – exemplifed by groups like BLACKPINK, EXO 
and TWICE – leaving an indelible mark on the industry. The city’s musical 
legacy, often called the “Hallyu” or Korean Wave, has produced iconic 
artists and fostered a unique cultural soft power that continues attracting 
fans worldwide. Contemporary acts like Stray Kids, ITZY and NewJeans 
carry this legacy forward, ensuring Seoul’s ongoing infuence in shaping 
global pop music trends. 
Creative and Cultural Industries and Economic Growth 49 
 
 
  
  
 
5 Brazil’s Carnival: In the heart of South America, Brazil’s Carnival stands 
as a testament to the nation’s vibrant cultural tapestry and unparalleled 
festive spirit. With its epicentre in Rio de Janeiro and Salvador, this an‑
nual celebration has become synonymous with exuberant parades, pul‑
sating samba rhythms, and elaborate costumes that transform the city’s 
streets into a kaleidoscope of colour and movement. Brazil’s comparative 
advantage in Carnival lies in its unique blend of African, European and 
indigenous infuences, creating a spectacle deeply rooted in tradition. The 
event’s economic impact is substantial, drawing millions of tourists and 
showcasing Brazil’s soft power on the global stage. From the legendary 
Sambadrome parades to the impromptu street parties known as “blocos,” 
Carnival embodies the Brazilian ethos of joy, creativity and community, 
ofering an unmatched cultural experience that has become one of the 
world’s most anticipated annual celebrations. 
These specialisations are not mere accidents of history or geography. They 
represent the culmination of decades, sometimes centuries, of cultural de‑
velopment, institutional support and human capital investment. As these 
creative hubs fourish, they generate signifcant export earnings, contributing 
substantially to their respective economies. Moreover, the infuence of these 
creative powerhouses extends beyond mere economic metrics. They serve as 
potent sources of soft power, shaping global perceptions and cultural trends. 
A Hollywood blockbuster can infuence fashion trends in Tokyo, while a 
popular K‑pop band might inspire art movements in Paris. This cultural 
cross‑pollination enriches the global creative landscape, fostering innovation 
and diversity. 
2.3.4.3 Digital Platforms and Global Reach of Cultural Products:  
The New Frontier 
As we venture into the 21st century, the digital revolution has emerged as a 
transformative force in the CCIs. Digital platforms have rewritten the rules 
of engagement, dismantling traditional barriers to cultural trade and usher ‑
ing in an era of unprecedented global access to cultural content. The rise of 
streaming services, e‑book platforms and online marketplaces has fundamen‑
tally altered how cultural products are distributed and consumed. No longer 
constrained by physical limitations, cultural goods can traverse the globe at 
the speed of light, fnding audiences in the world’s most remote corners. 
This digital shift has unleashed a cascade of changes across the creative 
landscape: 
1 Empowering Niche Cultural Products: In the pre‑digital era, niche cultural 
products often needed help fnding their audience, constrained by the eco‑
nomics of physical distribution and limited shelf space. The digital age has 
50 The Political Economy of the Creative and Cultural Industries 
 
 
 
 
 
 
shattered these constraints. A documentary flmmaker in Nepal can now 
reach viewers in Norway, while an indie musician in Iceland can build a fan‑
base in Indonesia. This democratisation of distribution has led to a fowering 
of diverse cultural expressions, enriching the global cultural tapestry. 
2 Challenging Traditional Gatekeepers: The digital revolution has disrupted 
long‑established power structures in cultural industries. Traditional gate‑
keepers – be they publishing houses, record labels or flm studios – have 
seen their infuence wane as artists gain direct access to global audiences. 
This shift has sparked a reimagining of business models and value chains 
in creative industries, fostering innovation and entrepreneurship. 
3 Cultural Policy in the Digital Age: As cultural goods fow freely across 
digital borders, policymakers face new challenges in promoting and pro‑
tecting cultural diversity. How does one ensure local cultural expressions 
thrive in a global digital marketplace? How can national cultural policies 
be efective in an era of borderless digital consumption? These questions 
are at the heart of ongoing debates in cultural policy circles, driving the 
evolution of regulatory frameworks and support mechanisms for creative 
industries. 
4 The Digital Divide and Cultural Access: While digital platforms have 
expanded access to cultural content, they have also highlighted and, in 
some cases, exacerbated existing inequalities. The digital divide – the gap 
between those with ready access to digital technologies and those with‑
out – has emerged as a critical issue in cultural policy. Ensuring equitable 
access to digital cultural content has become a key concern for policymak‑
ers and cultural organisations alike, recognising that access to diverse cul‑
tural expressions is vital for fostering understanding and creativity in our 
interconnected world. 
Bakhshi and Throsby (2012) delve deep into how these digital innovations 
reshape creative industries’ business models. Their research illuminates the 
profound implications of this digital shift, not just for individual creators 
and companies, but for how we conceptualise and measure the contribution 
of creative industries to economic growth in a global context. As we stand at 
this digital frontier, it’s clear that the interplay between globalisation and dig‑
italisation is crafting a new reality for CCIs. This evolving landscape presents 
challenges and opportunities – challenges in preserving cultural diversity and 
ensuring equitable access, as well as opportunities for unprecedented cultural 
exchange and creative innovation. 
Change is the only constant in this complex new world of global digi‑
tal culture. As policymakers, creators and consumers, we must navigate this 
shifting terrain with agility and foresight, always mindful of creative goods’ 
dual economic and cultural nature. In this delicate balance lies the key to fos‑
tering an economically vibrant and culturally rich global creative economy.

2.3.5 Case Study 1: Music Streaming Industry and Solow‑Swan 
limitations 
The standard neoclassical exogenous growth model, developed by Solow 
(1956), is widely used in economics for empirically estimating TFP. It is the 
frst infuential neoclassical model to attempt to explain diferences in levels 
and growth rates of per capita income across countries through an aggre‑
gate production function. In Solow’s model, the aggregate production func‑
tion Y is determined by three factors: capital (K), labour (L) and the state 
of technology (A). The aggregate production function is represented as a 
Cobb‑Douglas function: 
° (1−° )Y = A KL (1)t t t t 
where Y: total output or aggregate production of an economy, often meas‑
ured as real GDP. A: TFP or the level of technology, representing the efciency 
of combining inputs to produce output. K: capital stock in the economy, in‑
cluding physical capital such as machines, buildings and infrastructure. L: 
labour input, typically measured as the total number of workers or total 
hours worked. α: output elasticity of capital, measuring the responsiveness of 
output to changes in capital. 1–α: output elasticity of labour, measuring the 
responsiveness of output to changes in labour. 
The function assumes constant returns to scale and diminishing returns 
to capital, two fundamental assumptions underlying the model’s results. The 
long‑run growth rate of output is determined by the sum of the growth rates 
of capital, technological progress and the labour force (which often corre‑
lates with the population growth rate). If technological progress ofsets the 
efects of capital depreciation, economies will converge to a full‑employment 
steady state, driven primarily by demographic expansion. 
Assuming the labour force grows at the same rate as the population and 
technology remains constant, any positive variation in capital accumulation 
will, over time, face diminishing returns. This causes the economic growth 
rate to converge to zero in the long run. Without technological change, 
economic growth is constrained to the mere reproduction of a stationary 
equilibrium. 
As technical progress is treated as an exogenous factor or residual in this 
model, it implies that the model itself does not satisfactorily explain the 
main drivers of sustained economic growth and capital accumulation. This 
limitation highlights the need for models that endogenise technological pro‑
gress to better understand long‑term economic growth dynamics. Despite its 
limitations, the Solow model provides a simple yet powerful framework for 
analysing economic growth and has been foundational in developing more 
advanced growth theories. 
52 The Political Economy of the Creative and Cultural Industries 
 
 
Let’s examine the music streaming industry, focusing on Spotify, to il‑
lustrate how modern creative industries challenge traditional growth models 
like the one explained by Solow‑Swan. The music streaming industry has 
rapidly grown over the past decade, transforming how music is consumed 
and monetised. In 2023, streaming accounted for over 84% of recorded mu‑
sic revenue in the US. This case study explores how the music streaming 
industry, represented by Spotify, challenges critical assumptions of the neo‑
classical growth model. 
Founded in 2006 in Sweden, Spotify is a music streaming service that al‑
lows users to access a vast catalogue of licensed music on various devices. Us‑
ers can create and share playlists and choose between listening for free with 
ads or purchasing a premium subscription for ad‑free streaming. Spotify’s 
subscriber base has grown signifcantly recently, doubling since early 2019. 
By the fourth quarter of 2023, Spotify had reached 236  million premium 
subscribers worldwide, up from 205 million in the same quarter of 2022. The 
evolution of its subscriber numbers is illustrated in Figure 2.1. 
Data from Spotify’s results and fnancial statements were used to analyse 
the compatibility of the assumptions of the neoclassical growth model with

Property and Equipment (from the non‑current assets statement, as a stock 
variable); (ii) Intangible Capital (from the non‑current assets statement, as 
a stock variable); (iii) Gross Proft (from the income statement, as a fow 
variable); (iv) Research and Development Expenses (from the income state‑

statement in investments, as a fow variable). The company’s gross proft per‑
formance and the evolution of the proft per premium subscriber are depicted 
in Figure 2.2. 
The streaming industry’s products are considered non‑rivalrous goods, 
meaning that one person’s consumption does not reduce the availability for 
others. As a result, the cost of adding an extra subscriber is negligible, mak‑
ing positive returns to scale highly plausible – this contrasts with the standard 
Solow‑Swan model, which assumes constant returns to scale. Despite the 
consistent growth in Spotify’s subscriber base, the proft per subscriber has 
shown varying trends over the six years analysed. During the period ranging 
from the second quarter of 2020 to the frst quarter of 2022, the growth rate 
of proft was relatively higher than that of the premium subscribers, indicat‑
ing temporary positive returns to scale. Although the data cover a relatively 
short period, it is essential to note that the concept of constant returns to 
scale is primarily associated with producing physical goods, being less ap‑
plicable to streaming services and the creative and cultural industries (CCIs) 
in general. 
As previously mentioned, the diminishing returns to capital accumulation 
is a core assumption of the neoclassical growth model. The data obtained 
from Spotify’s consolidated fnancial statements indicate this assumption 
does not hold. Although the proft‑to‑capital relation shows an increasing 
trend (see Figure 2.3), it doesn’t necessarily mean increasing returns to capi‑
tal accumulation. Still, this is the case from 2019 to 2021, a period in which 
54 The Political Economy of the Creative and Cultural Industries 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
  
 
both gross proft and property and equipment assets are increasing, with the 
former growing relatively more (ΔP > 0, ΔK > 0, ΔP/ΔK > 1). 
However, besides the physical capital stock, intangible assets are central in 
CCIs, particularly in the music streaming industry, because they encompass 
the essential elements of content licensing, intellectual property and brand 
reputation, which are crucial for delivering and monetising creative products 
in a digital environment. Furthermore, CCIs thrive on continuous innovation 
and adaptation to new trends, technologies and consumer preferences. This 
requires a strong base of intangible assets derived from research, develop‑
ment and knowledge. Particularly for the streaming industry, R&D is cru‑
cial for developing new technologies, features and user experiences that keep 
streaming platforms competitive. 
The data obtained from the consolidated fnancial statements indicate 
that the accumulation of intangible assets grew faster than physical prop‑
erty and equipment, increasing by 6.54 times and 2.38 times, respectively, 
between 2017 and 2023. By 2023, the value of intangible assets was ap‑
proximately two‑thirds of the value of property and equipment. Further ‑
more, R&D expenses showed consistent growth over the period analysed, 
averaging 28% per year. These fndings suggest that not only do some of the 
core assumptions of the Solow‑Swan model not hold for the music stream‑
ing industry but also that the standard neoclassical growth model is not 
well equipped to account for the unique characteristics of CCIs and their 
growth determinants, particularly regarding innovation, knowledge and 
digitalisation.

2.3.5.1 Critical Perspective on the Solow‑Swan Model 
Whilst the Solow‑Swan model provides a foundational framework for un‑
derstanding economic growth, its application to creative industries reveals 
several limitations: 
1 Exogenous Technological Progress: The model’s treatment of technologi‑
cal progress as exogenous is particularly problematic for creative indus‑
tries, where innovation is often endogenous and driven by market forces. 
2 Homogeneous Capital: The model assumes capital is homogeneous, which 
fails to capture the crucial role of intangible assets in creative industries. 
As seen in the Spotify case, intangible assets grew much faster than physi‑
cal capital. 
3 Constant Returns to Scale: Creative industries often exhibit increasing re‑
turns to scale, especially in digital contexts, which the Solow‑Swan model 
doesn’t account for. 
4 Diminishing Returns to Capital: The assumption of diminishing returns 
to capital accumulation only holds in some creative industry contexts, as 
evidenced by Spotify’s increasing proft‑to‑capital ratio. 
5 Limited Role of Human Capital: The model needs to explicitly account for 
human capital, which is crucial in knowledge‑intensive creative industries. 
These limitations suggest that whilst the Solow‑Swan model provides useful 
insights into general economic growth processes, it may not adequately cap‑
ture the unique dynamics of creative industries. 
2.3.6 Case Study 2: Endogenous Growth Models  
and Games Industry 
Recognising the limitations of the Solow‑Swan model, particularly its treat‑
ment of technological progress as exogenous, economists developed “second 
generation” neoclassical models attempting to endogenise economic growth 
drivers. The novelty of the endogenous growth theories is that they all aban‑
don the hypothesis that the investment rate does not afect the steady‑state 
growth rate of output, which theoretically presupposes capital accumulation 
under diminishing returns since the other determinants of aggregate output, 
namely technology and the employment of labour, are exogenously given in 
the standard Solow‑Swan model. 
The AK model is the frst version of the endogenous growth theory, which 
came to fruition in the mid‑1980s and assumes that either technology or 
labour grows in proportion to capital and in which the growth of one of 
them counteracts the efects of diminishing returns, thus allowing output to 
increase in proportion to capital, resulting in a production function of the 
form Y=AK, with “A” constant. Following these assumptions, the capital 
56 The Political Economy of the Creative and Cultural Industries 
 
operates under conditions of constant returns. A more signifcant capital ac‑
cumulation implies a permanent efect that generates a higher steady‑state 
growth rate, characterising the economic growth driver. 
Later, the simple version of the AK model was extended, whereas Lu‑
cas (1988) developed an AK model where the creation and transmission of 
knowledge occur through human capital accumulation. Romer (1990) em‑
phasises the role of technological change and knowledge accumulation as 
drivers of economic growth. These extensions introduce the role of human 
capital, R&D and innovation as central components in driving economic 
growth, where knowledge drives technological progress and improves the 
TFP in such a way as to compensate for the decreasing returns of capital. 
Therefore, technical change is no longer seen as an externality from capital 
accumulation as in the earlier versions of the AK model. A remarkable fea‑
ture is that ideas are not considered a rival factor, diferent from the treat‑
ment given to regular factors such as capital, labour and land. It means that 
other frms can take advantage of new knowledge. In this new class of mod‑
els, innovation and human capital play an essential role in breaking produc‑
tion paradigms (Schumpeterian creative destruction) and pushing economic 
growth. To further illustrate how creative industries align more closely with 
endogenous growth theories, let’s examine the case of the games industry. 
The games industry has rapidly emerged as a dynamic and expanding sec‑
tor within the CCIs, driven by technological advancements and a growing 
global audience. In this context, the games industry has experienced positive 
and consistent revenue growth on a worldwide scale over the past decade, ac‑
cording to the data collected from the Newzoo Global Games Market Report 
and displayed in Figure 2.3. 
To explore the connections and applications of endogenous growth mod‑
els to this industry  –  specifcally Lucas (1988) and Romer (1990), which 
emphasise the critical role of knowledge accumulation in economic growth 
through human capital and innovation, respectively – we collected balance 
sheet data from the fnancial statements of three major companies in the sec‑
tor: Electronic Arts, Tencent Games and Take‑Two Interactive. These com‑
panies were chosen because they rank among the sector’s top ten in gaming 
revenue (Newzoo, 2023) and focus exclusively on the gaming industry, un‑
like companies such as Sony, Apple or Microsoft, where gaming is just one 
of several business segments. Moreover, since they are publicly listed, the 
companies’ fnancial statements are publicly available on their respective in‑
vestor relations websites. 
As in the music streaming case discussed before, the specifc variables we 
collected include (i) property and equipment, (ii) intangible capital, (iii) gross 
proft, (iv) research and development expenses and (v) capital expenditures. 
These variables provide an overview of the companies’ performance and

reveal trends in both fxed capital and knowledge accumulation (as seen in

ing a strong positive trend. Consistent with Romer’s (1990) approach, we 
observed that R&D expenses grew by an average of 8% for EA and 19% 
for Tencent and Take‑Two, suggesting a strong positive correlation between 
knowledge accumulation and growth. However, even though the trend is 
clearly correlated, causality cannot be defnitively inferred. The evolution of 
the gross proft and R&D expenses is illustrated in Figure 2.4. 
Additionally, the stock of intangible assets grew signifcantly between 2014 
and 2023, increasing by 212% for EA, 3,272% for Tencent and 1,536% for 
Take‑Two. This growth refects these companies’ strategic emphasis on hu‑
man capital development and intellectual property, both critical components 
of intangible assets. In line with Lucas’ (1988) model, which highlights the 
role of human capital in sustaining economic growth, this increase suggests 
that investments in knowledge, creativity and specialised skills are critical 
drivers of value creation in the gaming industry. This growth is shown in 
Figure 2.5 and underscores the importance of intangible assets in driving the 
industry’s expansion. 
While the rate of investment in property and equipment also showed posi‑
tive average growth during the period, it was lower than the robust proft 
growth observed. This suggests the existence of positive marginal returns 
to physical capital accumulation, although intangibles play a more central 
role in the industry’s success. These fndings suggest that endogenous growth
