models, such as Romer’s (1990) and Lucas’ (1988), are better suited than the 
standard neoclassical model for understanding the dynamics of CCIs, ofer ‑
ing more valuable insights into how these sectors grow. 
In conclusion, while the Solow‑Swan model provides a foundational 
understanding of economic growth, the unique characteristics of creative 
industries like music streaming and gaming necessitate more nuanced ap‑
proaches. Endogenous growth models, with their focus on knowledge, inno‑
vation and human capital, ofer more robust frameworks for understanding 
the growth dynamics of these sectors. As we continue to navigate the evolv‑
ing landscape of the creative economy, these models provide valuable in‑
sights for researchers and policymakers seeking to foster sustainable growth 
in these industries. 
2.3.6.1 Critical Perspective on Endogenous Growth Models 
Endogenous growth models, while addressing some limitations of the 
Solow‑Swan model, also have their limitations when applied to creative 
industries: 
1 Oversimplifcation of Innovation: While these models endogenise techno‑
logical progress, they may oversimplify the complex, non‑linear nature of 
innovation in creative industries. 
2 Measurement Challenges: Quantifying factors like knowledge accumula‑
tion or human capital in creative contexts can be challenging, potentially 
limiting the models’ practical applicability. 
Creative and Cultural Industries and Economic Growth 59 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
  
 
3 Assumption of Perfect Competition: Many endogenous growth models 
assume perfect competition, which may not refect the reality of creative 
industries, which are often characterised by monopolistic competition or 
winner‑take‑all dynamics. 
4 Neglect of Institutional Factors: These models may need to fully capture 
the role of institutions, cultural factors and policy environments crucial 
for creative industry growth. 
5 Focus on Steady‑State Growth: The emphasis on steady‑state growth 
paths may need to adequately refect many creative industries’ volatile and 
rapidly evolving nature. 
2.3.6.2 Synthesis and Future Directions 
While both neoclassical and endogenous growth models ofer valuable in‑
sights into the growth dynamics of creative industries, they also reveal the 
need for more tailored approaches: 
1 Hybrid Models: Future research could develop hybrid models that com‑
bine elements of neoclassical and endogenous growth theories with spe‑
cifc features of creative industries. 
2 Network Efects: Models could be extended to better incorporate network 
efects and platform dynamics prevalent in digital creative industries. 
3 Intangible Capital: More sophisticated modelling of intangible capital ac‑
cumulation and its impact on growth is needed. 
4 Non‑Linear Growth Patterns: Models that can account for the often 
non‑linear and disruptive growth patterns in creative industries could pro‑
vide more accurate predictions. 
5 Cultural and Social Factors: Incorporating cultural and social factors into 
growth models could enhance their explanatory power for creative industries. 
6 Data‑Driven Approaches: With the increasing availability of granular data 
in creative industries, more data‑driven, industry‑specifc models could be 
developed. 
In conclusion, while existing growth models provide valuable frameworks 
for understanding economic growth in creative industries, they also high‑
light the need for more nuanced, industry‑specifc approaches. The unique 
characteristics of creative industries – their reliance on intangible assets, the 
central role of innovation and human capital, and their disruptive growth 
patterns – call for continued evolution in economic growth theory. As these 
industries continue to play an increasingly signifcant role in the global econ‑
omy, developing more tailored theoretical frameworks will be crucial for 
both academic understanding and efective policymaking.

1 Economic externalities refer to the costs or benefts that afect a third party who 
did not choose to incur those costs or benefts. These are spillover efects of eco‑
nomic activity that impact individuals or entities not directly involved in pro‑
ducing or consuming a good or service. Externalities can be positive (external 
benefts) or negative (external costs). For example, a positive externality might 
be the societal benefts from education, where an individual’s education improves 
their prospects and contributes to overall social productivity. A negative external‑
ity could be pollution from a factory, which impacts the health and well‑being of 
nearby residents who are not involved in the factory’s operations. Externalities are 
signifcant in economics because they represent market failures, where the price 
mechanism fails to account for an economic activity’s total social costs or benefts, 
potentially leading to the over‑ or under‑production of certain goods or services. 
2 The chapter about innovation and cultural and creative industries further down 
in the book provides an in‑depth analysis of the Schumpeterian Theory applied to 
CCIs. 
3 Cambridge Analytica harvested personal data from millions of Facebook users 
without consent. They collected this data through a quiz app, including informa‑
tion on users’ friends. The company used the data to create voter profles and 
target political ads, notably during the 2016 US election. The scandal’s revelation 
in 2018 sparked outrage over privacy and concerns about election manipulation. 
The fallout led to investigations, hearings, a drop in Facebook’s stock, and in‑
creased scrutiny of data practices in social media.

CREATIVE AND CULTURAL 
INDUSTRIES AND SOCIAL‑ECONOMIC 
DEVELOPMENT 
3.1 The Necessary Debate: The Mirage of Prosperity: 
Unravelling the Paradox of Economic Growth and 
Development 
In the sweltering heat of Gujarat, India, March 2024 witnessed an event that 
captured the world’s attention. The air was excited as private jets descended 
from the sky, carrying a who’s who of global elites. Tech moguls rubbed 
shoulders with Bollywood stars, while world leaders mingled with indus‑
trial tycoons. The occasion? The pre‑wedding celebrations of Anant Ambani, 
the youngest scion of India’s wealthiest family. For three days, the city of 
Jamnagar transformed into a spectacle of opulence. International pop sensa‑
tions Rihanna and Dua Lipa serenaded guests in elaborate sets that married 
cutting‑edge technology with India’s rich cultural heritage. What is the price 
tag for this extravaganza? A staggering $150 million (Biswas, 2024). 
Yet, just a few hundred kilometres away, a diferent India struggles to sur‑
vive. In the sprawling slums of Mumbai, millions wake each day to the harsh 
realities of poverty. In the shadow of Antilia – the Ambanis’ 27‑story per ‑
sonal skyscraper – families are subject to inadequate housing, lack of clean 
water and limited access to primary healthcare and education (Roy, 2024). 
This stark juxtaposition is a powerful metaphor for a broader global phe‑
nomenon: the chasm between economic growth and genuine economic de‑
velopment. The Ambani celebrations, while impressive in scale and grandeur, 
highlight a crucial question facing many nations today: Does an increase in 
wealth necessarily translate to an improvement in the lives of all citizens? To 
understand this paradox, one must diferentiate the concepts of economic 
growth and development. Economic growth is typically measured by gross 
 
 
  
   
 
 
 
  
 
64 The Political Economy of the Creative and Cultural Industries 
domestic product (GDP) or gross national product (GNP) increases. It fo‑
cuses on the expansion of an economy in monetary terms, characterised by 
a quantitative approach that primarily measures the increase in goods and 
services production. Growth has a short‑term orientation, often measured 
quarterly or annually, and is income‑centric, concentrating on the overall 
rise in national income. The ability to host a $150 million pre‑wedding event 
undoubtedly showcases India’s signifcant economic growth over recent 
decades. 
In contrast, economic development paints a more nuanced picture. It goes 
beyond mere wealth accumulation, encompassing improvements in an entire 
population’s overall quality of life, health, education and well‑being. Eco‑
nomic development has a qualitative focus, considering enhancements in so‑
cial welfare and living standards. It takes a long‑term perspective, assesses 
over extended periods and adopts a holistic approach that includes social, 
cultural and environmental factors. This broader view of progress provides 
a more comprehensive understanding of a nation’s advancement beyond f‑
nancial metrics. 
3.1.1 Economic Theory Perspectives on Growth vs. Development 
The debate between economic growth and development is not just a mat‑
ter of practical policymaking; it’s deeply rooted in economic theory. Various 
schools of economic thought have grappled with this issue, ofering diferent 
perspectives on the relationship between growth and development. Below, 
you can fnd a map to explore in your further readings: 
i Classical Economics (see Chapter 1): Classical economists like Adam 
Smith and David Ricardo primarily focused on economic growth. Smith’s 
“The Wealth of Nations” (1776) emphasised the importance of division 
of labour, capital accumulation and free markets in economic growth. 
While Smith acknowledged the importance of education and institu‑
tional factors, the primary focus was increasing productive capacity and 
national wealth. Ricardo’s theory of comparative advantage further re‑
inforced the idea that specialisation and trade could lead to increased 
output and economic growth. However, these early theories should have 
explicitly addressed the broader concept of development as we under ‑
stand it today. 
ii Neoclassical Growth Theory (see Chapter 1): The neoclassical growth 
model, developed by Robert Solow and Trevor Swan in the 1950s, pro‑
vided a framework for understanding long‑term economic growth. This 
model emphasised the roles of capital accumulation, labour force growth 
and technological progress in driving fnancial expansion. While the 
Solow‑Swan model was ground‑breaking in its growth analysis, it focused 
primarily on quantitative expansion rather than qualitative improvement. 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
Creative and Cultural Industries and Social‑Economic Development 65 
The model assumed that benefts would naturally “trickle down” to all 
segments of society as economies grow. This assumption has been chal‑
lenged by the persistent inequality in many rapidly growing economies. 
iii Endogenous Growth Theory (see previous chapter): In the 1980s and 
1990s, economists like Paul Romer and Robert Lucas developed endog‑
enous growth theory, emphasising the role of human capital, innova‑
tion and knowledge spillovers in driving economic growth. This theory 
began to bridge the gap between growth and development by highlight‑
ing the importance of education, research and development and insti‑
tutional factors in fostering long‑term economic progress. Endogenous 
growth theory suggested that investments in human capital and inno‑
vation could increase returns to scale, potentially allowing developing 
countries to catch up with more advanced economies more quickly than 
the Solow‑Swan model predicted. 
iv Development Economics: The feld of development economics, which 
gained prominence in the mid‑20th century, explicitly focused on the 
broader concept of economic development rather than just growth. 
Economists like Arthur Lewis, Gunnar Myrdal and Amartya Sen ex‑
panded the focus beyond GDP growth to include structural economic 
changes, human welfare improvements and human capabilities develop‑
ment. Lewis’s dual‑sector model, for instance, highlighted the process of 
structural transformation as labour moves from traditional to modern 
sectors. This model addressed the qualitative changes during develop‑
ment, not just the quantitative growth. As mentioned earlier, Sen’s ca‑
pability approach argued that development should be seen as expanding 
human freedoms and capabilities. This perspective shifted the focus from 
national income to individual well‑being and agency. 
v Institutional Economics: institutional economists like Douglass North 
emphasised the role of institutions in shaping economic outcomes. This 
school of thought argues that the quality of institutions – such as prop‑
erty rights, rule of law and political stability – is crucial for both growth 
and development. Good institutions can foster economic growth and 
broader development by providing the framework for individuals and 
businesses to thrive. 
vi Ecological Economics: ecological economists like Herman Daly have chal‑
lenged the very notion of endless economic growth, arguing that it’s incom‑
patible with the fnite resources of our planet. This perspective suggests 
that we must move beyond growth as the primary economic goal instead 
of focusing on sustainable development that respects ecological limits. 
vii Synthesis in Modern Development Theory: Modern development theory 
attempts to synthesise these various perspectives. It recognises the impor‑
tance of economic growth but sees it to an end rather than an end. For 
instance, the United Nations’ Sustainable Development Goals (SDGs) 
encompass a wide range of objectives beyond just economic growth, 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
  
66 The Political Economy of the Creative and Cultural Industries 
including poverty reduction, health, education, gender equality and envi‑
ronmental sustainability. This modern synthesis acknowledges that while 
growth can provide the resources necessary for development, it must be 
accompanied by appropriate policies and institutions to ensure its ben‑
efts are widely shared and sustainable in the long term. 
3.1.2 Implications for the Growth vs. Development Debate 
The evolution of economic theory refects a growing recognition of the limi‑
tations of focusing solely on economic growth. While growth remains an 
important factor in economic progress, especially for low‑income countries, 
theory has increasingly emphasised the need for a more holistic approach to 
development. It suggests that policymakers should: 
1 Look beyond GDP: While GDP growth is essential, it shouldn’t be the sole 
focus of economic policy. 
2 Invest in human capital: Education, health and skill development are cru‑
cial for both growth and development. 
3 Build strong institutions: Good governance, rule of law and inclusive insti‑
tutions are vital in translating growth into broad‑based development. 
4 Consider sustainability: Economic policies should consider long‑term en‑
vironmental and social sustainability. 
5 Focus on inequality: Policies should aim to ensure that the benefts of 
growth are widely shared across society. 
In Table 3.1, you can see some fgures showing the disconnection between 
development and growth. 
3.1.2.1 Defnitions of Variables 
1 GDP Growth (%): The annual percentage growth rate of GDP at market 
prices based on constant local currency. 
2 GDP per capita (current US$): GDP divided by midyear population, con‑
verted to US dollars using ofcial exchange rates. 
3 Life Expectancy (years): The number of years a newborn infant would live 
if prevailing patterns of mortality at the time of its birth were to stay the 
same throughout its life. 
4 Gini Index: Measures how income distribution among individuals or 
households within an economy deviates from a perfectly equal distribu‑
tion. A Gini index of 0 represents perfect equality, while an index of 100 
implies perfect inequality. 
5 Human Development Index (HDI): A composite statistic of life expec‑
tancy, education and per capita income indicators used to rank countries 
into four tiers of human development. It ranges from 0 to 1, with higher 
values indicating higher levels of human development.

BOX 3.1 KEY TAKEAWAYS ECONOMIC GROWTH × 
DEVELOPMENT 
1 Economic growth alone is insufcient to measure a country’s development. 
Social indicators like wellbeing measurement inequality and HDI provide a 
more comprehensive picture. 
2 The relationship between economic growth and social development is com‑
plex and varies signifcantly between countries. 
3 Rapid economic growth can facilitate improvements in social indicators, but 
this is not automatic and requires targeted policies. 
4 Countries can make progress on social indicators even in periods of eco‑
nomic difculty, highlighting the importance of sustained social policies. 
5 While developing countries show faster improvement rates in many indica‑
tors, signifcant gaps with developed countries persist. 
6 The diminishing social returns on higher‑level development suggest that 
developed countries may need to fnd new ways to measure and achieve 
progress. 
7 A holistic approach to development, considering both economic and social 
factors, is crucial for creating sustainable and equitable societies. 
In the decade from 2009 to 2019, the world witnessed a tale of two trajecto‑
ries: the soaring line of economic growth and the more modest climb of social 
progress. This period, marked by the aftermath of the global fnancial crisis 
and the eve of a worldwide pandemic, ofers a revealing snapshot of how na‑
tions balance the pursuit of wealth with the well‑being of their citizens. 
At frst glance, the numbers tell a story of remarkable progress. China, 
the economic powerhouse of the developing world, saw its GDP per capita 
Creative and Cultural Industries and Social‑Economic Development 69 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
skyrocket by an astonishing 166.6%. India, not to be outdone, posted an 
impressive 86.7% increase. Even among developed nations, the United 
States boasted a robust 38.6% growth in GDP per capita. These fgures, 
trumpeted in fnancial reports and political speeches, paint a picture of na‑
tions on the rise, of prosperity blooming across the globe. Yet, when we 
peel back the layers of these economic success stories, a more nuanced and 
sometimes troubling picture emerges. The glittering facade of GDP growth 
often masks a more complex reality of uneven development and persistent 
social challenges. 
India’s story is striking. Despite its impressive economic growth, its HDI 
increased by 11.8% – a more signifcant percentage than China’s, but from a 
much lower base. This underscores a crucial point: rapid economic expansion 
doesn’t automatically solve deep‑rooted social issues. Despite its economic 
strides, India continues to grapple with signifcant healthcare, education and 
inequality challenges. 
The United States presents a diferent facet of this phenomenon. As the 
world’s largest economy, it saw healthy GDP per capita growth. However, its 
HDI grew by a mere 1.6%, the smallest increase among the countries we ex‑
amined. This suggests that at higher levels of development, economic growth 
yields diminishing returns regarding social progress. More troublingly, the 
US Gini index, a measure of income inequality, increased slightly, indicating 
that the fruits of economic growth were not evenly distributed. European na‑
tions like France and the United Kingdom tell a similar story. Despite being 
among the world’s wealthiest countries, they saw relatively modest improve‑
ments in HDI (3.3% and 3.7%, respectively). France experienced a concern‑
ing 8.7% increase in its Gini index, suggesting a widening gap between rich 
and poor despite overall economic growth. 
Perhaps the most poignant illustration of the disconnect between eco‑
nomic metrics and lived reality comes from Argentina. Despite facing eco‑
nomic headwinds with negative GDP growth rates, Argentina improved its 
HDI by 3.3% and reduced income inequality. This paradox emphasises that 
economic struggle doesn’t necessarily preclude social progress and that eco‑
nomic growth doesn’t guarantee it. Nigeria provides yet another perspective. 
With a 17.9% increase in GDP per capita and a signifcant 12.1% improve‑
ment in HDI, it showcases the potential for more balanced growth in de‑
veloping economies. However, starting from a low base, Nigeria still needs 
to catch up in absolute terms, highlighting the long road ahead for many 
developing nations. 
Life expectancy, a fundamental measure of a society’s health and well‑
being, tells a similar story of uneven progress. While all countries in our 
analysis saw improvements, the gains were often modest in developed na‑
tions. The United States, for all its wealth and medical advances, saw a mere 
70 The Political Economy of the Creative and Cultural Industries 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
  
 
 
 
 
 
0.4% increase in life expectancy. Contrast this with Nigeria, which achieved 
a 7.7% improvement, albeit from a much lower starting point. This pattern 
repeats across various indicators: economic growth races ahead, with so‑
cial development struggling to keep up. This trend raises profound questions 
about how we measure progress and what we value as a society. 
The implications are clear: While GDP growth is essential, it is insuf‑
cient to measure a nation’s true progress. It can mask growing inequalities, 
environmental degradation and stagnation in quality of life. Our analysis 
reveals that even as bank accounts grow fatter, societies don’t always grow 
healthier, more equitable or happier at the same rate. This disconnect be‑
tween economic indicators and social well‑being is not just a statistical cu‑
riosity; it has real‑world consequences. It can lead to social unrest, political 
instability and disconnection between ofcial narratives of progress and 
people’s lived experiences. For instance, the yellow vest protests in France 
erupted against a backdrop of economic growth that wasn’t felt by large 
population segments. 
As we move forward, policymakers, economists and citizens alike must 
recognise the limitations of GDP as a measure of societal progress. We need 
a more holistic approach that considers the quantity, quality and distribution 
of economic growth. Measures like the HDI, while not perfect, ofer a more 
comprehensive picture of a nation’s development. Moreover, this analysis un‑
derscores the need for targeted policies to translate economic gains into tan‑
gible improvements in people’s lives. Education, healthcare, environmental 
protection and measures to reduce inequality must be prioritised alongside 
fnancial growth. 
The story of the decade from 2009 to 2019 is more than just one of 
economic recovery and expansion. It’s a complex narrative of uneven pro‑
gress, of societies grappling with balancing economic dynamism with social 
well‑being. As we face the challenges of the coming decades – from climate 
change to technological disruption – this balancing act will only become 
more crucial. In the end, the accurate measure of a society’s progress lies 
not in the cold statistics of GDP, but in the lived experiences of its peo‑
ple – their health, opportunities and quality of life. Our analysis serves as a 
reminder that these human factors must be at the heart of how we conceive 
and pursue development. Only then can we ensure that the rising tide of 
economic growth truly lifts all boats, creating more afuent and better so‑
cieties for all their citizens. 
The debate between growth and development in economic theory reminds 
us that while growth can provide the resources for development, it doesn’t 
automatically lead to improved quality of life for all. It underscores the need 
for deliberate policies and institutions that can harness the power of eco‑
nomic growth to foster genuine, inclusive development. 
Creative and Cultural Industries and Social‑Economic Development 71 
 
 3.1.2.2 The Limitations of Economic Growth as a Measure of Progress 
The pursuit of economic growth has long been a primary goal for nations 
worldwide, often viewed as a panacea for societal ills. However, the limita‑
tions of focusing solely on economic growth become glaringly apparent when 
we look beyond the glittering facades of urban centres and mega‑events. 
One of the most pressing issues is the exacerbation of income inequality. 
Rapid economic growth can lead to a concentration of wealth in the hands of 
a few, creating social tensions and reducing overall well‑being. Brazil’s expe‑
rience in the early 2000s further illustrates this point. The country was hailed 
as an emerging economic powerhouse during signifcant economic growth 
fuelled by a global commodities boom. However, a closer examination re‑
vealed a more complex reality. Despite the surge in GDP, income inequality 
remained stubbornly high. The Gini coefcient, a key measure of income dis‑
tribution, showed slight improvement, indicating that this economic boom’s 
fruits were not evenly shared across society (Lustig et al., 2013). 
Environmental degradation is another critical concern often overlooked 
in pursuing unchecked growth. Over the past half‑century, China’s mete‑
oric rise as an economic superpower provides a cautionary tale. While the 
transformation of its cities, expansion of infrastructure and growing global 
infuence are undeniable testaments to its economic prowess, this growth has 
come at a severe environmental cost. Rapid industrialisation has led to severe 
air pollution in major cities, water scarcity in many regions and widespread 
soil contamination (Economy, 2010). The smog‑flled skies of Beijing serve as 
a stark reminder that economic growth, when pursued without consideration 
for environmental sustainability, can lead to signifcant challenges that ofset 
many of its benefts. 
Moreover, it’s crucial to recognise that GDP growth does not necessarily 
translate to improvements in quality of life. Some countries with lower GDP 
per capita manage to achieve higher levels of well‑being for their citizens. 
Costa Rica, for instance, consistently ranks high in global happiness indices 
and is a leader in environmental sustainability despite having a GDP per 
capita signifcantly lower than that of the United States or many European 
nations. As of 2023, nearly 25% of Costa Rica’s land is protected as national 
parks or reserves, and an impressive 98% of its electricity comes from renew‑
able sources (IRENA, 2023). By prioritising education, healthcare and envi‑
ronmental conservation alongside economic growth, Costa Rica has created 
a society that, in many ways, ofers a higher quality of life than some of its 
wealthier counterparts. 
The case of Saudi Arabia further underscores the limitations of economic 
metrics alone. Despite high GDP per capita due to oil wealth, the country 
has faced persistent criticism for human rights issues, particularly regard‑
ing women’s rights and political freedoms (Human Rights Watch, 2023). 
72 The Political Economy of the Creative and Cultural Industries 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
Economic prosperity doesn’t automatically translate into social progress or 
individual liberties. Some important structured refections in this perspective 
were developed by: 
1 Herman Daly’s Steady‑State Economy: Daly argued for a “steady‑state 
economy” that prioritises qualitative development over quantitative 
growth and emphasises the physical limits of our planet (Daly, 1991). 
2 Kate Raworth’s Doughnut Economics: Raworth’s model proposes a bal‑
ance between meeting human needs and respecting planetary boundaries 
(Raworth, 2017). 
3 Amartya Sen’s Capability Approach: Sen emphasised the importance of 
expanding human capabilities and freedoms as the accurate measure of 
development (Sen, 1999). 
4 Joseph Stiglitz on Rethinking GDP: Stiglitz has been a vocal critic of GDP 
as a measure of societal well‑being, advocating for more comprehensive 
metrics that account for sustainability and equity (Stiglitz et al., 2018). 
Given these limitations, there is a strong case for prioritising holistic eco‑
nomic development over a narrow focus on GDP growth. This approach 
encompasses several key elements: 
a Firstly, investing in human capital through education, healthcare and skill 
development can lead to long‑term economic benefts and improved qual‑
ity of life. South Korea’s economic miracle in the latter half of the 20th 
century is primarily attributed to its heavy investment in education and 
human capital development, creating a more resilient and prosperous so‑
ciety (Seth, 2017). 
b Secondly, prioritising sustainable development ensures long‑term envi‑
ronmental and economic viability. Bhutan’s Gross National Happiness 
(GNH) concept ofers a compelling alternative to the growth‑at‑all‑costs 
model. By focusing on sustainable development, preservation of cultural 
values, conservation of the natural environment and good governance, 
Bhutan presents a unique approach to national progress, despite its signif‑
cant economic challenges (Ura et al., 2012). 
c Thirdly, fostering inclusive growth by reducing inequality and improv‑
ing opportunities for all can lead to more stable and prosperous societies. 
Rwanda’s Vision 2050, for instance, aims for inclusive growth by focusing 
on poverty reduction, gender equality and rural development alongside 
economic transformation (Republic of Rwanda, 2020). 
d Lastly, prioritising overall well‑being and quality of life provides a more 
comprehensive view of a nation’s progress. New Zealand’s introduction 
of a “well‑being budget” in 2019 exemplifes this approach, focusing on 
Creative and Cultural Industries and Social‑Economic Development 73 
 
  
 
 
 
 
 
 
mental health, child well‑being and environmental protection alongside 
traditional economic indicators (New Zealand Treasury, 2019). 
While economic growth remains essential, it should not be pursued at the 
expense of broader societal and environmental concerns. By embracing a 
more holistic approach to economic development, nations can work towards 
creating societies that are not just wealthier but also more equitable, sus‑
tainable and fulflling for all their citizens. As we return to the image of 
the lavish Ambani celebrations juxtaposed against Mumbai’s slums, it’s clear 
that the path forward requires a fundamental shift in how we conceptualise 
and pursue progress. It calls for a move beyond the narrow focus on GDP 
growth towards a more holistic view of development that encompasses so‑
cial, environmental and economic factors. This doesn’t mean abandoning the 
pursuit of prosperity. Instead, it means redefning what prosperity looks like. 
It means creating economies that don’t just grow but develop in ways that 
enhance the quality of life for all citizens, preserve our natural world and 
build resilient, equitable societies. 
The challenge before us is signifcant, but so too is the opportunity. By 
broadening our understanding of actual progress, we open the door to creat‑
ing societies that are not just wealthy but also just, sustainable and fulflling 
for all their members. In doing so, the real celebration isn’t in hosting extrav‑
agant events but in building a world where everyone has reason to celebrate. 
3.2 Economic Development Theories: Is It Time for a 
Heterodox and Global South Expanded Revival? 
The creative and cultural industries (CCIs) have become pivotal forces in 
shaping economic trajectories worldwide, challenging traditional notions of 
growth and development. Their impact, however, manifests diferently across 
the global fnancial landscape, revealing intricate dynamics particularly vis‑
ible in developing economies. This chapter approaches these complexities, 
examining the nexus between CCIs and economic development worldwide 
through theories from the heterodox view. 
While conventional economic frameworks often struggle to capture 
the nuanced realities of developing nations’ creative sectors, a rich set of 
thought emerging from Latin America and other developing regions pro‑
vides illuminating perspectives. The structuralist approach by the Eco‑
nomic Commission for Latin America and the Caribbean (CEPAL), Raúl 
Prebisch’s centre‑periphery model and the broader dependency theory ofer 
profound insights into the structural inequalities and power dynamics that 
permeate global economic relations, including within the creative economy 
and industries. 
74 The Political Economy of the Creative and Cultural Industries 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
These Global South theories are particularly relevant in understanding 
the dual nature of CCIs in developing economies. On the one hand, CCIs 
represent a promising avenue for economic diversifcation, job creation and 
cultural expression. On the other hand, they often face signifcant challenges 
stemming from historical patterns of dependency, unequal global trade rela‑
tions and internal structural heterogeneities. The CEPAL school, for instance, 
highlights how the technological and economic disparities between the ‘cen‑
tre’ (developed countries) and the ‘periphery’ (developing countries) can lead 
to persistent underdevelopment. This concept can be extended to the creative 
industries. Furthermore, dependency theory provides a critical framework 
for analysing how global power structures infuence the production, distri‑
bution and consumption of cultural goods and services. This perspective is 
crucial in understanding developing countries’ challenges in building com‑
petitive and sustainable creative industries while preserving cultural diversity 
and autonomy. 
It is important to note that these Global South theories have become in‑
creasingly relevant for understanding economic dynamics in the Global 
North, particularly in the context of CCIs. This relevance stems from two 
key factors: 
1 Contemporary crises and increasing inequalities: The Global North is 
experiencing economic challenges and rising inequalities that resemble 
long‑standing issues in the Global South. Theories developed to address 
these challenges in the Global South may ofer fresh insights for tackling 
similar problems in developed economies. 
2 The dual nature of CCIs: These sectors often encompass highly developed, 
technologically advanced segments (such as software development) and 
less economically developed areas (such as performing arts). This dichot‑
omy within CCIs mirrors some of the economic structures in Global South 
countries, making theories from these regions particularly applicable. 
By integrating these theories into our analysis of CCIs and economic develop‑
ment, we can gain a more nuanced understanding of: 
1 The structural barriers that hinder the growth of CCIs in developing 
economies. 
2 The potential of CCIs to address issues of economic dependency and pro‑
mote endogenous development. 
3 The importance of cultural policies that recognise and address global in‑
equalities in the creative economy. 
4 The role of CCIs in fostering sustainable and inclusive development mod‑
els that go beyond mere economic growth. 
Creative and Cultural Industries and Social‑Economic Development 75 
 
 
 
 
This chapter, therefore, not only provides a theoretical foundation for un‑
derstanding the relationship between CCIs and economic development but 
also challenges us to rethink development strategies in a way that recog‑
nises the unique contexts, challenges and potentials in the CCIs feld. By 
doing so, we can work towards more equitable and culturally sensitive ap‑
proaches to harness creativity’s power of creativity for economic and social 
progress. 
Moreover, by reading these theories in the Global North context, one can 
gain new perspectives on addressing economic disparities and fostering more 
inclusive growth in developed economies, particularly within the context of 
CCIs. This approach allows for a more comprehensive and globally relevant 
analysis of the role of CCIs in economic development, bridging insights from 
diverse economic contexts and contributing to a more nuanced understand‑
ing of creative economies worldwide. 
Additionally, the scope of heterodox economic perspectives ofers valuable 
insights into the role of CCIs in economic development. These theories com‑
plement and enrich the analysis provided by the Global South perspectives, 
ofering a more comprehensive framework for understanding the complex 
dynamics of CCIs in both developing and developed economies. Amartya 
Sen’s Capability Approach provides a crucial lens through which to view 
the impact of CCIs on development. Sen argues that development should 
be understood as expanding the fundamental freedoms people enjoy rather 
than merely increasing economic output (Sen, 1999). In this context, CCIs 
can be seen as drivers of economic growth and vehicles for enhancing human 
capabilities and freedoms. They provide opportunities for self‑expression, 
cultural participation and creative skills, all of which contribute to what Sen 
would consider meaningful development. The Capability Approach aligns 
well with the potential of CCIs to address issues of economic dependency and 
promote endogenous development, as highlighted by the Global South theo‑
ries. It emphasises the importance of cultural policies that recognise global 
inequalities and focus on expanding individuals’ capabilities to participate in 
and beneft from the creative economy. 
While initially developed to explain the transition from agricultural to 
industrial economies, Arthur Lewis’s Dual‑Sector Model can be adapted to 
understand the dynamics within CCIs (Lewis, 1954). In many countries, we 
see a ‘dual’ creative economy, with a modern, high‑productivity sector (often 
in digital and tech‑oriented creative industries) coexisting with a traditional, 
lower‑productivity sector (such as conventional crafts or local performing 
arts). This model can help explain the challenges and opportunities in tran‑
sitioning creative workers from conventional to modern industries and the 
implications for overall economic development. Lewis’s model resonates with 
the concept of structural heterogeneities highlighted by the CEPAL school, 
76 The Political Economy of the Creative and Cultural Industries 
 
 
 
 
 
providing additional insights into the internal dynamics of CCIs in develop‑
ing economies. It can help illuminate the structural barriers that hinder CCIs’ 
growth and inform strategies for fostering more inclusive growth within 
these sectors. 
John Kenneth Galbraith’s concept of the Afuent Society ofers another 
valuable perspective, particularly relevant to understanding CCIs in devel‑
oped economies (Galbraith, 1958). Galbraith argued that as societies become 
wealthier, they often face a paradox of private wealth amid public squalor. 
This insight can be applied to the creative economy, where we might see lav‑
ish investment in commercial creative ventures alongside underinvestment in 
public cultural institutions and education. Galbraith’s work challenges us to 
consider how CCIs can contribute not just to private wealth creation but to 
public goods and overall societal well‑being. This perspective is particularly 
relevant when considering the role of CCIs in fostering sustainable and inclu‑
sive development models that go beyond mere economic growth, as empha‑
sised by the Global South theories. 
By incorporating these additional heterodox theories alongside the Global 
South perspectives, we can further enrich our understanding of: 
1 The multidimensional nature of development in the context of CCIs en‑
compasses economic, social and cultural aspects. 
2 The internal dynamics and transitions within CCIs, particularly in devel‑
oping economies. 
3 The balance between commercial success and public value in the creative 
economy. 
4 The potential of CCIs to contribute to human development and the expan‑
sion of individual freedoms. 
These additional theories complement the insights from Global South per ‑
spectives, providing a more comprehensive framework for analysing the 
role of CCIs in economic development across diverse global contexts. They 
reinforce the need for nuanced, context‑specifc approaches to harnessing 
the potential of CCIs for inclusive and sustainable development. Moreo‑
ver, integrating these diverse theoretical perspectives underscores the value 
of cross‑pollination between economic theories from diferent regions and 
schools of thought. It demonstrates how insights developed in response to 
specifc regional challenges can ofer valuable perspectives for understand‑
ing global economic phenomena, particularly in the dynamic and complex 
CCI feld. 
In conclusion, this expanded theoretical framework provides a rich analyt‑
ical toolkit for understanding and addressing the challenges and opportuni‑
ties CCIs present in economic development. It encourages a holistic approach 
Creative and Cultural Industries and Social‑Economic Development 77 
 
 
 
  
 
 
that considers economic growth, human capabilities, structural transitions 
and the balance between private and public value creation. This comprehen‑
sive perspective is crucial for developing efective policies and strategies to 
harness the full potential of CCIs for inclusive and sustainable development 
in both the Global South and North. 
These theories – including the Global South perspectives, Sen’s Capability 
Approach, Lewis’s Dual‑Sector Model and Galbraith’s concept of the Afu‑
ent Society – will be in the following sections of this chapter, with a specifc 
emphasis on their application to and implications for CCIs. This detailed 
examination will provide a comprehensive framework for understanding 
the complex role of CCIs in economic development across diverse global 
contexts. 
3.2.1 Structuralist Theories of Development 
Structuralist development theories emerged in the 1950s and 1960s, primar‑
ily from the work of economists and sociologists associated with the Eco‑
nomic Commission for Latin America and the Caribbean (ECLAC or CEPAL 
in Spanish). These theories represented a signifcant departure from the dom‑
inant modernisation theories of the time, positing that all countries would 
follow a linear development path, eventually converging with industrialised 
nations. The structuralist approach, in contrast, argued that underdevelop‑
ment was not merely a stage in a linear process but rather a distinct condi‑
tion resulting from the historical integration of peripheral economies into 
the global capitalist system. This integration, they argued, created structural 
barriers to development that could not be overcome simply by following the 
path of industrialised nations. 
Key fgures in the development of structuralist thought include Raúl Prebi‑
sch, Celso Furtado, Osvaldo Sunkel and Aníbal Pinto, and their work laid the 
foundation for dependency theory and continues infuencing development 
thinking today: 
1 Raúl Prebisch (1901–1986): An Argentine economist, Prebisch was a 
pioneer of structuralist economics. He served as the Executive Secretary 
of the United Nations Economic Commission for Latin America and the 
Caribbean (ECLAC) from 1950 to 1963. Prebisch is best known for the 
Prebisch‑Singer hypothesis, which posits that the terms of trade between 
primary products and manufactured goods tend to deteriorate over time, 
disadvantaging primary product‑exporting countries. 
2 Celso Furtado (1920–2004): A Brazilian economist and one of the 
most infuential development economists of the 20th century. Furtado 
was a crucial fgure in the foundation of structuralism and dependency 
78 The Political Economy of the Creative and Cultural Industries 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
theory. He served as the Minister of Planning and also Culture in Brazil 
and was a professor at the Sorbonne in Paris. His work “The Economic 
Formation of Brazil” (1959) is considered a classic in development 
economics. 
3 Osvaldo Sunkel (1929–): A Chilean economist and sociologist, Sunkel is a 
prominent fgure in Latin American structuralist and dependency thought. 
He worked at ECLAC and was a professor at the University of Chile. 
Sunkel is known for his work on development, underdevelopment and 
dependency, particularly his book El subdesarrollo latinoamericano y la 
teoría del desarrollo (1970). 
4 Aníbal Pinto (1919–1996): A Chilean economist who contributed signif‑
cantly to structuralist economics. Pinto served as the Director of ECLAC’s 
Economic Development Division. He is known for analysing “structural 
heterogeneity” in Latin American economies, highlighting the coexistence 
of modern and traditional sectors within these economies. 
Collectively, these scholars have signifcantly shaped the structural‑
ist school of thought. Their emphasis on the unique economic challenges 
faced by developing countries, particularly in Latin America, and their ideas 
on centre‑periphery dynamics, structural heterogeneity, and the need for 
state‑led industrialisation have had a profound and lasting impact on de‑
velopment economics and policymaking in the Global South. The following 
sessions will delve into their economic thoughts on CCIs. 
3.2.1.1 Celso Furtado’s Theory and the Myth of Development 
Celso Furtado, a prominent Brazilian economist and critical fgure in Latin 
American structuralism, signifcantly contributed to our understanding of 
development and underdevelopment. His work transcended traditional eco‑
nomic analysis by incorporating historical, social and cultural dimensions, 
ofering a holistic perspective on development processes. This essay explores 
Furtado’s ideas, mainly focusing on his emphasis on creativity and cultural 
aspects of development and their relevance to artistic and creative industries 
(CCIs) in industrial civilisation. 
3.2.1.1.1 Creativity as the Core of Development 
Furtado’s seminal work “Creativity and Dependency in Industrial Civiliza‑
tion” (1978) represents a paradigm shift in development thinking. He argues 
that genuine development is fundamentally a process of cultural creativity, 
extending far beyond mere economic growth. According to the author, devel‑
opment is, in essence, a method of activating and channelling social forces, 
improving the association’s capacity and exercising initiative and inventive‑
ness. Thus, it is a social and cultural process and only secondarily economic 
(Furtado, 1984). 
Creative and Cultural Industries and Social‑Economic Development 79 
 
  
  
 
 
 
 
This perspective is particularly pertinent to studying CCIs in the context 
of development. It suggests that these industries are not merely economic 
sectors but crucial components of a society’s capacity for self‑determined de‑
velopment. Using Furtado’s framework, CCIs can be seen as catalysts for 
activating social forces and channelling creative energies towards solving so‑
cietal challenges. 
3.2.1.1.1.1 The Critique of Mimetic Modernisation A cornerstone of Furtado’s 
theory is his critique of “mimetic modernisation,” a concept he developed to 
describe developing countries’ tendency to imitate developed nations’ con‑
sumption patterns without establishing corresponding productive structures. 
In The Myth of Economic Development (1974), Furtado argued: “The pro‑
cess of modernisation, based on the adoption of consumption patterns from 
advanced countries, implies the rising of a privileged minority, whose life‑
style contrasts with the majority of the population” (Furtado, 1974, p. 78). 
This critique has profound implications for CCIs. It raises crucial ques‑
tions about the uncritical adoption of Western cultural production and con‑
sumption models in developing countries. For instance, the dominance of 
Hollywood flms or Western pop music in many developing nations could 
be interpreted as cultural mimetic modernisation, potentially stifing local 
creative industries and artistic expressions. Furtado’s perspective challenges 
us to rethink the role of CCIs in development. Developing countries should 
focus on fostering their unique creative capacities rather than simply import‑
ing cultural products or replicating Western creative industry models. This 
approach aligns with Furtado’s vision of development as a process of artistic 
creativity, where societies tap into their creative potential to address their 
specifc challenges and express their unique cultural identities. 
3.2.1.1.1.2 Technological Autonomy and CCIs Furtado emphasised the cru‑
cial role of technological autonomy in genuine development. In Brazil: An 
Interrupted Construction (1992), he asserted: “The key to development lies 
in creating an autonomous capacity for technical progress” (Furtado, 1992, 
p. 37). This concept is particularly relevant to contemporary CCIs, which 
are increasingly digital and technology‑dependent. The ability of developing 
countries to create, adapt and control the technological platforms and tools 
used in cultural production and distribution could be crucial for developing 
their creative economies. 
In the context of CCIs, technological autonomy could mean: 
1 Developing local digital platforms for content distribution 
2 Creating software tools tailored to local creative practices 
3 Establishing educational programmes to build technical skills in the crea‑
tive sector 
4 Implementing policies that support technological innovation in CCI goods 
and services 
80 The Political Economy of the Creative and Cultural Industries 
 
  
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
Developing countries can better resist cultural homogenisation and foster 
more diverse and locally rooted creative economies by achieving greater tech‑
nological autonomy in their creative sectors. 
3.2.1.1.1.3 CCIs and Structural Change Furtado’s structuralist approach to 
development emphasises the need for fundamental changes in the economic 
and social structure of developing countries. This perspective is central to 
his analysis in Economic Development of Latin America (1970), where he 
argues: 
The problem of economic development is fundamentally one of structural 
change. In this context, structural change means the transformation of the 
economic structure in such a way that the process of capital formation 
and technological progress become endogenous, that is, determined by 
internal forces. 
(Furtado, 1970, p. 143) 
Furtado elaborates on this idea, explaining that structural change involves: 
1 Diversifcation of the productive structure 
2 Reduction of external dependency 
3 Homogenisation of productivity levels across diferent sectors of the 
economy 
4 Improvement in income distribution 
He further states: “The key to understanding the process of development 
lies in the study of the structural transformations of the social system” (Fur‑
tado, 1970, p. 145). For the author, development is about economic growth 
and fundamental economic and societal changes. In the context of CCIs, this 
structuralist approach suggests that developing countries should aim to grow 
their creative sectors and integrate them into a broader strategy of structural 
transformation. This could involve: 
1 Developing CCIs that build on local cultural strengths and address local 
needs 
2 Using CCIs to diversify the economy and reduce dependency on primary 
commodity exports 
3 Ensuring that the benefts of CCI are widely distributed, contributing to 
more equitable development 
4 Fostering linkages between CCIs and other sectors of the economy to pro‑
mote overall structural change 
Creative and Cultural Industries and Social‑Economic Development 81 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
3.2.1.2 Centre‑Periphery Model 
The centre‑periphery model, a cornerstone of structuralist economic thought, 
provides a framework for understanding global economic disparities and the 
dynamics of international trade. Initially proposed by the Argentine econo‑
mist Raúl Prebisch in the late 1940s, this model has since become a funda‑
mental concept in development economics and has been applied to various 
felds, including cultural studies. 
3.2.1.2.1 Origins and Core Concepts 
Raúl Prebisch, while serving as the Executive Secretary of the United Nations 
Economic Commission for Latin America (ECLA), introduced the centre‑pe‑
riphery model in his seminal 1949 work, “The Economic Development of 
Latin America and Its Principal Problems.” In this ground‑breaking study, 
Prebisch argued that the global economy is fundamentally divided into two 
distinct regions: the industrialised ‘centre’ and the primary goods‑producing 
‘periphery’ (Prebisch, 1949). These ideas were further developed by other 
Latin American economists, notably Celso Furtado, who contributed signif‑
cantly to the structuralist school of thought. Together, their work formed the 
basis of what became known as the Prebisch‑Singer hypothesis, named after 
Prebisch and Hans Singer, who independently arrived at similar conclusions 
(Love, 1980). 
3.2.1.2.2 Unequal Exchange in International Trade 
The core of the Prebisch‑Singer hypothesis is the concept of unequal exchange 
in international trade. They argued that the terms of trade between primary 
commodities (typically exported by peripheral countries) and manufactured 
goods (exported by central countries) tend to deteriorate over time, leading 
to a persistent transfer of value from the periphery to the centre. As Prebisch 
(1949, p. 10) famously stated: “While the centres kept the whole beneft of 
the technical development of their industries, the peripheral countries trans‑
ferred to them a share of the fruits of their technical progress.” 
This deterioration in terms of trade occurs for several reasons: 
1 Income elasticity of demand: The demand for primary goods tends to 
grow more slowly than manufactured goods as incomes rise (Engel’s Law). 
2 Technological progress: Advances in technology often lead to the creation 
of synthetic substitutes for primary goods, further depressing their prices. 
3 Market structure: Peripheral countries often face oligopolistic markets for 
their imports and oligopolistic markets for their exports, limiting their 
bargaining power. 
82 The Political Economy of the Creative and Cultural Industries 
 
 
 
 
 
4 Labour market diferences: Strong labour unions in central countries can 
secure wage increases in line with productivity gains, while weaker unions 
in peripheral countries cannot, leading to a decline in the relative prices of 
their goods (Singer, 1950). 
Emmanuel (1972) further developed the concept of unequal exchange, argu‑
ing that it results from wage diferentials between developed and developing 
countries rather than from the types of goods exchanged. The centre‑pe‑
riphery model challenges the classical and neoclassical assumptions about 
the benefts of free trade for all participants. Instead, it suggests that inter ‑
national trade participation can reinforce underdevelopment under certain 
structural conditions. This perspective had profound implications for devel‑
opment policy. It provided the theoretical foundation for import substitution 
industrialisation (ISI) strategies many Latin American countries adopted in 
the mid‑20th century. These policies aimed to break the cycle of dependency 
by fostering domestic industrial development (Birdsall et al., 2010). 
The centre‑periphery model and the concept of unequal exchange have 
been extended beyond purely economic considerations to analyse global 
fows of cultural goods and services. Similar patterns of inequality can be 
observed in the context of artistic and creative industries. As noted by UN‑
ESCO (2005, p. 44) in its Convention on the Protection and Promotion of 
the Diversity of Cultural Expressions: 
The unequal distribution of wealth between developed and developing 
countries is refected in unequal cultural exchanges and strong imbalances 
in the fows of cultural goods and services at the global level. 
This cultural dimension of the centre‑periphery model manifests in several 
ways: 
1 Market dominance: Cultural goods and services from the ‘centre’ (e.g., 
Hollywood flms, Western pop music) often dominate global markets, 
while cultural products from the ‘periphery’ struggle to reach interna‑
tional audiences. 
2 Value extraction: Even when cultural goods from the periphery succeed 
globally, the economic benefts often fow disproportionately to entities in 
the centre. For instance, a successful musician from a developing country 
might sign with a major record label based in a developed country, with 
most of the profts fowing to the label rather than the artist or their home 
country. 
3 Cultural homogenisation: The dominance of cultural products from the 
centre can lead to cultural imperialism, potentially threatening local cul‑
tural diversity. 
Creative and Cultural Industries and Social‑Economic Development 83 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
Considering this subject, David Throsby, in “The Economics of Cultural 
Policy” (2010, p. 165), observes: “The international trade in cultural goods 
and services is dominated by a small number of countries, primarily in the 
developed world, raising concerns about cultural diversity and the economic 
opportunities for cultural producers in developing countries.” While the 
centre‑periphery model has been infuential, it has also faced criticism. The 
centre‑periphery model remains a powerful tool for understanding global eco‑
nomic and cultural inequalities. While its policy implications have been de‑
bated, the international trace critical core insights about structural imbalances 
in the global economy continue to inform discussions about creative industries, 
development and cultural exchange. As the global economy evolves, so does 
the application and interpretation of this enduring conceptual framework. 
3.2.1.2.3 Structural Heterogeneity (Aníbal Pinto) 
The concept of structural heterogeneity, a cornerstone of structuralist eco‑
nomic thought, was developed by Chilean economist Aníbal Pinto in re‑
sponse to the unique economic landscapes of Latin American countries. This 
concept provides a robust framework for understanding the complexities of 
CCIs in developing nations. 
Pinto observed that underdeveloped economies were characterised by a 
striking duality: the coexistence of sectors with vastly diferent productivity 
levels. In his seminal 1970 paper, he articulated this idea: 
The concept of structural heterogeneity … refers to the existence of eco‑
nomic activities and regions where average labour productivity is close to 
that of developed countries, coexisting with activities that utilise back‑
ward technologies and whose levels of productivity are very low. 
(Pinto, 1970, p. 85) 
Pinto argued that this duality was not a temporary phase but a structural 
feature of underdevelopment, leading to persistent challenges such as income 
inequality, limited domestic markets and difculties in achieving sustained 
economic growth. 
Applying this concept to the CCIs in developing countries, we uncover a 
similar pattern of duality that shapes the sector’s dynamics and development 
trajectories. Finding a small but highly productive cultural industry closely 
linked to global markets and utilising advanced technologies is possible. This 
modern sector includes: 
1 Internationally successful flm production companies 
2 Global music labels and streaming platforms 
3 Digital start‑ups creating cutting‑edge apps and games 
84 The Political Economy of the Creative and Cultural Industries 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
4 High‑end fashion houses blending traditional motifs with contemporary 
design 
5 Modern art galleries with a presence in global art markets 
These entities often operate at productivity levels comparable to those in 
developed countries, leveraging digital technologies, global networks and so‑
phisticated management practices. Alongside this modern sector is a larger, 
often less visible world of traditional and informal cultural production. 
While rich in artistic value, this sector typically needs to catch up in terms of 
economic productivity and global reach. It encompasses: 
1 Traditional craftspeople and artisans 
2 Local musicians and performers 
3 Small‑scale cultural events and festivals 
4 Indigenous art and craft producers 
5 Street artists and performers 
6 Small, local cultural organisations and community centres 
These felds often need more access to advanced technologies, formal markets 
or global distribution networks. However, it plays a crucial role in preserving 
cultural heritage, maintaining community cohesion and providing livelihoods 
for a signifcant portion of the population. When applied to CCIs, the concept 
of structural heterogeneity reveals a complex landscape of duality and infor‑
mality. This perspective highlights both the challenges and opportunities facing 
CCIs in developing countries. By recognising the vital role of the informal econ‑
omy and the persistent structural divides within the sector, policymakers and 
cultural entrepreneurs can work towards more inclusive and efective strategies 
for cultural and economic development. As we move forward, the goal should 
be to harness the dynamism and authenticity of the informal cultural economy 
while providing pathways for growth, protection and global engagement. In 
doing so, we can work towards a future where CCIs drive economic growth 
and preserve and promote the rich cultural diversity of developing nations. 
3.2.1.3 Amartya Sen’s Capability Approach 
Amartya Sen, born in 1933 in West Bengal, India, is a renowned economist 
and philosopher who has signifcantly contributed to welfare economics, 
social choice theory and economic development. In 1998, he was awarded 
the Nobel Memorial Prize in Economic Sciences for his work on these sub‑
jects. Sen has held professorships at several prestigious institutions, including 
Harvard University and the London School of Economics. Sen developed 
the capability approach as an alternative framework for understanding de‑
velopment and well‑being. This approach, which has profoundly impacted 
development thinking and policy, shifts the focus from traditional economic 
progress measures to expanding human freedoms and capabilities. 
 
 
 
 
  
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
Creative and Cultural Industries and Social‑Economic Development 85 
BOX 3.2 AMARTYA SEN’S SEMINAL IDEAS 
1 Capability Approach 
The Capability Approach is one of Sen’s most infuential contributions to 
development theory. 
• Core Idea: Development should be understood as expanding people’s 
“capabilities” – the freedom to achieve various lifestyles. 
• Beyond Income: This approach moves beyond traditional income‑based 
development measures to focus on what people can do and be. 
• Functionings and Capabilities: Sen distinguishes between “func‑
tionings” (various states of human beings and doings) and “capabilities” 
(the alternative combinations of functionings that are feasible for a per ‑
son to achieve). 
• Application: This approach has infuenced the HDI used by the United 
Nations Development Programme. 
2 Freedom as Development 
Sen’s view of freedom as both the primary end and the principal means 
of development is elaborated in his book Development as Freedom (1999). 
• Five Types of Freedom: 
1 Political freedoms (e.g., democracy, civil rights) 
2 Economic facilities (e.g., opportunities for participation in trade and 
production) 
3 Social opportunities (e.g., education, healthcare) 
4 Transparency guarantees (e.g., disclosure, trust) 
5 Protective security (e.g., social safety nets, unemployment benefts) 
• Interconnectedness: Sen emphasises how these freedoms are inter ‑
connected and reinforce each other. 
• Instrumental Role: Freedoms are not just the ends of development, 
but also the principal means. 
3 Poverty and Famine 
Sen’s work on poverty and famine has revolutionised our understanding 
of these issues. 
• Entitlement Approach: Sen developed the concept of “entitle‑
ments” to explain how famines can occur even when food supplies are 
adequate. 
• Famine as Economic Disaster: He demonstrated that famines are of‑
ten caused by a collapse in people’s ability to command food through 
legal means available in society rather than just food scarcity. 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
86 The Political Economy of the Creative and Cultural Industries 
• Case Studies: Sen’s analysis of the Bengal famine 1943 and other fam‑
ines provided empirical support for his theories. 
• Policy Implications: His work has infuenced famine prevention strat‑
egies, emphasising the importance of maintaining people’s economic 
access to food. 
4 Gender Inequality 
Sen has been a strong advocate for gender equality and women’s em‑
powerment in development. 
• Missing Women: Sen introduced the concept of “missing women” to 
highlight the impact of gender bias in healthcare, nutrition and social 
status on female survival rates. 
• Agency and Well‑being: He emphasised the distinction between 
women’s well‑being and their agency, arguing for the importance of 
development. 
• Cooperative Conficts: Sen developed a “cooperative conficts” model 
to analyse intra‑household dynamics and gender relations. 
• Education and Empowerment: He consistently emphasised the 
role of women’s education and economic participation in fostering 
development. 
To know more: 
1 Sen, A. (1999). Development as freedom. Oxford University Press. 
2 Sen, A. (1970). Collective choice and social welfare. Holden‑Day. 
3 Sen, A. (1981). Poverty and famines: An essay on entitlement and dep‑
rivation. Oxford University Press. 
4 Sen, A. (1990). More than 100 million women are missing. The New 
York Review of Books. 
Sen’s seminal work “Development as Freedom” (1999) presents his core ar ‑
gument for a broader understanding of development. He writes: 
Development can be seen … as a process of expanding people’s real free‑
doms. Focusing on human freedoms contrasts with narrower views of de‑
velopment, such as identifying development with the growth of the gross 
national product, with the rise in personal incomes, industrialisation, tech‑
nological advance, or social modernisation. 
(Sen, 1999, p. 3) 
This perspective shifts the focus from traditional economic measures to ex‑
panding human freedoms and capabilities, challenging conventional develop‑
ment metrics such as GDP growth or industrialisation rates. 
Creative and Cultural Industries and Social‑Economic Development 87 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
Within this framework, cultural freedoms play a crucial role. These in‑
clude the freedom to participate in cultural life, to express oneself crea‑
tively and to access and enjoy diverse cultural goods and services. Sen 
argues that these freedoms are not just instrumental to development but 
are constitutive of development itself. In “Culture and Development” 
(2004), Sen elaborates: “Cultural matters are integral parts of the lives we 
lead. If development can be seen as an enhancement of our living stand‑
ards, then eforts geared to development can hardly ignore the world of 
culture” (Sen, 2004, p. 39). 
This perspective has signifcant implications for understanding the role of 
CCIs in development: 
1 CCIs as enablers of cultural freedom: CCIs can directly contribute to ex‑
panding cultural freedoms by providing artistic expression and participa‑
tion platforms. 
2 Cultural diversity as development: The fourishing of diverse artistic ex‑
pressions through CCIs can be seen as a development, not just a means to 
economic growth. 
3 Cultural capabilities and economic opportunities: Participation in CCIs 
can enhance individuals’ capabilities in ways that may have broader eco‑
nomic and social benefts. 
Capabilities Related to Cultural Participation and Creation Sen’s approach 
emphasises the importance of people’s capabilities – their real opportunities 
to achieve functions they value. In the context of culture and CCIs, relevant 
capabilities might include: 
1 The ability to express oneself creatively 
2 The capacity to access and appreciate diverse cultural goods and services 
3 The opportunity to participate in cultural traditions and practices 
4 The capability to earn a livelihood through cultural and creative work 
David Throsby, in “Economics and Culture” (2001), applies this thinking to 
cultural policy: “The capability approach … provides a framework within 
which cultural rights can be asserted as essential components of develop‑
ment processes” (Throsby, 2001, p. 165). Sen’s approach emphasises that 
development is a comprehensive process that cannot be reduced to a single 
dimension. Social and cultural factors play crucial roles in shaping develop‑
ment outcomes and processes. The author argues that culture should be seen 
as both a means to development and an end. As a means, cultural factors can 
contribute to other development goals, such as economic growth or social 
cohesion. Ultimately, the freedom to participate in and enjoy cultural life is a 
valuable aspect of human development. 
88 The Political Economy of the Creative and Cultural Industries 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
In “How Does Culture Matter?” (2004), Sen writes: 
Culture can serve both as a constituent of development (development, in 
part, as enhancement of our cultural lives) and as an important instrument 
of development (helping other forms of development, such as economic 
progress or improvement in the quality of life). 
(Sen, 2004, p. 40) 
This dual role of culture has essential implications for CCIs: 
1 Instrumental role: CCIs can contribute to economic growth, job creation 
and urban regeneration. 
2 Intrinsic role: The expansion and diversifcation of CCIs represent a form 
of development in themselves, enhancing people’s cultural capabilities and 
freedoms. 
CCIs’ Potential in Expanding Substantive Freedoms CCIs have signifcant 
potential to expand what Sen calls “substantive freedoms” – the real oppor‑
tunities people must live the kinds of lives they have reason to value. This can 
occur in several ways: 
1 Economic opportunities: CCIs can provide livelihoods and economic op‑
portunities, particularly for young people and in urban areas. 
2 Educational benefts: Participation in cultural activities can enhance cogni‑
tive skills, creativity and critical thinking. 
3 Social cohesion: CCIs can foster social bonds, intercultural dialogue and a 
sense of shared identity. 
4 Political participation: Cultural expressions can be a means of political 
engagement and critique. 
5 Personal fulflment: Creative work and cultural participation can contrib‑
ute to individuals’ sense of fulflment and well‑being. 
The UNESCO Creative Economy Report 2013 Special Edition echoes this 
perspective: “When culture is recognised as a driver and enabler of sustain‑
able development, new prospects open up for individuals and societies to 
innovate by drawing on their cultural heritage and creativity” (UNESCO & 
UNDP, 2013, p. 39). 
Applying Sen’s Approach to CCIs and Development Policy Sen’s capability 
approach provides a rich framework for understanding and promoting the 
role of CCIs in development: 
1 Broadening development goals: It encourages policymakers to look be‑
yond narrow economic metrics and consider how CCIs contribute to ex‑
panding human freedoms and capabilities. 
Creative and Cultural Industries and Social‑Economic Development 89 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
2 Participatory approach: Sen emphasises the importance of public partici‑
pation in defning development priorities. This suggests the need for inclu‑
sive cultural policies that refect diverse voices and artistic expressions. 
3 Addressing inequalities: The approach draws attention to inequalities in 
cultural capabilities and opportunities, highlighting the need for policies 
that promote equitable access to cultural resources and opportunities. 
4 Valuing diversity: Sen’s emphasis on freedom and choice aligns with pro‑
moting cultural diversity, seeing it as enriching rather than threatening 
development. 
5 Interdisciplinary perspective: The capability approach encourages a ho‑
listic view of development, suggesting the need for policies that recognise 
the interconnections between cultural, economic and social dimensions of 
development. 
Hasan Bakhshi, Alan Freeman and Graham Hitchen in “Measuring Intrinsic 
Value: How to Stop Worrying and Love Economics” (2009) argue: “Sen’s ca‑
pability approach … ofers a way of reconciling the intrinsic and instrumen‑
tal benefts of culture, by treating access to cultural resources as a substantive 
freedom” (Bakhshi et al., 2009, p. 19). 
Therefore, Amartya Sen’s capability approach provides a robust frame‑
work for understanding the multifaceted role of CCIs in development. It en‑
courages us to see CCIs not just as economic sectors but as crucial elements 
in expanding human freedoms and capabilities. This perspective can inform 
more holistic and human‑centred approaches to cultural policy and devel‑
opment strategies, recognising the intrinsic value of cultural participation 
alongside its instrumental benefts. 
3.2.2 Arthur Lewis, Development and CCIs 
The landscape of development economics was forever altered by the 
ground‑breaking work of Sir W. Arthur Lewis, whose dual‑sector model, of‑
ten referred to as the Lewis model, cast a new light on the intricate pathways 
of economic growth in developing nations. The model woven observations of 
labour markets, agricultural productivity and industrial expansion to create 
a theory awarded with Nobel Prize in Economics in 1979. 
3.2.2.1 The Dual‑Sector Model: Core Concepts 
At the heart of Lewis’s theory is a dual economy, a bifurcated system where 
two distinct sectors coexist in a delicate dance of progress and tradition. 
On one side, we fnd the traditional sector, typically embodied by subsist‑
ence agriculture, where productivity languishes and surplus labour is mainly 
present. This sector, characterised by its informal nature and familial ties, is 
a vast reservoir of untapped human potential. On the other side stands the 
90 The Political Economy of the Creative and Cultural Industries 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
modern industry, a beacon of industrial promise, urbanisation and techno‑
logical advancement. Lewis states that the key to economic development is 
the gradual labour migration from the low‑productivity traditional sector to 
the high‑productivity modern industry. This transfer, he argued, could occur 
without signifcantly impacting agricultural output due to the presence of 
“disguised unemployment” in rural areas. Workers moving to urban centres 
would fnd employment in factories and other industrial enterprises, contrib‑
uting to a surge in overall economic productivity. 
To fully grasp the dynamics of Lewis’s theory, their mathematical under ‑
pinnings are: 
1 Total Output (Y): Y = Ya + Ym, where: 
• Ya = Output in the agricultural (traditional) sector 
• Ym = Output in the manufacturing (modern) sector 
This equation refects the division of the economy into traditional and mod‑
ern sectors. As development progresses, we expect to see a shift in the relative 
sizes of Ya and Ym, with the modern sector growing faster. 
2 Labour Transfer: ΔLm = –ΔLa, where: 
• ΔLm = Change in labour in the modern sector 
• ΔLa = Change in labour in the agricultural sector 
This equation is at the heart of Lewis’s model. It represents the movement of 
surplus labour from agriculture to manufacturing, which Lewis saw as the 
critical driver of development. 
3 Wage Determination: Wm = Wa(1 + μ), where: 
• Wm = Wage in the modern sector 
• Wa = Subsistence wage in the agricultural sector 
• μ = Wage premium (typically around 0.3 or 30%) 
This illustrates why workers are incentivised to move to the modern sector. 
The wage premium μ represents the additional compensation needed to over‑
come the costs and risks of moving from rural to urban areas. 
4 Proft in Modern Sector: Πm = Ym – WmLm, where: 
• Πm = Proft in modern sector 
• Lm = Labour employed in modern sector 
5 Capital Accumulation: ΔK = sΠm, where: 
• ΔK = Change in capital stock 
• s = Savings rate of capitalists 
Creative and Cultural Industries and Social‑Economic Development 91 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
Equations 4 and 5 show how profts in the modern sector drive capital ac‑
cumulation. This reinvestment of profts is crucial for sustaining growth in 
Lewis’s model. The beauty of Lewis’s model lies in its elegant simplicity and 
profound implications for capital accumulation. As the modern sector absorbs 
surplus labour, it can maintain low wages, allowing for higher profts. When 
reinvested, these profts fuel further industrial expansion, creating a virtuous 
growth cycle. This process continues until the pivotal “Lewis turning point” is 
reached – a moment when the reservoir of surplus labour is exhausted, leading 
to rising wages and a fundamental restructuring of the economy. 
3.2.2.2 Application to CCIs 
While Lewis’s theory was primarily conceived with agriculture and industry in 
mind, its rationales can be applied to CCIs. In the contemporary global econ‑
omy, CCIs have emerged as dynamic forces of innovation, job creation and 
cultural expression. They represent a new frontier in the modern sector that 
challenges traditional notions of productivity and value creation. Imagine, if 
you will, a young artist in a rural village, her talents constrained by limited op‑
portunities and resources. In Lewis’s framework, they embody the surplus la‑
bour of the traditional sector. As they migrate to an urban centre, drawn by the 
allure of creative opportunities, they become part of the labour transfer Lewis 
saw as crucial to development. Her journey from village to city mirrors the 
transition from agriculture to industry but with a distinctly 21st‑century twist. 
To understand how CCIs ft into and extend Lewis’s development theory, we 
can examine a set of equations that capture their unique characteristics: 
6 CCI Output: Yc = f(Lc, Kc, H), where: 
• Yc = Output in CCIs 
• Lc = Labour in CCIs 
• Kc = Capital in CCIs 
• H = Human capital (creativity, skills) 
This function includes human capital (H) as a critical input, refecting the 
importance of creativity and specialised skills in these industries. 
7 Innovation Spillover: Ym = g(Yc) × f(Lm, Km), where: 
• g(Yc) = Positive function representing spillover efects from CCIs 
This equation captures one of CCIs’ most important contributions to the 
broader economy. By infuencing g(Yc), CCIs can enhance productivity 
across other sectors, acting as a catalyst for economy‑wide innovation. 
8 Cultural Capital Accumulation: ΔCC = α × Yc + β × CC, where: 
• CC = Cultural capital stock 
92 The Political Economy of the Creative and Cultural Industries 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
• α = Rate of new cultural capital creation 
• β = Cultural capital depreciation rate 
Cultural capital accumulation is a unique feature of CCIs. Unlike physical 
capital, cultural capital can appreciate over time, potentially increasing re‑
turns to scale in creative production. 
9 CCI Labour Dynamics: Lc = La_c + Lm_c + Lg, where: 
• La_c = Labour transferred from the agricultural sector to CCIs 
• Lm_c = Labour transferred from the manufacturing sector to CCIs 
• Lg = New labour force growth 
This equation shows how the creative sector draws workers from agriculture 
and manufacturing and new entrants to the labour force. It refects the com‑
plex labour markets of the creative economy, where skills and talent fow 
more fuidly between sectors. In the urban crucible of creativity, our artist is 
immersed in an ecosystem of galleries, design studios and digital media com‑
panies. This concentration of creative talent in urban areas aligns perfectly 
with Lewis’s emphasis on the importance of urban industrial growth. The 
CCIs, like their industrial counterparts, beneft from agglomeration efects, 
where proximity breeds innovation and productivity gains.
