As the CCIs fourish, they generate substantial profts, much like the in‑
dustrial frms in Lewis’s original model. A successful flm production com‑
pany, for instance, might reinvest its earnings into more advanced equipment 
or expanded studio space. A thriving music scene might attract investment in 
recording facilities and performance venues. This process of capital accumu‑
lation in the creative sector echoes the reinvestment cycle that Lewis saw as 
crucial for sustained economic growth. 
Moreover, the CCIs possess a unique ability to drive innovation and in‑
crease productivity across other sectors of the economy. A breakthrough in 
computer animation might fnd applications in architectural design or medi‑
cal imaging. The problem‑solving skills achieved in the creative industries 
can spark novel approaches in felds as diverse as education, healthcare and 
environmental sustainability. 
3.2.2.3 Challenges in Applying Lewis’s Theory to CCIs 
While Lewis’s development theory provides valuable insights, its application 
to CCIs presents several unique challenges: 
1 Intangible Value Creation: Unlike traditional industries, CCIs often gen‑
erate value through intangible assets such as intellectual property, brand 
recognition and cultural capital. These are difcult to quantify using con‑
ventional economic metrics, complicating the measurement of productiv‑
ity and output in creative sectors. 
Creative and Cultural Industries and Social‑Economic Development 93 
 
 
 
 
 
 
  
 
 
 
2 Fluid Labour Dynamics: The creative workforce comprises project‑based 
work, freelancing and frequent career shifts. This fuidity challenges Lew‑
is’s concept of a straightforward labour transfer from low to high‑pro‑
ductivity sectors. In CCIs, workers may move between various roles and 
industries, blurring the lines between sectors. 
3 Skill‑Intensive Production: Lewis’s model assumes relatively unskilled la‑
bour moving from agriculture to industry, but CCIs often require spe‑
cialised skills and talents. This alters the dynamics of labour transfer and 
wage determination in creative sectors. 
4 Global Interconnectedness: Lewis’s theory was primarily focused on na‑
tional economies, but CCIs are often globally integrated. For example: 
• A graphic designer in Nairobi might collaborate with clients in New York. 
• A musician in Kingston could fnd her largest audience in Tokyo. 
• Digital platforms allow for instant global distribution of creative content. 
This international dimension adds complexity to understanding labour 
markets, capital fows and economic development in the context of CCIs. 
5 Technology and Disruption: Rapid technological changes in CCIs can 
quickly alter productivity levels and skill requirements, making applying 
Lewis’s long‑term growth model without frequent adjustments challenging. 
6 Cultural and Social Factors: The value and growth of CCIs are often in‑
fuenced by cultural trends, social movements and consumer preferences, 
which are less predictable than the economic factors central to Lewis’s 
original theory. 
These challenges are valid for applying Lewis’s ideas to CCIs. Still, they re‑
quire careful consideration and potential modifcations to the theory when 
analysing the role of creative industries in economic development. Research‑
ers and policymakers must develop new frameworks that can account for 
these unique aspects of the creative economy while still drawing on the foun‑
dational insights of Lewis’s dual‑sector model. 
At the crossroads of tradition and innovation, Arthur Lewis’s develop‑
ment theory ofers valuable insights into economic transformation processes. 
By extending its principles to the CCIs, we gain a richer understanding of 
how talent, technology and artistic expression can intertwine to forge new 
paths of economic development. In this evolving landscape, the dual sectors 
of Lewis’s model are not just agriculture and industry but also the realms of 
tradition and creativity, each feeding into the other in a complex choreogra‑
phy of growth and cultural vitality. 
The mathematical framework provided by these equations ofers a valu‑
able starting point for a more rigorous analysis of the role of CCIs in eco‑
nomic development, building upon the foundational insights of Arthur 
Lewis’s dual‑sector model. By analysing these relationships quantitatively, 
policymakers and researchers can gain deeper insights into how to foster CCI 
94 The Political Economy of the Creative and Cultural Industries 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
growth as part of a broader development strategy. For instance, investments 
in education and training (increasing H in equation 6) might yield outsized 
returns by boosting CCI output and innovation spillovers to other sectors. 
Integrating Lewis’s development theory with the dynamics of CCIs pre‑
sents a compelling narrative of economic evolution. It challenges us to reim‑
agine the pathways to development, acknowledging the power of creativity 
and culture as engines of growth. As we continue to navigate the complexi‑
ties of global economic development, this expanded model provides a valu‑
able lens through which to view the interplay of tradition, innovation and 
human potential in shaping future economies. 
BOX 3.3 ARTHUR LEWIS, AN HONORARY MANCUNIAN 
Sir William Arthur Lewis (1915–1991) stands as a towering fgure in the feld of 
development economics, distinguished not only for his ground‑breaking theo‑
ries but also for breaking racial barriers in academia. Born on the Caribbean 
Island of St. Lucia, Lewis overcame the constraints of colonial‑era racism to 
become one of the most infuential economists of the 20th century. His journey 
from a small island to the pinnacle of academic achievement culminated in 
1979 when he was awarded the Nobel Prize in Economics, sharing the honour 
with Theodore Schultz. This made Lewis the frst person of African descent to 
receive a Nobel Prize in a feld other than peace or literature, marking a signif‑
cant milestone in recognising Black scholars in the sciences. 
Lewis’s connection to the University of Manchester played a crucial role 
in his academic career and in developing his most famous work. In 1948, 
at 33, he was appointed to a full professorship at Manchester, making him 
Britain’s frst Black full professor. During his tenure at Manchester until 1957, 
Lewis produced some of his most important works, including his seminal 
article “Economic Development with Unlimited Supplies of Labour” (1954). 
This paper, which introduced the dual‑sector model (also known as the 
Lewis model), was written mainly during his time at Manchester and would 
form the basis for his Nobel Prize‑winning contributions to development 
economics. 
The mancunian ecosystem provided Lewis with the intellectual environ‑
ment and resources to refne his theories on economic growth in developing 
countries. His work at Manchester challenged prevailing economic thought 
by focusing on the structural transformation of economies from traditional to 
modern sectors. Lewis’s dual‑sector model, conceived within the halls of Man‑
chester, ofered a new framework for understanding the development process 
in labour‑surplus economies. This model solidifed Lewis’s reputation as a pio‑
neering thinker and established the University of Manchester as a key centre 
for development economics. Lewis’s legacy continues to infuence economic 
policy and academic discourse, inspiring scholars worldwide. 
Creative and Cultural Industries and Social‑Economic Development 95 
 
 
 
 
3.2.3 Institutional Economics and CCIs 
Institutional economics, a school of economic thought, emphasises the role 
of social, cultural and legal institutions in shaping economic behaviour and 
outcomes. This approach is particularly pertinent to understanding the de‑
velopment of CCIs and their role in broader economic growth. Focusing on 
the institutional context gives us a more nuanced understanding of how CCIs 
evolve as a set of economic activities and contribute to multidimensional 
progress. 
3.2.3.1 Foundations of Institutional Economics 
Institutional economics emerged in the early 20th century, with crucial con‑
tributions from scholars such as Thorstein Veblen, John R. Commons and 
Wesley Mitchell.1 Veblen’s concept of “conspicuous consumption” and his 
analysis of the role of social institutions in economic behaviour laid the nec‑
essary groundwork for the feld. Commons contributed signifcantly to un‑
derstanding collective action and capitalism’s legal foundations. Mitchell’s 
work on business cycles highlighted the importance of institutional factors in 
economic fuctuations. 
BOX 3.4 VEBLEN SEMINAL IDEAS 
Thorstein Veblen (1857–1929) was an American economist and sociologist 
known for his unconventional approach to economic theory. Born to Norwe‑
gian immigrant parents in Wisconsin, Veblen received his Ph.D. from Yale Uni‑
versity in 1884. Despite his brilliant mind, he struggled to ft into academia 
due to his unorthodox views and personal eccentricities. Veblen’s most famous 
work, “The Theory of the Leisure Class,” published in 1899, became a corner ‑
stone of institutional economics and a biting critique of consumer culture. 
In “The Theory of the Leisure Class,” Veblen introduced the concept of “con‑
spicuous consumption,” which describes purchasing goods or services primar‑
ily to display wealth and status rather than to fulfl practical needs. Veblen 
argued that in modern capitalist societies, a “leisure class” emerges that does 
not engage in productive labour but demonstrates its status through wasteful 
expenditure and the ostentatious display of wealth. According to Veblen, this 
behaviour trickles down through society as those in lower classes attempt to 
emulate the consumption patterns of those above them, leading to a wide‑
spread culture of status‑seeking through consumption. 
Veblen’s theory, with its introduction of ‘conspicuous leisure’ and ‘con‑
spicuous consumption,’ was revolutionary in its time. It challenged prevail‑
ing economic theories by highlighting the role of social and cultural factors in 
96 The Political Economy of the Creative and Cultural Industries 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
shaping economic behaviour. More importantly, it continues to infuence felds 
beyond economics, such as sociology, cultural studies and consumer behav‑
iour research. It ofers valuable insights into the complex motivations behind 
consumer choices and the social dynamics of wealth display. 
To know more: 
Veblen, T. (1899). The theory of the leisure class: An economic study of institu‑
tions. Macmillan. 
The feld gained renewed prominence in the late 20th century through the 
work of Douglas North, Oliver Williamson and Elinor Ostrom,2 among oth‑
ers. North’s work on economic history and institutional change, Williamson’s 
transaction cost economics and Ostrom’s analysis of common‑pool resources 
have all signifcantly expanded our understanding of how institutions shape 
economic outcomes. 
Fundamental principles of institutional economics include: 
1 Institutions matter: Economic outcomes are signifcantly infuenced by the 
formal and informal rules that govern society. This principle challenges 
the notion that markets operate in an institutional vacuum. 
2 Path dependence: Historical processes shape current institutions and eco‑
nomic structures. This concept helps explain why specifc institutional ar‑
rangements persist even when they may seem suboptimal. 
3 Transaction costs: Institutions can reduce (or increase) the costs of eco‑
nomic transactions. This insight has profound implications for under ‑
standing organisational structures and market efciency. 
4 Bounded rationality: Economic actors have limited cognitive capacity and 
operate within institutional constraints. This challenges the neoclassical 
assumption of perfect rationality and information. 
5 Evolution: Institutions and economic systems evolve over time through 
complex change processes. This evolutionary perspective helps explain in‑
stitutional diversity and change over time. 
Douglas North, in his seminal work “Institutions, Institutional Change 
and Economic Performance” (1990), defnes institutions as “the rules of 
the game in a society or, more formally, the humanly devised constraints 
that shape human interaction” (North, 1990, p.  3). This defnition en‑
compasses formal institutions (laws, regulations) and informal institutions 
(norms, customs and traditions), highlighting the broad scope of institu‑
tional analysis. 
Creative and Cultural Industries and Social‑Economic Development 97 
 
 
 
 
  
 
 
 
 
 
 
3.2.3.1.1 Institutional Economics and CCIs 
The institutional approach ofers several valuable insights into understanding 
CCIs, providing a framework for analysing the complex ecosystem in which 
creative activities occur. 
1 Legal frameworks: The development of CCIs is heavily infuenced by 
intellectual property regimes, labour laws and other legal institutions. 
Copyright law plays a crucial role in shaping the music and publishing in‑
dustries, while labour regulations afect the structure of flm and television 
production. 
2 Cultural norms: Informal institutions, such as societal attitudes towards 
creativity and entrepreneurship, shape the growth of CCIs. For example, 
societies that highly value artistic expression may be more likely to sup‑
port and invest in creative industries. 
3 Organisational structures: How creative enterprises are organised (e.g., 
frms, networks, clusters) afect their performance and impact. For instance, 
the prevalence of project‑based work in many innovative industries is an 
institutional adaptation to the uncertain nature of creative production. 
4 Policy environments: Government policies and regulatory frameworks are 
crucial in fostering or hindering CCI development. This includes direct 
support through subsidies and grants and indirect support through educa‑
tion and infrastructure development. 
5 Market institutions: The functioning of markets for cultural goods and 
services depends on specifc institutional arrangements. For example, the 
rise of streaming platforms has fundamentally altered the market institu‑
tions in the music and flm industries. 
Jason Potts, in “Creative Industries and Economic Evolution” (2011), ap‑
plies evolutionary institutional economics to CCIs, arguing that “the creative 
industries are best understood as an evolutionary system in which new ideas 
are generated, adopted and retained” (Potts, 2011, p. 9). This perspective 
highlights the dynamic nature of CCIs and the capacity for innovation and 
adaptation, as follows: 
1 Path dependence: The historical development of cultural institutions in a 
society infuences the current structure and performance of its CCIs. For 
instance, the United Kingdom’s strong tradition of public broadcasting has 
shaped its television and radio industries in ways distinct from countries 
with predominantly commercial systems. 
2 Institutional complementarities: The success of CCIs depends on comple‑
mentary institutions in education, fnance and other sectors. For example, 
the presence of specialised art schools, venture capital frms familiar with 
98 The Political Economy of the Creative and Cultural Industries 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
creative businesses and supportive intellectual property regimes can create 
a fertile ecosystem for CCIs. 
3 Informal institutions: Trust, networks and cultural capital are crucial in 
creative production and consumption. In many creative felds, informal 
networks play a signifcant role in project formation, talent discovery and 
knowledge dissemination. 
4 Institutional change: Understanding how to foster institutional envi‑
ronments conducive to CCI growth and innovation is critical to policy 
development. This might involve adapting copyright laws to the digital 
age, developing new forms of creative education or fostering cross‑sector 
collaborations. 
3.2.3.2 Institutional Economics and Development Theory 
Institutional economics has signifcantly contributed to development theory, 
explaining why some countries achieve economic development while others 
struggle. This perspective is particularly relevant for understanding the role 
of CCIs in broader development processes. 
Key ideas: 
1 Institutions for economic growth: Secure property rights, efective legal 
systems and well‑functioning markets are crucial for economic develop‑
ment. In the context of CCIs, this might include robust intellectual prop‑
erty protection, efcient contract enforcement and transparent market 
mechanisms for creative goods and services. 
2 Institutional quality: The quality of institutions is a crucial determinant of 
long‑term economic performance. For CCIs, this might involve assessing 
the efectiveness of cultural policy institutions, the fairness of funding al‑
location mechanisms or the inclusiveness of creative sector representation 
in policymaking. 
3 Institutional change: Development involves transforming institutions to 
support more complex economic activities. In the creative sector, this 
could include evolving from traditional patronage systems to more mar ‑
ket‑oriented models or developing new institutional frameworks to sup‑
port digital creative economies. 
4 Informal institutions: Cultural norms, social capital and informal net‑
works play signifcant roles in development processes. For CCIs, this 
might involve understanding how social attitudes towards artistic careers 
afect talent development or how informal mentorship networks contrib‑
ute to skill transfer in creative industries. 
Daron Acemoglu and James Robinson, in “Why Nations Fail” (2012), argue 
that “inclusive economic institutions” are essential to long‑term prosperity, 
Creative and Cultural Industries and Social‑Economic Development 99 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
writing: “Inclusive economic institutions … are those that allow and encour‑
age participation by the great mass of people in economic activities that make 
best use of their talents and skills and that enable individuals to make the 
choices they wish” (Acemoglu & Robinson, 2012, p. 74). This concept of 
inclusivity is particularly relevant for CCIs, which have the potential to thrive 
on diversity and broad participation (with the proper policies). 
3.2.3.3 Implications for CCIs and Development 
1 Institutional foundations: The development of CCIs requires supportive 
institutional frameworks, including efective intellectual property regimes 
and cultural policies. This might involve developing specialised legal 
frameworks for creative industries, establishing cultural policy agencies or 
integrating creative sector development into broader economic planning. 
2 Inclusive institutions: CCIs can contribute to more inclusive economic de‑
velopment by providing opportunities for diverse economic participation. 
This could involve policies to support grassroots cultural production, ini‑
tiatives to broaden access to creative education or programmes to support 
underrepresented groups in creative industries. 
3 Institutional innovation: CCIs often involve new economic organisation 
and exchange forms, potentially driving broader institutional innovations. 
For example, crowdfunding platforms developed for creative projects have 
spurred innovations in fnancial regulations and business models that have 
applications beyond the creative sector. 
4 Cultural institutions: Cultural institutions play a signifcant role in shap‑
ing overall economic development trajectories. Museums, theatres and 
cultural centres can serve as anchors for urban regeneration, tourism de‑
velopment catalysts and creative cluster formation hubs. 
Ha‑Joon Chang, in “Institutions and Economic Development: Theory, Policy 
and History” (2011), emphasises the need to understand the historical and 
cultural specifcity of institutions, cautioning against a one‑size‑fts‑all ap‑
proach to institutional development. This insight is particularly relevant for 
CCIs, given the diverse cultural contexts in which they operate. 
3.2.3.3.1 New Institutional Economics and CCIs 
New Institutional Economics (NIE), associated with scholars like Oliver 
Williamson and Elinor Ostrom, ofers additional insights for understanding 
CCIs: 
1 Transaction cost economics: Helps explain the organisation of crea‑
tive industries, including the choice between in‑house production and 
100 The Political Economy of the Creative and Cultural Industries 
 
 
 
 
outsourcing. For instance, the prevalence of freelance work in many crea‑
tive felds can be understood as a response to the high transaction costs 
associated with maintaining permanent creative staf in a project‑based 
industry. 
2 Property rights: The complex nature of intellectual property in CCIs and 
its impact on industry structure and innovation is a crucial area of NIE 
analysis. This includes examining how diferent IP regimes afect creative 
production, distribution and consumption and how new technologies 
challenge traditional notions of intellectual property. 
3 Collective action: Understanding how creative communities solve com‑
mon‑pool resource problems, such as in open‑source software develop‑
ment or collaborative artistic projects, is another area where NIE provides 
valuable insights. Ostrom’s work on governing commons resources is par‑
ticularly relevant here. 
4 Governance structures: The various governance models in CCIs, from tra‑
ditional frms to network‑based collaboration, can be analysed through an 
NIE lens. This includes examining the efciency of diferent organisational 
forms in creative production and understanding how governance struc‑
tures evolve in response to technological and market changes. 
The work of Elinor Ostrom on governing the commons has proven particu‑
larly insightful for understanding collaborative modes of cultural produc‑
tion within CCIs. In her seminal work “Governing the Commons” (1990), 
Ostrom challenges the notion that external authorities can easily design and 
impose optimal institutional solutions. Instead, she argues that “getting the 
institutions right is a difcult, time‑consuming, confict‑invoking process” 
(Ostrom, 1990, p. 14). This perspective is especially pertinent when consider‑
ing the complexities of developing efective institutions for CCIs. 
Ostrom’s insights highlight the nuanced approach required when applying 
institutional economics to CCIs and development theory. This application 
presents a unique set of challenges and opportunities that merit careful con‑
sideration. One of the primary challenges lies in the realm of measurement. 
Institutions, particularly informal ones, often need easier quantifcation and 
comparison across diferent contexts. This difculty is amplifed in the cre‑
ative sector, where intangible factors such as cultural capital and creative 
networks play pivotal roles. While crucial to the functioning of CCIs, these 
elements are notoriously difcult to capture through traditional economic 
metrics. 
The complexity of institutional environments for CCIs presents another 
signifcant challenge. These environments are often highly intricate and con‑
text‑specifc, characterised by a complex interplay between formal and infor‑
mal institutions and between creative and non‑creative sectors. This elaborate 
ecosystem resists simplistic analysis or one‑size‑fts‑all policy prescriptions, 
Creative and Cultural Industries and Social‑Economic Development 101 
 
necessitating a more nuanced and multifaceted approach. Translating institu‑
tional insights into efective policy poses yet another challenge. The long‑term 
nature of institutional change and the difculty of designing appropriate in‑
terventions make policy development in this area particularly complex. Poli‑
cymakers must grapple with the tension between the need for immediate 
action and the recognition that meaningful institutional change often unfolds 
over extended periods. 
While a strength, the interdisciplinary nature of institutional economics 
also presents particular challenges. It requires integrating economic analysis 
with insights from diverse felds such as sociology, anthropology and other 
disciplines. While this interdisciplinary approach enriches our understanding 
of CCIs, it also introduces methodological challenges and can sometimes run 
up against entrenched academic silos. These silos can impede the free fow 
of ideas and methodologies across disciplines, potentially limiting the full 
potential of institutional analysis in the context of CCIs. 
Despite these challenges, applying institutional economics to CCIs also of‑
fers signifcant opportunities. Perhaps most importantly, it provides a holistic 
framework for understanding CCI development. Researchers and policymak‑
ers can develop more nuanced and efective strategies for supporting creative 
economies by considering the broader institutional context. This comprehen‑
sive approach allows for a deeper understanding of the complex factors that 
shape the development and success of CCIs. Another critical opportunity 
lies in the context sensitivity that institutional economics encourages. Paying 
close attention to local and historical specifcities in development strategies 
can lead to more appropriate and efective interventions. These interventions 
can be tailored to specifc cultural and institutional contexts rather than rely‑
ing on generic solutions that may only suit some environments. 
Finally, this school of economic thought ofers valuable insights into the 
innovation dynamics and institutional conditions that foster creativity and 
innovation. By understanding the institutional factors that support or hinder 
creative activity, societies can develop more efective strategies for promoting 
innovation in CCIs and beyond. This understanding of innovation dynamics 
can inform the design of institutional environments conducive to creative 
fourishing, potentially leading to more vibrant and productive creative econ‑
omies. While applying institutional economics to CCIs presents particular 
challenges, it also ofers rich opportunities for deepening our understand‑
ing and improving our approaches to supporting these vital industries. By 
embracing both the complexities and the potential of this approach, it is 
possible to work towards creating more robust, sustainable and innovative 
creative economies. 
Furthermore, the insights from institutional economics can help bridge 
the gap between cultural policy and economic development strategies. By 
recognising the economic signifcance of cultural institutions and the cultural 
102 The Political Economy of the Creative and Cultural Industries 
 
  
 
 
 
 
 
dimensions of economic institutions, more integrated approaches to fostering 
CCIs development can be shaped. 
As CCIs grow in economic importance and digital technologies reshape 
creative production and consumption, the institutional perspective will be‑
come increasingly valuable. It ofers a framework for understanding these 
changes and for developing adaptive strategies to support these industries’ 
continued growth and development. Future research in this area could fo‑
cus on creating better metrics for assessing institutional quality in creative 
sectors, exploring the institutional dynamics of digital creative economies 
and examining the role of CCIs in driving broader institutional innovations. 
There is also potential for more comparative work exploring how diferent 
institutional arrangements across countries and regions shape the develop‑
ment of CCIs. 
3.2.4 CCIs: Driving Forces for Sustainable and Inclusive 
Development 
David Throsby’s pioneering work in cultural economics provides a corner ‑
stone for understanding the long‑term value of cultural assets and activities. 
His concept of cultural capital, “an asset which embodies, stores or pro‑
vides cultural value in addition to whatever economic value it may possess” 
(Throsby, 1999, p. 6), ofers a framework for recognising the enduring sig‑
nifcance of cultural resources. 
This concept of cultural capital serves multiple purposes. It acknowledges 
the long‑term value of cultural assets, much like other forms of capital that 
generate goods and services over time. For instance, a historic building isn’t 
just a structure; it’s a source of aesthetic value, educational opportunities and 
potential tourism revenue for years. Cultural capital also bridges the economic 
and artistic value gap, providing a more holistic basis for policy decisions. 
Throsby’s model of sustainable cultural development takes this idea fur ‑
ther, balancing economic, social, cultural and environmental outcomes. It 
emphasises intergenerational equity, ensuring future generations can beneft 
from cultural resources. It also stresses the importance of maintaining cul‑
tural diversity, applying the precautionary principle in the face of uncertainty 
and recognising the interdependence of cultural, economic, social and envi‑
ronmental systems (Throsby, 2011). This framework encourages a long‑term 
view of CCIs’ impacts. It suggests that we should evaluate these industries 
not just on their immediate economic contributions, but on how they foster 
cultural sustainability over time. In practice, this might mean conducting cul‑
tural impact assessments alongside environmental ones for major projects, 
developing heritage management strategies that balance preservation with 
sustainable economic use, and ensuring support for a diverse range of cul‑
tural activities, not just those with immediate commercial appeal. 
Creative and Cultural Industries and Social‑Economic Development 103 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
  
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
3.2.4.1 The Creative Economy and Sustainable Development 
Building on these foundations, the United Nations Conference on Trade and 
Development (UNCTAD) has championed the creative economy as a devel‑
opment strategy, particularly for developing countries. This approach rec‑
ognises CCIs as drivers of economic innovation, catalysing new products, 
services and working methods. UNCTAD encourages countries to integrate 
CCIs into their national development strategies, emphasising their potential 
for inclusive growth. These industries ofer opportunities for marginalised 
groups, including women and youth, to participate in economic activities. 
Moreover, creative industries are recognised as critical components of sus‑
tainable urbanisation, contributing to urban regeneration and quality of life. 
As UNCTAD (2018) states, “The creative economy has the potential 
to support developing countries in diversifying their economies and leap‑
frogging into new high‑growth areas of the world economy” (p. 5). This 
perspective reframes CCIs not as luxury sectors, but as fundamental com‑
ponents of resilient and dynamic economies. We’ve seen this approach 
in action with policies like Colombia’s Orange Economy and Thailand’s 
Creative Economy initiatives, which use comprehensive national strategies 
to promote creative sectors through tax incentives, infrastructure develop‑
ment and skills training. 
3.2.4.1.1 Emerging Models: Circular Culture and Platform Cooperatives 
As the world’s understanding of sustainability evolves, new approaches 
are emerging that further illuminate the potential of CCIs to contribute 
to holistic development. For instance, the concept of circular culture ap‑
plies circular economy principles to CCIs, aiming to reduce waste and 
maximise resource efciency. This approach encourages designing cultural 
products for longevity and reuse, developing new business models based 
on sharing and redistribution and applying digital technologies to cre‑
ate more sustainable cultural production and consumption patterns. We 
see this with initiatives like sustainable fashion design, environmentally 
conscious flm, TV productions and circular music festivals like DGTL 
in Amsterdam. Meanwhile, platform cooperatives represent an alternative 
model for digital CCIs based on democratic ownership and governance of 
digital platforms. This approach addresses concerns about the concentra‑
tion of power and wealth in the digital economy, ofering a more equitable 
model for creative workers and consumers. Platform cooperatives in CCIs 
feature collective ownership of digital platforms by users and workers, 
democratic governance structures, fair value distribution, data sovereignty 
and often open‑source technology. Scholz (2016) proposes that “Platform 
cooperativism can reframe the future of work and life, responding to the 
challenges of our time” (p. 14).

BOX 3.5 CIRCULAR ECONOMY AND COOPERATIVISM  
IN CCIS 
Contemporary examples of circular economy in CCIs include: 
1 Sustainable Fashion: Stella McCartney continues to lead in sustainable 
luxury fashion, recently launching her Frayme Mylo™ bag made from my‑
celium, a sustainable alternative to animal leather. Similarly, Adidas has 
partnered with Parley for the Oceans to create shoes made from recycled 
ocean plastic, with over 30 million pairs sold as of 2022. 
2 Circular Design in Electronics: Fairphone, a Dutch social enterprise, pro‑
duces modular smartphones for easy repair and upgrade. This extends the 
lifespan of devices and reduces e‑waste in the electronics industry. 
3 Sustainable Music Industry: In addition to circular festivals like DGTL in Am‑
sterdam, we’re seeing innovations like Coldplay’s 2022 Music of the Spheres 
World Tour, which aimed to cut direct emissions by 50% compared to their 
previous tour using sustainable materials, renewable energy and even ki‑
netic dancefoors to generate electricity. 
4 Circular Publishing: Penguin Random House has committed to using 100% 
recycled paper for its publications by 2025 and has already achieved 80% 
usage of recycled or FSC‑certifed paper as of 2022. 
5 Sustainable Film and TV Production: Building on initiatives like Albert in the 
United Kingdom, signifcant studios are adopting more sustainable prac‑
tices. Warner Bros., for instance, achieved carbon neutrality for all its pro‑
duction in 2021 and aims to be ultimately carbon neutral by 2025. 
Platform cooperatives represent an alternative model for digital CCIs based 
on democratic ownership and governance of digital platforms. This approach 
addresses concerns about the concentration of power and wealth in the digi‑
tal economy, ofering a more equitable model for creative workers and con‑
sumers. Platform cooperatives in CCIs feature collective ownership of digital 
platforms by users and workers, democratic governance structures, fair value 
distribution, data sovereignty and often open‑source technology. 
Recent examples of platform cooperativism in CCIs include: 
1 Resonate: This stream‑to‑own music platform cooperative continues grow‑
ing, ofering a fair payment model where artists earn more as their songs are 
played. 
2 Stocksy United: This artist‑owned stock photography and video platform 
has paid out over $100 million in royalties to its members since its inception, 
demonstrating the viability of the cooperative model in the digital creative 
economy.

3 Ampled: Launched in 2019, this platform allows music artists to receive di‑
rect support from their fans. It’s cooperatively owned by the artists, workers 
and supporters who use the platform. 
4 Cooperative Art World (CoArt): Established in 2021, CoArt is a platform co‑
operative that aims to create a more equitable art market by allowing artists, 
collectors and art professionals to collectively own and govern the platform. 
5 Guilded: Founded in 2022, this platform cooperative provides creative in‑

3.3 Social Inclusion and CCIs 
CCIs are potent catalysts for social change, fostering diversity, preserving 
heritage and driving innovation across societies. They promote mental health 
and well‑being, ofer therapeutic benefts and build community connections. 
However, CCIs face signifcant challenges in accessibility, economic sustain‑
ability and authentic representation. Realising their full potential requires 
addressing these obstacles to create genuinely inclusive and impactful crea‑
tive ecosystems. Below, you’ll fnd a synthesis of the primary potentialities 
and challenges in the contemporary ecosystem when associating CCIs with 
social development. 
3.3.1 The Promise of Inclusion: CCIs as Catalysts for Social Change 
3.3.1.1 Fostering Diversity and Cultural Exchange 
CCIs are potent platforms for representing minority cultures and facilitating 
cultural exchange, challenging dominant narratives and promoting a more 
inclusive cultural landscape. This function manifests in various ways: 
• Representation in Media: Film festivals spotlighting LGBTQ+ themes or 
exhibitions of Indigenous art showcase underrepresented cultures and 
create opportunities for cross‑cultural dialogue and understanding. The 
global success of flms like Moonlight or Parasite has brought marginalised 
narratives into mainstream discourse, fostering greater cultural awareness 
and empathy. For instance, Moonlight (2016) broke new ground in its nu‑
anced portrayal of Black masculinity and queer identity, challenging ste‑
reotypes and opening conversations about intersectionality in mainstream 
cinema. 
• Breaking Stereotypes: CCIs play a crucial role in challenging and dis‑
mantling harmful stereotypes by providing spaces for diverse voices and 
106 The Political Economy of the Creative and Cultural Industries 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
perspectives. This representation extends beyond mere visibility; it can 
catalyse broader societal changes by shifting public perceptions and en‑
couraging more nuanced understandings of diferent cultures and experi‑
ences. For example, the #OwnVoices movement in publishing has pushed 
for authentic representation in literature, leading to a more diverse range 
of stories and characters in books, particularly in children’s and young 
adult literature. 
• Cultural Diplomacy: CCIs often serve as unofcial ambassadors, fa‑
cilitating international understanding and cooperation. Cultural ex‑
changes, international art exhibitions and global music festivals create 
bridges between nations and cultures, fostering mutual respect and 
appreciation. The Edinburgh International Festival, for instance, was 
founded in 1947 as a post‑World War II initiative to “provide a plat‑
form for the fowering of the human spirit” and has since become a ma‑
jor celebration of arts and culture, attracting performers and audiences 
from around the world. 
• Digital Platforms and Global Reach: The rise of digital platforms has am‑
plifed the potential for cultural exchange and diversity in CCIs. Social 
media platforms like Instagram and TikTok have become powerful tools 
for artists from underrepresented communities to showcase their work 
and connect with global audiences. For example, Indigenous artists have 
used these platforms to share traditional art forms and contemporary in‑
terpretations, reaching audiences far beyond their local communities. 
3.3.2 Preserving and Innovating Cultural Expression 
The dual role of CCIs in both preserving traditional arts and supporting new 
forms of expression creates a dynamic cultural landscape that honours herit‑
age while embracing innovation: 
• Preservation of Traditional Arts: Initiatives like UNESCO’s Intangible Cul‑
tural Heritage programme highlight the importance of safeguarding tradi‑
tional practices. CCIs play a vital role in this preservation, often adapting 
conventional art forms for contemporary audiences without losing their 
cultural essence. This is particularly crucial in maintaining cultural diver ‑
sity in the face of globalisation. For example, the Japanese art of Kabuki 
theatre has been preserved through strict traditional training and modern 
adaptations, including digital technology in set designs. 
• Fostering Innovation: Support for emerging artists and new art forms 
pushes the boundaries of cultural expression, refecting evolving societal 
values and experiences. This innovation ensures that culture remains a 
living, breathing entity responsive to current social issues and experiences. 
The rise of digital art and NFTs (non‑fungible tokens) has opened new 
Creative and Cultural Industries and Social‑Economic Development 107 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
avenues for artists to create, distribute and monetise their work, challeng‑
ing traditional art market structures. 
• Bridging Tradition and Modernity: Many successful CCI projects fnd 
innovative ways to blend traditional and contemporary elements, creat‑
ing new forms of expression that resonate with diverse audiences while 
maintaining cultural authenticity. For instance, fashion designer Ozwald 
Boateng merges conventional African textiles and patterns with contem‑
porary British tailoring, creating a unique aesthetic that celebrates cultural 
fusion. 
• Technological Integration: Technology integration in traditional art forms 
has created new possibilities for preservation and innovation. Virtual and 
augmented reality technologies are being used to create immersive experi‑
ences of historical sites and artefacts, allowing for broader access and new 
forms of engagement with cultural heritage. 
3.3.3 Fostering Social Cohesion and Civic Engagement 
CCIs possess a unique capacity to bring communities together and stimulate 
civic participation: 
• Community Building: Community arts projects, local festivals and shared 
cultural spaces can unite diverse groups, building social capital and 
strengthening community ties. These initiatives often transcend social, 
economic and cultural barriers, creating a shared identity and purpose. 
The Notting Hill Carnival in London, for example, has evolved from a 
small community event to one of Europe’s largest street festivals, celebrat‑
ing Caribbean culture and fostering community cohesion. 
• Platforms for Social Commentary: CCIs provide potent tools for civic 
engagement and political expression. Public art installations, socially en‑
gaged theatre and protest music can raise awareness about social issues 
and inspire collective action. The role of street art in urban social move‑
ments or the use of documentary flms to highlight social injustices demon‑
strates how CCIs can be catalysts for social change. For instance, Banksy’s 
politically charged street art has sparked global conversations about con‑
sumerism and refugee rights. 
• Participatory Culture: Many CCI initiatives encourage active participation 
rather than passive consumption, fostering a sense of ownership and en‑
gagement among community members. This participatory approach can 
efectively address local issues and build community resilience. The Ghetto 
Biennale in Haiti, for example, invites artists from around the world to 
create work in collaboration with local artists in the informal neighbour ‑
hoods of Port‑au‑Prince, challenging traditional art world hierarchies and 
fostering cross‑cultural dialogue. 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
108 The Political Economy of the Creative and Cultural Industries 
• Urban Regeneration: CCIs have been instrumental in urban regeneration 
projects, transforming neglected areas into vibrant cultural hubs. The 
transformation of Bilbao, Spain, through constructing the Guggenheim 
Museum, is a classic example. Still, smaller‑scale initiatives like revitalising 
former industrial spaces into artist studios and galleries have signifcantly 
impacted local communities. 
• Digital Civic Engagement: Online platforms have created new opportu‑
nities for civic engagement through CCIs. Crowdfunding platforms like 
Kickstarter have enabled community‑driven cultural projects. At the same 
time, social media has facilitated the rapid organisation of cultural events 
and protests, as seen in movements like the Arab Spring or Black Lives 
Matter. 
3.3.3.1 Mental Health and Well‑being: The Therapeutic Power of CCIs 
A crucial aspect of CCIs that deserves special attention is their potential to 
impact mental health and well‑being positively. This dimension adds another 
layer to their social signifcance: 
3.3.4 Art Therapy and Expressive Arts 
• Therapeutic Applications: Many CCIs, particularly visual arts, music and 
dance, are increasingly recognised for their therapeutic value. Art therapy 
programmes have shown signifcant benefts in treating various mental 
health conditions, from anxiety and depression to PTSD and schizophre‑
nia. For example, the Combat Paper Project in the United States helps 
veterans process their experiences through papermaking workshops, 
where they turn their military uniforms into paper and then create art on 
that paper. 
• Emotional Expression and Processing: Creative activities provide outlets 
for emotional expression, allowing individuals to process complex feelings 
and experiences in non‑verbal ways. This can be particularly benefcial for 
those who struggle with traditional talk therapies. The British Association 
of Art Therapists has documented numerous case studies demonstrating 
how art therapy has helped individuals, including children with autism 
and adults with dementia, to communicate and process emotions more 
efectively. 
• Mindfulness and Stress Reduction: Engaging in creative activities can in‑
duce a fow state, promoting mindfulness and reducing stress. Practices 
like colouring for adults have gained popularity as accessible stress‑relief 
tools. A study published in the Art Therapy journal in 2016 found that 
45 minutes of creative activity signifcantly reduced cortisol levels, regard‑
less of artistic experience or talent. 
• Body‑Mind Connection: Movement‑based creative expressions like dance 
therapy have shown promise in improving both physical and mental 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
Creative and Cultural Industries and Social‑Economic Development 109 
well‑being. Programmes like Dance for PD (Parkinson’s disease) have 
demonstrated how dance can improve motor function, balance and qual‑
ity of life for individuals with PD. 
3.3.5 Social Connection and Belonging 
• Combating Isolation: Participation in cultural activities, whether as crea‑
tors or audience members, can combat social isolation, a signifcant risk 
factor for mental health issues. For instance, community choirs, art classes 
and book clubs provide opportunities for meaningful social interaction. 
The United Kingdom’s “Men’s Sheds” movement, which brings men to‑
gether to work on practical projects in a social setting, has been particu‑
larly efective in addressing isolation and improving mental health among 
older men. 
• Building Identity and Self‑esteem: Engaging with one’s cultural heritage 
through CCIs can strengthen a sense of identity and belonging, which is 
crucial for mental well‑being, especially for minority groups and immi‑
grants. Programmes like the Amal Project in Germany use theatre and 
storytelling to help young refugees integrate into their new communities 
while maintaining connections to their cultural roots. 
• Intergenerational Connections: CCIs can bridge generational gaps, fos‑
tering understanding and connection between diferent age groups. Inter ‑
generational choirs and community art projects have shown promise in 
reducing ageism and improving social cohesion. 
3.3.5.1 Cognitive Benefts 
• Lifelong Learning: Engagement with CCIs promotes lifelong learning and 
cognitive stimulation, which is associated with better mental health out‑
comes, particularly in older adults. The University of the Third Age (U3A) 
movement in the United Kingdom, which ofers various cultural and edu‑
cational activities for older adults, has been linked to improved cognitive 
function and life satisfaction among participants. 
• Creativity and Problem‑solving: Regular engagement with creative ac‑
tivities enhances overall creativity and problem‑solving skills, which can 
translate into improved resilience and adaptability in daily life. The Lin‑
coln Center Education’s “Imagination Workshops” in New York schools 
have demonstrated how arts‑based learning can improve students’ critical 
thinking and problem‑solving skills across various academic subjects. 
• Neuroplasticity: Emerging research suggests that engaging in creative ac‑
tivities can promote neuroplasticity, the brain’s ability to form new neural 
connections. A study published in Frontiers in Human Neuroscience 2014 
found that creating art led to increased functional connectivity in the 
brain, particularly in areas associated with introspection, self‑monitoring 
and memory. 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
110 The Political Economy of the Creative and Cultural Industries 
3.3.6 Public Health and Well‑being Initiatives 
• Arts on Prescription: Some healthcare systems have begun implementing 
“arts on prescription” programmes, where patients are referred to cultural 
activities as part of their treatment plans, recognising the health benefts of 
creative engagement. In the United Kingdom, the Airlift programme has 
shown promising results, with participants reporting improved well‑being 
and decreased healthcare utilisation. 
• Community Well‑being Projects: CCIs are increasingly integrated into 
public health strategies, with community art projects, music festivals and 
cultural events being used to promote overall well‑being and social cohe‑
sion. For example, the New Orleans Musicians’ Clinic combines health‑
care services with performance opportunities, recognising the intrinsic link 
between creative expression and well‑being for musicians. 
• Workplace Well‑being: Some companies incorporate CCIs into their em‑
ployee well‑being programmes, recognising creative activities’ stress‑re‑
ducing and team‑building benefts. For instance, Google’s “Creative Skills 
for Innovation” programme ofers employees workshops in various art 
forms to boost creativity and well‑being. 
• Digital Mental Health Interventions: The intersection of CCIs and tech‑
nology has led to innovative digital mental health interventions. Apps that 
combine art therapy techniques with cognitive behavioural therapy princi‑
ples or virtual reality experiences designed to reduce anxiety represent new 
frontiers in mental health support. 
3.3.6.1 The Reality Check: Challenges and Criticisms 
Despite their potential, CCIs need help realising their capacity to promote 
social inclusion and well‑being. Signifcant accessibility issues, including ge‑
ographic disparities and digital exclusion, can reinforce social inequalities. 
In “Culture is Bad for You” (2020), Orian Brook, Dave O’Brien and Mark 
Taylor present a critical examination of the CCIs, challenging the notion that 
these sectors are bastions of diversity and opportunity. Through extensive 
data analysis, they reveal persistent inequalities based on class, gender and 
race within CCIs, arguing that these industries often reproduce and exacer ‑
bate existing social disparities. The authors debunk the myth of meritocracy 
in cultural sectors, highlighting how social capital, networks and economic 
privileges play crucial roles in determining success. Their research demon‑
strates that the cultural workforce remains predominantly white, male and 
middle class, especially in positions of power, directly afecting the diversity 
of artistic output. 
The implications of their work for CCIs are profound, calling for systemic 
changes rather than superfcial diversity initiatives. Their fndings suggest a 
 
 
 
 
 
Creative and Cultural Industries and Social‑Economic Development 111 
need to reassess how talent is defned and identifed, address economic bar ‑
riers through restructuring internships and funding models and implement 
targeted policy interventions. O’Brien and Brook’s research underscores the 
importance of early educational interventions to create more diverse pipe‑
lines into CCIs and challenges traditional cultural hierarchies. Their work 
serves as a crucial counterpoint to optimistic narratives about CCIs, urging 
industry leaders, policymakers and practitioners to confront and actively dis‑
mantle systems of privilege and exclusion for CCIs to truly fulfl their poten‑
tial as drivers of social inclusion and well‑being. 
Economic barriers, from high participation costs to career entry hurdles, 
limit engagement and diversity within these industries. The tension between 
authentic representation and tokenism remains a persistent challenge, risk‑
ing surface‑level diversity without meaningful inclusion. Additionally, CCIs 
must carefully balance innovation with tradition to avoid cultural commodi‑
fcation and the loss of heritage. Comprehensive policies at local, national 
and international levels are essential to address these challenges, ensure eq‑
uitable access and maximise the positive social impact of CCIs. Below, you 
can fnd some relevant topics that should be urgently addressed to unveil CCI 
potential: 
3.3.7 Accessibility and the Digital Divide 
• Geographic Disparities: Urban areas often have a concentration of cul‑
tural institutions and creative spaces, while rural or marginalised commu‑
nities may need more access to such resources. This uneven distribution 
can reinforce existing social and economic inequalities. For example, a 
2019 report by Arts Council England found signifcant disparities in arts 
funding between London and the rest of the country, highlighting the need 
for a more equitable distribution of cultural resources. 
• Digital Exclusion: While digital technologies have opened new avenues for 
cultural participation and creation, they have also created new forms of 
exclusion. Only some have equal access to the internet or digital devices, 
and disparities in digital literacy can prevent full participation in the digi‑
tal creative economy. The COVID‑19 pandemic has further highlighted 
this divide, as many cultural experiences moved online, potentially exclud‑
ing those without reliable internet access or digital skills. 
• Intersectional Challenges: The digital divide often intersects with other 
forms of social inequality, compounding exclusion based on age, socio‑
economic status and geographic location. For instance, older adults in 
rural areas may face multiple barriers to accessing digital cultural con‑
tent, including lack of infrastructure, limited digital skills and age‑related 
disabilities. 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
112 The Political Economy of the Creative and Cultural Industries 
• Language Barriers: Many digital platforms and cultural content are pre‑
dominantly in English or a few other major languages, creating barriers 
for non‑native speakers and diverse linguistic backgrounds. This can limit 
the global reach and inclusivity of digital CCI initiatives. 
3.3.8 Economic Barriers and Afordability 
• Cost of Participation: For many, cultural consumption  –buying art, at‑
tending performances or accessing digital content  –  can be prohibitive. 
This economic barrier can exclude signifcant portions of the population 
from the benefts of CCIs. For example, a 2019 UK Audience Agency 
survey found that cost was the primary barrier to arts attendance for 
lower‑income groups. 
• Career Entry Barriers: Pursuing a career in the creative industries often re‑
quires fnancial resources for education, equipment or unpaid internships, 
which can exclude individuals from lower socioeconomic backgrounds. 
The Sutton Trust’s 2018 report on internships in the United Kingdom 
found that the creative industries were among the sectors most reliant on 
unpaid internships, creating signifcant barriers to entry for those who 
cannot aford to work for free. 
• Funding Challenges: Many CCIs, especially those focused on social impact, 
need help with sustainable funding models. This can lead to short‑term 
thinking and difculty implementing long‑term, systemic change. The 
precarious nature of funding for many cultural organisations was starkly 
highlighted during the COVID‑19 pandemic, with many institutions fac‑
ing closure or signifcant cutbacks. 
• Gentrifcation and Displacement: The success of CCIs in urban regenera‑
tion can lead to gentrifcation, potentially displacing the very communities 
they initially aimed to serve. This has been observed in areas like Berlin’s 
Kreuzberg district or London’s East End, where thriving artistic communi‑
ties have contributed to rising property prices and changing neighbour ‑
hood demographics. 
3.3.9 Representation and Tokenism 
• Surface‑Level Diversity: While CCIs often champion diversity, there’s a 
risk of surface‑level representation that only translates to meaningful in‑
clusion or power redistribution within these industries. For instance, while 
there may be increased representation of diverse characters in flm and tel‑
evision, decision‑making positions in major studios remain predominantly 
white and male. 
• Cultural Appropriation: There’s a fne line between cultural exchange and 
appropriation. CCIs must navigate this carefully to ensure respectful and 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
Creative and Cultural Industries and Social‑Economic Development 113 
authentic representation of diverse cultures. Controversies surrounding 
Indigenous designs in fashion or the adoption of minority cultural ele‑
ments in mainstream music highlight the ongoing challenges in this area. 
• Stereotyping: Even well‑intentioned representation eforts can sometimes 
reinforce stereotypes if not handled carefully. This can be particularly 
problematic in media representations, where complex cultural identities 
may be reduced to simplistic tropes. 
• Gatekeeping: Despite eforts to increase diversity, many CCIs still need 
help with gatekeeping, where established networks and power structures 
limit opportunities for newcomers, particularly those from underrepre‑
sented groups. This can be seen in art, where a few galleries and institu‑
tions wield signifcant infuence over which artists gain recognition. 
3.3.10 Gender Inequalities in CCIs 
• Glass Ceiling Efect: Women in CCIs frequently face barriers to advance‑
ment into top leadership positions. This is evident in areas like music pro‑
duction, where female producers and sound engineers are signifcantly 
underrepresented in senior roles despite growing numbers entering the 
feld. 
• Pay Gap Persistence: Despite progress in representation, signifcant pay 
disparities persist in many creative industries. For instance, in the flm 
industry, female actors often earn substantially less than their male coun‑
terparts for equivalent roles. This gap extends to behind‑the‑scenes roles 
such as directors, producers and writers. 
• ·Gender Stereotyping in Roles: Certain creative roles remain heavily gen‑
dered, limiting opportunities for diverse participation. For example, in the 
gaming industry, women are often pigeonholed into marketing or design 
roles, while programming and development teams remain predominantly 
male. 
3.3.11 Global Majorities Exclusion 
• Eurocentric Narrative Dominance: In literature and flm, stories from 
Global North perspectives often overshadow narratives from the Global 
South. This leads to a narrow representation of global experiences and 
perpetuates cultural stereotypes. 
• Language Barriers in International Collaboration: The dominance of Eng‑
lish in global creative industries can exclude talented individuals from 
non‑English speaking backgrounds, limiting the diversity of voices and 
perspectives in international projects. 
• Unequal Resource Distribution: Funding and resources for cultural pro‑
jects are often concentrated in Western countries, making it challenging 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
114 The Political Economy of the Creative and Cultural Industries 
for artists and creators from Global South nations to access necessary sup‑
port for large‑scale or high‑budget productions. 
3.3.12 Lack of Access to Diverse Social Backgrounds 
• Economic Barriers to Entry: Many creative professions require unpaid 
internships or expensive education, creating signifcant obstacles for in‑
dividuals from lower‑income backgrounds to enter and succeed in these 
felds. 
• Networking Disparities: Success in CCIs often relies heavily on personal 
connections and networking. Individuals from less privileged backgrounds 
may need more access to these crucial networks, hindering their career 
progression and opportunities. 
• Geographic Limitations: Creative opportunities are often concentrated in 
major urban centres, disadvantaging talented individuals from rural or re‑
mote areas who may need more resources to relocate or commute to these 
hubs of creative activity. 
3.3.13 Innovation at the Cost of Tradition 
• Commodifcation of Culture: In the push for innovation, traditional arts 
are at risk of being repackaged and commodifed, potentially stripping 
them of their cultural context and deeper meanings. This can be seen in the 
mass production of “ethnic” crafts for tourist markets or the appropria‑
tion of traditional designs in fashion without proper acknowledgement or 
compensation. 
• Balancing Act: Finding the right balance between preserving cultural herit‑
age and fostering innovation remains a constant challenge for CCIs. This 
tension is particularly evident in felds like architecture, where preserving 
historical buildings must be balanced with the need for modern, sustain‑
able urban development. 
• Loss of Traditional Skills: As CCIs evolve, there’s a risk of losing tradi‑
tional skills and techniques. For example, digitising design processes in 
felds like textile production or furniture making can lead to a decline in 
traditional craftsmanship skills. 
• Authenticity vs. Accessibility: Eforts to make traditional art forms more 
accessible or appealing to contemporary audiences can sometimes lead to 
concern. 
CCIs stand at a critical juncture, embodying immense potential for positive 
social change and signifcant challenges that threaten to undermine their im‑
pact. On one hand, CCIs serve as powerful catalysts for fostering diversity, 
preserving cultural heritage, driving innovation and promoting mental health

Creative and Cultural Industries and Social‑Economic Development 125 
and well‑being. They ofer unique platforms for representing marginalised 
voices, facilitating cultural exchange and building community cohesion. The 
therapeutic power of creative expression, from art therapy to community 
arts projects, demonstrates the profound impact CCIs can have on individual 
and collective well‑being. Digital platforms have amplifed these positive ef‑
fects, creating unprecedented global cultural exchange and civic engagement 
opportunities. 
However, as Brook, O’Brien and Taylor’s work “Culture is Bad for You” 
illustrates, CCIs grapple with deeply entrenched inequalities and systemic 
barriers. Issues of accessibility, economic exclusion and authentic representa‑
tion persist, often reproducing and exacerbating existing social disparities. 
While ofering new avenues for participation, the digital divide has created 
new forms of exclusion. Balancing innovation with tradition and authenticity 
with commodifcation remains an ongoing challenge. Moving forward, realis‑
ing the full potential of CCIs as drivers of social development and well‑being 
will require comprehensive, systemic changes. This includes implementing 
targeted policies at local, national and international levels, reassessing how 
talent is identifed and nurtured, addressing economic barriers and actively 
dismantling systems of privilege within these industries. Only through such 
concerted eforts can CCIs truly fulfl their promise as inclusive, transforma‑
tive forces in society, fostering a cultural landscape that authentically refects 
and serves the diversity of our communities. 
Notes 
1 Thorstein Veblen (1857–1929) was an American economist and sociologist 
known for his critique of capitalism and concept of “conspicuous consumption.” 
He authored “The Theory of the Leisure Class” (1899), which introduced insti‑
tutional analysis to American economics. Veblen’s work laid the foundation for 
institutional economics by emphasising the role of social and cultural factors in 
economic behaviour. 
John R. Commons (1862–1945) was an American institutional economist and 
labour historian who contributed signifcantly to understanding collective action 
and labour economics. He developed the concept of “collective bargaining” and 
played a crucial role in shaping American labour law and policy. Commons’ work 
“Institutional Economics” (1934) was instrumental in defning the feld. 
Wesley Clair Mitchell (1874–1948) was an American economist known for his 
empirical work on business cycles and his leadership of the National Bureau of 
Economic Research. He emphasised the importance of quantitative analysis in 
economics and studying actual business behaviour. Mitchell’s work “Business Cy‑
cles” (1913) highlighted the role of institutions in economic fuctuations. 
2 Douglas North (1920–2015) was an American economist known for his work on 
economic history and institutional change. He won the Nobel Prize in Economics 
in 1993 for his research on the role of institutions in long‑term fnancial perfor ‑
mance. North’s seminal work “Institutions, Institutional Change and Economic 
Performance” (1990) signifcantly advanced institutional economic theory.

the frst woman to win the Nobel Prize in Economics in 2009. Her ground‑break‑
ing work on common‑pool resources and collective action challenged conven‑
tional wisdom about managing shared resources. Ostrom’s book “Governing the 
Commons” (1990) provided crucial insights into institutional arrangements for 
sustainable resource management.
