CREATIVE AND CULTURAL INDUSTRIES 
AND THE FUTURE (OR PRESENT) 
OF WORK 
4.1 The Critical Role of the Informal Economy in Creative 
Cultural and Industries (CCIs) 
The informal economy plays a highly relevant yet complex role in devel‑
oping countries’ CCIs, intersecting with the modern and traditional sectors 
but predominantly associated with the latter. As highlighted in the UNESCO 
report “Re|Shaping Cultural Policies” (2018, p. 84), “In many developing 
countries, the informal economy plays a vital role in the production and dis‑
tribution of cultural goods and services. However, this informality can also 
be a barrier to the growth and development of the sector.” 
The characteristics of the informal economy in CCIs are multifaceted. Its 
prevalence is signifcant, with some African countries, for instance, seeing 
up to 80% of music industry revenues generated through informal channels 
(CISAC, 2018). The underground music scene exhibits remarkable adapt‑
ability and creativity, as evidenced by the proliferation of DIY house con‑
certs and pop‑up gigs. Frustrated with traditional venue restrictions and 
high costs, independent musicians have begun transforming unconventional 
spaces into impromptu performance venues. Basements, rooftops, aban‑
doned warehouses and public parks become temporary concert halls, often 
organised through encrypted messaging apps and word‑of‑mouth networks. 
These informal shows allow artists to experiment with new material, foster 
intimate connections with fans and bypass traditional gatekeepers in the mu‑
sic industry. 
Some musicians have taken this concept further by creating “guerrilla 
gigs” – surprise performances in unexpected public spaces like subway sta‑
tions or busy street corners. Using battery‑powered amps and instruments, 
 
 
 
 
 
130 The Political Economy of the Creative and Cultural Industries 
these artists can set up and break down quickly, often performing short sets 
before authorities intervene. This approach provides exposure to new au‑
diences and challenges conventional notions of where and how live music 
should be experienced. Their works often address social issues or local cul‑
ture. They appear suddenly in public spaces and disappear without a trace 
by morning. This approach allows artists to bypass traditional gatekeepers 
and permit processes while reaching broad audiences. It provides opportuni‑
ties for cultural workers with limited formal education or capital, allowing 
broader participation in artistic production and distribution. Importantly, 
informal cultural activities often preserve and promote local cultural expres‑
sions that the formal, globalised cultural economy may overlook. Despite 
its ‘informal’ status, this sector contributes signifcantly to employment and 
income generation in many developing countries. 
However, the informal nature of these activities also poses several chal‑
lenges. Informal cultural workers often lack legal protection, leaving them 
vulnerable to exploitation and unable to capitalise on their creative works 
fully. Formal business status is necessary for access to fnance, hindering 
growth opportunities for many cultural entrepreneurs. The informal nature 
of operations can also create barriers to scaling, restricting the ability of cul‑
tural businesses to expand beyond a specifc size or reach international mar‑
kets. Furthermore, the prevalence of informal activities creates signifcant 
data gaps, making it difcult to accurately measure and understand the total 
economic impact of CCIs in developing countries. Lastly, the substantial role 
of the informal sector in CCIs is often overlooked in cultural policy formula‑
tion, leading to inefective or counterproductive blind spot interventions. 
These characteristics and challenges highlight the complex relationship be‑
tween informality and development in many countries’ cultural and creative 
sectors. They underscore the need for nuanced approaches that can harness 
the dynamism of the informal sector while addressing its limitations, ulti‑
mately fostering a more inclusive and sustainable growth of CCIs in develop‑
ing economies. 
BOX 4.1 CHALLENGES POSED BY INFORMALITY 
1 Lack of Legal Protection: Informal cultural workers often operate with‑
out copyright protection, leaving them vulnerable to exploitation and un‑
able to capitalise on their creative works fully. 
2 Limited Access to Finance: Many cultural entrepreneurs have formal 
business status, which allows them to access loans or investments needed 
for growth. 
3 Barriers to Scaling: The informal nature of operations can limit the ability 
of cultural businesses to grow beyond a specifc size or reach international 
markets. 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
Creative and Cultural Industries and the Future (or Present) of Work 131 
4 Data Gaps: The informal nature of many cultural activities makes it difcult 
to accurately measure and understand the total economic impact of CCIs in 
developing countries. 
5 Policy Blind Spots: The signifcant role of the informal sector in CCIs is 
often overlooked in cultural policy formulation, leading to inefective or 
counterproductive interventions. 
4.1.1 The Informal Economy and Structural Heterogeneity 
The prevalence of informality in CCIs both refects and reinforces the struc‑
tural heterogeneity observed by Pinto (see Chapter 3). It contributes to the 
persistence of a dual economy within the cultural sector, where a small, for ‑
mal, highly productive (in economic terms) segment coexists with a large, in‑
formal, less productive (economically) segment. This duality presents unique 
challenges for policymakers and cultural entrepreneurs. On one hand, the 
informal sector’s dynamism and cultural authenticity are valuable assets that 
should be preserved. On the other hand, the limitations imposed by infor ‑
mality can hinder the overall development and global competitiveness of a 
country’s creative industries (CI). 
4.1.2 Policy Implications and Development Strategies 
Understanding the structural heterogeneity of CCIs and the crucial role of the 
informal economy is essential for developing efective policies and strategies. 
Some key considerations include: 
1 Tailored Approaches: Policies must address the specifc needs of the mod‑
ern and traditional/informal sectors, recognising their diferent capacities 
and challenges. 
2 Gradual Formalisation: Strategies to encourage the gradual formalisation 
of informal cultural activities, providing incentives and support rather 
than punitive measures. 
3 Bridging Initiatives: Programmes facilitating knowledge and technology 
transfer between the modern and traditional sectors help reduce produc‑
tivity gaps. 
4 Preserving Cultural Diversity: Ensuring eforts to develop and formalise 
CCIs are not at the cost of cultural diversity and authenticity. 
5 Improved Data Collection: Investing in better data collection methods to 
capture the full extent of cultural and creative activities, including those in 
the informal sector. 
6 Access to Finance: Developing innovative fnancing mechanisms that can 
serve the needs of both formal and informal cultural entrepreneurs. 
 
 
 
   
 
132 The Political Economy of the Creative and Cultural Industries 
7 Education and Training: Providing targeted education and training pro‑
grammes that respect traditional knowledge while imparting skills rele‑
vant to the modern creative economy. 
8 Intellectual Property Rights: Developing fexible approaches to intellectual 
property that can protect the rights of informal cultural workers while 
encouraging innovation and growth. 
4.2 The Evolving Landscape of Creative Work in CCIs: 
The Individual, the Space and the Tensions 
of Time and Space 
In the rapidly evolving portrait of the CI, the individual emerges as a central 
fgure, navigating a complex web of digital and physical realms. This crea‑
tive professional embodies the French sociologist Pierre Bourdieu’s concept 
of habitus, which he describes as “systems of durable, transposable disposi‑
tions, structured structures predisposed to function as structuring structures” 
(Bourdieu, 1990, p. 53). Possessing various forms of capital – cultural, social 
and symbolic – the creative individual employs in analogy what Michel de 
Certeau calls “tactics,” or “ways of operating” that allow them to “use, ma‑
nipulate, and divert” the spaces created by dominant economic and political 
forces (de Certeau, 1984, p. 30). They are at once empowered by democ‑
ratised tools and challenged by the blurring boundaries between work and 
leisure, constantly adapting their skills and redefning their creative identity 
in a fuid, often precarious environment. This post‑industrial creative meets 
the promise of increased leisure time for innovation and the reality of an 
always‑on digital economy, seeking to balance personal vision with market 
pressures in a globally connected yet often isolating creative ecosystem. 
The urban space, once solely the physical backdrop of creative activity, 
now extends into the digital realm, creating a hybrid environment that ena‑
bles and challenges creative work. Physical, creative clusters and co‑working 
spaces fnd their digital counterparts in online platforms and virtual collabo‑
ration environments. These new urban spaces often bear the characteristics 
of what Marc Augé terms “non‑places,” drawn as spaces “which cannot be 
defned as relational, or historical, or concerned with identity” (Augé, 1995, 
pp. 77–78). However, these non‑places are transformed by creative profes‑
sionals through their practices, echoing de Certeau’s assertion that “space is 
a practised place” (de Certeau, 1984, p. 117). The tension between the global 
reach ofered by digital platforms and the need for local embodied creative 
communities creates a dynamic interplay between physical and virtual envi‑
ronments. Creative individuals navigate this expanded urban space, seeking 
to forge meaningful connections and identity‑afrming experiences amidst 
the fuidity and impermanence of digital realms while simultaneously lever ‑
aging the unprecedented opportunities for collaboration and exposure these 
new spaces provide. 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
Creative and Cultural Industries and the Future (or Present) of Work 133 
The time‑space of production in the CI has undergone a profound trans‑
formation, characterised by compression and reconfguration of temporal 
and spatial dimensions of work. This shift aligns with Bourdieu’s observa‑
tion that “the new logic of the economy rejects the Aristotelian view of the 
economic feld as an autonomous sphere” (Bourdieu, 2003, p. 7), as the 
boundaries between work and leisure time blur in a 24/7 global economy. 
The deterritorialisation of work, enabled by cloud‑based tools and digi‑
tal platforms, allows for unprecedented fexibility and blurred work‑life 
separation. De Certeau’s concept of “making do” becomes crucial here, as 
creatives “make use of the cracks that particular conjunctions open in the 
surveillance of the proprietary powers” (de Certeau, 1984, p. 37), fnding 
ways to navigate and subvert traditional temporal and spatial constraints 
of work. 
This new paradigm of creative production, while ofering exciting pos‑
sibilities for global cooperation and diverse inputs, also necessitates new 
work organisation and project management models. Creative professionals 
must balance the demands for instantaneous responsiveness with the time 
required for creative incubation and development, all while striving to main‑
tain personal well‑being and creative integrity in an ever‑connected world 
that increasingly resembles Augé’s “world of supermodernity” characterised 
by “overabundance and excess” (Augé, 1995, p. 30). 
In a similar palette, the concept of “creative leisure,” introduced by Ital‑
ian sociologist Domenico De Masi, ofers a compelling perspective on the 
evolving nature of work and creativity in the post‑industrial era. De Masi 
argues that as automation and technological advancements reduce the need 
for traditional labour, society must redefne its relationship with work and 
leisure. He envisions a future where the boundaries between work, study 
and play become increasingly blurred, leading to a new paradigm of creative 
productivity. In this model, leisure is not mere idleness but a fertile ground 
for innovation and personal growth. De Masi states, “Creative leisure is the 
ability to work, study, and play at the same time” (De Masi, 2000, p. 226), 
suggesting that the most valuable and fulflling activities seamlessly integrate 
these elements. 
De Masi’s theory of creative leisure aligns closely with the realities faced 
by many professionals in the CI. In this sector, inspiration and innovative 
ideas often emerge during apparent leisure or unstructured time. The cofee 
shop conversation, the museum visit or the late‑night browsing session can 
all become catalysts for creative breakthroughs. However, this blending of 
work and leisure also presents challenges. As De Masi warns, “The risk is 
that instead of work becoming more pleasurable, free time becomes more 
productive” (De Masi, 2000, p. 230). This caution resonates with the expe‑
riences of many creative professionals who struggle to fnd downtime in an 
always‑connected world where every moment holds the potential for produc‑
tive creativity. 
 
 
134 The Political Economy of the Creative and Cultural Industries 
When considered together, the theories of Bourdieu and de Certeau pro‑
vide a rich framework for understanding the complex dynamics of work in 
the CI in the contemporary world. Bourdieu’s concept of habitus helps ex‑
plain how creative professionals internalise and navigate the structures of 
their feld. At the same time, de Certeau’s notion of tactics illuminates how 
these individuals creatively adapt to and subvert systemic constraints. Augé’s 
non‑places refect the often transient and anonymous spaces (both physical 
and digital) in which creative work increasingly occurs. De Masi’s creative 
leisure concept captures the evolving nature of work‑life integration in the 
creative sector. Together, these theories paint a picture of the creative profes‑
sional as a navigator of fuid spaces and temporalities, constantly negotiat‑
ing between structure and agency, work and leisure, global connectivity and 
local identity. They highlight the tensions and opportunities inherent in a 
creative ecosystem characterised by deterritorialisation, temporal fexibility 
and the blurring of traditional boundaries, providing valuable insights for 
practitioners and policymakers in the CI. 
The following sessions will explore deeper Bourdieu and Certeau’s 
thoughts, more specifcally to understand the context and challenges sur ‑
rounding creative work in contemporaneity. 
4.2.1 The Space in Certeau 
The landscape of creative work is undergoing a profound transformation, 
driven by technological advancements and shifting social paradigms. To bet‑
ter grasp the implications of this revolution in the CI, one should look to the 
insightful work of French scholar Michel de Certeau. His theory of place, ar‑
ticulated in “The Practice of Everyday Life” (de Certeau, 1984), ofers a nu‑
anced framework for understanding the complex dynamics of creative work 
that can be updated to the digital age. 
At the heart of de Certeau’s theory lies a crucial distinction between 
“place” and “space.” In de Certeau’s view, a place is a stable, ordered loca‑
tion where elements are distributed in relationships of coexistence. It repre‑
sents the strategic vision of institutions and power structures – a fxed, proper 
location with its own rules and order. On the other hand, space is a place 
brought to life through human activity. As de Certeau puts it, it is a “prac‑
tised place” animated by the movements, actions and experiences of people 
within it (de Certeau, 1984). 
This distinction becomes particularly relevant when considering creative 
workplaces – studios, ofces, workshops – that can be seen as “places” in 
de Certeau’s sense. They are physical locations with predetermined purposes 
and structures. However, the CI revolution, accelerated by digital technolo‑
gies, has introduced new complexities to this concept. In the digital age, we 
see the emergence of new “places” for creative work: social media platforms, 
 
 
 
Creative and Cultural Industries and the Future (or Present) of Work 135 
digital marketplaces, collaborative software and virtual studios. These digi‑
tal environments might, at frst glance, seem to align with Augé’s concept of 
“non‑places”  –  transient, anonymous spaces lacking in history or identity 
(Augé, 1995). 
However, de Certeau’s theory shows how creative professionals transform 
these digital places into meaningful spaces through their practices. For in‑
stance, consider how artists and designers repurpose Instagram, originally a 
social networking platform, into a curated portfolio and networking tool. Or 
how writers use project management software like Trello, which is designed 
for task organisation and as a tool for story mapping and character develop‑
ment. These are examples of what de Certeau calls “tactics” – how individu‑
als navigate and repurpose the structures imposed upon them. 
4.2.1.1 Tactics and Strategies in the CI 
De Certeau’s concepts of “tactics” and “strategies” provide a valuable frame‑
work for understanding the power dynamics at play in the CI. In de Certeau’s 
view, strategies are the operative tools of institutions and structures of power. 
In the context of CI, think of the algorithms that govern visibility on social 
media platforms, the guidelines set by publishing houses or the terms of ser ‑
vice on freelance marketplaces. 
Conversely, tactics are individuals’ tools – the creative ways they navigate, 
subvert or repurpose these strategic structures (de Certeau, 1984, pp. 35– 
37). We see this in the rise of indie game developers who bypass traditional 
publishing routes or in the emergence of webcomics that challenge conven‑
tional distribution models. These tactical approaches allow creatives to carve 
out their spaces within the strategic places defned by larger institutions and 
platforms. As de Certeau notes, individuals “make use of the cracks that 
particular conjunctions open in the surveillance of the proprietary powers” 
(de Certeau, 1984, p. 37). 
4.2.1.2 The Home as a Contested Creative Space 
The COVID‑19 pandemic has brought another dimension of de Certeau’s 
theory into sharp focus: the home’s transformation into a creative workspace. 
Here, we see a vivid example of how a place (the house) is transformed into a 
space (a workspace) through the practices of the creative worker. This trans‑
formation has its challenges. The home, once a place of rest and personal 
life, now becomes a contested space where work and personal life coexist 
and sometimes confict. Creative professionals must employ new tactics to 
navigate this blurred boundary, creating makeshift studios in living rooms, 
establishing work routines in domestic settings, and fnding ways to separate 
professional and personal life within the exact physical location. 
136 The Political Economy of the Creative and Cultural Industries
4.2.1.3 Digital Flânerie and Creative Exploration
De Certeau’s concept of the urban walker, or flaneur (de Certeau, 1984, 
pp. 91–110), finds a new expression in the digital realm. Just as the flaneur 
wanders the city, discovering its hidden corners and unexpected connections, 
the creative professional in the digital age engages in digital flânerie. This 
might involve exploring niche online communities, diving into rabbit holes of 
research or stumbling upon unexpected inspirations through algorithmic rec‑
ommendations. This digital wandering becomes a crucial source of inspiration 
and innovation. It allows for serendipitous discoveries and connections that 
might not occur in more structured, traditional work environments. However, 
it also presents challenges – the risk of distraction, the potential for informa‑
tion overload and the need to cultivate intentionality in digital explorations.
BOX 4.2 IMPLICATIONS FOR INNOVATION AND THE 
FUTURE OF CREATIVE WORK
Understanding the CI revolution through de Certeau’s lens has significant im‑
plications for how we approach innovation and the future of creative work:
1 We need to recognise and value tactical practices’ innovative potential. This 
means creating environments that allow experimentation, appropriation 
and unexpected uses of tools and platforms.
2 The design of digital tools and workspaces should anticipate and encourage 
user modification. Platforms that allow creatives to shape their work envi‑
ronments can foster greater engagement and innovation.
3 Informal, user‑created spaces play a crucial role in fostering creativity and 
should be acknowledged and supported alongside more formal, institu‑
tional places of work.
4 There’s a need to cultivate a form of digital psychogeography – encourag‑
ing exploration and serendipitous discovery in digital environments to spark 
new ideas and connections.
5 Finding the right balance between providing structured “places” for work 
and allowing the freedom to create meaningful “spaces.” This balance will 
likely be different for each creative discipline and individual.
4.2.1.4 Challenges and A New Understanding of Creative Work
The power dynamics between platforms (which create “places”) and users 
(who make “spaces”) remain a critical issue. How do we ensure that the 
tactical practices of creatives are not co‑opted or exploited by strategic pow‑
ers? The digital divide poses another significant challenge. As creative work 
increasingly relies on digital tools and platforms, how can we address in‑
equalities in access to these resources? The blurring of work and personal 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
Creative and Cultural Industries and the Future (or Present) of Work 137 
spaces, while ofering fexibility, also risks leading to burnout and overwork. 
How can we protect the well‑being of creative workers in this new paradigm? 
Finally, there’s the question of authenticity in digital spaces. As our profes‑
sional and creative lives become increasingly mediated by digital platforms, 
how do we maintain genuine connections and experiences? 
By recognising the interplay between strategic places and tactical spaces in 
the CI, we can foster environments that support innovation, respect creative 
autonomy and navigate the challenges of our increasingly digital work land‑
scape. This perspective complements and enriches our earlier discussions on de 
Masi’s post‑industrial society and Augé’s non‑places, providing a more com‑
prehensive view of the evolving nature of creative work. As we move forward, 
the insights ofered by de Certeau’s work can guide us in creating a future of 
creative work that is more inclusive, dynamic and attuned to the complex ways 
individuals and communities navigate and shape their environments. The CI 
revolution, viewed through this lens, is not just about new technologies or 
changing business models  –  it’s about the ongoing, creative negotiation be‑
tween the structures we inhabit and the spaces we create within them. 
4.2.2 The individual: agency in Pierre Bourdieu 
Integrating Pierre Bourdieu’s sociological theories with Michel de Certeau’s con‑
cepts provides a more comprehensive framework for understanding individual 
agency within the CI revolution as we focus on transforming creative work in 
the digital age. This synthesis ofers valuable insights into how creative profes‑
sionals navigate, shape and are shaped by their evolving work environments. 
4.2.2.1 Bourdieu’s Key Concepts: Habitus, Field and Capital 
Pierre Bourdieu developed a set of interconnected concepts that help explain 
social behaviour and individual agency, having three core structures of analy‑
sis as follows: 
a Habitus: defned by the ingrained habits, skills and dispositions individu‑
als acquire through life experiences. It’s a system of embodied tenden‑
cies that shapes how people perceive and react to the world around them 
(Bourdieu, 1990, p. 53). 
b Field: is a structured social space with its own rules, schemes of domina‑
tion and legitimate opinions. In the context of CCIs, various creative dis‑
ciplines or industries might be considered distinct felds (Bourdieu, 1993, 
pp. 29–73). 
c Capital: Bourdieu expanded the concept of capital beyond economic re‑
sources to include cultural capital (knowledge, skills, education), social 
capital (networks, relationships) and symbolic capital (prestige, honour) 
(Bourdieu, 1986, pp. 241–258). 
 
 
138 The Political Economy of the Creative and Cultural Industries 
Pierre Bourdieu’s and Michel de Certeau’s theoretical frameworks ofer com‑
plementary lenses through which we can examine the complex interplay 
between individual agency and social structures in CCIs. While de Certeau 
focuses on the tactical ways individuals navigate and subvert structures, 
Bourdieu provides a framework for understanding how these individuals are 
themselves shaped by social structures. 
As seen in the previous item, De Certeau’s work, particularly in “The Prac‑
tice of Everyday Life,” emphasises the creative and often subversive ways 
individuals navigate and manipulate the structures imposed upon them. He 
introduces the concept of “tactics” as the tools of the individual, contrast‑
ing them with the “strategies” of institutions and power structures. In the 
context of CCIs, these ideas help us understand how creative professionals 
fnd innovative ways to use digital platforms and tools, often in ways not 
intended by their creators. On the other hand, Bourdieu’s notion of habitus 
can enrich De Certeau’s concept of transforming places into spaces through 
practice. Creative professionals bring their embedded dispositions to digital 
platforms, shaping how they interact with and repurpose these tools. For 
instance, a graphic designer’s habitus, formed through years of training and 
practice, infuences how they approach and utilise digital design tools, po‑
tentially in ways unintended by the platform creators. Consider a graphic 
designer who has spent years working with traditional media. When they 
transition to digital tools, their habitus – their entrenched sense of composi‑
tion, colour theory and artistic style – doesn’t disappear. Instead, it shapes 
their interaction with software like Adobe Photoshop or Illustrator. They 
might use layers to mimic their process with physical media or fnd innova‑
tive ways to blend digital efects with traditional artistic techniques. This 
intersection of habitus and digital tools can lead to unique creative styles and 
workfows that the software designers never anticipated. 
Moreover, the habitus of creative professionals is dynamic. As they engage 
with digital tools and spaces, their habitus evolves. A web designer who starts 
working with virtual reality (VR) platforms brings their existing understand‑
ing of user experience and interface design. However, as they become more 
familiar with VR’s three‑dimensional space, their habitus adapts, incorporat‑
ing new spatial and interactive elements into their design thinking. 
4.2.2.2 Fields and Digital Platforms 
Digital platforms can be understood as new felds in Bourdieu’s sense, with 
their own rules and forms of capital. De Certeau’s tactics become ways of 
navigating these felds. For example, social media platforms like Instagram 
become felds where creatives compete for attention and recognition, employ‑
ing tactics that align with their habitus and the platform’s implicit rules. Take 
Instagram as a case study. For a photographer, Instagram isn’t just a place 
to share images – it’s a feld with unique regulations and forms of capital. 
 
 
 
 
Creative and Cultural Industries and the Future (or Present) of Work 139 
Followers, likes and comments become forms of social and symbolic capital. 
The platform’s algorithm determines post visibility and becomes part of the 
feld’s structure that photographers must navigate. In this context, we can see 
de Certeau’s tactics at play. Photographers might use hashtags strategically, 
timing their posts for maximum engagement or creating Instagram‑specifc 
content like behind‑the‑scenes stories. These tactics are ways of navigating the 
feld and accumulating capital within it. The photographer’s habitus – their 
artistic style, their understanding of composition and light – interacts with 
these platform‑specifc tactics to create a unique Instagram presence. 
We can extend this analysis to other digital platforms. On YouTube, for 
instance, video creators navigate a diferent set of rules and forms of capital. 
Watch time becomes a crucial metric, leading to tactics like crafting engaging 
thumbnails and strategically structuring video content to maintain viewer 
interest. The feld of YouTube demands a diferent set of skills and tactics 
compared to Instagram, illustrating how creative professionals must adapt 
their approaches to diferent digital felds. 
4.2.2.3 Capital in the Digital Age 
Bourdieu’s forms of capital take on new dimensions in the digital CI land‑
scape. Cultural capital might include profciency with specifc software or 
understanding digital trends. Social capital extends to online networks and 
follower counts. De Certeau’s tactics can be seen as accumulating and lever‑
aging these forms of capital within digital felds. In the digital age, cultural 
capital for a creative professional might include profciency with industry‑
standard software like the Adobe Creative Suite, understanding UX/UI prin‑
ciples or knowledge of the latest digital art trends like NFTs or generative AI 
art. A 3D artist’s understanding of modelling software like Maya or Blender 
becomes a form of cultural capital that can be leveraged across various 
projects and industries. Social capital in the digital realm extends far beyond 
traditional professional networks. A game developer’s connections on plat‑
forms like GitHub or participation in online developer communities can be 
crucial forms of social capital. These connections can lead to collaborations, 
job opportunities or valuable knowledge sharing. 
Symbolic capital, too, takes on new forms. For a podcaster, for instance, 
symbolic capital might include verifed status on social media platforms, fea‑
tures in digital publications or high rankings in podcast directories. These 
markers of prestige and recognition can signifcantly impact a creator’s career 
trajectory. De Certeau’s tactics come into play as ways of accumulating and 
leveraging these forms of capital. A writer might use tactics like guest blog‑
ging, engaging in Twitter threads, or creating a newsletter to build their on‑
line presence and accumulate social and symbolic capital. When successful, 
these tactics can translate into economic capital through book deals, speak‑
ing engagements or paid subscriptions. 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
140 The Political Economy of the Creative and Cultural Industries 
4.2.2.4 The Individuals in the CCIs 
Creative professionals must navigate the tension between their established 
habitus and the rapidly changing digital landscape. This can lead to innova‑
tion as individuals unexpectedly apply their ingrained dispositions to new 
tools and platforms. For example, a traditional painter venturing into digital 
art brings a habitus formed through years of working with physical media. 
When they encounter digital painting software, their habitus infuences how 
they approach the digital canvas. They might seek out brushes that mimic the 
feel of their favourite physical brushes or develop techniques that recreate the 
textures they’re accustomed to in oil painting. 
This navigation can be challenging. A seasoned print journalist tran‑
sitioning to digital media might need help with online news’s fast‑paced, 
real‑time nature. Their habitus, formed in an environment that valued careful 
fact‑checking and longer‑form writing, must adapt to a feld that often priori‑
tises speed and brevity. This tension can lead to innovative approaches, such 
as developing new formats that combine traditional journalism’s depth with 
digital media’s immediacy. Navigating digital habitus isn’t just about adapt‑
ing to new tools; it’s also about bringing established skills and perspectives 
to bear on new challenges. Classically trained musicians exploring electronic 
music production bring a deep understanding of music theory and composi‑
tion. This habitus might lead them to create electronic music with complex 
harmonies or unusual time signatures, pushing the boundaries of the genre. 
4.2.2.5 Tactical Accumulation of Capital 
De Certeau’s tactics can be understood as ways of accumulating Bourdieu’s 
forms of capital. For instance, a musician might use social media guerrilla 
marketing tactics to build both social and symbolic capital, leveraging these 
to gain economic capital through increased sales or streaming revenue. 
Consider an independent musician in the age of streaming. They might em‑
ploy tactics like creating behind‑the‑scenes content for Instagram, engaging 
directly with fans on Twitter or collaborating with other artists for cross‑
promotion. Each of these tactics helps accumulate diferent forms of capital: 
• Social Capital: Building a loyal fanbase and network of fellow artists. 
• Cultural Capital: Demonstrating their musical skills and knowledge of the 
industry. 
• Symbolic Capital: Gaining recognition and prestige within their genre or 
local scene. 
These forms of capital can then be leveraged for economic gain. A solid social 
media following (social capital) might attract the attention of a record label. 
Collaborations with respected artists (symbolic capital) might lead to more 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
Creative and Cultural Industries and the Future (or Present) of Work 141 
prestigious gig opportunities. Deep knowledge of music production (cultural 
capital) might open doors to additional income through teaching or produc‑
ing for other artists. 
In the world of visual arts, an illustrator might use tactics like participat‑
ing in online art challenges, streaming their creative process on Twitch or 
creating educational content on YouTube. These tactics, in its turn, also ac‑
cumulate various forms of capital: 
• Cultural Capital: Showcasing their skills and knowledge. 
• Social Capital: Building a community of fellow artists and fans. 
• Symbolic Capital: Gaining recognition as an expert or infuencer in their 
feld. 
This accumulated capital can then translate into economic opportunities 
such as commissions, sponsorships or sales of online courses. 
4.2.2.6 Field Disruption and Creation 
The CCI revolution in the work feld is also about creating new ones. Indie 
game developers, for example, have created a new feld with its own rules 
and forms of capital, distinct from the traditional gaming industry. The rise 
of indie game development illustrates this concept perfectly. Platforms like 
Steam and itch.io have enabled independent developers to bypass traditional 
publishing routes, creating a new feld with unique characteristics: 
• Cultural Capital: Knowledge of game design principles, profciency with 
game engines like Unity or Unreal, and understanding of player psychology. 
• Social Capital: Connections within the indie dev community, relationships 
with streamers and gaming journalists. 
• Symbolic Capital: Awards from indie game festivals, positive reviews and 
cult followings for niche games. 
This new feld operates under rules diferent from those of the traditional 
AAA gaming industry. Success might be measured in sales fgures and critical 
acclaim, innovation in gameplay mechanics or impact on the broader gam‑
ing culture. The tactics for success in this feld – like engaging directly with 
players during development or leveraging crowdfunding platforms – are dis‑
tinct from those used in traditional game publishing. We can see similar feld 
creation in other areas of the CI revolution. For instance, virtual YouTubers 
(VTubers) have emerged as a new feld blending elements of animation, live 
streaming and performance art. This feld has its forms of capital (like the 
quality of the avatar design or the ability to maintain character during long 
streams) and its tactics for success (like collaborating with other VTubers or 
engaging in character‑appropriate social media interactions). 
 
 
 
142 The Political Economy of the Creative and Cultural Industries 
4.2.2.7 Habitus Evolution 
Their habitus evolves as creative professionals engage with new digital tools 
and spaces. This evolution adapts to new technologies and a complex interplay 
between established dispositions and new possibilities. Take, for example, a 
traditional flmmaker adapting to the world of virtual production, using tools 
like LED walls and real‑time rendering engines. Their existing habitus – their 
understanding of shot composition, lighting and storytelling – doesn’t disap‑
pear. Instead, it evolves to incorporate new possibilities. They might fnd that 
their instinct for practical efects translates well to blending physical sets with 
digital extensions. Their experience directing actors now extends to directing 
performance capture for digital characters. 
This evolution isn’t always smooth or linear. A graphic designer transi‑
tioning to UX/UI design brings a strong sense of visual aesthetics and com‑
position. As they engage with the feld of UX design, their habitus evolves to 
incorporate new priorities like user fow and interaction patterns. This might 
initially create tension – their instinct for visual fair might clash with UX 
best practices for simplicity and clarity. Over time, however, their evolving 
habitus might lead to innovative approaches that blend beautiful visuals with 
highly usable interfaces. The evolution of habitus can also lead to the devel‑
opment of entirely new creative practices. A traditional sculptor exploring 
3D printing doesn’t simply transfer their skills directly. Their habitus evolves 
as they grapple with the possibilities and limitations of the new medium. 
They might develop new approaches to form and structure only possible with 
3D printing, leading to a unique artistic style that blends traditional sculp‑
tural knowledge with digital fabrication techniques. 
Integrating Bourdieu’s and de Certeau’s theories provides a robust frame‑
work for understanding the complexities of creative work in the digital age. 
This perspective illuminates the dynamic interplay between individual agency 
(through de Certeau’s tactics) and social structures (through Bourdieu’s 
concepts of habitus, feld and capital). At the same time, this framework 
highlights the challenges creative professionals face in the digital age. The 
pressure to accumulate various forms of capital across multiple digital felds 
can lead to stress and potential burnout. The commodifcation of creative 
expression in pursuit of digital success raises questions about authenticity 
and artistic integrity. 
This integrated perspective can inform more nuanced approaches to crea‑
tive education, platform design and policy making. It suggests the need for 
training programmes that teach technical skills and foster adaptability and 
tactical thinking. It points towards designing more fexible and customisable 
creative tools that accommodate diverse habitus. It highlights the need for 
policies that consider the complex ecosystem of the digital creative economy, 
addressing issues of inequality, worker protection and cultural preservation. 
 
  
 
Creative and Cultural Industries and the Future (or Present) of Work 143 
4.3 The Present State of Creative Work and Creative 
Occupations 
The landscape of creative work is undergoing a profound transformation, 
driven by a convergence of technological innovation, economic restructuring, 
global interconnectedness and an increasing awareness of social and environ‑
mental responsibilities. This evolving paradigm presents both unprecedented 
opportunities and signifcant challenges for creative professionals. 
Central to this transformation is the digital revolution, which has rede‑
fned the essence of creative work. As Lev Manovich explores in “Software 
Takes Command” (2013), digital technologies have revolutionised many 
creative felds. Manovich elucidates how digital tools have fundamentally 
transformed design processes, enabling rapid prototyping and iteration at 
an unprecedented pace. Moreover, the proliferation of online platforms has 
established new channels for distributing creative content. In contrast, virtual 
and augmented reality advancements are pioneering frontiers for immersive 
experiences that challenge traditional boundaries between imagination and 
reality. 
Concurrent with these technological shifts is the rise of the gig economy, 
which is fundamentally restructuring employment patterns in the creative sec‑
tor. Many creative professionals now operate as freelancers or independent 
contractors. Platforms such as Upwork, Fiverr and Behance have emerged as 
crucial intermediaries, connecting creatives with clients globally. While this 
shift ofers unprecedented fexibility and autonomy, it also introduces signif‑
cant job security and benefts challenges. Guy Standing, in “The Precariat: 
The New Dangerous Class” (2011), ofers a critical perspective on this trend, 
highlighting the emergence of a new class of workers – the precariat – facing 
increased economic uncertainty. 
BOX 4.3 THE BITTERSWEET SYMPHONY OF SARA – A 
MANCHESTER GIG 
Disclaimer: it contains acidity and irony, and Sara is a 
piece of fction inspired by millions but not by any specifc 
person. 
Avant-garde Adventure in the Gig Economy Jungle 
Meet Sara a 28‑year‑old graphic designer whose day is less 9‑to‑5 and more 
24/7 creative chaos. Welcome to Manchester, where the rain is constant, and 
the Wi‑Fi signal is as unpredictable as a client’s feedback. 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
144 The Political Economy of the Creative and Cultural Industries 
8:00 AM: Sara wakes up in her shared Northern Quarter fat, immediately 
embodying Bourdieu’s notion that “the habitus is a spontaneity without con‑
sciousness or will.” They instinctively reaches for her phone, juggling messages 
from New York, Sao Paulo, and that one client in Kolkata who thinks time zones 
are a conspiracy theory. 
9:30 AM: Instead of a soul‑crushing commute, Sara takes a brisk walk to 
her co‑working space, headphones on, absorbing a podcast on UX design. 
De Masi would approve, nodding sagely that “creative leisure is the ability to 
work, study, and play at the same time.” Sara’s not multitasking; she’s 
‘multi‑existing.’ 
10:30 AM: In the co‑working space (or as Augé might call it, a “non‑place” 
where everyone is temporarily at home), Sara toggles between designing a 
website for a Berlin startup and a VR meeting with Tokyo. They not just a de‑

would say, Sara “making do,” turning a casual café meet‑up into a networking 
goldmine. They not just eating a sandwich; they consuming opportunity with 
a side of chai latte. 
3:00 PM: Back at her desk, Sara joins an online workshop on sustainable 
design. In Bourdieu’s terms, she’s accumulating cultural capital faster than their 
student loans accumulated interest. 
6:00 PM: As the traditional workday ends (whatever that means), Sara 
heads to a local tech meet‑up. It’s an Augé‑style “non‑place” where everyone’s 
a stranger, yet somehow, she lands a new gig. Networking or social alchemy? 
You decide.

your personal brand is your armour, and Sara polishing hers to a high sheen. 
As de Certeau might observe, she’s using the “tactics of the weak” to navigate 
the strategies of the digital marketplace. 
11:00 PM: As she sketches ideas for a personal project, Sara embodies De 
Masi’s prediction: “In the future, work will be less and less a place where you 
go and increasingly a thing that you do.” Her living room is her studio, her 
mind is her ofce, and creativity knows no time clock. 
Sara day is a masterclass in modern creative labour: 
• Work–life balance? More like work–life smoothie. 
• Continuous learning: because in this industry, if you snooze, you lose … 
clients. 
• Global connectivity: making the world her ofce, one Wi‑Fi hotspot at a 
time. 
• Networking: turning every conversation into a potential invoice. 
• Personal branding: because in the gig economy, you’re not just a person, 
you’re a walking portfolio.

In this brave new world of creative work, Sara is the poster child for what 
happens when you throw Bourdieu’s habitus, de Certeau’s tactics, Augé’s 
non‑places, and De Masi’s creative leisure into a blender and hit ‘smoothie.’ The 
result? A day that’s equal parts exhausting and exhilarating, a constant dance 
between freedom and precarity, creativity and commerce. 
As Sara fnally drifts of to sleep, her last thought isn’t of deadlines or design

In this 24/7 creative sweatshop, we call the gig economy, we’re not just

the illusion of freedom. 
This digital renaissance is unfolding globally, a phenomenon thoroughly 
examined by Terry Flew in “The Creative Industries: Culture and Policy” 
(2011). Flew explores how the global nature of CI has led to increased com‑
petition on an international scale while simultaneously fostering opportuni‑
ties for cross‑cultural collaboration. However, this globalisation of creativity 
also complicates the protection of intellectual property across international 
borders, presenting new challenges for creative professionals and policymak‑
ers alike. 
Amidst these economic and technological shifts, there is a growing em‑
phasis on sustainability and social responsibility within the CI. Kate Raw‑
orth’s “Doughnut Economics” (2017) provides a compelling framework for 
understanding how CI can contribute to a more sustainable and equitable 
economy. This paradigm shift manifests in various ways, from adopting sus‑
tainable practices in design and production to using creative skills in address‑
ing social and environmental issues. Furthermore, there is an increasing focus 
on promoting inclusive representation in innovative content and workforces, 
recognising the role of CI in shaping societal narratives and norms. 
This evolving landscape is immersed in challenges. The life of a creative 
professional in this new paradigm is often characterised by a high degree 
of uncertainty and volatility. Many creatives fnd themselves navigating a 
project‑based economy with irregular income streams. Intellectual property 
protection has become increasingly complex in the digital age, with the 
internet serving as both a platform for showcasing creative work and a 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
146 The Political Economy of the Creative and Cultural Industries 
potential site for unauthorised use and distribution. Moreover, the relent‑
less pace of technological change necessitates continuous skill updating, 
creating a form of ‘digital Darwinism’ where adaptability is critical to pro‑
fessional survival. Financial sustainability remains a perennial challenge, 
particularly for emerging creative ventures. Many creative endeavours need 
help to secure adequate funding, especially in their initial stages, under ‑
scoring the ongoing need for supportive ecosystems and investment in the 
creative sectors. 
In this context, it is evident that the world of creative work is simulta‑
neously exhilarating and precarious, flled with potential and challenges. It 
demands creativity in one’s craft, career management and professional devel‑
opment. The creative professionals of the future will need to be multifaceted: 
artists and entrepreneurs, innovators and adapters, global citizens and com‑
munity advocates. 
The complexity and scale of the issues facing the CI necessitate a robust 
and coordinated policy response. Policymakers play a pivotal role in shaping 
an environment where creative work can fourish amidst rapid technologi‑
cal change, economic uncertainty and global competition. Several key policy 
areas demand urgent attention: 
1 Labour laws and social security systems must be adapted to better protect 
and support freelance and gig economy workers in the creative sector, ad‑
dressing the precarity. 
2 Intellectual property regulations must evolve to protect creative works in 
the digital age while fostering innovation and fair use. 
3 Education and training policies should be designed to equip creative 
professionals with the diverse skill sets required in this rapidly changing 
landscape, including digital literacy, entrepreneurship and cross‑cultural 
competencies. 
4 Funding mechanisms and tax incentives should be established to support 
sustainable creative enterprises, particularly in their early stages. 
5 Policies promoting sustainability and social responsibility in the CI should 
be developed, aiming at a more equitable and sustainable economy. 
6 International cooperation and agreements are needed to address the chal‑
lenges of globalised CI, including fair competition and cross‑border intel‑
lectual property protection. 
The future vitality of the CI hinges on the development and implementation 
of forward‑thinking, adaptive policies that can keep pace with technological 
advancements and changing work patterns. By creating a supportive policy 
framework, the creative sector can not only survive but also produce real de‑
velopment, continuing to drive innovation, cultural expression and economic 
growth in the years to come.

The challenges are signifcant but possible with the right policy interven‑
tions. The creativity that defnes these industries, combined with thoughtful 
and responsive policymaking, can pave the way for a future where creative 
work can fourish in diverse forms, contributing to a more vibrant, sustain‑
able and equitable society. 
4.3.2 Challenges Facing Creative Workers 
Despite the growth and opportunities in CCI, creative workers face several 
challenges. Many creative jobs are project‑based or temporary, leading to in‑
come instability and precarious employment. Angela McRobbie’s “Be Crea‑
tive” critically examines the “passionate work” ethos in CI and its potential 
for exploitation (McRobbie, 2016). Protecting and monetising creative work 
in the digital age can be complex, raising intellectual property issues. The 
passion‑driven nature of creative work can lead to burnout and exploitation, 
making work‑life balance a signifcant concern. Rapid technological changes 
require continuous learning and adaptation, creating the risk of skill obsoles‑
cence. Additionally, many creative endeavours need help to secure adequate 
funding, especially in the early stages. 
This comparison highlights the dynamic nature of the CCIs, demonstrat‑
ing how they continually evolve in response to technological advancements 
and changing societal needs. It also underscores the importance of adaptabil‑
ity and lifelong learning for professionals in these felds as the required skills 
and job roles continue to transform rapidly. 
4.3.3 The Gig Economy and Precarious Work 
Despite their economic importance, creative occupations often characterise 
precarious employment conditions. Many creative professionals work on a 
freelance or project basis, needing more job security, benefts and protec‑
tions than traditional employment. The rise of the “gig economy,” facilitated 
by digital platforms and remote work, has further normalised short‑term, 
fexible work arrangements. While this fexibility can ofer creative work‑
ers greater autonomy and variety in their work, it can also contribute to 
income instability, stress and difculty in career planning. The COVID‑19 
pandemic has heightened these challenges, with many creative professionals 
losing work and income due to lockdowns and economic disruption. 
Despite their economic importance, creative occupations often character ‑
ise precarious employment conditions. Many creative professionals work on 
a freelance or project basis, needing more job security, benefts and protec‑
tions than traditional employment. The rise of the “gig economy,” facilitated 
by digital platforms and remote work, has further normalised short‑term, 
fexible work arrangements. While this fexibility can ofer creative workers 
148 The Political Economy of the Creative and Cultural Industries 
 
 
greater autonomy and variety in their work, it can also contribute to income 
instability, stress and difculty in career planning. The COVID‑19 pandemic 
has heightened these challenges, with many creative professionals losing 
work and income due to lockdowns and economic disruption. 
For instance, in the flm industry, many professionals, such as cinematog‑
raphers, sound designers and costume artists, work on a project‑by‑project 
basis. Between projects, these individuals often face periods of unemploy‑
ment or underemployment. A study by the UK Film and TV Charity found 
that 93% of flm and TV freelancers experienced periods without work in 
2020, with an average of 100 days lost per person due to the pandemic. 
Similarly, in the music sector, touring musicians and sound engineers saw 
their livelihoods disappear overnight as live events were cancelled. The Music 
Workers Alliance reported that 71% of music workers lost more than 75% 
of their income in 2020, highlighting the vulnerability of creative profession‑
als to economic shocks. 
In the visual arts sector, gallery closures and the cancellation of art fairs 
have profoundly impacted artists and art dealers. The Art Basel and UBS 
Global Art Market Report 2021 revealed that gallery sales decreased by 36% 
on average in 2020, with smaller galleries experiencing even steeper declines. 
Graphic designers and illustrators, often freelancers, have also faced chal‑
lenges. A survey by the Association of Illustrators found that 57% of re‑
spondents reported a decrease in commissions during the pandemic, forcing 
many to seek alternative sources of income or rely on government support 
schemes. These examples underscore the precarious nature of creative work 
and the urgent need for policies that provide more excellent stability and 
protection for professionals in the CCIs. 
4.3.4 Diversity, Equity and Inclusion 
The CCI has long faced challenges around diversity, equity and inclusion. 
Despite some progress, many creative felds remain dominated by privileged 
groups, with barriers to entry and advancement for women, people of colour, 
disabled individuals and other marginalised communities. These inequities 
limit opportunities for individual creators and constrain the range of cul‑
tural expression and representation. As audiences become more diverse and 
demand more inclusive storytelling, the CCIs are under increasing pressure 
to address systemic biases and create more equitable pathways for creative 
talent. 
In the vibrant world of CCIs, where imagination supposedly knows no 
bounds, a paradox emerges from the shadows, reaching far more profound 
than many care to admit. These realms of innovation and expression, cele‑
brated for their ability to push boundaries and challenge norms, grapple with 
a persistent, almost intractable challenge: the stubborn spectre of inequality. 
Creative and Cultural Industries and the Future (or Present) of Work 149 
 
How can industries that pride themselves on creativity be so unimaginative 
in addressing their own systemic biases? 
Picture a grand theatre where not all players have equal access to the stage, 
where the same hands have written the script for generations. This is the 
uncomfortable reality of the CCIs today, where despite the narrative of open‑
ness and innovation, the spotlight often shines brightest on a privileged few, 
casting long shadows over vast pools of untapped talent. Women, people of 
black background, global majorities, individuals with disabilities and mem‑
bers of the LGBTQ+ community frequently fnd their talents underutilised, 
raising the question: in an industry that thrives on diverse perspectives, how 
can such a situation persist? 
The numbers tell a story of glacial progress intertwined with stubbornly 
persisting disparities, a narrative of two steps forward and one step back. 
In the United Kingdom, women make up nearly half of the creative work‑
force. Yet they remain conspicuously absent from the director’s chair, their 
ascent to leadership positions blocked by an invisible yet impenetrable glass 
ceiling. Across the pond, in the glittering world of Hollywood, female direc‑
tors helmed a mere 18% of top‑grossing flms in 2022. It is a step forward, 
certainly, but still a far cry from equality. In an era of #MeToo and height‑
ened awareness, one must wonder why gender parity in the CCIs remains an 
elusive dream. 
The palette of racial and ethnic diversity in the CCIs remains frustrat‑
ingly muted, a monochrome in an age of technicolour. In Britain, the creative 
canvas is noticeably bland, with only 11% of creative jobs held by those 
from minority ethnic backgrounds. The American scene isn’t much brighter, 
having just over a quarter of creative industry jobs, their representation as 
faded as an old flm reel when compared to their 42% share of the overall 
workforce. This disparity raises the question: Are the CCIs truly refecting 
the societies they represent, or are they merely perpetuating outdated power 
structures? 
However, the story of diversity in the CCIs isn’t just about gender and 
race. It’s a complex scene woven with threads of socioeconomic background, 
disability and sexual orientation, each strand revealing another layer of ex‑
clusion. Those from lower socioeconomic backgrounds fnd the ladder to 
creative success missing a few crucial rungs. At the same time, individuals 
with disabilities make up only 14% of the United Kingdom’s creative work‑
force, their talents an untapped reservoir in a drought of representation. In 
industries that pride themselves on thinking outside the box, one might ask 
why the inclusion box is still so small. 
The consequences of this lack of diversity are far‑reaching, like ripples in 
a pond that grow into waves of missed opportunities and stifed innovation. 
The dominance of certain voices leads to a narrower range of stories, a lim‑
ited palette of cultural expression that fails to capture the rich diversity of 
150 The Political Economy of the Creative and Cultural Industries 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
human experience. It’s not just a matter of fairness; it’s an economic impera‑
tive. McKinsey’s report reveals a compelling vision: addressing racial equity 
in the US CI could unleash a torrent of creativity worth $100 billion in an‑
nual revenues. Yet, one must critically ask, why does it take the promise of 
proft to spur action on what should be a fundamental moral imperative? 
As global audiences evolve, becoming as diverse as a box of crayons, they 
hunger for stories refecting their experiences. The CCIs fnd themselves at a 
crossroads, facing increasing pressure to address systemic biases and forge 
more equitable paths for creative talent. This challenge goes beyond mere 
representation; it’s about fostering environments where diverse voices can 
truly fourish. But are the industries ready to relinquish their grip on power 
and truly embrace change, or will they continue to ofer tokenism instead of 
transformation? 
Yet, amidst these challenges, green shoots of progress are emerging, 
though one might argue they’re more like fragile seedlings needing nurtur ‑
ing rather than robust plants. Industry giants are making grand pledges, like 
characters in a Shakespearean drama vowing to change their ways. But how 
many of these pledges will translate into meaningful action, and how many 
will remain mere performative gestures? The BBC’s 50:50 Project stands as a 
beacon, striving to balance the scales of gender representation. Programmes 
like Creative Access in the United Kingdom are opening doors, ofering in‑
ternships and training to those who’ve long found the CI a locked fortress. 
But are these initiatives enough to dismantle centuries of systemic exclusion, 
or are they merely scratching the surface of a much deeper problem? 
So far, the path to accurate equity in the CCIs is as twisted as the plot 
of a mystery novel, with far more dead ends than breakthrough moments. 
Systemic biases lurk like villains in the shadows, infuencing hiring, pro‑
motion, and content selection with a subtlety that makes them even more 
pernicious. With its freelance gigs and contract roles, the precarious nature 
of creative work often hits marginalised groups the hardest, turning their 
career paths into tightrope walks without a safety net. In this gig economy, 
are we witnessing the democratisation of opportunity or the casualisation 
of exploitation? Some actions are essential to solve this puzzle towards 
democratisation. 
The accessibility of education, a fundamental equaliser in society, re‑
mains a signifcant challenge due to prohibitive costs and limited avail‑
ability. This situation presents a paradox: how can industries that rely on 
innovation and fresh perspectives continue to draw from such a restricted 
talent pool? For individuals facing multiple forms of marginalisation, the 
CI can appear as an intricate maze with no apparent exit, each turn pre‑
senting a new obstacle. In this complex landscape of intersectionality, the 
policies of CCIs must be adequately equipped to comprehend and address 
these educational barriers.

The transformation of gender, race and racial representation in CCIs ne‑

that champions change. However, this raises the question of whether those 
in positions of power will voluntarily relinquish their privileged status or if 
change will only be catalysed by disruptive forces. Furthermore, there is a 
pressing need for a revolution in narrative representation that elevates di‑

across various media platforms. 
The industry must adopt a long‑term approach to talent development, 
nurturing diverse creatives from their initial creative impulses to established 
careers. However, in an era dominated by short‑term fnancial objectives, 
whether the CCIs will possess the necessary patience and vision to cultivate 
this diverse talent pool remains to be seen. 
Structural reforms are essential, addressing various issues, from pay dis‑

must be implemented to monitor progress and ensure that rhetoric translates 
into substantive action. The question arises as to who will hold those in 
power accountable and what consequences will be imposed for failing to 
fulfl commitments to inclusivity. 
As the CCIs face an evolving future, it is evident that addressing these 
challenges is not merely a moral imperative but a business necessity. In an 
increasingly diverse global landscape, those CI that genuinely embrace diver‑
sity, equity and inclusion will likely achieve the most tremendous success, 
producing work that resonates with a broad spectrum of global audiences. 
The critical question remains whether they will rise to this challenge or con‑
tinue to present a superfcial version of diversity that creates an illusion of 
change without efecting meaningful transformation. 
The trajectory of the CCIs is yet to be determined. Whether they will em‑
brace a future as diverse and vibrant as the world they aim to refect or 
adhere to familiar paradigms remains to be seen. The ultimate test will be 
whether they dare to empower those who have historically been denied the 
opportunity to shape their narratives. While the outcome is uncertain, a 
global audience will closely observe and critique this ongoing evolution of 
the CCIs, potentially calling for signifcant reforms in this infuential sector. 
4.3.5 Present and Future: The Multidimensional Landscape 
In the ever‑shifting landscape of the CCIs, a wind of change blows with in‑
creasing force, reshaping the very foundations of creative work. Picture, if 
you will, a grand puzzle with intricate patterns. This is the world of the crea‑
tive professional today, where technology, entrepreneurship and collabora‑
tion intertwine to form new and exciting designs. 
152 The Political Economy of the Creative and Cultural Industries 
 
At the heart of this transformation lies a technological revolution, a digi‑
tal big bang expanding the creative universe at a dizzying pace. From the 
ethereal realms of VR to the blockchain‑secured vaults of NFTs, emerging 
technologies are not just opening doors – they’re blowing the roof of tra‑
ditional creative paradigms. AI‑assisted design tools now dance alongside 
human imagination, their algorithmic brushstrokes blending seamlessly with 
the artist’s vision. As these digital marvels become as commonplace as pencils 
and paintbrushes, they’re redefning how we create and what it means to be 
a creator. 
But to survive in the new world, simply wielding these technological 
wands isn’t enough. Today’s creative professionals must become technologi‑
cal polyglots, fuent in the languages of code, data and digital collaboration. 
The future belongs to those who can pirouette between the tangible and the 
virtual, their creativity unbound by the limitations of any single medium. Yet, 
as AI and automation fex their silicon muscles, a question echoes through 
studio spaces and creative hubs: in this dance between human and machine, 
who will lead and follow? The spectre of job displacement looms, a shadow 
puppet on the wall of progress. 
As we pan out from this technological close‑up, we fnd ourselves in the 
bustling marketplace of the “creator economy.” Here, independent artists 
and innovators are breaking free from the constraints of traditional gate‑
keepers, building their own stages in the digital amphitheatre. Platforms like 
Patreon, Substack and OnlyFans have become the new patrons of the arts, 
enabling creators to monetise their passions directly through the support of 
their admirers. In this democratic creative utopia, niche is the new main‑
stream, and every creator has the potential to carve out their own kingdom. 
But to thrive in this new landscape, creative professionals must wear the 
entrepreneur’s hat, becoming as adept at balancing books as they are at bal‑
ancing compositions. The successful creator of tomorrow is part artist, part 
marketer, part CEO – a Renaissance individual for the digital age. They must 
navigate the choppy waters of personal branding, fnancial management and 
networking with the skills they apply to their craft. As the lines between art 
and commerce blur, educational institutions fnd themselves racing to keep 
pace, their curricula evolving to nurture creative talent and business acumen. 
Yet, the future of creative work is more than a solitary journey. The need 
for collaborative, interdisciplinary approaches has never been greater in a 
world grappling with challenges as vast and varied as climate change and so‑
cial inequality. Imagine a symphony where scientists, engineers, policymak‑
ers and artists play their parts in harmony, their diverse expertise combining 
to compose solutions to our most pressing global issues. The creative profes‑
sionals leading this orchestra can traverse disciplinary boundaries as easily as 
a melody fows between instruments.

In this rapidly evolving landscape, adaptability isn’t just an asset – it’s a 
survival skill. The creative professional of the future is a chameleon, continu‑

environment. Lifelong learning becomes not just a noble aspiration, but a 
professional necessity. The boundaries between creative disciplines, once as 
rigid as canvas stretchers, now fex and bend, giving rise to hybrid roles that 
defy traditional categorisation. To navigate this fuid terrain, creatives must 
cultivate not just technical prowess, but the cognitive and interpersonal skills 
to thrive amidst complexity and change. 
As the CCIs continue to grow in importance, nurturing this sector be‑

investing in the skills, infrastructure and support systems needed for creative 
work to fourish, we’re not just fostering economic growth – we’re cultivat‑
