CREATIVE AND CULTURAL INDUSTRIES 
AND INNOVATION 
In the summer of 1857, Manchester stood as a testament to the transforma‑
tive power of innovation. Known as “Cottonopolis,” the city’s factories 
produced textiles at an unprecedented rate, embodying the essence of the 
Industrial Revolution. Yet, amidst this industrial fervour, Manchester sought 
to prove its cultural credentials by hosting the Art Treasures Exhibition – the 
most signifcant art show ever assembled. 
Held in a purpose‑built iron and glass structure in Old Traford, the exhi‑
bition showcased over 16,000 works of art, including paintings, sculptures 
and decorative pieces. For 142 days, it drew over 1.3 million visitors, a testa‑
ment to the city’s ambition and the public’s appetite for art. This juxtaposi‑
tion of industrial ethos and artistic aspiration set the stage for a profound 
critique that would resonate through the ages. 
In this context, John Ruskin, the eminent art critic and social thinker, was 
invited to deliver a keynote lecture at the Manchester Athenaeum. On 10 and 
13 July 1857, Ruskin presented his seminal work, “The Political Economy 
of Art.” Manchester’s industrial elite was gathered inside the venue, adorned 
in silks and jewels. Outside, in stark contrast, factory workers gathered in 
smoke‑flled streets, their faces tired by long hours and poor conditions. Rus‑
kin, known for his distaste for industrialisation, saw Manchester as the epi‑
centre of much of the wrong with modern society. He used this platform to 
challenge the very foundations of the city’s alleged prosperity. His lecture was 
a passionate argument against the commodifcation of art and the dehuman‑
ising efects of industrial capitalism. He proclaimed: 
We have warped the word “economy” in our English language into a 
meaning that it has no business to bear. In our use of it, it constantly 
 
    
  
  
 
 
 
 
156 The Political Economy of the Creative and Cultural Industries 
signifes merely sparing or saving; economy of money means saving 
money—economy of time, sparing time, and so on. But that is a wholly 
barbarous use of the word—barbarous in a double sense, for it is not Eng‑
lish, and it is bad Greek; barbarous in a treble sense, for it is not English, 
it is bad Greek, and it is worse sense. Economy no more means saving 
money than it means spending money. It means, the administration of a 
house; its stewardship; spending or saving, that is, whether money or time, 
or anything else, to the best possible advantage. In the simplest and clear‑
est defnition of it, economy, whether public or private, means the wise 
management of labour; and it means this mainly in three senses: namely, 
frst, applying your labour rationally; secondly, preserving its produce 
carefully; lastly, distributing its produce seasonably. 
— John Ruskin, “The Roots of Honour,” from Unto This Last 
and Other Essays on Political Economy (1860/1862) 
Ruskin’s critique extended beyond art to encompass the broader impacts of 
industrialisation. He argued that the pursuit of wealth at the expense of hu‑
man well‑being and the environment was fundamentally fawed: 
It is not, in reality, the labour that is divided; but the men: Divided into 
mere segments of men – broken into small fragments and crumbs of life; 
so that all the little piece of intelligence that is left in a man is not enough 
to make a pin, or a nail, but exhausts itself in making a point of a pin or 
the head of a nail. 
(Ruskin, 1857) 
Ruskin’s lectures in Manchester marked a turning point in his career and the 
broader discourse on art, industry and society. His ideas would be further 
developed in his seminal work “Unto This Last” (1860), which infuenced 
social reformers (such as the founders of the Labour Party and Mahatma 
Gandhi). 
As we explore creative and cultural industries (CCIs) and innovation in 
this chapter, Ruskin’s words from over 160 years ago provide a powerful 
starting point. They remind us of the ongoing tension between economic 
progress and human well‑being and the potential of creativity to bridge this 
divide. In our contemporary context, where technology and innovation are 
rapidly reshaping the landscape of cultural production and consumption, 
Ruskin’s challenge remains relevant: How can we harness the power of in‑
novation in CCIs to enrich the human experience rather than diminish it? 
This chapter will trace the evolution of innovation through successive in‑
dustrial revolutions, examining how each wave of technological change has 
transformed creative work. It will explore Schumpeter’s infuential theories on 
innovation, particularly his concept of “creative destruction,” and apply these 
 
 
 
 
 
Creative and Cultural Industries and Innovation 157 
ideas to the dynamic world of CCIs. Through examples spanning digital media, 
fashion, music, and more, we will investigate how CCIs are at the forefront of 
innovation, constantly reshaping cultural production and consumption. 
There will be a look around how the new technologies are blurring the 
lines between creator and consumer, how digital platforms democratise ac‑
cess to creative content while raising new questions about value and owner ‑
ship, and how sustainable and ethical concerns drive innovation in creative 
industries. Throughout, we will consider the double‑edged nature of innova‑
tion in CCIs – its potential to unleash creativity and connect people across 
the globe and its capacity to disrupt traditional ways of working and exac‑
erbate inequalities. Readers are invited to consider Ruskin’s critique as these 
topics are reached. In exploring the transformative power of innovation in 
creative sectors, we must continually ask ourselves: What is the value of our 
creations? How can we ensure that our pursuit of progress doesn’t come at 
the cost of our humanity? How can we use innovation tools to create a more 
just, sustainable and beautiful world? 
By raising these questions, we can better understand the dynamic nature 
of CCIs in the 21st century and their potential to drive positive change in our 
society. The contrast between the luxury of the Art Treasures Exhibition and 
the harsh realities of industrial life in 1857 Manchester embodied the very 
contradictions Ruskin sought to address. Today, as we navigate the oppor ‑
tunities and challenges of the digital age, we face similar contradictions and 
ethical dilemmas. By examining the past and present of innovation in CCIs, 
this chapter aims to provide insights that can help shape a more equitable 
and enriching creative future. 
5.1 An Overview of the Industrial Revolutions 
5.1.1 The First: Steam and Coal 
In the late 18th century, a profound transformation swept across Britain, for‑
ever transforming the fabric of society and the nature of human productivity. 
This seismic shift, which we now call the First Industrial Revolution, was not 
merely a series of technological innovations but a complex interplay of eco‑
nomic, social and cultural forces that would reshape the world. At the heart 
of this revolution lay a paradox: unprecedented economic growth coupled 
with wrenching social upheaval. As steam engines roared to life and factories 
threw smoke into once‑clear skies, a new world was born – one of immense 
productive capacity and human cost. 
Consider the humble steam engine, perhaps the most iconic innovation 
of the era. James Watt’s improvements to this technology in the 1760s and 
1770s unleashed a cascade of changes that would ripple through every aspect 
of society (Nuvolari et al., 2011). Steam power drove pumps that drained 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
158 The Political Economy of the Creative and Cultural Industries 
mines, allowing for deeper excavation of coal  –  the lifeblood of this new 
industrial age. It powered the machines that spun cotton into thread and 
wove cloth at speeds unimaginable to earlier generations. And it would soon 
propel ships across oceans and locomotives across continents, shrinking dis‑
tances and knitting the world ever closer together. 
But the steam engine was more than just a machine; it symbolised a new 
economic order. As historian E.A. Wrigley (2010) argues, the shift from an or‑
ganic economy, limited by the constraints of plant photosynthesis, to a min‑
eral‑based economy powered by seemingly limitless coal reserves unlocked 
sustained economic growth. This transition allowed for a level of productivity 
that would have been inconceivable in earlier ages. Yet, as the gears of indus‑
try turned ever faster, they ground against the established rhythms of rural 
life. Nowhere was this tension more evident than in textiles, where a series 
of innovations – from James Hargreaves’ Spinning Jenny to Edmund Cart‑
wright’s Power Loom – revolutionised production (Allen, 2009). These ma‑
chines, marvels of human ingenuity, could produce cloth at a fraction of the 
time and cost of traditional methods. But they also turned obsolete the skills 
of hand spinners and weavers, many of whom were displaced and desperate. 
It was in this context of rapid change and social dislocation that the mod‑
ern industrial city was born. And no city more than Manchester embodied 
the promise and peril of this new age more than Manchester. Once a modest 
market town, Manchester exploded into a metropolis, its population swell‑
ing from around 25,000 in 1772 to 367,000 by 1851 (Williamson, 1990). 
The city became a reference of the new industrial order, and its labyrinths 
of factories were a testament to the transformative power of this revolution. 
Manchester’s cotton mills were marvels of productivity, their spindles and 
looms churning vast quantities of cloth that would clothe the world. But 
they were also sites of exploitation and sufering. Children as young as nine 
worked long hours in dangerous conditions (Humphries, 2010), and women, 
drawn into factory work in unprecedented numbers, faced harsh conditions 
and societal disapproval (Berg, 1993). 
The contrast between the wealth generated by Manchester’s industries and 
the squalor in which many of its workers lived was stark. Friedrich Engels, 
who would later collaborate with Karl Marx, was so struck by the conditions 
he observed in Manchester that he penned his seminal work, “The Condition 
of the Working Class in England” (Engels, 1845). His vivid descriptions of 
overcrowded slums, polluted air and widespread disease painted a damn‑
ing picture of industrial capitalism’s human cost. Yet, viewing the Industrial 
Revolution solely through the lens of exploitation and misery would be a 
mistake. For many, the new industrial cities ofered opportunities unimagi‑
nable in the rigid hierarchies of rural life. A new middle class of industrialists, 
managers and professionals emerged, their success stories fuelling dreams of 
social mobility (Gunn, 2000). And even among the working class, new forms 
of solidarity and political consciousness began to take shape. 
 
 
Creative and Cultural Industries and Innovation 159 
For all its harshness, the factory system brought workers together in un‑
precedented numbers, creating the conditions for collective action. Though 
initially outlawed, trade unions began to form, fghting for better wages and 
working conditions (Thompson, 1963). Political movements like Chart‑
ism gained strength, demanding democratic reforms and universal sufrage 
(Royle, 1996). The concentration of people and problems in cities like Man‑
chester sparked new thinking about public health, urban planning and social 
reform. 
Innovation, too, was not solely the province of lone inventors or wealthy 
industrialists. The historian Maxine Berg (1991) has argued persuasively for 
the importance of incremental innovations and adaptations workers make 
in driving productivity gains. The factory foor became a site of constant 
experimentation and improvement as workers and managers sought to re‑
fne processes and increase efciency. This culture of innovation extended 
beyond the factory walls. Manchester became known not just for its industry 
but for its intellectual ferment. The Manchester Literary and Philosophical 
Society, founded in 1781, became a forum for exchanging ideas, where in‑
dustrialists rubbed shoulders with scientists and social reformers (Thackray, 
1974). In this venture of industry and intellect, John Dalton developed his 
atomic theory, a breakthrough that would revolutionise chemistry and lay 
the groundwork for future scientifc advances (Smith, 1856). 
Though not fully understood at the time, the environmental consequences 
of this great transformation were profound. The shift to a coal‑based econ‑
omy began humanity’s large‑scale impact on the global climate (McNeill, 
2000). The polluted air and water of industrial cities like Manchester were 
a starting point of the broader environmental challenges that would come 
to the fore in later centuries. As we refect on the First Industrial Revolu‑
tion from our vantage point in the 21st century, we are struck by its tre‑
mendous achievements and painful costs. It was a period of unprecedented 
economic growth and technological innovation that laid the foundations for 
the modern world. But it was also a time of great social upheaval, exploita‑
tion and environmental degradation. The tensions and contradictions of this 
era – between economic progress and social justice, technological innovation 
and human welfare, urban growth and quality of life – continue to resonate 
today. As of today, the world is embracing a period of rapid technological 
change and economic transformation, and the lessons of the First Industrial 
Revolution remain profoundly relevant. 
The First Industrial Revolution ushered in an era of unprecedented pro‑
ductivity growth, fundamentally altering society’s economic landscape and 
social fabric. This transformation was not merely a matter of technological 
change but a complex interplay of economic forces, social shifts and cultural 
adaptations. 
Some aspects of the First Industrial Revolution can be summarised as 
follows:

SYNTHESIS 
Productivity Leap 
• Dramatic increase in productivity across various sectors, especially textiles

• Steam engines played a crucial role, allowing for mechanisation and geo‑

• Marked the beginning of sustained economic growth (GDP per capita in

• Structural changes: agricultural workforce declined; industrial sector

• Financial innovations: rise of joint‑stock companies and stock exchanges

banking 
Social Upheaval 
• Mass migration from rural areas to cities led to rapid, often chaotic 
urbanisation 
• New social classes emerged: industrial working class and expanded middle 
class 
• Harsh working conditions in factories sparked social unrest and labour 
movements 
• Trade unions and political movements like Chartism gained strength 
Cultural and Intellectual Impact 
• Transformed conceptions of work, time and society 
• Spurred development of modern economics (Smith, Ricardo, Marx) 
• Public health concerns led to urban infrastructure improvements 
• New rhythms of industrial work reshaped social norms and values 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
Creative and Cultural Industries and Innovation 161 
5.1.2 The Second: Steel and Power 
The Second Industrial Revolution, which lasted roughly from the 1870s to the 
eve of World War I, would be no mere sequel to its predecessor but a trans‑
formation that would reshape the world in more profound and far‑reaching 
ways. If the First Industrial Revolution was characterised by iron, coal and 
steam, its successor was defned by steel, electricity and chemistry. These 
new forces of production would not only accelerate the pace of change but 
fundamentally alter the very fabric of society, laying the groundwork for the 
modern world we inhabit today. 
At the heart of this transformation was the Bessemer process, a method 
for mass‑producing steel invented by Henry Bessemer in 1856. This seem‑
ingly technical and obscure innovation would be the key to a new age of 
construction and engineering. Suddenly, steel – stronger, lighter and cheaper 
than ever– became the material of choice for everything from railroads to 
skyscrapers (Misa, 1995). The skylines of cities like Chicago and New York 
began to reach for the heavens, a tangible symbol of the ambition and pos‑
sibility of the age. But it was the harnessing of electricity that genuinely set 
this revolution apart. The invention of the dynamo and the perfection of 
the electric motor opened entirely new vistas of productivity and possibility. 
No longer were factories bound to the rhythms of daylight or the proximity 
of water sources. Electric lighting allowed for round‑the‑clock production, 
while electric motors provided fexibility and precision of power that steam 
engines could never match (Hughes, 1983). 
The impact on productivity was staggering. In the United States, for in‑
stance, manufacturing output grew at an average annual rate of 4.3% be‑
tween 1880 and 1900, while the number of workers increased by only 2.4% 
annually (Gordon, 2016). This surge in productivity was not just a matter 
of working harder or longer but of fundamentally reimagining the nature of 
work itself. Enter Frederick Winslow Taylor, the controversial father of scien‑
tifc management. While often criticised for their dehumanising aspects, Tay‑
lor’s time‑and‑motion studies represented a new way of thinking about work 
as something that could be systematically analysed and optimised (Kanigel, 
1997). This approach, combined with innovations like the assembly line pio‑
neered by Ransom Olds and perfected by Henry Ford, would transform not 
just how goods were produced but the very nature of work itself. 
Yet, as with the First Industrial Revolution, these gains in productivity and 
efciency came at a human cost. The pace and intensity of work in these new 
factories often increased stress and physical strain on workers. The division 
of labour, while increasing efciency, also led to the deskilling of many jobs, 
turning skilled craftsmen into interchangeable machine operators (Braverman, 
1974). The tension between economic progress and human welfare, evident 
in the mills of Manchester, had not disappeared but had taken on new forms. 
 162 The Political Economy of the Creative and Cultural Industries 
The Second Industrial Revolution also saw the rise of a new middle class, 
fuelled by the explosion of white‑collar jobs in management, engineering 
and administration. The growth of large corporations created new career 
paths and opportunities for advancement that would have been unthinkable 
a generation earlier (Chandler, 1977). Moreover, the fruits of this new in‑
dustrial order began to touch the lives of ordinary people in ways both large 
and small. The advent of mass production made a wide range of consumer 
goods – from bicycles to sewing machines – afordable to a broader swath 
of society. The development of canned foods and refrigeration transformed 
diets and food safety. And new forms of transportation and communica‑
tion, from the automobile to the telephone, began to shrink distances and 
connect people in unprecedented ways (Smil, 2005). Yet the most profound 
impact of the Second Industrial Revolution was not in the realm of produc‑
tion at all but in the sphere of knowledge and innovation. The rise of in‑
dustrial research laboratories, pioneered by companies like General Electric 
and DuPont, marked the beginning of the systematic application of science 
to industry (Reich, 1985). This marriage of scientifc inquiry and industrial 
production would prove to be one of the most fruitful partnerships in hu‑
man history, giving birth to a seemingly endless stream of new materials, 
processes and technologies. The chemical industry exemplifed this new ap‑
proach. The development of synthetic dyes, beginning with William Perkin’s 
accidental discovery of mauveine in 1856, sparked a revolution in industry 
and science. German companies like BASF and Bayer, recognising the poten‑
tial of this new feld, invested heavily in research and development, creating 
a model of industrial innovation that would be emulated around the world 
(Murmann, 2003). 
However, as the frontiers of knowledge expanded, so too did the scale 
and potential consequences of human activity. The same chemical processes 
that produced vibrant dyes and life‑saving medicines also gave birth to ex‑
plosives and poison gases. While transformative, the ability to generate and 
transmit electricity on a massive scale also began to reveal the environmental 
costs of unchecked industrial development. The seeds of the 20th century’s 
triumphs and tragedies were sown in the laboratories and factories of this 
second industrial age. The global impact of these changes was profound and 
often wrenching. As the new industrial powers of Germany and the United 
States began to challenge Britain’s economic supremacy, the balance of global 
economic power began to shift. The demand for raw materials to feed the 
voracious appetite of these new industries intensifed colonial exploitation 
and reshaped global trade patterns. The world was becoming more intercon‑
nected, competitive and confictual (Beckert, 2014). 
Yet amidst this tumult, new ideas and movements were taking shape that 
would profoundly infuence the 20th century. Building on the foundations 
of the First Industrial Revolution, the labour movement gained strength and 
Creative and Cultural Industries and Innovation 163 
 
 
sophistication. The introduction of scientifc management techniques para‑
doxically gave workers new tools to analyse and argue for the value of their 
labour (Montgomery, 1979). While rooted in earlier struggles, the women’s 
sufrage movement gained momentum as women entered the workforce in 
more signifcant numbers and new roles. 
As we refect on the Second Industrial Revolution from our vantage point 
in the 21st century, we are struck by its uncanny resonance with our time. The 
debates over automation and job displacement, the concerns about the pace 
of technological change and its impact on society, and the excitement and 
anxiety about the transformative potential of new technologies – all fnd their 
echoes in this earlier period of rapid change and dislocation. Indeed, many 
of the institutions and assumptions we take for granted today – from the 
modern corporation to the idea of continuous technological progress – have 
their roots in this transformative era. The Second Industrial Revolution was 
a period of economic and technological change and a starting point for the 
modern world. 
In the present era of rapid technological change – from artifcial intelli‑
gence (AI) to biotechnology – the lessons of the Second Industrial Revolution 
remain profoundly relevant. They remind us of innovation’s transformative 
power and the need to guide technological progress towards humane and 
sustainable ends. They caution us about the potential for technology to ex‑
acerbate inequality and social dislocation while showing us examples of how 
society can adapt and create new institutions to meet these challenges. 
5.1.3 The Third: The Digital Revolution and the Internet 
As the 20th century ended, a new wave of technological innovation began 
to reshape the global economy and society. This transformation, often called 
the Third Industrial Revolution or the Digital Revolution, marked a shift 
from mechanical and analogue electronic technology to digital and infor ‑
mation technology. Unlike its predecessors, this revolution was character ‑
ised not by a single breakthrough but by a convergence of technologies that 
would fundamentally alter how we communicate, work and live. 
At the heart of the Third Industrial Revolution lay the development and 
widespread adoption of digital technologies. The invention of the transistor 
in 1947 by William Shockley, John Bardeen and Walter Brattain at Bell Labs 
laid the groundwork for this transformation (Riordan & Hoddeson, 1997). 
This tiny device would become the building block of modern electronics, 
enabling the creation of increasingly powerful and compact computers. The 
subsequent development of integrated circuits in the late 1950s and early 
1960s further accelerated this trend. Gordon Moore, co‑founder of Intel, 
observed in 1965 that the number of transistors on a microchip doubled 
about every two years while the cost halved – a trend known as Moore’s Law 
164 The Political Economy of the Creative and Cultural Industries 
 
(Moore, 1965). This exponential growth in computing power, coupled with 
falling costs, would drive the proliferation of digital technologies across all 
sectors of the economy. 
The introduction of the personal computer in the 1970s and its rapid adop‑
tion in the 1980s marked a pivotal moment in the Third Industrial Revolu‑
tion. Companies like Apple, IBM and Microsoft brought computing power 
into homes and ofces, democratising access to information technology 
(Isaacson, 2014). This shift had profound implications for productivity and 
creativity, enabling individuals and small businesses to perform tasks that 
once required large, expensive mainframe computers. However, the develop‑
ment and widespread adoption of the Internet truly defned the Third Indus‑
trial Revolution. The creation of the World Wide Web by Tim Berners‑Lee 
in 1989 transformed the Internet from a tool used primarily by academics 
and government agencies into a global network accessible to anyone with 
a computer and a phone line (Berners‑Lee, 1999). The Internet revolution 
fundamentally altered how information was shared, businesses operated, and 
individuals communicated. 
The early 2010s marked a signifcant acceleration in the evolution of so‑
cial media and mobile technology, building upon the foundations laid by the 
Internet revolution. While LinkedIn was founded in 2003, it gained signif‑
cant traction as the premier professional networking platform. The introduc‑
tion of the iPhone in 2007 revolutionised mobile computing, paving the way 
for the explosive growth of the app economy. Twitter, launched in 2006, be‑
came a global phenomenon for real‑time news and commentary. Instagram, 
founded in 2010, quickly rose to prominence as a photo‑sharing platform, 
capitalising on the improved smartphone cameras. The decade also saw the 
meteoric rise of TikTok, which launched in 2016 and transformed short‑form 
video content and became especially popular among younger users. These 
platforms, along with others like Facebook (founded in 2004), collectively 
reshaped how people communicate, share information and consume media, 
further cementing the internet’s central role in daily life and accelerating the 
pace of the Digital Revolution. 
As we move further into the 21st century, the technologies and trends 
of the Third Industrial Revolution continue to evolve and shape our world. 
The lines of this revolution and what some call the Fourth Industrial Revo‑
lution  –  characterised by the convergence of digital, biological and physi‑
cal innovations  –  are becoming increasingly blurred (Schwab, 2017). The 
COVID‑19 pandemic has accelerated many trends associated with the Third 
Industrial Revolution, from remote work to e‑commerce to digital health 
solutions (Organisation for Economic Co‑operation and Development 
[OECD], 2020). As we grapple with global challenges like climate change 
and inequality, the technologies and systems developed during this period 
will likely play a crucial role in shaping our responses. 
Some crucial aspects of the Third Industrial Revolution are:

Like its predecessors, the Third Industrial Revolution has brought about pro‑
found changes in how we live, work and interact with the world around 
us. It has created new opportunities and challenges, reshaped industries and 
labour markets and altered the balance of global economic power. As we 
166 The Political Economy of the Creative and Cultural Industries 
 
 
 
 
 
stand on the cusp of further technological revolutions, this era’s lessons and 
unresolved questions will continue to shape our future. 
5.1.4 The Fourth 
The threads of the digital age spun during the Third Industrial Revolution, 
are now intertwining with strands of the physical and biological worlds, cre‑
ating a fabric of innovation called the Fourth Industrial Revolution. This 
revolution, a concept introduced by Klaus Schwab of the World Economic 
Forum, is not merely a continuation of its predecessor but a quantum leap 
into a future where the boundaries between man and machine, between the 
tangible and the virtual, begin to blur and fade (Schwab, 2017). 
Imagine, if you will, a world where your refrigerator not only keeps your 
food cold but also orders groceries when supplies run low, where your car 
navigates city streets without your hands on the wheel, and where doctors 
can print replacement organs tailored to your DNA. This is not the stuf of 
science fction but the emerging reality of the Fourth Industrial Revolution. 
It is a symphony of technologies, each playing its part in a grander compo‑
sition. AI sits at the heart of this technological orchestra, the maestro con‑
ducting an ensemble of intelligent machines. These AI systems, far removed 
from the clunky robots of the past, are learning, adapting and even creating. 
They’re driving our cars, diagnosing our illnesses, and even composing music 
(Russell & Norvig, 2020). Alongside AI, the Internet of Things hums quietly 
in the background, and a vast network of sensors and devices collect data 
and talk to each other, making our world more intelligent and responsive 
(Atzori et al., 2010). 
However, the Fourth Industrial Revolution is not content to remain digi‑
tal. It reaches out into the physical world through advanced robotics and 
3D printing. Once confned to factory foors, robots vent into our homes 
and hospitals, becoming caregivers and companions (Siciliano & Khatib, 
2016). 3D printers, meanwhile, are rewriting the rules of manufacturing, al‑
lowing us to create complex objects layer by layer, customised to our exact 
specifcations (Ngo et  al., 2018). Perhaps most provocatively, this revolu‑
tion dares to tinker with the very building blocks of life itself. Biotechnology 
and genetic engineering are opening new frontiers in healthcare and human 
enhancement. We are on the brink of being able to edit our genes, cure inher‑
ited diseases, and perhaps even enhance our physical and cognitive abilities 
(Church & Regis, 2012). 
As these technologies converge and interact, they’re reshaping our eco‑
nomic landscape. Traditional business models are being upended by the 
rise of platforms and sharing economies. Companies like Uber and Airbnb 
have shown how technology can turn our cars and spare rooms into 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
Creative and Cultural Industries and Innovation 167 
valuable assets, blurring the lines between consumer and producer (Parker 
et al., 2016). In this new world, the nature of work itself is changing. As 
machines become more capable, many jobs that once seemed secure are 
now under threat. Yet, as some doors close, others open. The Fourth In‑
dustrial Revolution is creating new roles that prize creativity, emotional 
intelligence, and technological savvy – skills that remain uniquely human 
(Frey & Osborne, 2017). 
But with great power comes great responsibility, and the Fourth Indus‑
trial Revolution brings a host of ethical and social challenges. As more of 
our lives become digitised, privacy and data security questions loom large 
(Zubof, 2019). The potential for these technologies to exacerbate existing 
inequalities is a pressing concern (Piketty, 2014). As we gain the ability 
to enhance human capabilities, we must grapple with profound questions 
about the nature of humanity itself (Bostrom & Sandberg, 2009). The envi‑
ronmental implications of this revolution are equally complex. On the one 
hand, intelligent technologies ofer hope for more efcient resource use and 
cleaner energy (Ramchurn et al., 2012). On the other hand, the growing 
energy demands of our digital infrastructure raise concerns about sustain‑
ability (Sedlmeir et al., 2020). 
On the global stage, the Fourth Industrial Revolution is redrawing the 
map of power and infuence. Nations compete for technological supremacy 
and economic and military advantage (Lee, 2018). At the same time, the 
interconnected nature of these technologies creates new vulnerabilities, mak‑
ing cybersecurity a paramount concern (Singer & Friedman, 2014). Educa‑
tion, too, must evolve to keep pace with this changing world. The rapid 
march of technology demands a workforce committed to lifelong learning, 
constantly updating their skills to remain relevant (World Economic Forum, 
2020). STEM education is more crucial than ever, but so is the cultivation of 
creativity and emotional intelligence – the qualities that set us apart from ma‑
chines (National Academies of Sciences, Engineering, and Medicine, 2018). 
In healthcare, the convergence of digital, physical and biological technolo‑
gies promises a revolution in preventing, diagnosing and treating disease. 
Personalised medicine, tailored to our genetic makeup, could transform our 
approach to healthcare (Ginsburg & Phillips, 2018). Telemedicine, acceler ‑
ated by recent global events, is breaking down geographical barriers to care 
(Dorsey & Topol, 2016). 
Yet, for all its promise, the Fourth Industrial Revolution is not without 
its critics. Some worry about widespread job displacement (Acemoglu & 
Restrepo, 2018), while others argue that the transformative potential of these 
technologies needs to be increased (Fernandez‑Carmona et al., 2019). There 
are also valid concerns about the concentration of power in the hands of a 
few tech giants (Moore & Tambini, 2018). 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
168 The Political Economy of the Creative and Cultural Industries 
As we stand on the threshold of this new era, we are flled with excitement 
and trepidation. The Fourth Industrial Revolution ofers immense opportuni‑
ties to increase efciency, improve quality of life and solve some of our most 
pressing global challenges. But realising these benefts while mitigating risks 
will require thoughtful governance, inclusive policies and a commitment to 
ensuring that technological progress serves humanity. The future, as always, 
remains unwritten. Even though some visionaries are still dealing with the 
implications of the Fourth Industrial Revolution, some are already speaking 
of the ffth and sixth revolutions to come (Skobelev & Borovik, 2017). What‑
ever the future holds, one thing is sure: the pace of change shows no signs of 
slowing. As we weave this new technological standard, we must ensure that 
the pattern we create is one of progress, sustainability and shared prosperity 
for all humanity.

5.1.4.1 Economic and Business Implications 
The Fourth Industrial Revolution is reshaping the global economy and busi‑
ness landscape in profound ways: 
1 New Business Models: The rise of platform economies and the sharing 
economy (e.g., Uber, Airbnb) are challenging traditional business models 
and blurring industry boundaries (Parker et al., 2016). 
2 Shift in Labour Markets: While automation threatens many existing 
jobs, new roles emerge. There’s an increasing premium on creative, emo‑
tional and technological skills that machines can’t easily replicate (Frey & 
Osborne, 2017). 
3 Customisation and Personalisation: Technologies like AI and 3D printing 
are enabling mass customisation, allowing businesses to tailor products 
and services to individual preferences at scale (Xu & Chen, 2016). 
4 Supply Chain Transformation: IoT, AI and blockchain are making supply 
chains more transparent, efcient and resilient (Treiblmaier, 2018). 
5 Data as a Key Asset: The ability to collect, analyse and leverage vast 
amounts of data is becoming a critical source of competitive advantage 
(Mayer‑Schönberger & Cukier, 2013). 
5.1.4.2 Social and Ethical Considerations 
The rapid pace of change brought about by the Fourth Industrial Revolution 
raises signifcant social and ethical questions: 
1 Privacy and Data Security: As more of our lives become digitised, concerns 
about data privacy and security are intensifying (Zubof, 2019). 
2 Inequality: There’s a risk that the benefts of the Fourth Industrial Revo‑
lution could be unevenly distributed, exacerbating existing inequalities 
(Piketty, 2014). 
3 Human Enhancement: Advances in biotechnology and neurotechnology 
raise ethical questions about the limits of human enhancement (Bostrom & 
Sandberg, 2009). 
4 AI Ethics: As AI systems become more autonomous and infuential, en‑
suring they make decisions aligned with human values becomes crucial 
(Russell, 2019). 
5 Digital Divide: Ensuring equal access to the technologies driving the 
Fourth Industrial Revolution is a signifcant challenge (Van Dijk, 2006).

5.1.4.3 Environmental Impact and Sustainability 
The Fourth Industrial Revolution ofers both challenges and opportunities 
for environmental sustainability: 
1 Smart Cities: IoT and AI technologies are enabling the development of 
smart cities that can manage resources more efciently (Albino et  al., 
2015). 
2 Circular Economy: Advanced technologies are facilitating the transition to 
a more circular economy, where waste is minimised and resources are used 
more efciently (Geissdoerfer et al., 2017). 
3 Clean Energy: AI and IoT are optimising renewable energy systems, while 
advances in materials science are improving energy storage technologies 
(Ramchurn et al., 2012). 
4 Environmental Monitoring: Sensor networks and satellite technologies are 
enhancing our ability to monitor and respond to environmental changes 
(Pettorelli et al., 2016). 
5 Energy Consumption: However, the increasing use of data centres and 
blockchain technologies raises concerns about growing energy consump‑
tion (Sedlmeir et al., 2020). 
5.1.4.4 Geopolitical Implications 
The Fourth Industrial Revolution is reshaping global power dynamics: 
1 Technological Leadership: Countries are vying for leadership in key tech‑
nologies like AI and quantum computing, seeing them as crucial for eco‑
nomic and military advantage (Lee, 2018). 
2 Cybersecurity: As more critical infrastructure becomes connected, cyber ‑
security becomes a major national security concern (Singer & Friedman, 
2014). 
3 Regulatory Challenges: Governments are grappling with how to regulate 
rapidly evolving technologies that often transcend national boundaries 
(Yeung, 2019). 
5.1.4.5 Education and Skills 
The Fourth Industrial Revolution is necessitating changes in education and

1 Lifelong Learning: The rapid pace of technological change is making con‑

processes (Pfeifer, 2017).

tain technologies (like blockchain or quantum computing) is overstated

4 Concentration of Power: There are concerns about the concentration of

5.2 Innovation in the Economic Point of View: Schumpeter 
5.2.1 Waves and Types of Innovation 
Joseph Schumpeter was one of the most infuential economists of the 20th 
century, known for his theories on innovation, entrepreneurship and the 
dynamism of capitalist economies. His work has profoundly impacted our

understanding of how innovation drives economic growth and transforma‑
tion. An essential aspect of Schumpeter’s theory is that innovations tend to 
cluster together in long waves or cycles rather than evenly distribute over 
time. He identifed several types of these innovation waves: 
1 Kondratiev Waves (45–60 years): The most prolonged economic cycles 
were named after Russian economist Nikolai Kondratiev. Schumpeter 
associated each Kondratiev wave with a signifcant technological revo‑
lution fundamentally altering the economy’s structure. Examples include 
the Industrial Revolution (1780s–1840s), the Age of Steam and Rail‑
ways (1840s–1890s), the Age of Steel, Electricity and Heavy Engineer ‑
ing (1890s–1940s), the Age of Oil, Automobiles and Mass Production 
(1940s–1990s) and potentially the Age of Information and Telecommuni‑
cations (1990s–present). 
2 Kuznets Swings (15–25 years): Named after Simon Kuznets, these are 
intermediate economic cycles often associated with infrastructure invest‑
ments and demographic changes. 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
Creative and Cultural Industries and Innovation 173 
3 Juglar Cycles (7–11 years): Clément Juglar identifed these medium‑term 
cycles related to frm investment behaviour. 
4 Kitchin Cycles (3–5 years): Named after Joseph Kitchin, these are short 
business cycles associated with inventory fuctuations. 
In addition to describing innovation cycles, Schumpeter also distinguished 
between diferent types of innovation: 
1 New Products: Introducing a new good or quality of an existing good. 
Examples include the frst automobiles and smartphones. 
2 New Methods of Production: The introduction of a new way of making 
products, such as the assembly line in manufacturing or computer‑aided 
design. 
3 New Markets: The opening of new markets for an industry’s products. 
This could involve geographic expansion or tapping into new demo‑
graphic segments. 
4 New Sources of Supply: Securing a new source of raw materials or 
semi‑fnished goods. An example would be new oil reserves or new syn‑
thetic materials. 
5 New Forms of Organisation: Changes in how frms or industries are or ‑
ganised. An example is the rise of “Fordism” – standardised mass produc‑
tion combined with high wages – in the early 20th century. 
Joseph Schumpeter’s theory of innovation is not merely a description of 
technological change but a comprehensive framework for understanding the 
multi‑faceted nature of innovation and its role in economic transformation. 
Central to this framework is Schumpeter’s distinction between diferent types 
of innovation, each of which plays a unique role in economic development. 
The author identifes the frst type of innovation as introducing a new 
good or a new quality of a good (Schumpeter, 1939). This type of innova‑
tion is the most easily recognisable, as it directly introduces new products 
into the market. Historical examples abound, from the introduction of the 
automobile in the early 20th century to the advent of the smartphone in 
the early 21st. These product innovations often open new markets and cre‑
ate new consumer demands, fundamentally altering the economic landscape 
(Christensen, 2013). 
The second type of innovation is the introduction of a new method of 
production. These process innovations involve new ways of making exist‑
ing products, often more efciently or with new materials. Henry Ford’s 
pioneering of the assembly line in manufacturing in the early 20th century 
is a prime example. More recent examples include using 3D printing in 
 
 
 
 
 
 
174 The Political Economy of the Creative and Cultural Industries 
manufacturing or applying machine learning algorithms to optimise produc‑
tion processes. These innovations can lead to signifcant productivity gains 
and cost reductions. 
The third type of innovation is opening a new market (Schumpeter, 1942). 
This could involve expanding into new geographic regions or targeting new 
demographic segments. The globalisation of commerce in the late 20th 
century, facilitated by innovations in transportation and communication 
technology, opened up vast new business markets (Friedman, 2005). On a 
smaller scale, a company might innovate by identifying and serving a previ‑
ously overlooked market segment. 
The fourth type of innovation involves securing a new source of raw ma‑
terials or semi‑fnished goods (Freeman & Soete, 1997). This could include 
discovering new natural resources, such as oil reserves, or developing new 
synthetic materials. For example, the development of plastics in the mid‑20th 
century provided a new source of supply for many industries (Bijker et al., 
1987). Innovations in recycling and circular economy practices in recent 
years also fall under this category. 
The ffth and fnal type of innovation is carrying out a new organisa‑
tion in any industry. This could involve the creation or breaking up of a 
monopoly position or, more broadly, changes in the structure of an in‑
dustry. The rise of “Fordism” in the early 20th century, which combined 
mass production with high wages and led to a mass consumer market, 
is an example of this type of organisational innovation. More recently, 
the emergence of the “sharing economy,” exemplifed by companies like 
Airbnb and Uber, represents a signifcant organisational innovation in sev‑
eral industries. 
It’s important to note that these types of innovation are not mutually ex‑
clusive and often occur in combination (Fagerberg, 2003). The development 
of the personal computer, for example, involved product innovations (the mi‑
crocomputer itself), process innovations (the use of microprocessors and new 
production techniques), the opening of new markets (personal computing 
for homes and small businesses), new sources of supply (the development of 
new electronic components) and new industry organisation (the emergence 
of new hardware and software companies). Moreover, innovation is not a 
one‑time event but an ongoing process. Successful innovations often lead to 
further innovations, creating a self‑reinforcing cycle of economic change. For 
example, the digital revolution of the late 20th century continues to spur in‑
novations in felds like AI, robotics and the Internet of Things. 
The author emphasised that innovation is not just about technology but 
can encompass changes in products, processes, markets, inputs and industry 
structures. He saw entrepreneurs as the key agents driving innovation by in‑
troducing these “new combinations.” In this sense, innovation was the engine 
of economic growth and a force of “creative destruction.” He challenged the 
 
 
Creative and Cultural Industries and Innovation 175 
neoclassical economics view of perfect competition, arguing that dynamic, 
innovation‑driven competition was more important for development than 
the static price competition assumed in traditional models. Accordingly, to 
this view, successful innovators enjoy temporary monopoly profts, which 
incentivise them to invest in innovation. These profts are then eroded as imi‑
tators enter the market, spurring the next round of innovation. This dynamic 
process, while disruptive, leads to higher productivity and living standards 
in the long run. 
In today’s rapidly changing digital and knowledge‑based economy, these 
insights on the role of innovation and entrepreneurship in driving economic 
transformation are as relevant as ever. Concepts like “disruptive innova‑
tion,” “creative destruction,” and “innovation ecosystems” can trace their 
intellectual roots back to Schumpeter’s work. 
While some aspects of his theories remain debated, and innovation pat‑
terns have emerged since his time, Schumpeter’s core ideas provide an endur‑
ing framework for understanding the dynamics of innovation and growth 
in capitalist economies. Particularly with the opportunities and challenges 
posed by AI, biotechnology, renewable energy and other emerging technolo‑
gies, the vision of innovation as the driving force of economic change contin‑
ues to resonate. 
5.2.2 The Industrial Revolutions in Schumpeter’s Lens 
Joseph Schumpeter’s ground‑breaking theory of innovation and his concept 
of “long waves” or “Kondratiev waves” can be elegantly applied to the 
four industrial revolutions that have transformed our economic landscape 
(Schwab, 2017). With steam‑powered marvels and mechanised textile pro‑
duction, the First Industrial Revolution (1760–1840) aligns with Schumpet‑
er’s frst Kondratiev wave (Allen, 2009; Schumpeter, 1939). The luminaries 
of this age, such as James Watt and Richard Arkwright, embody the spirit 
of entrepreneurial innovation that Schumpeter held so dear (Landes, 2003; 
Mokyr, 2012). 
Walking into the Second Industrial Revolution (1870–1914), the changing 
tempo accelerates, propelled by the electric dynamo, the internal combustion 
engine and the marvels of mass production (Gordon, 2016; Landes, 1969). 
This era, corresponding to Schumpeter’s second Kondratiev wave, gave birth 
to titanic industries like steel, chemicals and automobiles (Chandler, 1990). 
Visionary fgures like Thomas Edison and Henry Ford stand as the quintes‑
sential Schumpeterian innovators, their names etched into the annals of eco‑
nomic history (McCraw, 2007). 
The Third Industrial Revolution (1960s–2000s), straddling the bound‑
ary between Schumpeter’s fourth and ffth Kondratiev waves, heralds the 
dawn of the digital age (Dosi, 1988). This period saw a quantum leap in 
 
 
176 The Political Economy of the Creative and Cultural Industries 
innovation, from the humble transistor to the global embrace of the inter ‑
net (Castells, 2010). The characteristic industries of this time  – electronics, 
telecommunications, software and biotechnology – reshaped the very fabric 
of our society (Freeman & Louçã, 2001). The luminaries of this revolution, 
fgures like Bill Gates and Steve Jobs, embody the Schumpeterian ideal of the 
entrepreneur as a force for creative destruction (Isaacson, 2014). 
And now, as we stand on the cusp of the Fourth Industrial Revolution, 
Schumpeter’s ideas resonate with renewed vigour. AI, robotics, the Internet 
of Things and the promise of clean energy technologies are the instruments 
of this new wave of change (Brynjolfsson & McAfee, 2014). Though Schum‑
peter himself could not have foreseen these specifc developments, his intel‑
lectual legacy provides an indispensable framework for understanding the 
innovation‑driven metamorphosis of our economy (Perez, 2010). As we navi‑
gate the uncharted waters of this new era, Schumpeter’s insights into the vital 
role of entrepreneurship and the ceaseless gale of creative destruction remain 
our essential compass (Fagerberg, 2003). 
5.2.3 Types of Innovation and the Creative Industries 
Schumpeter categorising innovation types provides a robust framework for 
understanding economic changes in the scope of CCIs. By distinguishing be‑
tween diferent kinds of innovation, Schumpeter highlights how entrepre‑
neurs can introduce “new combinations” that disrupt existing economic 
structures and drive economic development. From the arts and media to 
design and digital content, creative sectors are nestling of innovation, con‑
stantly pushing the boundaries of what is possible and driving economic and 
cultural transformation (Caves, 2000). 
Product innovation is the most visible type of innovation in creative in‑
dustries. Creating new cultural goods, such as a ground‑breaking flm, a best‑
selling novel or a chart‑topping album, can signifcantly impact the market 
and popular culture (Hesmondhalgh, 2018). In the digital age, product in‑
novations in creative industries also increasingly take the form of new digital 
content and experiences, such as virtual reality games or interactive online 
narratives. 
Process innovations are also critical in creative industries, although they 
often happen behind the scenes. Developing new technologies and techniques 
for creating, distributing and consuming cultural content has transformed 
many creative sectors (Landry, 2012). The rise of digital animation and spe‑
cial efects, for example, has revolutionised flmmaking, while digital pub‑
lishing has transformed the book industry (Thompson, 2013). The increasing 
use of data analytics and AI in creative industries also represents a signifcant 
process innovation, enabling new forms of personalisation and optimisation 
(Srnicek, 2016). 
 
 
 
Creative and Cultural Industries and Innovation 177 
Opening new markets is another crucial type of innovation in creative in‑
dustries. The globalisation of culture and the rise of the internet have created 
vast new audiences for cultural content and new opportunities for creative 
professionals to reach niche markets. For example, the growth of the Chinese 
flm market has opened new opportunities for Hollywood studios. At the 
same time, the rise of online streaming has created new markets for inde‑
pendent musicians and flmmakers (Curtin & Sanson, 2016). 
Innovations in sourcing new inputs are also prevalent in creative indus‑
tries. For instance, the use of new materials and technologies in fashion and 
design can create entirely new types of products (Brydges & Hracs, 2019). 
In the music industry, new instruments and sound technologies have often 
spurred new genres and styles (Pinch & Bijsterveld, 2004). The increasing 
incorporation of user‑generated content and data into creative processes, 
enabled by digital technologies, also represents a new source of inputs 
(Manovich, 2009). 
Finally, organisational innovations are transforming the structure and dy‑
namics of many creative industries. The rise of fexible, project‑based forms 
of organisation in sectors like flm and television production represents a sig‑
nifcant organisational innovation, enabling greater agility and adaptability 
(Grabher, 2002). The growth of creative clusters and networks, facilitated 
by digital technologies, also allows new collaboration and knowledge shar ‑
ing (Florida, 2002). In the art world, the increasing use of online platforms 
and social media is changing how artists connect with audiences and patrons 
(Joselit, 2013). 
It’s important to note that innovation in creative industries often involves a 
unique blend of artistic creativity and economic entrepreneurship (Howkins, 
2002). Creative professionals frequently innovate to create fnancial value, 
express new ideas, challenge conventions and push the boundaries of their 
art (Becker, 1982). This means that the drivers and impacts of innovation in 
creative industries are often complex and multidimensional, involving a mix 
of cultural, social and economic factors (Potts & Cunningham, 2008). 
Nonetheless, this framework provides a valuable starting point for ana‑
lysing how innovation drives change and growth in creative industries. By 
highlighting the diverse types of innovation at play – from new products and 
processes to new markets, inputs and organisational forms – Schumpeter’s 
theory helps us better understand the dynamic and transformative nature of 
creativity in the economy. 
5.2.4 Creative Destruction in the Creative Industries 
Joseph Schumpeter’s “creative destruction” concept is perhaps his most 
enduring and infuential contribution to our understanding of innovation 
and economic change. The term refers to the incessant innovation process 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
178 The Political Economy of the Creative and Cultural Industries 
by which new products, methods and organisations replace outdated ones 
(Schumpeter, 1942). Schumpeter argued that this process is the fundamental 
impulse that sets and keeps the capitalist engine in motion (McCraw, 2007). 
At the heart of the concept is the idea that innovation is not just about 
creating new things but also about destroying old ones. As Schumpeter put 
it, creative destruction “incessantly revolutionises the economic structure 
from within, incessantly destroying the old one, incessantly creating a new 
one” (Schumpeter, 1942b, p. 83). This means that innovation is inherently 
disruptive, constantly challenging established ways of doing things and up‑
setting the status quo (Christensen, 2013). The automobile industry provides 
a classic example of creative destruction in action. The rise of the car in 
the early 20th century didn’t just create a new industry; it also destroyed or 
transformed many others, from horse‑drawn carriages and blacksmiths to 
railroads and urban design. More recently, the rise of digital photography 
destroyed the flm photography industry, while streaming services have fun‑
damentally disrupted the business models of traditional media companies 
(Gans, 2016). 
Schumpeter saw this process of creative destruction as an essential fact of 
capitalism. In his view, it is not price competition that counts but competition 
from new commodities, technologies, sources of supply and types of organi‑
sations. This competition strikes not at the margins of existing frms but their 
foundations and lives. 
The concept of creative destruction highlights the dynamic, evolutionary 
nature of the capitalist economy. It suggests that economic growth and devel‑
opment are not smooth, gradual processes but are characterised by constant 
upheaval and discontinuous change (Freeman & Louçã, 2001). It also empha‑
sises the central role of the entrepreneur as an agent of innovation and change, 
constantly introducing new combinations that challenge the existing order. 
However, the concept also highlights innovation’s inherently confictual 
and disruptive nature. The process of creative destruction necessarily involves 
winners and losers as new industries and frms rise and old ones decline 
(Perez, 2003). This can lead to signifcant social and economic dislocation, as 
workers lose their jobs and communities are disrupted. Moreover, the benefts 
of creative destruction are only sometimes evenly distributed. Schumpeter 
acknowledged that creative destruction could lead to the concentration of 
economic power as successful innovators gain market dominance. This has 
led some critics to argue that Schumpeter’s vision celebrates the role of mono‑
poly power in driving innovation (Dolfsma, 2004). 
Despite these criticisms, creative destruction remains central to our under‑
standing of innovation and economic change. It highlights the constant in ‑
terplay of innovation and destruction that characterises dynamic economies 
and the central role of entrepreneurship in driving this process (Metcalfe, 
1998). The concept also has important policy implications. It suggests that 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
Creative and Cultural Industries and Innovation 179 
policies promoting innovation and growth should focus on supporting the 
creation of new industries and technologies and facilitating the smooth real‑
location of resources from declining to rising sectors. It also highlights the 
importance of policies that support workers and communities afected by the 
disruptive impacts of innovation. 
The concept of creative destruction is highly relevant to the dynamics of 
creative industries. These industries, including media, entertainment, design 
and the arts, are characterised by a constant drive for novelty and original‑
ity (Caves, 2000). In this context, innovation often takes the form of new 
creative products, styles and forms of expression that challenge and disrupt 
established norms and conventions. 
For example, the rise of digital flmmaking and special efects in the 1990s 
and 2000s transformed how movies are made and experienced. The fashion 
industry is another creative sector where creative destruction is a constant 
force. The cyclical nature of fashion trends means that new styles and designs 
are constantly emerging, often in reaction to and at the expense of older ones. 
The rise of “fast fashion” in recent years, enabled by new technologies and 
globalised supply chains, has signifcantly disrupted the traditional fashion 
industry, challenged established players and created new opportunities for 
innovation. 
In all these cases, creative destruction is driven by the inherent need for 
novelty and diferentiation in creative industries. There is constant pressure 
to innovate and challenge the status quo in a market where the primary value 
is derived from originality and uniqueness (Caves, 2000). This means creative 
industries are inherently dynamic and unstable, with new ideas and forms 
constantly emerging and displacing older ones (Potts & Cunningham, 2008). 
However, creative destruction in creative industries also has unique chal‑
lenges and complexities. Unlike in other sectors, where innovation is often 
driven by technological change or efciency gains, innovation in creative in‑
dustries is usually more subjective and unpredictable (Caves, 2000). The suc‑
cess of a new innovative product or style depends not just on its objective 
qualities but also on its ability to capture the zeitgeist and resonate with audi‑
ences’ changing tastes and preferences (Hirsch, 1972). Moreover, the disrup‑
tive impact of new ideas and forms in creative industries is only sometimes 
immediately apparent. Unlike in industries where innovation leads to clear and 
measurable efciency gains, the value of new creative products and styles often 
emerges over time as they gain cultural signifcance and infuence. This means 
that creative destruction in creative industries is usually more gradual and 
iterative, with new ideas and forms co‑existing with older ones for extended 
periods before eventually displacing them (Potts & Cunningham, 2008). 
Despite these complexities, the concept of creative destruction remains a 
powerful tool for understanding the dynamics of change and innovation in 
creative industries. It highlights the constant tension between tradition and 
 
  
 
 
 
 
 
 
180 The Political Economy of the Creative and Cultural Industries 
innovation, between established and emerging forms, that characterises these 
sectors. It also emphasises the central role of creative entrepreneurs and in‑
novators in driving this process, constantly introducing new ideas and chal‑
lenging the status quo. 
For policymakers and industry leaders, understanding the process of crea‑
tive destruction in creative industries is crucial for fostering a dynamic and 
innovative creative economy. This means creating an environment that sup‑
ports risk‑taking and experimentation, values originality and uniqueness, 
and can adapt and evolve in response to changing tastes and technologies 
(Pratt & Jefcutt, 2009). It also means supporting the creative entrepreneurs 
and innovators who drive the process of creative destruction, providing them 
with the resources, skills and opportunities they need to bring their ideas to 
life (Florida, 2002). 
Ultimately, creative destruction reminds the world that change and disrup‑
tion are inherent to the nature of creative industries. In an era where the only 
constant is change, the ability to innovate and adapt is not just a competitive 
advantage but a necessity for survival. By embracing the process of creative 
destruction and fostering an environment that supports and encourages in‑
novation, we can ensure our creative industries’ continued vitality and rel‑
evance in the face of a progressively changing world. 
5.2.5 Schumpeterian Innovations in Creative and Industries: 
Examples 
The CCIs, encompassing sectors such as media, entertainment, design and 
the arts, serve as a vibrant arena for innovation in the Schumpeterian tradi‑
tion. You can fnd some examples below: 
5.2.5.1 Introduction of a New Good 
The creative industries are prolifc in introducing new products that capture 
the public’s imagination and reshape cultural landscapes. These innovations 
often blend technological advancements with artistic vision, creating entirely 
new categories of cultural goods, such as: 
Digital Revolution in Art Consumption: The iPad’s introduction in 2010 
transformed digital content interaction, creating new possibilities in digital 
publishing, interactive storytelling and mobile gaming. Art galleries and mu‑
seums developed apps for virtual tours and artwork exploration, increasing 
fne art accessibility. Digital art platforms like DeviantArt and ArtStation 
democratised art creation and distribution, leading to new forms of artistic 
expression such as generative art and interactive installations. 
 
 
 
 
Creative and Cultural Industries and Innovation 181 
Streaming Services and Content Creation: Netfix’s streaming service launch in 
2007 and subsequent original content production introduced a new model 
of content creation and consumption. This innovation changed viewing 
habits, disrupted traditional TV networks, led to “binge‑watching,” and 
altered storytelling formats. It afected the entire entertainment ecosystem, 
changing success metrics, production budgets, acting careers and facilitat‑
ing international content exchange. 
Virtual (VR) and Augmented Reality (AR) in Entertainment: Consumer‑grade 
VR headsets and AR applications created new forms of immersive enter ‑
tainment, expanding beyond gaming into virtual art exhibitions, immer ‑
sive journalism and AR‑enhanced live performances. The potential of VR 
and AR in creative industries is still being explored, with applications in 
virtual concerts, immersive storytelling in flm and AR‑based virtual art 
installations. 
Innovative Musical Instruments and Production Tools: Introducing new in‑
struments and production tools, such as synthesisers and digital audio 
workstations (DAWs), has led to new genres and democratised music 
production. Cloud‑based DAWs enable global collaboration, AI‑powered 
tools ofer automated services, and blockchain technology is being ex‑
plored for managing music rights and royalties. 
E‑Books and Digital Publishing: E‑readers like the Amazon Kindle cre‑
ated a new market for digital books, afecting pricing models, enabling 
self‑publishing, and leading to interactive and multimedia books. This has 
impacted reading habits, spawned new literary forms like the “Kindle Sin‑
gle,” and democratised the publishing industry by allowing new voices to 
reach readers without traditional gatekeepers. 
5.2.5.2 Introduction of a New Method of Production 
Innovations in production methods have transformed how creative content is 
made and distributed, often leading to entirely new aesthetic possibilities and 
business models. Relevant examples are: 
Digital Animation and Computer‑Generated Images (CGI): Digital anima‑
tion techniques, pioneered by Pixar, have revolutionised the flm indus‑
try. Toy Story (1995) was the frst fully computer‑animated feature flm, 
setting a new industry standard. The technology has evolved to create 
photorealistic efects, blurring the line between animation and live‑action. 
CGI’s impact extends beyond animated flms, transforming visual efects 
in live‑action movies and fnding applications in architectural visualisa‑
tion, product design and medical imaging. It has raised questions about 
flm performance and authorship. 
 
 
182 The Political Economy of the Creative and Cultural Industries 
3D Printing in Fashion and Design: 3D printing has introduced new produc‑
tion methods in fashion and industrial design. Designers like Iris van Her‑
pen use it to create intricate pieces impossible with traditional methods. It 
enables rapid prototyping and small‑batch production of custom designs. 
3D printing allows for customisation, on‑demand production and sustain‑
able design. It could potentially democratise design and manufacturing. 
AI in Creative Processes: AI is introducing new production methods across 
creative felds. Tools like AIVA and Amper Music can compose original 
scores, while GAN technology creates AI‑generated artworks. This raises 
questions about creativity and authorship. AI is also used in recommenda‑
tion algorithms and predictive tools for creative projects. 
Virtual Production in Film and Television: LED walls and real‑time render ‑
ing engines, as used in The Mandalorian (2019), represent a shift in visual 
efects integration. This method allows real‑time compositing of digital 
backgrounds, reducing post‑production time and costs. Virtual production 
could decentralise the flm industry and open new creative possibilities. 
Algorithmic Composition and Generative Art: Algorithms generate new mu‑
sic and visual artworks. Generative music creates ever‑changing composi‑
tions, while artists like Refk Anadol use machine learning for data‑driven 
artworks. This approach raises questions about authorship and creativity 
and opens possibilities for interactive and personalised art experiences. 
5.2.5.3 Opening of a New Market 
Creative industries have been adept at opening new markets, often by tap‑
ping into previously underserved demographics or creating entirely new cat‑
egories of cultural consumption. Some relevant examples are: 
Streaming and the Global TV Market: Netfix’s global expansion strategy, 
focusing on local‑language content in markets like India, Brazil and South 
Korea, has opened new markets for high‑quality, streaming‑frst television 
content. Shows like Money Heist and Squid Game have found global audi‑
ences, challenging English‑language content dominance. This strategy has 
increased investment in local production industries, fostered cultural ex‑
change, challenged the Hollywood‑centric content distribution model and 
created opportunities for non‑English‑speaking creators to reach global 
audiences. 
Gaming and New Demographics: Nintendo’s Wii and mobile games like 
“Candy Crush Saga” opened up new markets for casual gamers, attract‑
ing previously uninterested demographics. Games like “Fortnite” have 
expanded beyond gaming to become social platforms and virtual concert 
venues. This expansion has led to the gamifcation of non‑gaming activi‑
ties, the rise of esports and mobile gaming becoming a signifcant industry. 
 
 
Creative and Cultural Industries and Innovation 183 
Podcasting and Audio Entertainment: Podcasting has created a new audio 
entertainment and education market. Shows like “Serial” demonstrated 
the potential for long‑form investigative journalism in audio format. Com‑
panies like Gimlet Media have established a professional podcast produc‑
tion industry. Podcasting has revitalised audio storytelling, created new 
opportunities for creators and advertisers and built engaged communities 
around specifc topics or personalities. 
STEM Toys and Edutainment: Companies like LittleBits and Kano have cre‑
ated a market at the intersection of toys and education, teaching coding 
and electronics through play. This “edutainment” market expands the toy 
industry into STEM education, refecting societal shifts towards valuing 
STEM skills and gamifcation of learning. These products also address the 
gender gap in STEM felds by making coding and electronics more acces‑
sible to all children. 
Virtual Infuencers and Digital Celebrities: Creating virtual infuencers like 
Lil Miquela has opened a new market in the infuencer economy. These 
AI‑powered, computer‑generated characters engage with followers, en‑
dorse products and even release music. This phenomenon raises questions 
about celebrity and authenticity in the digital age, creates new brand op‑
portunities and could be used to create persistent digital avatars for real 
celebrities. 
5.2.5.4 Conquest of a New Source of Supply 
Innovations in sourcing new inputs have led to novel creative outputs and 
reshaped industry supply chain, such as: 
User‑Generated Content Platforms: Platforms like YouTube, TikTok and Ins‑
tagram have provided new sources of talent and content for the entertain‑
ment industry. Many social media stars have transitioned to mainstream 
media, reshaping the talent pipeline. This democratisation of content crea‑
tion has altered the media landscape, challenging traditional gatekeepers, 
creating new paths to stardom and allowing diverse voices to fnd audi‑
ences. It has also led to new business models like infuencer marketing and 
new forms of content. 
Sustainable and Recycled Materials in Fashion: The fashion industry has seen 
a signifcant trend towards sustainable and recycled materials. Companies 
like Adidas have created shoes from ocean plastic, while Stella McCart‑
ney pioneers’ sustainable materials in high fashion. This includes innova‑
tive materials like Piñatex and mushroom leather. This shift challenges 
the traditional fast fashion model, creating new supply chains, potentially 
reducing environmental impact and responding to changing consumer 
preferences. 
 
 
184 The Political Economy of the Creative and Cultural Industries 
Crowdsourcing in Design: Platforms like 99designs have created a new sup‑
ply source for graphic design services, allowing businesses to crowdsource 
design work globally. This has disrupted traditional design agency models 
and opened opportunities for freelance designers worldwide. While it has 
democratised access to design services, it has also raised concerns about 
the devaluation of design work and the ethics of speculative work. 
Big Data as Creative Input: Big data has become a new source of input for 
creative decisions. Companies like Netfix and Spotify use viewer and lis‑
tener data to inform content creation and curation. This data‑driven ap‑
proach is changing decision‑making in creative industries, allowing for 
more personalised experiences. However, it raises questions about the role 
of human intuition and the potential for reinforcing biases or creative 
homogenisation. 
AI‑Generated Content: AI is becoming a new source of creative content, with 
applications in automated journalism, chatbots and conceptual art. Tools 
like GPT‑3 and DALL‑E can generate human‑like text and images. This 
raises profound questions about creativity, authorship and the value of 
human labour in creative industries. The legal and ethical implications of 
AI‑generated content, particularly around copyright and authenticity, are 
still being debated. 
5.2.5.5 New Organisation of an Industry 
The creative industries have seen numerous innovations in organising them‑
selves as an industry (shaping a whole system of productive chain). Repre‑
sentative examples are: 
Gig Economy in Creative Services: Platforms like Fiverr and Upwork have 
created new marketplaces for freelance creative services, transforming 
how creative professionals work. This ofers more fexibility and diverse 
opportunities but also brings challenges like job insecurity. It provides 
businesses with access to a global talent pool but raises questions about 
labour rights and fair compensation. 
Blockchain in Music Rights Management: Blockchain technology is reshap‑
ing music rights and royalties tracking and distribution. Platforms like 
Musicoin and Ujo Music are experimenting with blockchain‑based sys‑
tems that could disintermediate traditional industry players. This could 
create more transparent and efcient systems but faces adoption chal‑
lenges and environmental concerns. 
Direct‑to‑Consumer Models in Publishing: Authors like Andy Weir have 
found success self‑publishing online, bypassing traditional gatekeepers. 
Platforms like Amazon’s Kindle Direct Publishing have facilitated this 
 
 
 
 
 
Creative and Cultural Industries and Innovation 185 
shift. This has democratised publishing but created challenges in discover‑
ability and quality control. 
Virtual Production Studios: The COVID‑19 pandemic accelerated remote 
work adoption in flm and TV production. Companies like Ziva Dynam‑
ics and Unity are creating virtual production tools for collaborative flm‑
making in virtual environments. This could decentralise the industry but 
requires signifcant upfront investment. 
Subscription Models in Creative Software: Adobe’s shift to a subscription‑based 
model for its Creative Suite in 2013 marked a change in creative software 
distribution and monetisation. This provides more regular revenue for 
companies and potentially lowers upfront costs for users, but it has been 
criticised for potentially increasing long‑term costs. 
Collaborative Consumption in Fashion: Clothing rental services like Rent the 
Runway and peer‑to‑peer fashion resale platforms like Depop represent a 
shift towards collaborative consumption in fashion. These models could 
reduce waste and provide broader access to clothing but face logistical chal‑
lenges and need to change consumer attitudes towards clothing ownership. 
The creative industries stand at the forefront of Schumpeterian innovation, 
embodying the “creative destruction” process that drives economic pro‑
gress. From the introduction of new goods like virtual reality experiences 
and AI‑generated art to new methods of production like virtual flm sets and 
3D‑printed fashion, to the opening of new markets through global stream‑
ing platforms and mobile gaming, to the conquest of new sources of supply 
through user‑generated content and sustainable materials, to new ways of 
organising industries through blockchain and the gig economy – the crea‑
tive sectors are constantly reinventing themselves and, in the process, reshap‑
ing our cultural and economic landscape. These innovations are not merely 
technological; they represent fundamental shifts in creating, distributing and 
consuming culture. They challenge traditional notions of authorship, owner‑
ship and value in the creative economy. Also, these industries blur the lines 
between creator and consumer, between professional and amateur, and be‑
tween diferent creative disciplines. 
The pace and scale of these changes present both opportunities and chal‑
lenges. On one hand, they ofer unprecedented opportunities for creativity, 
accessibility and diversity in cultural production. They allow for more per ‑
sonalised and immersive cultural experiences, and they open new avenues 
for creative expression and entrepreneurship. On the other hand, they raise 
important questions about the future of creative work, the ethics of AI and 
data‑driven creativity, the sustainability of new business models and the po‑
tential for technological disruption to exacerbate existing inequalities in the 
creative industries. 
 186 The Political Economy of the Creative and Cultural Industries 
For policymakers, the challenge will be to create regulatory frameworks 
that encourage innovation while protecting the rights of creators and the 
interests of consumers. For creative professionals, ongoing learning and 
adaptation will be crucial to navigate this rapidly changing landscape. For 
businesses, anticipating and quickly responding to technological and cultural 
shifts will be essential for survival and success. 
Ultimately, CCIs remind us that innovation is not just about technology 
but about reimagining how we express ourselves, tell our stories and make 
sense of the world around us. As these industries continue to evolve, they will 
undoubtedly play a crucial role in shaping our economy, culture and society.
