6 
CREATIVE AND CULTURAL INDUSTRIES 
AND PUBLIC POLICY 
6.1 Perspectives on Creative and Cultural Industries Policies: 
Rationales 
Cultural and creative industries (CCIs) can be robust drivers of growth, so‑
cial cohesion and national identity engines in today’s interconnected world. 
Governments worldwide have recognised this potential, crafting diverse pol‑
icy frameworks to nurture and harness the power of these industries. This 
chapter invites readers to explore the policy models to support CCIs, exam‑
ine the underlying rationales and assess the impact on creative sectors and 
broader socio‑economic landscapes. 
The rationale for public policies in CCIs is rooted in several economic and 
social considerations. While market mechanisms can drive innovation and 
efciency in many sectors, they often need to catch up in fully capturing the 
value and addressing the unique challenges of CCIs. This necessitates state 
intervention through well‑crafted public policies. 
One primary justifcation for government involvement is the presence of 
market failures in the creative sector. These failures occur when the free mar‑
ket fails to allocate resources efciently or fails to produce socially optimal 
outcomes (Towse, 2019). In CCIs, market failures can manifest in several 
ways: 
1 Information asymmetry: Consumers often need more complete informa‑
tion about the quality of cultural goods before consumption, leading to 
the undervaluation of creative products. 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
192 The Political Economy of the Creative and Cultural Industries 
2 Public goods characteristics: Many cultural products, once created, can be 
consumed by additional users at little or no extra cost, making it difcult 
for creators to capture the total value of their work. 
3 Natural monopolies: In some areas of cultural production, high fxed costs 
and low marginal costs can lead to natural monopolies, potentially stifing 
diversity and innovation. 
Another crucial aspect is the presence of positive externalities in CCIs. Ex‑
ternalities are the efects of an economic activity on unrelated third parties. 
In the case of CCIs, these are often positive, meaning they beneft society 
beyond the immediate consumers or producers. Examples include: 
1 Educational externalities: Exposure to arts and culture can enhance criti‑
cal thinking and creativity, benefting society beyond the cultural sector 
(Crossick & Kaszynska, 2016). 
2 Innovation spillovers: Creative industries often drive innovation that spills 
over into other sectors of the economy (Bakhshi & McVittie, 2009). 
3 Social cohesion: Cultural activities can foster social bonds and community 
identity, contributing to social stability and cohesion (Belfore & Bennett, 
2007). 
State intervention through public policies can help internalise these positive 
externalities, ensuring that the total social value of creative activities is rec‑
ognised and supported. 
Furthermore, public policies in CCIs aim to ensure that the economic po‑
tential of these industries translates into tangible social benefts. These ben‑
efts can include: 
1 Job creation: CCIs are often labour‑intensive and can create signifcant em‑
ployment opportunities, particularly for young people (UNESCO, 2022). 
2 Urban regeneration: Cultural initiatives can be crucial in revitalising ur ‑
ban areas, attracting investment and improving quality of life (Grodach & 
Loukaitou‑Sideris, 2007). 
3 Cultural diversity: Public support can help maintain and promote diverse 
cultural expressions that might not be commercially viable but are socially 
valuable (Throsby, 2010). 
4 Soft power: A thriving cultural sector can enhance a country’s interna‑
tional reputation and infuence (Nye, 2004). 
Policies can also create an environment where CCIs thrive economically and 
contribute signifcantly to societal well‑being by addressing market failures, 
internalising positive externalities and promoting tangible social benefts. 
This balanced approach recognises culture and creativity’s unique value as 
economic drivers and essential components of social progress. 
Creative and Cultural Industries and Public Policy 193
However, it’s important to note that designing effective policies for CCIs 
is complex and context‑dependent. Policymakers must carefully consider the 
specific characteristics of their cultural ecosystems, balancing economic ob‑
jectives with cultural and social goals. Different countries have developed 
varied strategies to support their CCIs in the last decades, reflecting their 
unique circumstances, priorities and challenges.
Policies can be defined as systematic courses of action governments pro‑
duce to address specific issues or achieve desired societal outcomes. They 
represent the government’s intentions and are implemented through various 
instruments such as laws, regulations, executive orders and funding priorities 
(Birkland, 2015). In the context of CCIs, public policies encompass vari‑
ous interventions designed to support, regulate and promote creative sectors, 
from tax incentives for film production to copyright laws protecting artistic 
works. These policies are formulated through complex processes involving 
multiple stakeholders, including policymakers, industry representatives, cul‑
tural institutions and civil society organisations.
The relevance of public policies and state participation in guiding mar ‑
ket forces towards social progress must be considered, particularly in CCIs. 
While market mechanisms can drive innovation and efficiency, they may only 
sometimes align with broader societal goals such as cultural preservation, 
equitable access to creative content or nurturing diverse artistic expressions 
(Throsby, 2018). State intervention through well‑crafted public policies can 
help correct market failures, internalise positive externalities generated by 
creative activities, and ensure that the economic potential of CCIs translates 
into tangible social benefits. By setting strategic frameworks and providing 
targeted support, governments can foster an environment where creative in‑
dustries thrive economically and contribute to social cohesion, cultural diver‑
sity and sustainable development (UNESCO, 2022). This balanced approach 
recognises the unique value of culture and creativity as economic drivers and 
essential components of societal well‑being.
BOX 6.1 POLICIES: WHAT, WHY AND HOW?
What: CCI policies are comprehensive strategic frameworks designed to nur -
ture, support and develop cultural and creative sectors.
Why: These policies are essential for:
1 Directing economic growth towards a holistic development strategy
2 Leveraging the unique value of CCIs for societal and cultural benefit
3 Fostering innovation and creativity across various sectors
4 Preserving and promoting cultural heritage while encouraging new forms 
of expression
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
194 The Political Economy of the Creative and Cultural Industries 
How: CCI policies are implemented through a multifaceted approach, 
including: 
1 Financial incentives (e.g., grants, tax breaks, subsidies) 
2 Regulatory frameworks (e.g., intellectual property protection, fair competi‑
tion laws) 
3 Educational initiatives (e.g., arts education, creative skills training) 
4 Infrastructure development (e.g., cultural spaces, digital platforms) 
5 International promotion and cultural diplomacy 
6 Public‑private partnerships 
7 Support for research and development in creative technologies 
Each nation tailors these elements to its specifc context, cultural strengths and 
long‑term goals. 
As we stand at the threshold of a new era in global cultural and economic 
dynamics, it is imperative to critically examine the policies that have shaped 
diferent approaches in CCIs over the past few decades. The turn of the mil‑
lennium ushered in a period of unprecedented globalisation, technological 
integration and homogenised consumption trends. While these forces unde‑
niably fostered economic growth and cultural exchange, they also gave rise 
to unforeseen challenges and deepening disparities. 
The frst cycle of global CCI policies, emerging in the late 1990s, was a 
product of its time. It embraced the promise of an interconnected world, 
where borders seemed to fade in the face of digital innovation and global 
markets. However, in our pursuit of growth and integration, we often over ‑
looked the importance of diversity and the persistence of inequalities. The 
result was a widening chasm of social and economic disparities, felt acutely 
in both the Global North and South. 
The year 2020 marked a pivotal moment in this trajectory. The global pan‑
demic disrupted our way of life and catalysed reassessment and change. It 
accelerated the integration of artifcial intelligence and information and com‑
munication technologies into our markets, reshaping the landscape of pro‑
duction and consumption. Moreover, it challenged our assumptions about 
globalisation and highlighted the need for more resilient, equitable systems. 
As the world navigates this new phase of the 21st century, the approach of 
policies for CCIs must evolve. We can only aford to pursue innovation and 
GDP growth with careful consideration of their broader impacts. The poten‑
tial for CCIs to exacerbate wage gaps, media monopolies and various forms of 
social divide – be it gender, race or digital – demands our attention and action. 
 
 
 
Creative and Cultural Industries and Public Policy 195 
As we stand at this crossroads in 2024, the creative industries ofer a vi‑
sion of how we might come together, reimagining our shared future through 
the lens of human ingenuity and cultural expression. The path forward must 
be clarifed and easy, but the creative industries have repeatedly shown their 
capacity for adaptation and innovation (UNESCO, 2022). As they navigate 
the challenges of this new landscape  –  from digital disruption to cultural 
wars – they continue to play a crucial role in shaping our understanding of 
ourselves and our world. In doing so, they refect our society and help shape 
its future, ofering new ways of seeing, thinking and being in an increasingly 
complex world. 
The following pages will ofer a panoramic fight around a few models of 
policies for CCIs and will highlight an overview of commonalities and difer‑
ences among these policies. 
6.2 A Global View of Policies for CCIs 
In this overview of CCI policies across 13 diverse countries, readers will gain 
insight into three crucial cultural policy and management aspects. The ex‑
ploration begins with examining funding models and sector prioritisation, 
revealing how nations balance public and private investment and which 
cultural sectors are receiving global attention. The discussion then lands on 
policy stability and eforts to address inequalities, highlighting the interplay 
between political environments, cultural policy consistency and various strat‑
egies to promote diversity and rectify historical imbalances. Finally, the anal‑
ysis turns to the organisational structures countries employ to oversee their 
cultural policies, from centralised ministries to specialised agencies, demon‑
strating how governmental approaches shape the development of cultural 
sectors. Throughout this journey, readers will encounter resumed and com‑
parative analyses of established cultural powerhouses and emerging play‑
ers in the global creative economy, uncovering key trends, unique national 
strategies, and the challenges countries face in nurturing their CCIs in an 
increasingly digital and globalised world. The proposal is a panoramic view 
and not an in‑depth analysis, so it can guide your future research to uncover 
specifcities if you want more information about one of these countries. 
6.2.1 Part 1: Model for Funding and Sectors Prioritised 
The funding models and sector prioritisation in CCIs refect each country’s 
economic structure, cultural heritage and policy priorities. Developed econo‑
mies tend to have more sophisticated hybrid models, balancing public and 
private investments, while emerging economies are still evolving their ap‑
proaches. This diversity in funding strategies highlights the complex nature 
of supporting cultural production in a globalised digital age. For instance, 
 
 
  
196 The Political Economy of the Creative and Cultural Industries 
France’s model of solid‑state support and public‑private partnerships exem‑
plifes the European tradition of viewing culture as a public good. This ap‑
proach has allowed France to maintain a robust flm industry and protect its 
cultural heritage. In contrast, the United States’ primarily private sector‑driven 
model refects its market‑oriented economy, resulting in globally dominant 
entertainment industries but potentially underserving less commercially vi‑
able cultural sectors. 
Interestingly, despite varied economic and cultural backgrounds, there’s a 
noticeable convergence in the prioritised sectors across countries. This trend 
suggests a global recognition of specifc industries – particularly flm, digital 
media and music – as critical drivers of cultural expression and economic 
growth in the 21st century. 
South Korea’s focus on K‑pop, flm, gaming and animation showcases a 
strategic approach to leveraging cultural soft power for economic gain. The 
global success of Korean popular culture demonstrates how targeted support 
for specifc sectors can yield signifcant returns. Similarly, India’s emphasis on 
its flm industry (Bollywood) capitalises on a long‑standing cultural strength 
while adapting to new digital platforms (Table 6.1).

The comparison reveals that while most countries employ a hybrid fund‑

nifcantly. Countries like France and South Korea demonstrate strong 
state support, while the United States and India lean more towards private 
sector‑driven models. This spectrum refects difering philosophies on the role 
of government in cultural production and preservation. Colombia’s emphasis 
on the “Orange Economy” represents an innovative approach to conceptu‑
alising and supporting CCIs, particularly in a developing economy context. 
The Latin American country aims to attract investment and foster digital 
content and cultural tourism growth by framing creative industries as a criti‑
cal economic sector. 
6.2.2 Part 2: Policy Stability and Actions to Address Inequalities 
Policy stability in the CCI sector is closely tied to each country’s broader 
political and economic stability. Nations with longer‑standing democratic 
traditions or centralised governance tend to exhibit more consistent cultural 
policies. However, this stability only sometimes correlates with the efective‑
ness or progressiveness of these policies. The challenge lies in maintaining 
policy continuity while adapting to rapidly changing technological and social 
landscapes that impact cultural production and consumption. 
China’s stable policy environment, aligned with its Five‑Year Plans, allows 
for long‑term strategic development of cultural industries. This approach has 
enabled signifcant growth in digital media and cultural tourism. However, 
it also raises questions about the balance between state control and artistic 
freedom. In contrast, Brazil’s fuctuating policies refect its complex political 
landscape. The Lei Rouanet (Rouanet Law), a signifcant cultural funding 
 
   
198 The Political Economy of the Creative and Cultural Industries 
mechanism, has undergone multiple changes with diferent administrations, 
creating uncertainty in the sector. Despite these challenges, Brazil’s commit‑
ment to programmes like Cultura Viva demonstrates an ongoing efort to 
democratise culture amid political changes. 
Addressing inequalities within the cultural sector is common across most 
countries, refecting a growing awareness of the importance of diverse repre‑
sentation in artistic expression. However, the approaches vary widely, from 
quota systems to targeted funding programmes, demonstrating that there is 
no one‑size‑fts‑all solution to this complex issue. 
Canada’s strong focus on supporting Indigenous arts and regional devel‑
opment stands out as a comprehensive approach to addressing historical in‑
equalities and promoting cultural diversity. This policy direction supports 
underrepresented voices and enriches the country’s overall cultural landscape 
(Table 6.2).

6.2.3 Part 3: Leading Policy Organisms and Dedicated CCI 
Institutions 
The organisational structure of cultural policy administration provides in‑
sight into how diferent countries prioritise and manage their CCIs. A dedi‑
cated ministry or department for culture is almost universal, indicating the 
recognised importance of this sector. However, the scope and infuence of 
these bodies vary considerably, refecting diferences in governmental struc‑
tures and the perceived role of culture in national development. 
The United Kingdom’s Department for Digital, Culture, Media & Sport 
(DCMS) exemplifes a comprehensive approach, integrating traditional cul‑
tural sectors with digital media and sports. This structure refects the United 
Kingdom’s recognition of the interconnected nature of these felds in the mod‑
ern economy and their collective contribution to the country’s soft power 
and creative industries. In contrast, the United States stands out because it 
lacks a centralised federal body for cultural policy. The limited roles of the 
National Endowment for the Arts (NEA) and National Endowment for the 
 
  
200 The Political Economy of the Creative and Cultural Industries 
Humanities (NEH) refect a more decentralised approach, with signifcant re‑
sponsibility falling to state‑level initiatives and the private sector. This model 
has led to a diverse and dynamic cultural landscape but can also result in 
uneven support across regions and disciplines. 
Establishing dedicated CCI institutions represents a more focused ap‑
proach to nurturing specifc sectors within the broader cultural landscape. 
These institutions often refect national priorities and strengths, such as flm 
agencies in countries with solid cinematic traditions. 
France’s Centre National du Cinéma et de l’Image Animée (CNC) is a 
prime example of a specialised institution that has played a crucial role in 
maintaining the country’s infuential position in global cinema. The CNC’s 
comprehensive support for flm production, distribution and preservation has 
been instrumental in sustaining France’s unique cinematic culture. Similarly, 
South Korea’s Korea Creative Content Agency (KOCCA) has been pivotal in 
the global success of Korean popular culture. KOCCA has helped position 
South Korea as a major exporter of cultural content by providing targeted 
support for sectors like K‑pop, animation and gaming (Table 6.3).

In conclusion, examining CCI policies across various countries reveals 
a complex global landscape characterised by diverse approaches, shared 
challenges and evolving strategies. From the market‑oriented models of the 
United Kingdom and the United States to the more state‑centric approaches 
of France and China and the hybrid systems seen in countries like Brazil, 
South Korea and Canada, it’s clear that there is no one‑size‑fts‑all solution 
to fostering vibrant and sustainable creative economies. Each nation grapples 
with balancing economic imperatives with cultural preservation, addressing 
regional disparities and adapting to rapid technological changes. The varying 
degrees of policy stability and eforts to address inequalities further under ‑
score the intricate nature of developing efective CCI policies. 
As we look to the future, it is evident that CCIs will continue to play a cru‑
cial role in shaping national identities, driving economic growth and fostering

social cohesion. The global trends identifed in this chapter – including the 
push for more inclusive and equitable policies, the need for adaptability in the 
face of digital transformation, and the growing recognition of CCIs’ contribu‑

must become more holistic, fexible and responsive to both local needs and 
global dynamics. Policymakers, industry leaders and cultural practitioners 
worldwide must collaborate to create frameworks that drive innovation and

contribute to the overall well‑being of societies. As CCIs continue to evolve, 
so must the policies supporting them, ensuring that creativity remains a pow‑
