THE CHALLENGES AND 
OPPORTUNITIES IN THE 
MEASUREMENT OF CREATIVE AND 
CULTURAL INDUSTRIES  
AND ECONOMIC DEVELOPMENT 
In an increasingly interconnected global economy, the role of creative and cul‑
tural industries (CCIs) in driving economic development has raised signifcant 
attention from policymakers, economists and cultural practitioners alike. As 
nations strive to diversify their economic bases and tap into the potential of 
the knowledge economy, CCIs have emerged as a promising sector capable of 
generating both monetary value and social impact (Throsby, 2008; UNESCO, 
2018). However, quantifying the precise contribution of CCIs to economic de‑
velopment presents a complex challenge. Traditional economic indicators, such 
as Gross Domestic Product (GDP), often fail to capture the total value created 
by creative and cultural activities (Bakhshi et al., 2015; Cunningham & Potts, 
2015). This chapter approaches the intricate relationship between CCIs and 
economic development, examining available data and analytical approaches. 
Our exploration begins with a critical assessment of conventional eco‑
nomic metrics, highlighting their limitations in measuring the multifaceted 
impact of CCIs. The need for more GDP as a sole measure of economic 
well‑being has been widely recognised (Stiglitz et al., 2009), and this short‑
coming is particularly acute when it comes to capturing the value of cul‑
tural and creative activities. The chapter examines alternative approaches 
and complementary indicators that provide a more comprehensive picture 
of CCIs’ economic and social contributions. Also, it brings attention to the 
diverse data sources on CCIs worldwide and gives a global overview of 
the sector’s fnancial footprint. This comprehensive approach allows us to 
paint a more accurate picture of CCIs’ contribution to national and regional 
economies, drawing on data from international organisations such as UN‑
ESCO and UNCTAD, national statistical ofces (NSOs) and industry reports 
(UNCTAD, 2021; UNESCO, 2022). 
 
 
204 The Political Economy of the Creative and Cultural Industries 
This chapter also presents two examples of research aimed at developing 
innovations in measuring CCIs to illustrate the practical aspects of knowl‑
edge exchange between research and policy. These examples, conducted by 
the author of this book, ground our analysis in real‑world contexts. The 
frst case study examines the interplay between GDP and creative occupa‑
tions in Brazil. The second explores the Relative Values Project, an innova‑
tive research initiative representing a signifcant step towards developing a 
more nuanced understanding of CCIs’ socio‑economic impacts. The Relative 
Values Project, through a co‑creative partnership with arts organisations in 
Britain and Brazil, aimed to construct a new narrative about the transforma‑
tional change generated by cultural activities. By building multidimensional 
indicators in collaboration with arts organisations, the project ofers a fresh 
perspective on measuring CCIs’ impact beyond traditional economic metrics. 
This chapter aims to go beyond mere statistical analysis, delving into the 
qualitative aspects of CCIs’ impact on economic development. Doing so 
seeks to provide a more holistic understanding of the sector’s role in shap‑
ing modern economies and societies. This approach aligns with recent schol‑
arship emphasising the need for a more nuanced understanding of cultural 
value and its relationship to economic outcomes (Throsby & Zednik, 2014). 
Navigating this complex landscape, we will also identify gaps in current data 
and methodologies, highlighting areas where further research and more re‑
fned measurement tools are needed. This critical approach will contribute to 
more accurately assessing CCIs’ economic impact and inform future policy 
decisions and research directions in this vital feld. 
The chapter concludes by discussing the policy implications of our fnd‑
ings and proposing directions for future research. By comprehensively ana‑
lysing the available data on CCIs and economic development, the aim is to 
equip policymakers, researchers and industry practitioners with the insights 
needed to foster a thriving creative economy in diverse global contexts. 
7.1 Limitations of GDP and Quantitative Metrics 
The Evolution and Strengths of GDP (Gross Domestic Product) 
GDP is the total monetary value of all fnished goods and services pro‑
duced within a country’s borders in a specifc time, typically a year or quarter. 
It can be calculated using three main approaches: the production approach, 
the income approach and the expenditure approach (Landefeld et al., 2008). 
The development of GDP represented a signifcant advancement in economic 
measurement, providing a standardised, comprehensive and simple tool for 
comparing economic growth across countries and guiding economic policy. 
The strengths of GDP as an economic indicator are numerous. It ofers a 
uniform measure for comparisons across countries and periods, attempts to 
capture all economic activity within a nation’s borders and distils complex 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
Challenges and Opportunities in Creative and Cultural Industries Measurement 205 
fnancial data into a single, easily understood fgure. Governments and in‑
ternational organisations use GDP as a critical indicator for economic poli‑
cymaking, including monetary and fscal policy decisions. At the same time, 
businesses rely on GDP forecasts for strategic planning and investment deci‑
sions (Coyle, 2014). 
7.1.1 Early Critiques and Adaptations 
Even as GDP gained prominence, economists recognised its limitations. Si‑
mon Kuznets warned against using GDP as a measure of welfare, noting that 
“the welfare of a nation can scarcely be inferred from a measure of national 
income” (Kuznets, 1934). In the 1970s, Nordhaus and Tobin proposed ad‑
justments to GDP for leisure time, unpaid work and environmental dam‑
age, introducing their Measure of Economic Welfare (MEW) (Nordhaus & 
Tobin, 1972). Around the same time, Richard Easterlin’s research highlighted 
the disconnect between rising GDP and stagnant happiness levels in devel‑
oped countries, sparking debates about the relationship between economic 
growth and well‑being (Easterlin, 1974). 
7 .1.1.1 Limitations of GDP in Capturing Cultural and Creative Value 
As the importance of CCIs has grown, the shortcomings of GDP in refecting 
their actual value have become more pronounced. These limitations can be 
categorised into several key areas: 
1 Exclusion of Non‑Market Activities 
GDP primarily focuses on market transactions, overlooking many 
cultural activities that don’t involve direct monetary exchange. This 
omission is particularly problematic for CCIs, where signifcant value 
is often created outside traditional market structures. Amateur arts and 
crafts, community festivals, volunteer work in cultural organisations, 
open‑source creative projects and peer‑to‑peer sharing of cultural con‑
tent contribute to cultural richness. Still, they are largely invisible in 
GDP calculations. 
Throsby (2001) argues that these non‑market activities often form the 
foundation of a society’s cultural capital. He proposes the concept of “cul‑
tural capital” as a complement to traditional economic measures, encom‑
passing both tangible assets (like heritage buildings) and intangible ones 
(like conventional knowledge and practices). Klamer (2017) goes further, 
suggesting that the most valuable aspects of culture often lie outside the 
market sphere. He introduces the concept of the “value‑based economy,” 
where cultural and social values are given equal weight to economic ones 
in assessing societal progress. 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
206 The Political Economy of the Creative and Cultural Industries 
2 Inability to Measure Quality 
GDP is inherently quantity‑focused, failing to diferentiate between high‑
and low‑quality cultural products. This quantitative bias can lead to sig‑
nifcant distortions in valuing cultural and creative outputs. For example, 
mass‑produced art might contribute more to GDP than a single, highly in‑
fuential original artwork, and blockbuster flms with signifcant box ofce 
returns would be valued more in GDP terms than lower‑budget, critically 
acclaimed flms that may have more signifcant cultural impact (Klamer, 
2017). Bakhshi et al. (2015) highlight the need for new metrics to capture 
the qualitative aspects of cultural production. They propose approaches 
like expert evaluations, audience impact assessments and longitudinal stud‑
ies of cultural infuence to complement traditional economic measures. 
3 Disregard for Long‑Term Value 
The long‑term cultural and social impacts of creative works often fail to 
be refected in GDP calculations, which typically focus on annual or quar‑
terly economic activity. This short‑term focus fails to capture the endur ‑
ing value of many cultural and creative outputs, such as cultural heritage’s 
educational value, art’s inspirational efects on future generations and the 
long‑term infuence of innovative designs (Bakhshi et  al., 2015). Throsby 
(2010) introduces the concept of “cultural sustainability,” arguing that 
the preservation and development of cultural capital should be considered 
alongside economic and environmental sustainability in policymaking. 
4 Neglect of Digital Value 
The digital revolution has dramatically changed how cultural content 
is created, distributed and consumed. GDP struggles to account for many 
aspects of the digital creative economy, signifcantly undervaluing digital 
cultural production. Free digital content, user‑generated content, value 
created through data collection and analysis and new digital cultural ex‑
periences all present challenges for traditional economic measurement 
(OECD, 2018). 
Bakhshi and Throsby (2012) explore the concept of “cultural value 
in the digital age,” arguing for new frameworks that can capture the 
unique ways digital technologies enable cultural creation, distribution and 
consumption. 
5 Failure to Capture Externalities 
CCIs often generate positive externalities that beneft society but are 
not refected in market transactions. These spillover efects, such as en‑
hanced social cohesion, improved mental health and well‑being, increased 
tourism, innovation spillovers, urban regeneration and educational ben‑
efts, remain largely invisible in GDP calculations (Throsby, 2010). 
Sacco et al. (2013) developed the concept of “culture 3.0,” emphasis‑
ing the role of culture as a platform for social and economic development. 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
Challenges and Opportunities in Creative and Cultural Industries Measurement 207 
They argue that traditional fnancial measures fail to capture the trans‑
formative potential of culture in areas like social innovation, lifelong 
learning and sustainable development. 
6 Inability to Refect Cultural Diversity 
GDP treats all economic activity equally, failing to account for the 
importance of cultural diversity. This can lead to policies prioritising 
economic growth at the expense of cultural richness and diversity. The 
undervaluation of minority cultural expressions, traditional cultural prac‑
tices and diverse local cultural productions in GDP calculations poses 
signifcant challenges to maintaining and promoting cultural diversity 
(UNESCO, 2009). 
Yúdice (2003) argues for a broader understanding of culture as a resource 
for social and economic development, emphasising the need to value diverse 
cultural expressions and practices in an increasingly globalised world. 
7.2 The Need for Qualitative and Alternative Metrics 
Recognising the limitations of Gross Domestic Product (GDP) in capturing the 
full value of CCIs, researchers and policymakers have developed various alterna‑
tive metrics. These approaches aim to provide a more comprehensive and nu‑
anced understanding of the cultural and creative sectors’ contributions to society 
and the economy (Throsby, 2008). This paper synthesises fve critical categories 
of alternative metrics: cultural participation indicators, cultural vitality indices, 
creative economy frameworks, well‑being indicators and innovation metrics. 
7.2.1 Cultural Participation Indicators 
Cultural participation indicators measure engagement with cultural activities, 
ofering insight into the reach and impact of CCIs. UNESCO’s Framework 
for Cultural Statistics (2009) provides guidelines for collecting and analys‑
ing these indicators, emphasising both active and passive forms of cultural 
participation. Key metrics include attendance rates at cultural events, time 
spent on creative pursuits, digital engagement with cultural content, cultural 
education participation and media consumption patterns. 
These indicators provide a more direct measure of how artistic and crea‑
tive outputs are consumed and valued by society, addressing some of the 
limitations of GDP in capturing non‑market cultural activities (Crossick & 
Kaszynska, 2016). 
7.2.2 Cultural Vitality Indices 
Cultural vitality indices are composite measures that assess the overall health 
of a community’s cultural ecosystem. Jackson et  al. (2006) developed a 
comprehensive framework for measuring cultural vitality in communities, 
 
 
 
208 The Political Economy of the Creative and Cultural Industries 
emphasising three main dimensions: opportunities for cultural participation, 
cultural participation itself and support for cultural activities. 
Components often included in cultural vitality indices are: 
Presence of cultural institutions 
Availability of cultural education 
Support for local artists and creators 
Cultural diversity measures 
Community engagement in culture 
Markusen and Gadwa (2010) build on this work, proposing the concept of 
“creative placemaking” to understand and measure how cultural activities 
contribute to community development and quality of life. 
7.2.3 Creative Economy Frameworks 
Creative economy frameworks ofer holistic approaches that consider the 
broader economic impacts of creativity, including direct, indirect and induced 
efects across various economic sectors. The UNCTAD Creative Economy 
Report series (2008, 2010, 2013) has been instrumental in developing and 
promoting these broader frameworks, emphasising the interconnectedness of 
culture, economy and technology in driving creative sector growth. 
Critical components of creative economy frameworks include: 
Direct economic contributions of CCIs 
Indirect efects on other sectors 
Induced efects through increased spending 
Creative intensity of occupations 
Innovation metrics related to creative activities 
These frameworks attempt to capture the total economic impact of CCIs, 
addressing GDP’s limitation in measuring spillover efects and intersectoral 
infuences (Potts & Cunningham, 2008). 
7 .2.3.1 Well‑being Indicators 
Well‑being indicators attempt to capture the contribution of culture and 
creativity to the overall quality of life, recognising that the value of cultural 
activities extends beyond economic measures to include social, psychologi‑
cal and community benefts. The New Zealand Treasury’s Living Standards 
Framework (2018), for example, incorporates cultural identity as one of its 
crucial well‑being domains, including indicators such as a sense of belonging, 
the ability to express identity and engagement with artistic practices. 
 
 
 
 
Challenges and Opportunities in Creative and Cultural Industries Measurement 209 
Fujiwara et al. (2014) researched the well‑being value of culture in the 
United Kingdom, using large‑scale survey data to monetise the benefts of 
cultural participation. Their fndings suggest that the well‑being value of cul‑
tural engagement often far exceeds the market value captured by traditional 
economic measures. 
The OECD’s Better Life Index includes measures of civic engagement and 
social connections, which are often infuenced by cultural participation. This 
provides a more holistic view of societal progress beyond GDP (OECD, 2020). 
7 .2.3.2 Innovation Metrics 
Innovation metrics measure the role of CCIs in driving innovation across the 
economy, recognising that creative activities often spark new ideas, processes 
and technologies that beneft multiple sectors. Bakhshi et al. (2008) demon‑
strate how creative industries support innovation on the broader economy, 
emphasising the need for metrics that capture these spillover efects. 
Key innovation metrics related to CCIs include: 
Patents and trademarks in creative felds 
Cross‑sector collaboration rates 
Adoption of creative technologies 
Creative problem‑solving methodologies 
Cultural and creative education in STEM felds (STEAM approach) 
The European Commission’s Cultural and Creative Cities Monitor (2019) in‑
cludes innovation metrics in its assessment of cultural and creative city perfor‑
mance, recognising the crucial role of CCIs in driving urban innovation and 
economic development. Cunningham and Potts (2015) propose a four‑model 
framework for understanding the relationship between creative industries 
and innovation. They argue that the innovation model is increasingly domi‑
nant, necessitating new metrics to capture these complex relationships. 
Developing these alternative metrics represents a signifcant advancement 
in our ability to measure and understand the total value of CCIs. By ad‑
dressing many of GDP’s limitations, these new approaches provide a more 
comprehensive picture of the cultural, social and economic impacts of CCIs. 
However, challenges still need to be solved in data collection, standardisation 
and integration of these metrics into policy‑making processes. 
Future research should focus on refning these metrics, improving data col‑
lection methodologies and developing frameworks to integrate these diverse 
measures into cohesive assessments of CCI value. Additionally, policymakers 
and industry stakeholders should increase the adoption of these alternative 
metrics to ensure that the total value of CCIs is recognised and supported in 
economic and cultural policy decisions. 
 
  
 
 
 
 
210 The Political Economy of the Creative and Cultural Industries 
7.3 Challenges in Measuring Intangible Assets and Spillover 
Efects 
The CCIs have gained increasing recognition for their economic and social 
contributions. However, accurately measuring their value remains a sig‑
nifcant challenge due to the unique characteristics of cultural and creative 
production. Below, you will fnd the four critical difculties in quantifying in‑
tangible assets and externalities in CCIs, focusing on four main areas: intan‑
gible assets, knowledge spillovers, network efects and cultural externalities. 
7.3.1 Intangible Assets 
CCIs rely heavily on intangible assets, which are notoriously difcult to 
quantify using traditional economic measures. This reliance systematically 
undervalues creative sectors in national accounts and fnancial statements 
(Haskel & Westlake, 2018). The challenges in measuring intangible assets in 
CCIs span several categories, including intellectual property (IP) valuation, 
brand value and reputation, human capital and organisational culture. 
IP valuation in CCIs is particularly complex due to the difculty in pre‑
dicting future earnings from copyrights, trademarks and design rights. The 
variability in the lifespan and impact of creative works further complicates 
this process. Similarly, brand value and reputation in creative industries are 
highly intangible and subject to rapid fuctuations, making them challenging 
to measure accurately. 
Human capital in CCIs, comprising skills, creativity and know‑how, presents 
its own measurement challenges. The implicit nature of much creative knowl‑
edge and the rapid evolution of skills in these sectors make it difcult to quan‑
tify this crucial asset. Additionally, the value of collaborative creativity, which is 
often central to CCIs, takes a lot of work to measure using traditional metrics. 
Organisational culture and creative ecosystems also contribute signif‑
cantly to the value of CCIs but are inherently intangible. The innovative 
potential of creative organisations and the value of creative networks and 
clusters are examples of assets crucial to CCIs but challenging to quantify. 
Haskel and Westlake (2018) argue that traditional accounting and eco‑
nomic measures need to be equipped to capture the value of these intangible 
assets. They propose several approaches to address this issue, including de‑
veloping new accounting standards for intangible assets, creating satellite ac‑
counts for intangibles in national economic statistics and using big data and 
AI to track the creation and use of intangible assets. 
7.3.2 Knowledge Spillovers 
Ideas and innovations from CCIs often difuse into other sectors, creating 
value that is difcult to track and attribute. This difusion process, usu‑
ally informal and occurring through multiple channels, presents signifcant 
 
 
Challenges and Opportunities in Creative and Cultural Industries Measurement 211 
measurement challenges (Bakhshi et al., 2008). One of the primary difcul‑
ties in measuring knowledge spillovers from CCIs is attribution. Tracing the 
origin of creative ideas as they spread across sectors and quantifying the 
contribution of creative inputs to innovations in other felds is a complex 
task. This challenge is compounded by the often‑lengthy time lag between 
introducing innovative ideas and their impact in diferent sectors, making it 
challenging to link current economic outcomes to past creative investments. 
The informal nature of knowledge transfer in creative sectors adds another 
layer of complexity. Tacit knowledge exchange, often facilitated through so‑
cial networks and creative clusters, is signifcant in CCIs but challenging to 
track through traditional economic metrics. Furthermore, the wide‑ranging 
infuence of innovative ideas across diverse industries makes it challenging 
to measure how creative methodologies (e.g., design thinking) impact other 
sectors. 
To address these challenges, Bakhshi et  al. (2008) propose several ap‑
proaches, including developing more sophisticated input–output models to 
track creative inputs across the economy, using network analysis to map 
knowledge fows between creative and non‑creative sectors, and conducting 
longitudinal studies to track the long‑term impacts of creative innovations. 
7 .3.2.1 Network Efects 
The value created through creative clusters and ecosystems often exceeds the 
sum of individual contributions, known as network efects. These synergistic 
efects are difcult to measure using traditional economic metrics, leading to 
the potential undervaluation of creative hubs and cultural districts (Pratt & 
Jefcutt, 2009). 
Measuring network efects in CCIs involves several challenges. Quanti‑
fying informal collaboration and knowledge exchange between co‑located 
creative businesses is complex, as is assessing the collective drawing power of 
innovative clusters. While signifcant, the cumulative attraction of talent and 
investment to creative hubs is difcult to attribute directly to the presence of 
creative ecosystems. 
Enhanced innovation through knowledge exchange within creative net‑
works is another crucial aspect of network efects that poses measurement 
challenges. Tracking the fow of ideas within these networks and quantifying 
the innovation premium of creative cluster participation require new meth‑
odological approaches. 
Pratt and Jefcutt (2009) emphasise the complexity of these network ef‑
fects and propose a “cultural ecosystem” approach that considers the inter ‑
dependencies between various actors in creative clusters. Comunian (2011) 
suggests using social network analysis techniques to map and quantify the 
complex relationships within creative ecosystems, providing a more nuanced 
understanding of how value is created and distributed in these networks. 
 
 
212 The Political Economy of the Creative and Cultural Industries 
7.3.3 Cultural Externalities 
The broader societal benefts of cultural activities, or cultural externalities, 
represent a signifcant portion of the value created by CCIs. However, these 
benefts are often intangible and challenging to quantify, remaining largely 
invisible in traditional economic measures (Throsby, 2010). 
Cultural externalities include enhanced national or local identity, im‑
proved intercultural understanding, educational and cognitive benefts, and 
health and well‑being impacts. Measuring these efects presents numerous 
challenges. For instance, quantifying the economic value of a stronger cul‑
tural identity or assessing the long‑term benefts of cultural heritage preserva‑
tion is complex and often subjective. 
While widely recognised, cultural engagement’s educational and cognitive 
benefts are difcult to measure in economic terms. This includes challenges 
in quantifying how cultural engagement contributes to lifelong learning and 
assessing the spillover efects of creative skills on other domains. Similarly, 
while cultural participation has been linked to health and well‑being benefts, 
measuring healthcare cost savings or quantifying mental health improve‑
ments presents signifcant methodological challenges. 
Throsby (2010) proposes several approaches to better capture these cul‑
tural externalities, including using contingent valuation methods to estimate 
willingness to pay for cultural benefts, developing cultural satellite accounts 
to track non‑market cultural activities and employing multi‑criteria analysis 
to assess the diverse impacts of cultural investments. 
The challenges in measuring intangible assets and externalities in CCIs 
are multifaceted and complex. Traditional economic metrics and accounting 
practices often need to be revised to capture the full value created by these 
sectors. Addressing these challenges requires interdisciplinary approaches 
that combine economic analysis with insights from cultural studies, sociol‑
ogy, psychology and other relevant felds. 
As the creative economy continues to grow in importance, refning our 
ability to measure and value these intangible assets and externalities becomes 
increasingly crucial. This will provide a more accurate picture of the eco‑
nomic contribution of CCIs and inform better policymaking and investment 
decisions in these vital sectors. Future research should focus on developing 
and refning new methodologies that can capture the unique value‑creation 
processes in CCIs, ensuring that the full impact of these industries is recog‑
nised and supported. 
The limitations of GDP and traditional quantitative metrics in captur ‑
ing the total value of CCIs are signifcant and multifaceted. From excluding 
non‑market activities to the inability to measure quality, long‑term value and 
digital contributions, GDP falls short of providing a comprehensive picture 
of the cultural and creative economy. Developing and adopting alternative 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
Challenges and Opportunities in Creative and Cultural Industries Measurement 213 
metrics for measuring the impact of CCIs is a challenging yet crucial en‑
deavour. These new metrics are essential for several interconnected reasons. 
Firstly, they are vital in informing evidence‑based policymaking by provid‑
ing more accurate and comprehensive measures of CCIs’ contributions, 
leading to better‑targeted policies and support mechanisms for the sector. 
Secondly, they allow for a more precise assessment of CCIs’ contribution to 
development. They ofer a fuller understanding of how culture and creativity 
drive social and economic progress, which can inform broader development 
strategies. 
Moreover, these alternative metrics are instrumental in justifying public 
and private investment in CCIs. By demonstrating the wide‑ranging im‑
pacts of cultural and creative initiatives, they can help secure much‑needed 
funding and support. Additionally, new metrics are crucial for recognising 
diverse forms of value creation and acknowledging and promoting the vari‑
ous ways CCIs contribute to society beyond mere economic output. Lastly, 
developing these metrics signifcantly aligns economic measures with soci‑
etal goals. By capturing a broader range of cultural and social values, they 
can help ensure that monetary policies are more closely attuned to overall 
societal well‑being. In essence, adopting alternative metrics for CCIs is not 
just about better measurement but about fostering a more holistic and nu‑
anced understanding of the role of culture and creativity in our societies 
and economies. 
As economies evolve, with intangible assets and creative capital playing 
increasingly central roles, the need for more sophisticated measurement ap‑
proaches becomes more pressing. Future research and policy eforts should 
focus on: 
• Developing robust, comprehensive frameworks that capture the full spec‑
trum of value created by CCIs. 
• Integrating qualitative and quantitative approaches to provide a more nu‑
anced understanding of cultural and creative impacts. 
• Leveraging new technologies, such as big data analytics and AI, to track 
and measure the complex interactions within creative ecosystems. 
• Fostering interdisciplinary collaboration between economists, cultural 
theorists, data scientists and policymakers to develop innovative measure‑
ment methodologies. 
• Promoting international standardisation of new metrics to enable mean‑
ingful comparisons and benchmarking across countries and regions. 
By addressing these challenges and embracing new approaches to measuring 
the value of CCIs, societies can ensure that the vital contributions of culture 
and creativity to our societies and economies are fully recognised, supported 
and nurtured. 
 
 
 
  
 
 
  
 
 
214 The Political Economy of the Creative and Cultural Industries 
7.4 Sources of Data in CCIs Worldwide 
Collecting and analysing data on CCIs is a complex and multifaceted en‑
deavour involving various sources and methodologies. This section explores 
the primary data sources for CCIs worldwide, examining their strengths, 
limitations and challenges in collecting and harmonising data across diferent 
contexts. Below, you can fnd an overview of the primary sources of data for 
CCIs that can be useful for research and policymaking: 
7.4.1 International Organisations 
International organisations are crucial in collecting, analysing and dissemi‑
nating data on CCIs worldwide. Their work often provides the foundation 
for cross‑country comparisons and global analysis, shaping policy discus‑
sions and research agendas. 
7 .4.1.1 UNESCO (United Nations Educational, Scientifc and Cultural 
Organisation) 
UNESCO has been at the forefront of eforts to measure and analyse cultural 
activities globally, providing crucial frameworks and methodologies for un‑
derstanding CCIs. 
Key contributions: 
• Framework for Cultural Statistics (FCS): First developed in 1986 and re‑
vised in 2009, the FCS provides a conceptual foundation for defning and 
measuring cultural activities (UNESCO, 2009). The FCS defnes six cul‑
tural domains (Cultural and Natural Heritage; Performance and Celebra‑
tion; Visual Arts and Crafts; Books and Press; Audiovisual and Interactive 
Media; Design and Creative Services) and two related domains (Tourism, 
Sports and Recreation). 
• Culture for Development Indicators (CDIS): A suite of 22 indicators meas‑
uring the contribution of culture to sustainable development across seven 
dimensions: Economy, Education, Governance, Social Participation, Gen‑
der Equality, Communication, and Heritage (UNESCO, 2014). For ex‑
ample, the CDIS measures the contribution of cultural activities to GDP, 
cultural employment and household expenditure on culture. 
• Periodic Reports on the Convention on the Protection and Promotion of the 
Diversity of Cultural Expressions: These quadrennial reports provide valua‑
ble data on policies and measures implemented by member states to support 
CCIs. They cover cultural policies, international cooperation, preferential 
treatment and integration of culture in sustainable development policies. 
 
  
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
Challenges and Opportunities in Creative and Cultural Industries Measurement 215 
• Institute for Statistics (UIS): UNESCO’s statistical arm produces 
global databases on cultural employment, trade in cultural goods 
and services and cultural participation. For instance, the UIS data on 
feature films provides information on the number of national films 
produced, the market share of domestic films and cinema admissions 
across countries. 
Strengths: 
• Comprehensive coverage of cultural domains, providing a holistic view 
of CCIs 
• Standardised defnitions and methodologies, enabling cross‑country 
comparisons 
• Global scope and participation of member states, ofering a truly interna‑
tional perspective 
• Integration of culture into broader development frameworks, highlighting 
CCIs’ wider societal impacts 
Limitations: 
• Reliance on national data sources of varying quality, potentially afecting 
data consistency 
• Challenges in ensuring timely and regular data updates due to resource 
constraints and varying national capacities 
• Limited granularity in some areas of CCIs, particularly emerging digital 
subsectors 
• Difculties in capturing informal cultural activities, which are signifcant 
in many developing countries 
Furthermore, UNESCO’s Culture|2030 Indicators UNESCO has recently 
developed the Culture|2030 Indicators framework to monitor the contri‑
bution of culture to the implementation of the UN 2030 Agenda for Sus‑
tainable Development. This framework includes 22 indicators across four 
thematic dimensions: Environment and Resilience, Prosperity and Liveli‑
hoods, Knowledge and Skills, and Inclusion and Participation. For example, 
under Prosperity and Livelihoods, the framework measures cultural employ‑
ment, cultural businesses, household expenditure on culture, and trade in 
cultural goods and services. This initiative demonstrates UNESCO’s ongoing 
eforts to integrate cultural data into broader development metrics and to 
provide policy‑relevant information on CCIs’ contributions to sustainable 
development. 
 
  
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
216 The Political Economy of the Creative and Cultural Industries 
7 .4.1.2 UNCTAD (United Nations Conference on Trade and 
Development) 
UNCTAD has been instrumental in highlighting the economic potential of 
CCIs, particularly in developing countries, through its focus on trade in crea‑
tive goods and services. 
Key contributions: 
• Creative Economy Reports: Published in collaboration with UNDP, these re‑
ports provide a global analysis of trade in creative goods and services (UNC‑
TAD, 2008, 2010, 2018). The reports classify creative industries into nine 
categories: Traditional Cultural Expressions, Performing Arts, Audiovisuals, 
New Media, Creative Services, Design, Publishing, Visual Arts, and Crafts. 
• Creative Economy Network: An online platform for sharing data, best 
practices and analysis on the creative economy. It facilitates knowledge 
exchange among policymakers, researchers and creative professionals. 
• Creative Economy Outlook: Regular publications update trends in the 
creative goods and services trade. For example, according to UNCTAD’s 
Creative Economy Outlook 2022, the global market for creative goods 
has shown signifcant growth. The report indicates that the value of the 
international creative goods market reached $715 billion in 2020, despite 
the challenges posed by the COVID‑19 pandemic. This represents a sub‑
stantial increase from the $509 billion reported in 2015. 
• Global Database on Creative Economy: UNCTAD maintains a compre‑
hensive database on international trade in creative goods and services, 
covering over 200 countries and territories. Data on creative goods goes 
back to 2002 and on creative services to 2006. 
Strengths: 
• Focus on international trade in creative goods and services, providing cru‑
cial data on the global creative economy 
• Emphasis on developing countries and emerging markets, highlighting the 
potential of CCIs for economic development 
• Consistent time series data on creative exports and imports, enabling 
trend analysis 
• Detailed product‑level data, allowing for granular analysis of specifc crea‑
tive sectors 
Limitations: 
• Limited coverage of domestic cultural production and consumption, 
which are signifcant aspects of CCIs 
• Challenges in capturing digital trade in creative services, an increasingly 
important component of the creative economy 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
Challenges and Opportunities in Creative and Cultural Industries Measurement 217 
• Difculty in distinguishing between creative and non‑creative components 
of goods, potentially leading to overestimation of creative trade 
• Reliance on customs data, which may not fully capture intangible or digi‑
tal creative products 
Furthermore, UNCTAD’s analysis of South‑South trade in creative goods 
UNCTAD’s research has highlighted the growing importance of South‑South 
trade in creative goods. For example, their analysis showed that between 
2002 and 2015, South‑South trade in creative goods more than tripled, from 
$7.8 billion to $27.8 billion. This trend is particularly signifcant in sec‑
tors like design, where developing countries have become major exporters. 
For instance, China has emerged as the world’s largest exporter of creative 
goods, with its exports growing from $32 billion in 2002 to $168 billion 
in 2015. This data has been crucial in highlighting the shifting dynamics of 
the global creative economy and the growing role of developing countries 
in CCIs. 
7 .4.1.3 WIPO (World Intellectual Property Organisation) 
WIPO provides valuable data on IP rights, which is crucial for many CCIs. 
Its work ofers insights into countries’ and industries’ innovation and creative 
outputs. 
Key contributions: 
• WIPO IP Statistics Data Centre: This centre provides data on patents, 
trademarks, industrial designs and other forms of IP across countries. For 
example, it ofers time series data on trademark applications by sector, 
including those related to CCIs. 
• Studies on Copyright Industries: Periodic reports on the economic con‑
tribution of copyright‑based industries. These studies use a standardised 
methodology to measure the value added, employment and trade perfor ‑
mance of industries that rely on copyright protection. 
• Global Innovation Index: Annual rankings of countries’ innovation ca‑
pabilities, including creative outputs. The creative outputs pillar includes 
indicators such as trademark applications, industrial designs, ICTs and 
business model creation. 
• Creative Economy Notes: A series of publications focusing on specifc as‑
pects of IP in the creative economy. For example, a recent note examined 
the role of IP rights in the global flm industry. 
Strengths: 
• Comprehensive data on IP registrations and applications, providing a 
proxy for creative and innovative activity 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
218 The Political Economy of the Creative and Cultural Industries 
• Standardised methodologies for measuring copyright industries, enabling 
cross‑country comparisons 
• Global coverage and long‑time series, allowing for trend analysis and in‑
ternational benchmarking 
• Detailed sector‑specifc data ofering insights into diferent segments of the 
creative economy 
Limitations: 
• Focus on formal IP registrations may underrepresent informal creative ac‑
tivities, which are signifcant in many developing countries 
• Challenges in linking IP data directly to economic outcomes in CCIs, as 
not all IP translates into market success 
• Limited coverage of non‑IP‑intensive cultural activities, potentially un‑
dervaluing certain forms of traditional or community‑based cultural 
expression 
• Difculty in capturing the total value of IP in the digital age, where en‑
forcement and monetisation challenges are prevalent 
As a highlight, WIPO’s analysis of the economic contribution of copyright 
industries WIPO’s studies on the economic contribution of copyright indus‑
tries have provided valuable insights into the importance of CCIs in national 
economies. For example, a study in Canada found that copyright‑based in‑
dustries contributed 5.59% to GDP and 5.96% to employment in 2019. In 
China, these industries accounted for 7.35% of GDP and 8.46% of national 
employment in 2018. These studies have been conducted in over 50 coun‑
tries, ofering a rich dataset for understanding the economic impact of copy‑
right‑intensive creative sectors across diferent economic contexts. 
7.4.2 National Statistical Ofces 
NSOs are primary sources of ofcial statistics on CCIs within individual 
countries. Their approaches and coverage vary signifcantly, refecting difer‑
ences in national priorities, resources and conceptions of CCIs. 
Key roles of NSOs in CCI data: 
• Conducting cultural participation surveys to measure engagement with 
various cultural activities 
• Compiling economic statistics on cultural and creative sectors, including 
GDP contribution, employment and business demographics 
• Developing national satellite accounts for culture to provide a more com‑
prehensive picture of the cultural economy 
• Collecting data on cultural employment and occupations, including both 
cultural and non‑cultural sectors 
 
 
  
 
 
  
 
 
 
 
  
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
Challenges and Opportunities in Creative and Cultural Industries Measurement 219 
Examples of leading practices: 
• United Kingdom’s Department for Digital, Culture, Media & Sport 
(DCMS): Produces annual Creative Industries Economic Estimates, using 
a robust methodology for defning and measuring the creative economy 
(DCMS, 2021). The DCMS approach includes both creative industries 
and creative occupations outside these industries, providing a comprehen‑
sive view of the creative economy. 
• Statistics Canada: Developed the Canadian Framework for Culture Statistics 
and produced regular reports on the cultural economy (Statistics Canada, 
2011). Their approach includes culture and sport domains and provides 
detailed data on GDP, jobs and consumer spending related to culture. 
• Australian Bureau of Statistics: The ABS conducts comprehensive surveys on 
cultural participation and produces detailed satellite accounts of cultural and 
creative activity (ABS, 2020). These satellite accounts provide insights into the 
direct and indirect economic contribution of cultural and creative activities. 
• Netherlands’ Centraal Bureau voor de Statistiek (CBS): Produces the Cul‑
ture and Media Monitor, which provides detailed statistics on cultural 
participation, employment in the cultural sector and government expendi‑
ture on culture. 
• Brazil’s Instituto Brasileiro de Geografa e Estatística (IBGE): Developed 
the System of Information and Cultural Indicators (SIIC) in collabora‑
tion with the Ministry of Culture. IBGE produces regular reports on the 
cultural economy, including data on cultural employment, household ex‑
penditure on culture and the contribution of cultural activities to GDP. 
• Colombia’s Department of National Statistics (DANE): DANE has developed 
the Satellite Account of Culture and Orange Economy in partnership with 
the Ministry of Culture. This account provides detailed information on the 
economic contribution of cultural and creative activities, including their share 
of GDP, employment fgures and export data. The initiative is part of Colom‑
bia’s broader strategy to promote its “Orange Economy” (creative economy). 
• Korea’s Ministry of Culture, Sports and Tourism: Conducts an annual 
survey on the creative industries, providing detailed data on revenues, em‑
ployment and exports across various creative sectors. 
Strengths: 
• Detailed national‑level data, often with regional breakdowns 
• Integration with broader economic and social statistics, allowing for con‑
textual analysis 
• Ability to conduct specialised surveys and data collection exercises tai‑
lored to national priorities 
• Ofcial status, providing credibility and often mandatory reporting from 
businesses 
 
 
 
  
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
220 The Political Economy of the Creative and Cultural Industries 
Limitations: 
• Lack of international comparability due to varying defnitions and 
methodologies 
• Resource constraints, particularly in developing countries, leading to gaps 
in data collection 
• Challenges in keeping pace with rapidly evolving digital and creative 
sectors 
• Potential political infuence on defnitions and methodologies, particularly 
in countries where CCIs are seen as strategically important 
7.4.3 Industry Associations and Trade Bodies 
Industry associations and trade bodies often collect and publish data specifc 
to their sectors, providing valuable insights into the dynamics of creative 
industries. These organisations directly access industry players and can often 
provide timelier and sector‑specifc data than ofcial statistics. 
Examples of crucial industry data sources: 
• International Federation of the Phonographic Industry (IFPI): Publishes 
annual Global Music Reports with data on recorded music revenues and 
consumption patterns. For example, their 2021 report revealed that global 
recorded music revenues grew by 7.4% in 2020, despite the COVID‑19 
pandemic, driven by streaming growth. 
• International Publishers Association (IPA): This organisation produces an‑
nual reports on global publishing statistics. Its data covers book publish‑
ing across various markets, including information on new titles published, 
market size and digital vs. print sales. 
• Motion Picture Association (MPA): Releases data on global box ofce 
revenues and flm industry economics. Their THEME Report provides a 
comprehensive analysis of the global theatrical and home/mobile enter ‑
tainment market. 
• Interactive Software Federation of Europe (ISFE): Provides data on the Eu‑
ropean video game market, including information on market size, player 
demographics and gaming platforms. 
• World Association of Newspapers and News Publishers (WAN‑IFRA): 
WAN‑IFRA publishes the World Press Trends report, which ofers in‑
sights into newspaper circulation, advertising revenues and digital news 
consumption. 
• International Confederation of Societies of Authors and Composers 
(CISAC): Produces the Global Collections Report, providing data on roy‑
alties collected for creators across diferent repertoires (music, audiovis‑
ual, visual arts, drama, literature). 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
Challenges and Opportunities in Creative and Cultural Industries Measurement 221 
Strengths: 
• Detailed industry‑specifc data, often providing granular insights not avail‑
able from ofcial sources 
• Access to proprietary information from member companies, ofering 
unique perspectives on industry dynamics 
• Timely insights into market trends and developments, often more 
up‑to‑date than government statistics 
• Global coverage for many industries, allowing for international comparisons 
• Deep understanding of sector‑specifc issues and metrics 
Limitations: 
• Potential bias towards more prominent industry players, as smaller or in‑
formal actors may be underrepresented 
• Inconsistent methodologies across diferent associations, making cross‑sec‑
tor comparisons challenging 
• Limited coverage of informal or emerging subsectors that may not have 
established industry bodies 
• Potential confict of interest in data presentation, as associations may have 
advocacy agendas 
• Varying levels of methodological transparency and data accessibility 
7.5 Challenges in Data Collection and Harmonisation 
The measurement and analysis of CCIs have gained signifcant attention in 
recent years due to their growing economic importance and cultural signif‑
cance (UNESCO, 2009). However, despite the rich data sources, research‑
ers and policymakers need help collecting and harmonising data on CCIs 
worldwide. These challenges stem from the creative economy’s complex and 
rapidly evolving nature and methodological and practical issues in data col‑
lection (Bakhshi et al., 2013). 
One of the primary challenges in CCI data collection is the need for a 
universally accepted defnition of what constitutes the CCIs. Countries defne 
CCIs diferently based on cultural, economic and political contexts (OECD, 
2018). For example, some nations include software development in their 
defnition of creative industries, while others do not. This variability in def‑
nitions leads to signifcant difculties in international comparisons and can 
result in under‑ or over‑estimating the size and impact of CCIs depending on 
the defnition used (Flew, 2012). 
Moreover, there are ongoing debates over including specifc sectors, such 
as gastronomy, sports or gambling, within the scope of CCIs. The chal‑
lenge of distinguishing between creative and non‑creative activities within 
 222 The Political Economy of the Creative and Cultural Industries 
industries further complicates this defnitional issue (Throsby, 2008). As new 
forms of creative expression and business models continually emerge, static 
defnitions of CCIs are increasingly challenged, making it difcult to develop 
consistent time series data. 
To address these defnitional challenges, various international organi‑
sations have proposed standardised frameworks. UNESCO’s FCS and the 
OECD’s work on developing guidelines for measuring cultural and creative 
sectors represent signifcant eforts in this direction (OECD, 2018; UNESCO, 
2009). Additionally, academic proposals for more fexible and inclusive def‑
nitions of CCIs have emerged, such as the work by Bakhshi et al. (2013), 
which advocates for a more dynamic approach to defning creative industries. 
The rapid digitalisation of many creative activities poses another set of sig‑
nifcant challenges for traditional data collection methods. Capturing value 
creation in free digital services, measuring cross‑border digital transactions 
and accounting for user‑generated content and prosumption activities have 
become increasingly complex (OECD, 2020). The economics of streaming 
and on‑demand content delivery, as well as the impact of social media plat‑
forms on creative industries, require new measurement approaches that are 
still in development. These digital challenges can lead to a potential underes‑
timation of the economic contribution of digital CCIs in ofcial statistics and 
create difculties in measuring cultural participation in online environments. 
The OECD’s work on measuring the digital economy, including eforts to 
develop new indicators for digital trade, represents a step towards addressing 
these issues (OECD, 2020). Researchers such as Hadley et al. (2021) have 
also tried to develop new metrics for digital cultural participation. 
A third major challenge is measuring the informal sector and non‑market 
activities within CCIs. Many cultural and creative activities occur outside 
formal economic structures, making them difcult to capture in ofcial sta‑
tistics. These include amateur and volunteer cultural activities, informal cul‑
tural employment and entrepreneurship, traditional cultural practices, and 
community‑based cultural events (Throsby, 2010). The implications of this 
challenge are particularly pronounced in developing countries, where a sig‑
nifcant portion of creative activities may occur in the informal sector. This 
can lead to underestimating the true scope and impact of CCIs and create 
difculties in designing appropriate support policies for informal creative 
workers (UNESCO, 2018). To address this, some countries have begun de‑
veloping cultural satellite accounts to capture non‑market activities. The use 
of time‑use surveys to measure participation in informal cultural activities 
and the adaptation of ILO methodologies for measuring informal employ‑
ment in the cultural sector are other promising approaches (ILO, 2018). 
Data gaps in developing countries present another signifcant challenge. 
Many developing nations need more fnancial resources, human capital and 
institutional capacity to collect comprehensive data on their cultural and 
 Challenges and Opportunities in Creative and Cultural Industries Measurement 223 
creative sectors (UNESCO, 2018). This results in an incomplete global pic‑
ture of CCIs, with signifcant blind spots in the Global South. It also cre‑
ates difculties in assessing the potential of CCIs for development in these 
countries and challenges in designing appropriate policies and support 
measures. International organisations have responded to this challenge with 
capacity‑building initiatives, such as UNESCO’s Culture|2030 Indicators 
programme. South‑South cooperation in cultural statistics and using alterna‑
tive data sources (e.g., big data, satellite imagery) to complement traditional 
surveys are other strategies being explored to address these data gaps (UN‑
ESCO, 2019). 
Finally, even when data is available, ensuring its comparability across dif‑
ferent sources, countries and periods remains a signifcant challenge. Incon‑
sistent classifcations and defnitions, varying data collection methodologies 
and frequencies, and challenges reconciling data from various administra‑
tive systems contribute to this issue (OECD, 2018). The rapid evolution of 
industry structures and business models, mainly due to digitalisation, can 
make historical comparisons problematic. These harmonisation challenges 
make meaningful international comparisons of CCIs difcult to assess global 
trends and patterns in the creative economy. They also create obstacles to 
evidence‑based policy‑making at the international level. Eforts to address 
these challenges include international initiatives to standardise cultural statis‑
tics, the development of crosswalks between diferent classifcation systems, 
and promoting metadata sharing and methodological transparency (OECD, 
2018; UNESCO, 2009). 
In conclusion, while signifcant progress has been made in measuring 
CCIs, substantial challenges remain. Addressing these challenges requires 
collaboration between NSOs, international organisations, researchers and 
industry stakeholders. As the creative economy continues to evolve, so must 
our approaches to measuring and understanding its dynamics. Future re‑
search should focus on developing more robust methodologies for capturing 
digital and informal creative activities, improving data collection in develop‑
ing countries and enhancing the comparability of CCI data across diferent 
contexts. 
Developing new economic models is another important avenue. These 
models should be more sophisticated and capable of better capturing the 
value creation processes in CCIs, including non‑market and spillover efects. 
Improving metadata standards is also crucial to developing more comprehen‑
sive standards for CCI statistics to enhance transparency and comparability. 
Fostering public‑private partnerships is also a crucial strategy, encouraging 
collaboration between government statistical agencies, academic research‑
ers and private sector data providers to create more comprehensive data‑
sets on CCIs. Additionally, integrating cultural and creative indicators into 
broader development metrics is essential, as well as working to incorporate 
 
  
 
 
 
 
 
 
224 The Political Economy of the Creative and Cultural Industries 
CCI measures into broader frameworks for measuring social progress and 
sustainable development. 
As the creative economy continues to evolve and grow, developing ro‑
bust, comprehensive and nuanced data sources will be crucial for informed 
policymaking and a deeper understanding of the role of CCIs in social and 
economic development. By addressing current challenges and embracing in‑
novative data collection and analysis approaches, we can work towards a 
more accurate and holistic understanding of the creative economy’s impact 
and potential. This ongoing efort to improve CCI data collection and anal‑
ysis refects the growing recognition of the sector’s importance in driving 
innovation, economic growth and cultural enrichment. As these initiatives 
progress, they will provide policymakers, researchers and industry stakehold‑
ers with increasingly valuable tools for understanding and nurturing the crea‑
tive economy in diverse forms. 
7.6 A Brief Representative (fnd the complete report in www. 
valiatileandro.com) Case I: Brazilian CCIs GDP Based on 
Creative Occupations 
The measurement of GDP for the CCIs in Brazil, as presented by Valiati 
et al. (2023), represents an innovative approach to quantifying the economic 
contribution of these sectors using a creative occupations methodology. This 
work, commissioned by Itau Foundation, ofers valuable insights into new 
ways of capturing the impact of the creative economy, particularly in devel‑
oping country contexts. It provides a template for other nations seeking to 
understand better and leverage their creative sectors. 
A key strength of the approach is its use of the creative intensity model 
to defne the boundaries of the CCI sector. This model, initially developed 
by Bakhshi et al. (2013), identifes creative sectors based on the percentage 
of workers in creative occupations. This allows the defnition to evolve with 
economic changes over time. The researchers applied fve criteria to evaluate 
creative occupations in Brazil: ability to generate new processes, resistance 
to mechanisation, non‑repetitive work, creative contribution to the value 
chain and work involving interpretation rather than mere transformation. 
This methodology resulted in the identifcation of 13 creative sectors, ranging 
from fashion and artisanal activities to software development and visual arts. 
The GDP calculation takes an income‑based approach, aiming to capture 
the nuances of the CCI sector better than traditional production or expenditure 
methods. The researchers combined data from multiple sources to estimate 
four key components: wage mass, proft mass, taxes and other remunerations 
like digital income. Importantly, this method allows for the inclusion of both 
formal and informal economic activity, addressing a signifcant gap in many 
previous studies of the creative economy in developing countries. 
 
 
 
 
 
  
 
  
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
Challenges and Opportunities in Creative and Cultural Industries Measurement 225 
This approach to measuring CCIs ofers several advantages over tra‑
ditional methods. Focusing on creative occupations rather than industry 
classifcations can better capture the dynamic nature of creative work that 
often spans multiple sectors. The income‑based calculation also provides 
a more comprehensive picture of economic value creation in CCIs, includ‑
ing informal and digital activities that conventional GDP measurements 
may miss. 
Methodology: The researchers employed an innovative income‑based ap‑
proach to calculate the GDP of CCIs, diverging from traditional production 
or expenditure methods. This approach was chosen to capture better the 
nuances and specifcities of the creative sector, including informal economic 
activities and digital income streams. 
The calculation of GDP for CCIs was based on four main components: 
1 Wage Mass (WM): Calculated using data from the Continuous National 
Household Sample Survey (PNADc), which covers both formal and in‑
formal employment. This approach allowed for a more comprehensive 
capture of labour income in the sector, including second jobs and infor ‑
mal work. 
2 Proft Mass (PM): Estimated using data from the Annual Service Survey 
(PAS) and the Annual Industrial Survey – Enterprise (PIA‑Enterprise). 
The researchers applied a double‑weighting method based on wage 
mass and business fabric composition to estimate revenues for specifc 
CCI sectors. 
3 Taxes: Calculated using data from the Tables of Resources and Uses (TRU) 
provided by the Brazilian Institute of Geography and Statistics (IBGE). 
4 Other Remunerations: This category included income from digital 
sources, such as data from the Central Ofce for Collection and Distribu‑
tion (ECAD) for music rights. 
The researchers also incorporated data from the Rouanet Law accountability 
reports to capture information on intermittent or freelance work and rentals, 
which are signifcant in the CCI sector. 
Sectors Considered: The study used a creative intensity model to defne 
the boundaries of the CCI sector. This dynamic approach identifes creative 
sectors based on the percentage of workers in creative occupations. The re‑
searchers applied fve criteria to evaluate creative occupations: 
1 Ability to generate new processes 
2 Resistance to mechanisation 
3 Non‑repetitive work 
4 Creative contribution to the value chain 
5 Work involving interpretation rather than mere transformation 
 
  
  
  
  
  
  
  
  
  
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
226 The Political Economy of the Creative and Cultural Industries 
Based on these criteria, 13 creative sectors were identifed: 
1 Fashion 
2 Artisanal activities 
3 Publishing 
4 Cinema, radio and TV 
5 Music 
6 Development of software and digital games 
7 Other information technology services 
8 Architecture 
9 Advertising and business services 
10 Design 
11 Performing arts 
12 Visual arts 
13 Museums and heritage 
This comprehensive list encompasses various creative activities, from tra‑
ditional cultural industries to more technology‑driven sectors. The results 
indicate that the GDP contribution of CCIs in Brazil was between 2% and 
3.5% in 2020, placing it among the top ten sectors of the economy. While 
this fgure is broadly in line with global UNESCO estimates of around 3% 
GDP contribution for CCIs, the methodology reveals exciting insights into 
the composition of this economic activity in Brazil. 
Key fndings include: 
1 Proft mass and taxes represent a signifcant portion of the CCI sector’s 
GDP, refecting the prevalence of micro‑entrepreneurs and small busi‑
nesses in the sector. 
2 The wage mass of the CCI sector showed an increasing trend in recent 
years, but with some volatility. It averaged about 0.19% of the national 
GDP over the ten‑year period covered by the PNADc data. 
3. Second incomes, captured through the PNADc, represented, on average, 
1.82% of the wage mass in the CCI sector. 
4 The proft mass of the CCI sector represented about 2% of the national 
GDP in 2020. 
5 While income from digital sources is growing, it still represents a relatively 
small portion of the CCI sector’s total GDP. This is partly due to meas‑
urement challenges rather than necessarily refecting the true economic 
impact of digital activities. 
6 The study revealed signifcant regional variations in the contribution of 
CCIs to GDP, with São Paulo state being a clear outlier regarding its share 
of CCI economic activity. 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
Challenges and Opportunities in Creative and Cultural Industries Measurement 227 
The work also highlights several ongoing challenges in measuring the creative 
economy. The researchers note difculties in fully capturing income from the 
digital sector, second jobs and rental activities, which likely leads to some under‑
estimation of the sector’s actual economic impact. There are also limitations in 
the available data, particularly around informal economic activity and disaggre‑
gated fnancial information for creative businesses. Despite these challenges, the 
Brazil methodology ofers valuable lessons for measuring CCIs in other contexts, 
especially developing economies. The creative occupations approach allows for a 
more dynamic and context‑specifc defnition of the creative economy compared 
to static industry classifcations. The income‑based GDP calculation method also 
provides a framework for capturing economic activity that may be missed by 
traditional national accounts, particularly informal and digital transactions. 
The study’s recommendations for improving data collection are also note‑
worthy. These include developing better microdata on revenues and profts 
in creative sectors, improving tracking of digital income streams and creat‑
ing mechanisms to capture gig economy activity in CCIs. Implementing such 
measures could signifcantly enhance the accuracy and comprehensiveness of 
creative economy measurements globally. 
As the global economy continues to evolve towards knowledge‑based and 
creative activities, such innovative measurement techniques will be crucial 
for informing efective policy and investment decisions in the CCI sector. The 
work done in Brazil demonstrates the potential for more nuanced and compre‑
hensive assessments of the creative economy’s contribution to national GDP. 
Moreover, this approach to measuring CCIs can have broader implications 
for economic policy and development strategies. A more accurate picture of 
the creative economy’s size and composition can help policymakers better 
target support and investment in these sectors. This is particularly important 
in developing economies where CCIs can ofer opportunities for inclusive 
growth and job creation. 
The methodology also highlights the importance of considering formal and 
informal economic activities in assessing the true impact of CCIs. This is espe‑
cially relevant in countries with large informal sectors, where traditional GDP 
measurements may signifcantly underestimate the contribution of creative work. 
Furthermore, the focus on creative occupations rather than industry clas‑
sifcations aligns well with emerging trends in the labour market, such as 
the rise of the gig economy and increasing fuidity between sectors. This ap‑
proach may prove more adaptable to future changes like work and the struc‑
ture of creative industries. 
In conclusion, the work done by Valiati et  al. in measuring the GDP 
contribution of CCIs in Brazil represents a signifcant advancement in our 
understanding of the creative economy. While further refnement and ad‑
aptation may be needed for application in diferent national contexts, this 
 
  
 
 
 
 
 
  
 
 
 
228 The Political Economy of the Creative and Cultural Industries 
methodology ofers a promising template for more accurate and comprehen‑
sive assessments of the economic value of creative and cultural activities. As 
countries worldwide seek to diversify their economies and tap into the poten‑
tial of the creative sectors, such innovative measurement techniques will be 
invaluable in guiding policy and investment decisions. The complete study is 
here: https://issuu.com/itaucultural/docs/obs34_eng 
7.7 A Brief Representative Case II: Relative Values (fnd the 
complete report in www.culturalvalue.org) 
The Relative Values project, funded by the UK Arts and Humanities Research 
Council (AHRC), was a ground‑breaking initiative led by principal investi‑
gators Dr. Leandro Valiati and Professor Paul Heritage. The project aimed 
to develop a new methodology for evaluating arts organisations’ socio‑
economic impact in fragile urban communities. This collaborative research 
efort brought together People’s Palace Projects at Queen Mary University of 
London and the Federal University of Rio Grande do Sul’s Centre of Studies 
in Creative Economy and Culture (NECCULT), focusing on four arts organi‑
sations in the United Kingdom and Brazil: 
1 Contact Theatre (Manchester, United Kingdom) 
2 Battersea Arts Centre (London, United Kingdom) 
3 Maré Centre for the Arts (Rio de Janeiro, Brazil) 
4 The Agency of Youth Networks (Rio de Janeiro, Brazil) 
Co‑created Methodology: A distinguishing feature of the Relative Values 
project was its deeply collaborative, co‑created methodology. This approach 
was fundamental to developing a system that could efectively capture and 
articulate cultural activities’ complex, nuanced impacts in vulnerable urban 
contexts. The co‑creation process involved several key stages: 
1 Initial consultations: The research team conducted in‑depth discussions with 
each partner organisation to comprehensively understand their work, impact 
goals and the specifc challenges they face in their respective communities. 
2 Collaborative workshops: A series of workshops brought together re‑
searchers and representatives from the arts organisations to collectively 
defne key areas of impact and brainstorm potential indicators. This en‑
sured that the resulting framework would be both academically rigorous 
and practically relevant. 
3 Iterative development: The questionnaires and survey methods were de‑
veloped through an iterative process, with multiple rounds of feedback 
and refnement involving all stakeholders. This approach allowed for the 
incorporation of diverse perspectives and expertise. 
 
 
 
  
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
Challenges and Opportunities in Creative and Cultural Industries Measurement 229 
4 Training of local agents: To ensure consistent and efective data collection, 
local agents from each organisation were trained to apply the question‑
naires. This step was crucial in building capacity within the organisations 
and ensuring the sustainability of the evaluation process. 
5 Joint analysis and interpretation: The research team worked closely with 
the partner organisations to analyse and interpret the results, ensuring that 
local context and insights were fully integrated into the fndings. 
This co‑created approach resulted in a academically robust methodology 
deeply rooted in the practical realities and needs of the arts organisations 
and the communities they serve. 
Methodology Structure: The research methodology combined quantitative 
and qualitative approaches, structured around three main axes: 
1 Context: This axis involved gathering publicly available socio‑
economic data about each territory. It included indicators such as the 
Index of Multiple Deprivation (IMD), life expectancy, infant mortal‑
ity rates and unemployment fgures. This contextual information was 
crucial for understanding the baseline conditions in which each organi‑
sation operates. 
2 Operations: Information about each arts organisation was collected via 
detailed questionnaires. This included data on organisational structure, 
funding sources, programming, audience demographics and economic re‑
sults. The operational data provided insights into how each organisation 
functions within its context. 
3 Impact: This core component involved surveying participants in the or ‑
ganisations’ projects using a comprehensive questionnaire of approxi‑
mately 40 questions. The survey was designed to capture various impacts, 
from individual‑level changes to broader community efects. 
System of Indicators: The project developed a sophisticated system of indica‑
tors covering three interconnected areas: 
1 Context Indicators: These measured local development and social vulner ‑
ability, providing a clear picture of the challenges and opportunities in 
each territory. 
Example indicators: 
• IMD score 
• Life expectancy 
• Infant mortality rate 
• Unemployment rate 
• Access to public services (e.g., healthcare, education) 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
  
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
230 The Political Economy of the Creative and Cultural Industries 
2 Operation Indicators: These refected the organisations’ self‑image, focus‑
ing on the relationship between activities and territories, project imple‑
mentation, audience profles and economic results. 
Example indicators: 
• Number of projects delivered annually 
• Percentage of participants from low‑income backgrounds 
• Diversity of creative teams (e.g., percentage of BAME artists) 
• Annual budget and funding sources 
• Number of partnerships with local organisations 
3 Impact Indicators: The most innovative aspect of the methodology, these 
indicators focused on three key dimensions: 
a Individual capacity for agency: Measuring participants’ ability to 
refect, process social experiences, and develop everyday skills. Ex‑
ample indicators: 
• Capacity for refection 
• Social mobilisation 
• Increase in social capital 
• Expansion of perspectives 
b Relationship to arts and creativity: Assessing changes in cultural en‑
gagement, understanding of the arts sector, and creative skills devel‑
opment. Example indicators: 
• Cultural democracy 
• Understanding of working in the arts sector 
• Cultural engagement 
• Perceived proximity to the arts sector 
• Viability of working in the arts sector 
• Creative skills development 
c Relationship to territory: Evaluating how participation afects indi‑
viduals’ connection to and understanding of their local area. Exam‑
ple indicators: 
• Access to local spaces 
• Lived space (changes in how participants move through and use their 
local area) 
• Recognition of local creative networks 
• Understanding of social reality 
• Identifcation and belonging to the territory 
Each indicator’s performance was classifed on a scale from Low (0.0–0.5) to 
Outstanding (0.9–1.0), providing a nuanced picture of impact across difer ‑
ent dimensions. 
Additionally, the methodology included indicators for direct territorial 
transformation: 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
  
 
 
 
Challenges and Opportunities in Creative and Cultural Industries Measurement 231 
Example indicators: 
• New use for spaces 
• Community engagement 
• Better perception of the territory by residents 
• New business creation 
• Increased access to spaces within the territory 
• Safety in public spaces 
• Improved use of public spaces 
• Creation of new social spaces 
• More positive description of the territory 
• Infrastructure improvements 
Key Findings: The research yielded several signifcant fndings: 
1 Incubators of innovation: Small‑scale arts organisations were found to 
create conditions for incubating creative economy initiatives in fragile ur‑
ban communities, often acting as catalysts for local development. 
2 Startup‑like characteristics: These organisations’ impact was characterised 
by qualities typically associated with rapid‑growth startups, including in‑
novation, entrepreneurship, resilience, networking, social capital develop‑
ment, sustainability and enhanced agency. 
3 Breaking isolation: The methodology demonstrated the efectiveness of 
strategies these organisations develop to break their isolation from other 
makers/practitioners, fostering creative networks and knowledge exchange. 
4 Alternative value creation: The research showed how peripheral, vulner ‑
able urban communities use creativity as a positive value that ofers an 
alternative to resource‑intensive business models. 
5 Territorial signifcance: The study highlighted the importance of under ‑
standing arts organisations’ impact on their specifc territories, revealing 
how their efectiveness is deeply tied to the local context. 
Specifc Results: The study produced detailed impact profles for each partici‑
pating organisation. For example: 
Contact Theatre greatly impacted individual capacity for agency and out‑
standing performance in cultural democracy and proximity to the arts. How‑
ever, it had only an intermediate impact on participants’ understanding of 
social reality and sense of belonging. 
Battersea Arts Centre demonstrated high impact on personal agency of 
participants and outstanding performance in cultural democracy and prox‑
imity to the arts. However, it showed lower impact on cultural engagement 
and territorial transformation. 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
232 The Political Economy of the Creative and Cultural Industries 
Maré Centre for the Arts achieved a high impact on participants’ personal 
agency, outstanding performance in cultural democracy and proximity to the 
arts, and a signifcant impact on participants’ relationship to the territory. 
The Agency of Youth Networks showed consistently high impact across 
all indicators of personal agency, outstanding performance in cultural de‑
mocracy, and high impact on participants’ understanding of social reality 
and sense of belonging. 
Implications and Future Directions: The Relative Values methodology 
represents a signifcant advancement in measuring the impact of arts or ‑
ganisations in vulnerable communities. Combining economic and social in‑
dicators through a co‑created process provides a more comprehensive and 
contextually relevant picture of value creation than traditional GDP‑based 
approaches. 
The project demonstrates the potential for arts organisations to act as key 
drivers of local development in areas facing multiple challenges. It also high‑
lights the importance of context‑specifc measurement, recognising that the 
value created by these organisations is deeply tied to the territories in which 
they operate. 
The Relative Values methodology shows promise for broader application 
and further refnement. The researchers have already adapted it into a toolkit 
for young artists and cultural producers developing new projects in fragile ter‑
ritories. Additionally, it will be used to evaluate 25 arts projects across Brazil 
between 2018 and 2024, providing an opportunity for longitudinal study and 
methodology refnement. This ongoing development and application will likely 
lead to more robust and generalisable indicators over time, while emphasising 
co‑creation and local relevance. As such, the Relative Values project provides 
valuable insights into the current impact of arts organisations in vulnerable 
communities and lays the groundwork for more nuanced, context‑sensitive 
impact evaluation in the cultural sector more broadly.
